Data-Driven Tuning in Kernel Specification Tests: Selection Asymptotics, Validity, and Power Thanks: I acknowledge support from the Research Development Fund (RDF-23-02-022) of the Xi’an Jiaotong-Liverpool University. I thank valuable comments from Migule A. Delgado, Juan Carlos Escanciano, Xiaojun Song and seminar participants at the The 7th International Symposia on Nonparametric Statistics (ISNPS 2026). All errors are my own.
Yuhao Li

Abstract
We develop model checks for conditional moment restrictions whose tuning parameters, the kernel bandwidth and the regularization level, are selected by cross-validation. The tests regress the estimated residuals on the covariates via kernel ridge regression (KRR), yielding a coefficient function in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) that vanishes under the null and is nonzero for any misspecification not annihilated by the Neyman projection. Our central contribution is a complete asymptotic theory of the data-driven tuning parameter: its limit law under the null, under fixed alternatives, and under local alternatives, and bootstrap size control under sample splitting with no convergence of the selector required. An exact energy identity shows that cross-validation maximizes the signal captured by the test (its noncentrality) and, absent signal, shrinks its noise floor. The tests are consistent against fixed alternatives, achieve n − 1 / 2 n^{-1/2} -rate sensitivity to local alternatives, and use Neyman-orthogonal projections so the model is never re-estimated within bootstrap resamples. Simulations show size close to nominal without user-chosen tuning, power comparable to the kernel conditional moment (KCM) test at its best kernel parameter, and robustness where fixed kernel parameters fail in higher dimensions. Three empirical applications illustrate the diagnostic role of the tests; we recommend a 15 15 – 20 % 20\% selection share for the sample-splitting protocol.



Keywords:
Conditional moment restriction, Kernel ridge regression, Model checking, Reproducing kernel Hilbert space, Tuning selection.

1
Introduction

Many models in the social sciences are characterized by conditional moment restrictions of the form

\displaystyle Y=\mathcal{M}_{\theta_{0}}(X)+\varepsilon_{0},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}\mid X)=0\quad\text{almost surely (a.s.), for some unknown }\theta_{0}\in\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}},

where the random vectors
S_{i}=(Y_{i},X_{i})
,
i=1,2,\dots
, are i.i.d. with probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{S}
,
Y\in\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
X\in\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and the residual function
\varepsilon:\mathcal{S}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
is known up to the finite-dimensional parameter
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
. We focus on the univariate case
q=1
; extension to
q>1
is straightforward, since the statistics aggregate over residual components (Remark
1
). This framework encompasses linear and nonlinear conditional mean regression, quantile regression, treatment effect models, and instrumental variables regression.

The objective is to test

H_{0}:\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon(S;\theta_{0})\mid X]=0\right)=1\text{ for some }\theta_{0}\in\Theta

against the alternative

H_{1}:\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon(S;\theta)\mid X]\neq 0\right)>0\text{ for all }\theta\in\Theta.

A natural starting point is to regress the estimated residuals on the covariates,
\varepsilon_{\hat{\theta}}=X^{\top}\omega+e
, and test
\omega=\boldsymbol{0}
against
\omega\neq\boldsymbol{0}
. This appealing idea has two limitations: the test is inconsistent, detecting only deviations representable by the chosen finite set of moments, and the estimated parameter
\hat{\theta}
enters the null distribution, leaving the statistic non-pivotal. Modern goodness-of-fit techniques transcend it, often at the cost of complexity that hinders implementation and interpretation.

The tuning problem.

Kernel-based omnibus tests, in particular the classical kernel conditional moment (KCM) test, require a bandwidth
\gamma
and, in regularized formulations, a smoothing parameter
\lambda
, to which size and power are highly sensitive. Standard practice fixes the bandwidth a priori, typically
\gamma=0.5
for a Gaussian kernel
[
Bierens, 1990
]
, or delegates it to a data-driven median heuristic
[
Escanciano, 2024
]
. The Gaussian-process framework of
Escanciano [2024]
unifies these kernel model checks in a single class of Neyman orthogonal kernel tests and holds the kernel fixed throughout; selecting the kernel-side tuning from data is posed there as an open problem (Section 7 of that paper). Both conventions are known failure modes in higher-dimensional settings: at
d=20
, the fixed default drives the size of the KCM test to essentially zero, while the median rule leaves it with almost no power. The tuning parameters are therefore not a technical detail but a first-order component of the testing procedure, and a specification test intended for use without expert intervention must treat them as such. This paper develops a framework for kernel-based CMR specification testing in which the tuning parameters are selected by cross-validation and the selection itself is part of the theory: we derive the asymptotic law of the data-driven selector, and we show that the resulting bootstrap test controls size without any convergence of the selector.

Related work.

Among practitioners, the most widely used specification tests are M-tests, introduced by
Newey [1985a]
,
Newey [1985b]
,
Tauchen [1985]
,
Wooldridge [1990]
. These tests assess a finite set of unconditional moment restrictions implied by the model and are inherently “directional”, probing only a limited set of directions in the space of alternatives. By contrast, omnibus tests are consistent against any form of misspecification, which matters when the nature of potential model failure is unknown. Two approaches exist. One compares the fitted parametric model to a nonparametric regression estimate, typically smoothed, in
Eubank and Spiegelman [1990]
,
Hardle and Mammen [1993]
,
Hong and White [1995]
,
Zheng [1996]
,
Ellison and Ellison [2000]
; the other applies integral transforms of the residuals rather than the residuals themselves, giving rise to the Integrated Conditional Moment (ICM) tests of
Bierens [1982]
,
Bierens and Ploberger [1997]
,
Stute [1997]
,
Delgado et al. [2006]
. For a comprehensive review, see
González-Manteiga and Crujeiras [2013]
.

The KRR approach.

In this paper we return to the regression idea and push it to its natural infinite-dimensional limit. Instead of a finite set of moments, we map the covariates into a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS)
\mathcal{H}_{k}
with reproducing kernel
k(\cdot,\cdot)
and consider the regularized population coefficient function

w^{*}_{\lambda}=(C+\lambda I)^{-1}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot)\right],

where
C=\mathbb{E}[k(X,\cdot)\otimes k(X,\cdot)]
is the second-moment operator on
\mathcal{H}_{k}
,
\lambda>0
is a fixed regularization parameter, and
\varepsilon_{0}:=\varepsilon(S;\theta_{0})
; the empirical analogue of
w^{*}_{\lambda}
is the kernel ridge regression (KRR) estimator. We show in Section
2
that the null hypothesis holds if and only if
w^{*}_{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{0}
in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
: for a universal kernel, the RKHS is dense in the space of continuous functions
[
Micchelli et al., 2006
]
, so
w^{*}_{\lambda}
approximates the conditional mean of the residual, which vanishes exactly under
H_{0}
. The function
w^{*}_{\lambda}
is thus the infinite-dimensional analogue of the regression coefficient
\omega
, and hence an interpretable diagnostic of model adequacy.

Contribution 1: projection test statistics.

Our first contribution is a class of test statistics based on projecting
w^{*}_{\lambda}
onto functions in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
. We consider two projections: (i) onto
w^{*}_{\lambda}
itself, which measures the overall magnitude of the misspecification through the RKHS norm of the fit; and (ii) onto the mean embedding of the residual,
m^{*}=\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot))
, the cross-product of the fit with the raw residual signal. The two statistics share a common structure and differ only in their spectral weights: deviations along the eigen-directions of the kernel operator are weighted by
\mu_{i}/(\mu_{i}+\lambda)
or
\mu_{i}/(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}
, where
\{\mu_{i}\}
are the eigenvalues of the integral operator, instead of by the eigenvalues themselves as in the classical KCM statistic; these weights define the null and local limit laws of Section
2
, and of the two the second lies closer to the classical form. The tests detect local (Pitman) alternatives at the parametric
n^{-1/2}
rate.

Our statistics can also be viewed as a modification of the RKHS-based tests of
Muandet et al. [2020]
,
Escanciano [2024]
: classical ICM statistics are V- or U-statistics with a Gaussian kernel at a fixed bandwidth, typically
0.5
;
Muandet et al. [2020]
allow general kernel choices but do not account for the estimation effect of nuisance parameters, and
Escanciano [2024]
restore validity through a Gaussian process approach with a Neyman orthogonal kernel; within that class, the present paper adds the data-driven selection of the kernel-side tuning, the object that paper poses as an open problem (Remark
6
and Section
4
). Our approach is further related to the orthogonal series regression of
Eubank and Spiegelman [1990]
, who test the significance of the first
p_{n}
series coefficients of an orthogonal basis expansion, with
p_{n}
growing with the sample size. Their test detects local alternatives only at the rate
O(p_{n}^{1/4}/n^{1/2})
, slower than the
n^{-1/2}
rate of our tests; the price is that our null distributions are non-pivotal, which necessitates the multiplier bootstrap. The RKHS formulation itself is connected to the integral transformation approach: the RKHS is isometrically isomorphic to the space of square-integrable functions
[
Carrasco et al., 2007
]
, and for shift-invariant kernels the connection is made explicit by Bochner’s theorem (Theorem 2.1 of
Muandet et al. [2020]
).

Estimation effects.

The statistics are completed by an estimation-effect correction. When the residuals are computed from an estimated parameter
\hat{\theta}
, the null distributions of the statistics are altered by the estimation effect. Following
Escanciano and Goh [2014]
,
Escanciano [2024]
,
Sant’Anna and Song [2019]
, we eliminate the first-order estimation effect through a Neyman orthogonality projection onto the orthogonal complement of the conditional score. The remainder is asymptotically negligible, and the orthogonalized statistics inherit the spectral form of their infeasible counterparts, with the projected null laws of Corollary
1
(Section
3
). Critical values come from the multiplier bootstrap, whose conditional consistency we establish.

Contribution 2: the tuning problem.

The statistics depend on two tuning parameters, the bandwidth
\gamma
of the kernel and the regularization parameter
\lambda
, which act as nuisance parameters for the testing problem. The central contribution of this paper is a complete theory of the tuning problem. The pair
(\gamma,\lambda)
is selected jointly by
k
-fold cross-validation on a separate selection sample and held fixed on the testing sample, under the protocol of Section
4
. We derive the asymptotic law of the selector in the three regimes relevant to the testing problem: under the null it converges to a random limit supported on the implemented tuning grid, so the selection is noise-driven and must not be read as an estimate of an optimal value; under a fixed alternative it is pinned down at the signal-maximizing configuration; under local alternatives it converges to a drifted random limit whose drift depends on the split between the selection and the testing samples. We further show that the bootstrap test controls size under this protocol without requiring any convergence of the selector at all. The key observation is that conditioning on the realized split makes the selected tuning a fixed grid point for the multiplier bootstrap, so size control needs only pointwise consistency of the bootstrap at that fixed tuning. The analysis also clarifies why the regularization parameter must be bounded away from zero. In contrast to the classical bandwidth-selection analysis for specification tests, which approximates the power function to second order
[
Gao and Gijbels, 2008
]
or selects the smoothing adaptively without a limit theory for the selection itself
[
Horowitz and Spokoiny, 2001
]
, the selector is here given a first-order theory in its own right. The projection family thus supplies the missing tuning layer for the kernel members of the Gaussian-process class of
Escanciano [2024]
, whose data-driven tuning that paper poses as an open problem; Remark
6
makes the relation precise, and the resolution is not the routine extension that the derivative-interchange route of their Remark 2 suggests, because the selector’s criterion fluctuates at the degenerate
m^{-1}
scale and retains a non-degenerate random limit.

Contribution 3: power and the selection mechanism.

The literature on power properties of conditional moment goodness-of-fit tests is largely diagnostic: omnibus tests are shown to have substantial local power only against alternatives lying in a finite-dimensional space, generally unknown to the researcher
[
Escanciano, 2009
]
; power is documented to deteriorate with the dimension of the conditioning variables, the curse of dimensionality
[
Lavergne and Patilea, 2008
,
Escanciano, 2006
]
; minimax bounds delimit the alternatives that consistent tests can detect
Guerre and Lavergne [2002]
; and the smoothing parameter is chosen by second-order approximations to the power function
Gao and Gijbels [2008]
or by adaptive selection without a limit theory for the choice itself
Horowitz and Spokoiny [2001]
. Power-enhancement techniques developed for high-dimensional tests
Fan et al. [2015]
combine test statistics rather than analyze tuning, and the kernel-choice literature for two-sample tests maximizes the signal-to-noise ratio of non-degenerate V-statistics
[
Gretton et al., 2012
,
Sutherland et al., 2017
,
Liu et al., 2020
]
, a setting without estimated nuisance parameters. None of these strands treats the tuning parameter as a nuisance of the test and analyzes data-driven tuning as a power mechanism. We show that the cross-validated choice does act as such a mechanism, through an exact energy decomposition of the misspecification energy into an uncaptured and a captured part: cross-validation minimizes the former and therefore maximizes the latter, namely the signal energy captured by the fitted coefficient (the test’s noncentrality); under the null it instead shrinks the noise floor of the statistic. The split between the selection and the testing samples controls the magnitude of the effect: a larger selection share pulls the selected configuration toward the signal-maximizing one, at the price of attenuating the alternative that the testing sample sees.

Simulations and applications.

Simulation studies confirm that the projection tests control size without any user-chosen tuning in most configurations and attain power comparable to the KCM test at its best kernel parameter. Their advantage is robustness rather than peak performance: in higher dimensions, where fixed kernel parameters (the default
\gamma=0.5
or the median rule) are catastrophic, the tests remain fully operational without user intervention. Three applications illustrate the method: the National Supported Work dataset, where the specification of a propensity score model and the null of a zero conditional average treatment effect are tested; the Mincer wage equation, where neither the linear schooling specification nor its augmentation is rejected; and a log-linear demand equation for cigarettes, where the specification is rejected under instrumental-variables residuals. Further simulation designs and results are reported in Appendix
G
.

Residual and projection notation.

Throughout,
\varepsilon_{0}:=\varepsilon(S;\theta_{0})
denotes the residual at the true parameter, decomposing into signal plus mean-zero noise (
\varepsilon_{0}=f(X)+v
, Section
2
), and the Neyman-orthogonality projection operator
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
and its empirical counterpart
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
, together with the projected residuals
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}
and related objects, are defined in Sections
2
and
3
.

Notation.

Convergence in probability is denoted
\xrightarrow{p}
and almost-sure convergence by
\xrightarrow{a.s.}
; deterministic convergence of real sequences is written with the ordinary arrow
\to
. Convergence in distribution, of real-valued statistics, random vectors, and random elements of Hilbert spaces, is denoted
\xrightarrow{d}
, while weak convergence of stochastic processes and other random elements of function spaces is denoted
\rightsquigarrow
in the relevant metric space, typically
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
, in the sense of
van der Vaart and Wellner [1996]
. The symbol
\Rightarrow
is reserved for logical implication. For the multiplier bootstrap we write
\xrightarrow{d^{*}}
for almost-sure conditional weak convergence given the data (Chapter 2.9 of
van der Vaart and Wellner [1996]
). The symbols
O_{p}
and
o_{p}
denote the usual stochastic orders.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
(test statistics): establishes the equivalence between the null hypothesis and the vanishing of
w^{*}_{\lambda}
, introduces the projection statistics, and derives their asymptotic laws under the assumption that
\theta_{0}
is known.
Section
3
(estimation effects): extends these results to the estimated case through Neyman orthogonal projections and constructs the multiplier bootstrap.
Section
4
(nuisance parameter selection), the centerpiece of the paper, develops the theory of tuning parameter selection: the cross-validation procedure and the process-level limits, and then the behavior of the data-driven test in the three regimes of the testing problem, namely its null distribution and size control, its consistency under fixed alternatives, and the laws of the selector and of the implemented statistic under local alternatives, closing with the energy-based power mechanism.
Section
5
(simulations and applications): provides simulation evidence and three empirical applications.
Section
6
concludes. All proofs are gathered in Appendix
B
.

2
Test Statistics and Their Asymptotic Properties

This section develops the building blocks of the proposed tests. Section
2.1
establishes that, under an integrally strictly positive definite kernel, the null hypothesis holds if and only if the population coefficient function
w^{*}_{\lambda}
vanishes in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
. Section
2.2
turns this equivalence into test statistics built on projections of
w^{*}_{\lambda}
onto
\mathcal{H}_{k}
, deriving their laws under the null, under fixed alternatives, and under local (Pitman) alternatives. Throughout,
(\gamma,\lambda)
are held fixed; their data-driven selection is analyzed in Section
4
, and the multiplier bootstrap implementing the null limits derived here is constructed in Section
3
.

Throughout this section, we assume that the true parameter
\theta_{0}
is known, and we denote the true residual vector by
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=\boldsymbol{Y}-\mathcal{M}_{\theta_{0}}(X)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Estimation effects are eliminated by the Neyman orthogonality projections of Section
3
.

2.1
Equivalence of the Null Hypothesis

The null hypothesis
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}|X)=0
is equivalent to the vanishing of the population coefficient function
w^{*}_{\lambda}
: a KRR fit
\hat{w}
that is nonzero in the RKHS therefore constitutes evidence against model adequacy. This subsection proceeds in four steps: the spectral setup linking population and empirical objects (
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
with its spectrum, the second-moment operators
C
and
C_{n}
, and the singular value decomposition of
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}
), yielding the empirical eigenbasis expansion of
\hat{w}
; a rotated basis, needed because empirical eigenvectors converge to population eigenfunctions only up to a rotation within eigenvalue clusters (Lemma
1
); the geometry of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
and the conditions (Assumptions
1
and
2
) under which
w^{*}_{\lambda}
is well defined and consistently estimated (Lemma
2
); and the equivalence itself (Theorem
1
).

Spectral setup.

We define the integral operator
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}:L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
as

(\boldsymbol{L}_{k}f)(y)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}k(x,y)f(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}(x)

where
\mathbb{P}_{X}
is a probability measure on
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
and
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
denote the eigenfunctions and eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
, respectively: the eigenfunctions are orthonormal in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
, and the eigenvalues are non-negative. While the Gram matrix
\boldsymbol{K}
with entries
K_{ij}=k(x_{i},x_{j})
satisfies
\boldsymbol{K}/n\to\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
in a suitable sense, the two objects act on fundamentally different spaces. To bridge this gap, we introduce the second-moment operator
C:\mathcal{H}_{k}\to\mathcal{H}_{k}
defined as

C=\mathbb{E}\left(k(X,\cdot)\otimes k(X,\cdot)\right),

and its empirical counterpart
C_{n}:\mathcal{H}_{k}\to\mathcal{H}_{k}
defined as

C_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}k(x_{i},\cdot)\otimes k(x_{i},\cdot).

For
f\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
, the tensor product acts as
(k(x_{i},\cdot)\otimes k(x_{i},\cdot))f=k(x_{i},\cdot)\langle k(x_{i},\cdot),f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
.

Next, endow
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the scaled inner product
\langle a,b\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}b_{i}
and define the restriction operator
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}:\mathcal{H}_{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}f=\left(f(x_{1}),\dots,f(x_{n})\right)^{\top}
. Its adjoint
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{H}_{k}
(with respect to the scaled inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
) is then given by
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}u=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}k(x_{i},\cdot)
for
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})^{\top}
. These definitions yield the factorizations
C_{n}=\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}_{n}
and
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}=\boldsymbol{K}/n
.

The empirical expansion and the rotated basis.

Our analysis of the KRR estimator
\hat{w}
uses the singular value decomposition (SVD) of the restriction operator
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}
:

\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\sigma_{i}}{n}\,\hat{e}_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\top}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nu_{i}^{1/2}n^{-1}\hat{e}_{i}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}^{\top},

where
\nu_{i}=\sigma_{i}^{2}/n
are the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{K}/n
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}
are the corresponding eigenvectors normalized as
n^{-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}=1
, and
\hat{e}_{i}
are the empirical eigenfunctions of the empirical second-moment operator
C_{n}
(the factor
n^{-1}
reflects the scaled adjoint defined above). This is equivalent to the representation
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\hat{e}_{i}
with standard-normalized eigenvectors
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}=1
(since
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}=\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
). Details, including the spectral relationship between
\boldsymbol{K}
and
C_{n}
, appear in Proposition
7
(Appendix
D
).

The KRR estimator
\hat{w}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\hat{w}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n+\lambda\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}^{1/2}n^{-1}\hat{e}_{j}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{j}^{\top}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\nu_{i}+\lambda)^{-1}n^{-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}^{\top}\right)\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\nu_{i}+\lambda)^{-1}\nu_{i}^{1/2}(n^{-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})\hat{e}_{i}.

The expansion is in the empirical eigenbasis
\{\hat{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. Because the population eigenvalues
\mu_{i}
may have multiplicity greater than one, the estimated eigenfunctions
\hat{e}_{i}
need not converge to the population eigenfunctions
e_{i}
individually: within a cluster of empirical eigenvalues converging to the same population value, the empirical eigenvectors are identified only up to an orthogonal rotation, and what converges is the eigenspace spanned by each cluster (Appendix
F
). The following lemma re-expresses the scaled KRR estimator in a rotated basis that converges to the population eigenfunctions uniformly within each cluster. Throughout,
\hat{m}:=n^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{0,l}k(X_{l},\cdot)=\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
denotes the empirical mean embedding.

Lemma 1
(Rotated eigenbasis of the KRR estimator)

Let the distinct positive eigenvalues of the population operator
C
be
\mu^{(1)}>\mu^{(2)}>\cdots
with multiplicities
m_{1},m_{2},\ldots
, and set
v_{k}:=\sum_{l=1}^{k}m_{l}
. Fix a cluster boundary
V
: throughout, assume the eigengap condition
\mu^{(V)}>\mu^{(V+1)}
together with the sample-size condition of Lemma
25
(
n>128\bar{k}^{2}\tau/(\mu^{(V)}-\mu^{(V+1)})^{2}
for the relevant confidence level), which guarantees
\lVert\hat{P}_{V}-P_{V}\rVert_{HS}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2}(\mu^{(V)}-\mu^{(V+1)})^{-1})=o_{p}(1)
; the expansion below is stated for each such fixed
V
. Under the null hypothesis and under the local alternative
H_{1,n}
of Theorem
3
, the scaled KRR estimator admits the expansion

\sqrt{n}\,\hat{w}=\sum_{k=1}^{V}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{k}}\frac{1}{\mu^{(k)}+\lambda}\,\langle\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m},\tilde{e}_{k,j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\,\tilde{e}_{k,j}\;+\;\sum_{i=v_{V}+1}^{n}\frac{1}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\,\langle\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m},\hat{e}_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\,\hat{e}_{i}+o_{p}(1),

where
\tilde{e}_{k,j}:=\sum_{l=1}^{m_{k}}(O_{k,n})_{jl}\,\hat{e}_{v_{k-1}+l}
and
O_{k,n}
is a random orthogonal matrix of size
m_{k}\times m_{k}
constructed as in Appendix
F
, so that
\lVert\tilde{e}_{k,j}-e_{k,j}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
for a suitable orthonormal basis
\{e_{k,j}\}_{j=1}^{m_{k}}
of the
k
-th population eigenspace; the tail (indices
i>v_{V}
) keeps the empirical eigenfunctions, and it is stated with the population weights
\mu_{i}
; the empirical weights
\nu_{i}
differ from these by an
o_{p}(1)
term in the expansion, as shown in the proof. Relabeling the eigenvalues (accounting for multiplicity) as
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
and the rotated eigenfunctions as
\{\tilde{e}_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
gives the compact form

\sqrt{n}\,\hat{w}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\,\langle\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m},\tilde{e}_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\,\tilde{e}_{i}+o_{p}(1).

Proof.
See Appendix
B.1.1
.

Lemma
1
serves the estimation-effect and nuisance-parameter analyses of Sections
3
and
4
, which compare empirical and population eigenbases within clusters; the proofs of Theorems
2
and
3
bypass it, working with resolvent convergence and Slutsky’s theorem in the population basis. Wherever the empirical eigenbasis enters, the rotated basis tracks the aligning rotation at rate
\lVert\tilde{e}_{k,j}-e_{k,j}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
, and the spectral weights of the limiting statistics act on the population coefficients
\langle\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m},e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}/\sqrt{\mu_{i}}
.

The geometry of the RKHS.

Characterizing the geometry of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
requires an orthonormal basis (ONB). Mercer’s theorem provides one from the eigenfunctions
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
but assumes a compact input space; the following conditions extend it to general
\mathcal{X}
.

Assumption 1

(i)
k(x,\cdot)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
; (ii)
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
is a bounded and positive operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
, and for every
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
,
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}(g)\in C(\mathcal{X})
, the space of continuous functions on
\mathcal{X}
; (iii)
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
has at most countably many positive eigenvalues
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, with corresponding orthonormal eigenfunctions
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
.

Assumption
1
yields two results: the Mercer expansion
k(x,y)=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}\phi_{i}(x)\phi_{i}(y)
, which converges absolutely and uniformly on any compact subset of
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
, and the fact that
\{\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\phi_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
forms an ONB of the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.
A classical example of an input space and kernel satisfying Assumption
1
is
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
coupled with the Gaussian kernel
k(x,y)=\exp(-\gamma\|x-y\|^{2})
for some
\gamma>0
. For detailed discussions and proofs of these results, see
Sun [2005]
. Assumption
1
is assumed throughout the remainder of the paper.

The structure of
w^{*}_{\lambda}
.

The analysis of the population KRR coefficient function
w^{*}_{\lambda}
requires additional conditions.

Assumption 2

(i) The reproducing kernel is universal and bounded, in the sense that
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}k(x,x)=\bar{k}<\infty
; (ii) the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
satisfy
\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}<\infty
(implied by (i): the Mercer diagonal
k(x,x)=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}\phi_{i}(x)^{2}
and monotone convergence give
\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}=\mathbb{E}[k(X,X)]\leq\bar{k}
); (iii)
|\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}\phi_{i}(X))|<M
for all
i\geq 1
(implied by (v) and
\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}(X)^{2}]=1
via Cauchy–Schwarz); (iv) the kernel ridge regularization parameter satisfies
\lambda>0
and is fixed; (v) the residual has a finite second moment,
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}^{2})<\infty
; (vi) the kernel is integrally strictly positive definite (ISPD), i.e.
\iint k(x,y)f(x)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}(x)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}(y)>0
for every nonzero
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
(the Gaussian kernel satisfies this).

Condition (i) is standard: universality makes
\mathcal{H}_{k}
dense in
C(\mathcal{X})
, and boundedness ensures each eigenfunction of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
is bounded. Condition (ii) ensures that
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is well defined and the eigenvalues decay sufficiently fast; it holds for most common kernels, including the Gaussian. Condition (iii) bounds the projections of the residuals onto the eigenfunctions. Condition (iv) is a design choice rather than an analytical necessity. Classical KRR sends
\lambda\to 0
at a suitable rate to recover the unregularized population function; here the estimand is the regularized
w^{*}_{\lambda}
itself,
\hat{w}
is consistent at any fixed
\lambda
(Lemma
2
), and the equivalence of Theorem
1
does not depend on the value of
\lambda
. What fixed
\lambda
provides is a non-degenerate limit theory: with
\lambda
bounded away from zero, the null statistics have non-degenerate weighted chi-square limits, uniform process convergence over the tuning set holds, and the cross-validated selector admits the asymptotic characterization of Section
4
; each of these steps breaks down under a vanishing regularization parameter, and the admissible set
\Upsilon
is accordingly bounded away from zero in
\lambda
.

We now record the structure of
w^{*}_{\lambda}
.

Lemma 2

Under Assumption
2
and i.i.d. sampling (Assumption
3
(i) below),
w^{*}_{\lambda}
can be written as

w^{*}_{\lambda}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}\phi_{i}(X))\phi_{i},

and

\|\hat{w}-w^{*}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\xrightarrow{p}0.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.1.2
.

The parameters defining
w^{*}_{\lambda}
are the expectations of the residuals projected onto the eigenfunctions
\phi_{i}
of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
, each weighted by the factor
\mu_{i}/(\mu_{i}+\lambda)
: the regularization parameter
\lambda
controls the influence of each eigenfunction in the expansion of
w^{*}_{\lambda}
.

Equivalence with the null hypothesis.

Under the null the expected projections
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}\phi_{i}(X))
vanish, so
w^{*}_{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{0}
; conversely,
w^{*}_{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{0}
forces every expected projection to vanish. The following theorem records this equivalence.

Theorem 1

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, with
k
bounded and integrally strictly positive definite (ISPD, Assumption
2
(vi)) and
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}^{2})<\infty
, the null hypothesis
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}|X)=0
is equivalent to
w^{*}_{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{0}
in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
(i.e.,
w^{*}_{\lambda}
vanishes
\mathbb{P}_{X}
-almost everywhere).

Proof.
See Appendix
B.1.3
.

2.2
Projection-Based Test Statistics

Given the equivalence of Section
2.1
, every departure from the null manifests itself as a nonzero element
w^{*}_{\lambda}
of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
, so a test can be built on projections of the fitted coefficient function onto
\mathcal{H}_{k}
. This subsection defines the two projection statistics and derives their laws under the null and fixed alternatives (Theorem
2
) and under local (Pitman) alternatives (Theorem
3
); Assumption
3
collects the sampling conditions, and the proofs are deferred to Appendices
B.1.4
and
B.1.5
. The orthogonalized statistics valid when
\theta_{0}
is estimated are constructed in Section
3
; the fixed-
(\gamma,\lambda)
laws derived here anchor the bootstrap validity of Section
4
.

The projection principle.

Under the null, we expect

\langle w^{*}_{\lambda},f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=0,\quad\forall f\in\mathcal{H}_{k},

whereas under the alternative,

\langle w^{*}_{\lambda},f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\neq 0,\quad\text{for some }f\in\mathcal{H}_{k}\text{ (e.g.\ }f=w^{*}_{\lambda}\text{ itself)}.

We focus on two choices of
f
: (i)
f=w^{*}_{\lambda}
, giving the squared RKHS norm of the fit, and (ii)
f=m^{*}=\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot))
, the mean embedding of the residual, giving the cross-product of the fit with the raw residual signal:

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(1)}=n\langle\hat{w},\hat{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=n\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{K}\left(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0},

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(2)}=n\langle\hat{w},\hat{m}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{K}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0},

where

\hat{m}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{0,i}k(X_{i},\cdot)=\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}.

For brevity, write

W_{n}(\lambda):=(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol{K}(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I})^{-1},

so that
n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(1)}=n\,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}W_{n}(\lambda)\,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
.

Remark 1

In the case of
\varepsilon_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
with
q\in\mathbb{N}
, we can rewrite the test statistics as

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(1)}=n\sum_{r=1}^{q}\langle\hat{w}_{r},\hat{w}_{r}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=n\sum_{r=1}^{q}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0,r}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{K}\left(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0,r},

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(2)}=n\sum_{r=1}^{q}\langle\hat{w}_{r},\hat{m}_{r}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sum_{r=1}^{q}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0,r}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{K}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0,r},

with
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0,r}
being the
r
-th column of
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
.

Asymptotic laws.

Assumption 3

(i) The random vectors
S_{i}=(Y_{i},X_{i})
,
i=1,2,\dots
, are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) with probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{S}
; extensions to weakly dependent (e.g. mixing) data require the corresponding limit theorems and are not pursued here; (ii) Regularity Conditions: (1) the residual function
\varepsilon:\mathcal{S}\times\Theta\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is continuous on
\Theta
for each
s\in\mathcal{S}
; (2)
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon(S;\theta)|X=x)
exists and is finite for every
\theta\in\Theta
and
\mathbb{P}_{X}
-almost every
x\in\mathcal{X}
; (3)
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon(S;\theta)|X=x)
is continuous on
\Theta
for
\mathbb{P}_{X}
-almost every
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

These conditions are standard in the literature on conditional moment models and model checks; see, e.g.,
Muandet et al. [2020]
.

Theorem 2

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
hold. Then, under the null hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}Z_{i}^{2},

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}Z_{i}^{2},

where
(Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots)^{\top}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\Sigma)
is a
Gaussian sequence with covariance matrix

\Sigma_{ij}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\,\phi_{i}(X)\,\phi_{j}(X)\bigr]
.
In particular,
\Sigma_{ii}=\tau_{i}:=\mathrm{Var}(\varepsilon_{0}\,\phi_{i}(X))
(using
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\phi_{i}(X)]=0
under
H_{0}
).
Under homoscedasticity (
\mathbb{E}[v^{2}\mid X]=\sigma^{2}

a.s.) the
Z_{i}
reduce to independent
N(0,\sigma^{2})
variables.
Under the fixed alternative hypothesis, for any fixed
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(1)}>t\right)\to 1,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(2)}>t\right)\to 1.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.1.4
.

We turn finally to local (Pitman) alternatives, in which the misspecification shrinks at the parametric rate:

H_{1,n}:Y-\mathcal{M}_{\theta_{0}}(X)=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}+\frac{R(X)}{\sqrt{n}}\quad\text{a.s.},

where
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=v
continues to denote the mean-zero noise component, satisfying
\mathbb{E}(v|X)=0
as under
H_{0}
, and
R(X)
is a nonzero measurable function, square integrable with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{X}
.

Theorem 3

Under the local alternative hypothesis
H_{1,n}
and Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
, we have

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}\left(Z_{i}+\mathbb{E}(R(X)\phi_{i}(X))\right)^{2},

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\left(Z_{i}+\mathbb{E}(R(X)\phi_{i}(X))\right)^{2},

where
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
are as defined in Theorem
2
.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.1.5
.

3
Estimation Effects

Thus far,
\theta_{0}
has been taken as known. In practice it is estimated by some consistent
\hat{\theta}
, and plugging
\hat{\theta}
into the statistics of Section
2
alters their laws unless the estimation effect is removed; this section removes it. Throughout, the bandwidth
\gamma
of the Gaussian kernel and the regularization parameter
\lambda
are held fixed, with their data-driven selection deferred to Section
4
.

The section proceeds in two steps. Section
3.1
eliminates the estimation effect through Neyman orthogonality projections: it introduces the required assumptions (Assumptions
4
,
5
, and
6
), constructs the population projection
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
and its empirical counterpart
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
, and shows that the estimation-effect remainder is asymptotically negligible, so that the residuals of Section
2
can be replaced by their orthogonalized counterparts; the resulting null laws are the projected laws of Corollaries
1
and
2
. Section
3.2
constructs the multiplier bootstrap that approximates these null distributions in practice and establishes its conditional consistency in probability (Theorem
4
); the sample-splitting variant that remains valid under data-driven tuning is treated in Section
4
.

3.1
Eliminating Estimation Effects via Projection

We adopt the approach of
Escanciano and Goh [2014]
,
Escanciano [2024]
,
Sant’Anna and Song [2019]
, which employs Neyman orthogonality projections to remove the impact of parameter estimation (extended to penalized high-dimensional settings by
Sancetta [2022]
). In contrast to wild-bootstrap methods
[
Delgado et al., 2006
]
, this approach does not depend on a linear Bahadur representation for the estimator of
\theta_{0}
, nor does it require re-estimating parameters within each bootstrap sample, a flexibility valuable when
\hat{\theta}
comes from non-standard procedures whose asymptotic linear expansions may be unavailable.

Assumptions on the estimator and the residual function.

The following assumptions are required for this purpose.

Assumption 4

(i) The parameter space
\Theta
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}
; (ii) the true parameter
\theta_{0}
is an interior point of
\Theta
; (iii) the consistent estimator
\hat{\theta}
satisfies
\lVert\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}\rVert=O_{p}(n^{-\varrho})
, with
\varrho>1/4
.

Assumption
4
requires only convergence in probability at rate
\varrho>1/4
, with no linear representation, accommodating non-standard estimators such as the LASSO; the proof of Lemma
3
confirms that this
O_{p}
rate suffices for the remainder bounds.

Remark 2
(Conditionally deterministic matrices)

A standing convention of the appendix proofs treats matrices carrying the estimated objects (
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
,
\hat{\mathbb{G}}
) as conditionally deterministic given
\mathbf{X}
. The convention is valid whenever
\hat{\theta}
is a smooth functional of sample averages, in particular for
\sqrt{n}
-regular estimators; under it the projection-quadratic forms it licenses, e.g.
\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=O_{p}(\sqrt{n})
and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=O_{p}(1)
with
H
the score hat projector, hold as stated.

Additional regularity conditions on the smoothness of the residual function are also required.

Assumption 5

(i) The residual
\varepsilon(s;\theta)
is twice continuously differentiable with respect to
\theta
, with its first derivative
g_{\theta}(x)=\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s;\theta)|X=x)
satisfying
\mathbb{E}\left(\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\lVert g_{\theta}(X)\rVert\right)<\infty
and the conditional score uniformly bounded,
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\lVert g_{\theta}(x)\rVert\leq\bar{G}<\infty
; and its second derivative satisfying
\mathbb{E}\left(\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\lVert\nabla g_{\theta}(X)\rVert\right)<\infty
; (ii) the matrix
\Gamma_{\theta}=\mathbb{E}\left[g_{\theta}(X)g_{\theta}(X)^{\top}\right]
is nonsingular in a neighborhood of
\theta_{0}
; (iii) the raw Hessian is uniformly integrable in the strong sense,
\mathbb{E}\left(\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\lVert\nabla_{\theta}^{2}\varepsilon(S;\theta)\rVert^{2}\right)<\infty
, the raw score has a finite fourth moment,
\mathbb{E}\left(\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\lVert\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta)\rVert^{4}\right)<\infty
, and the score satisfies
\mathbb{E}\left(\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\lVert g_{\theta_{0}}(X)\rVert^{2}\right)<\infty
.

Remark 3
(Uniform score bound)

The uniform score bound in Assumption
5
(i) is a standard bounded-score condition: it holds automatically when
\mathcal{X}
is compact and each
g_{\theta}
is continuous, and in the sieve case of Assumption
6
it is implied by
\sup_{x}\lVert g_{\theta_{0}}(x)\rVert<\infty
together with the sup-norm deviation clause of Assumption
6
(iii), the supremum over the parameter segment between
\theta_{0}
and
\hat{\theta}
following from the second-derivative integrability of Assumption
5
(i); it supplies the leverage control in the uniform and bootstrap theory (Lemmas
10
and
11
) and grounds the symbol
\bar{G}
used there.

Assumption 6

(i) The conditional score
g_{\theta_{0}}(X)=\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta_{0})\mid X]
is identifiable from
X
and lies in the
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
-closure of a sieve span; (ii) the sieve basis
\{z_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{L}
is uniformly bounded, and, with
\boldsymbol{z}:=(z_{\ell}(X_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq n,\,1\leq\ell\leq L}
the
n\times L
sieve design matrix, the smallest eigenvalue of the Gram matrix
\boldsymbol{z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}/n
is bounded away from zero,
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}/n)\geq c>0
with probability tending to one; (iii)
g_{\theta_{0}}
is sufficiently smooth and
L=L_{n}\to\infty
at a rate such that
\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(X)\xrightarrow{p}g_{\theta_{0}}(X)
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
and the sieve estimation error satisfies
\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})}=o_{p}(n^{\varrho-1/2})
, the rate at which the product
\lVert\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}\rVert^{2}\,n\,\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})}^{2}
consumed by the estimation-effect remainder of Lemma
3
vanishes; in addition, for the CLT arguments of the projected-statistic proofs that involve the estimated score at sample points, the sup-norm deviation satisfies the side condition
\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{\infty}=O_{p}(n^{-1/4})
.

Assumption
6
is required only when the score
\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon
is not
X
-measurable (e.g., nonlinear IV/GMM or quantile residuals). When
\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon
is
X
-measurable, as in conditional mean regression
\varepsilon=Y-\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(X)
, the raw score equals
g_{\theta_{0}}(X)
and the sieve step is vacuous. The sup-norm side condition in item (iii), invoked in the CLT arguments for the estimated score at sample points, holds under Lipschitz sieve bases: the sup-norm error decomposes into the approximation bias
L^{-\varsigma}
and the sup-norm variance term
O_{p}(\sqrt{L\log n/n})
, and for any
\varsigma>1/2
the dimension restriction
L^{2\varsigma+1}\log n=o(n)
forces both terms to
o_{p}(n^{-1/4})
; the bias condition
L^{-\varsigma}=o(n^{-1/4})
reduces to
\varsigma/(2\varsigma+1)>1/4
, while
L\log n=o(\sqrt{n})
follows directly from the dimension restriction. The
L^{2}
clause of item (iii) is attained by the same bias–variance decomposition: under the dimension restriction the variance term obeys
O_{p}(\sqrt{L/n})=o_{p}(n^{\varrho-1/2})
for every
\varrho\geq 1/4
, and the bias condition
L^{-\varsigma}=o(n^{\varrho-1/2})
reduces to the same
\varsigma/(2\varsigma+1)>1/4
; the clause thus interpolates between consistency of
\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}
at
\varrho=1/2
and the
n^{-1/4}
rate that
Escanciano [2024]
imposes on nonparametric score estimators at
\varrho=1/4
.

The population projection.

Under Assumptions
4
and
5
, we introduce a projection operator
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
defined on the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{S})
of square-integrable random variables, equipped with the inner product
\langle f,g\rangle=\mathbb{E}[f(S)g(S)]
. For any
\omega\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{S})
, the value of
\boldsymbol{\Pi}\omega
at
s=(y,x)
is

(\boldsymbol{\Pi}\omega)(s)=\omega(s)-\mathbb{E}(\omega(S)g_{\theta_{0}}^{\top}(X))\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}g_{\theta_{0}}(x),

\boldsymbol{\Pi}
thus acts on random variables; it is the residual from the population
L^{2}
-regression of
\omega(S)
on
g_{\theta_{0}}(X)
, hence the orthogonal projection onto
\operatorname{span}\{g_{\theta_{0},1}(X),\ldots,g_{\theta_{0},d_{\theta}}(X)\}^{\perp}
: idempotent, self-adjoint, and score-annihilating,
\boldsymbol{\Pi}g_{\theta_{0}}(X)=\boldsymbol{0}
a.s.
Applying this projection operator to the residual function leads to modified versions of
w^{*}_{\lambda}
and
m^{*}
:

\displaystyle w^{*}_{\lambda,\perp}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}\varepsilon_{0}\phi_{i}(X))\phi_{i},

\displaystyle m^{*}_{\perp}=\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot)).

Under
H_{0}
, the law of iterated expectations gives
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}g_{\theta_{0}}^{\top}(X)]=\boldsymbol{0}
, hence
\boldsymbol{\Pi}\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}
a.s., and the projected population objects coincide with their unprojected counterparts; the projection exists to deliver robustness against the estimation effect of
\hat{\theta}
(and, under alternatives, to reshape the mean shift through
\boldsymbol{\Pi}R
). The sample analogue does not inherit this coincidence: the correction
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}-\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=-\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
is
O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
per component and survives at the
O_{p}(1)
scale of the scaled mean embedding
\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}_{\perp}
. The null laws below are therefore those of Corollary
1
, not of Theorem
2
; Remark
4
identifies the resulting limit.

Local robustness.

To analyze the local behavior near
\theta_{0}
, consider the derivatives of these functions with respect to
\theta
evaluated at
\theta_{0}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}w^{*}_{\lambda,\perp}(\theta)\bigg|_{\theta=\theta_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\mathbb{E}\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}g_{\theta_{0}}(X)\phi_{i}(X)\right)\phi_{i}=\boldsymbol{0},

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}m^{*}_{\perp}(\theta)\bigg|_{\theta=\theta_{0}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}g_{\theta_{0}}(X)k(X,\cdot)\right)=\boldsymbol{0}.

This establishes local robustness to perturbations of
\theta
near
\theta_{0}
; the vanishing derivatives follow from dominated convergence and iterated expectations. The identity holds in expectation, not pointwise: since
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
is fixed at
\theta_{0}
, the pointwise derivative is

\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\boldsymbol{\Pi}\varepsilon(s;\theta)\bigg|_{\theta=\theta_{0}}=\boldsymbol{\Pi}(\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s;\theta_{0}))=\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s;\theta_{0})-g_{\theta_{0}}(x),

which is generally nonzero: it is the score net of its conditional mean. What vanishes is the expectation against any
X
-measurable function
h(X)
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\boldsymbol{\Pi}\varepsilon(S;\theta)\,h(X)\right]\bigg|_{\theta=\theta_{0}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\boldsymbol{\Pi}(\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta_{0}))\,h(X)\right]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\boldsymbol{\Pi}g_{\theta_{0}}(X)\,h(X)\right]=0,

where the second equality replaces
\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta_{0})
by
g_{\theta_{0}}(X)=\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta_{0})\mid X]
inside
\mathbb{E}[\,\cdot\,h(X)]
, legitimate because both
h(X)
and the
g_{\theta_{0}}^{\top}(X)
entering the regression coefficient of
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
are
X
-measurable, and the last equality uses
\boldsymbol{\Pi}g_{\theta_{0}}(X)=\boldsymbol{0}
. Setting
h=\phi_{i}
and
h=k(X,\cdot)
yields the two derivatives displayed above.

The empirical projection.

To build a sample analogue of
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
, which projects off
g_{\theta_{0}}(X)=\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon\mid X]
(a function of
X
alone), we project off an estimate of
g_{\theta_{0}}(X)
rather than the raw scores: regressing the residuals off the raw scores
\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i})
would, for
Y
-dependent scores, also remove the idiosyncratic component
\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon-g_{\theta_{0}}(X)
, which is correlated with
\varepsilon_{0}
under
H_{0}
and would make the statistic diverge. Let
\boldsymbol{Z}
be the
n\times L
sieve design matrix with columns
Z^{(1)}(X),\ldots,Z^{(L)}(X)
, and
\boldsymbol{P}_{Z}=\boldsymbol{Z}(\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z})^{-1}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}
. With
\mathbb{G}
the
n\times d_{\theta}
raw-score matrix whose
i
th row is
\hat{g}_{i}^{\top}=(\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\theta)|_{\theta=\hat{\theta}})^{\top}
, the sieve estimate of the conditional score is
\hat{\mathbb{G}}=\boldsymbol{P}_{Z}\mathbb{G}
, whose
i
th row
\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(X_{i})^{\top}
estimates
g_{\theta_{0}}(X_{i})^{\top}
. The empirical projection operator is

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{n}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}\left(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}\right)^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top},

so that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\hat{\mathbb{G}}=\boldsymbol{0}
. Under Assumption
6
,
\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(X)\xrightarrow{p}g_{\theta_{0}}(X)
, so
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
is the empirical analogue of
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
: it projects the residual vector
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
off the estimated conditional score
\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(X)
. When
\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon
is
X
-measurable, the sieve step is vacuous,
\hat{\mathbb{G}}=\mathbb{G}
, and the projection reduces to the raw-score annihilator, which we denote by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}^{\mathrm{raw}}:=\boldsymbol{I}_{n}-\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{G}^{\top}\mathbb{G})^{-1}\mathbb{G}^{\top}
; the sieve-based
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
is the special case obtained from
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}^{\mathrm{raw}}
by replacing
\mathbb{G}
with
\hat{\mathbb{G}}
. We write
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}
for the resulting orthogonalized residual vector.

The estimation-effect remainder.

The following lemma establishes how this projection operator eliminates estimation effects in finite samples.

Lemma 3
(Estimation Effect Remainder)

Suppose Assumptions
4
,
5
, and
6
and the i.i.d. sampling of Assumption
3
(i) hold. Let
\boldsymbol{r}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\theta}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
, and define the RKHS remainders
R_{w}=\sqrt{n}\,\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n+\lambda\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol{r}
and
R_{m}=\sqrt{n}\,\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{r}
. Then, if
\varrho>1/4
,
\|R_{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=o_{p}(1)
and
\|R_{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=o_{p}(1)
.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.2.1
.

For the projection-based test statistics, we apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to the RKHS inner products:

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\|W_{0}+R_{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|W_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}+2\langle W_{0},R_{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+\|R_{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}

\displaystyle=n(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{K}\left(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})+o_{p}(1),

where the cross-term is bounded by
|2\langle W_{0},R_{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}|\leq 2\|W_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\|R_{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=O_{p}(1)o_{p}(1)=o_{p}(1)
, and the remainder term
\|R_{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=o_{p}(1)
. Here
W_{0}:=\sqrt{n}\,\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n+\lambda\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=(C_{n}+\lambda\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}_{\perp}
and
M_{0}:=\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}_{\perp}
with
\hat{m}_{\perp}:=\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
. The bound
\|W_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=O_{p}(1)
(and likewise
\|M_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=O_{p}(1)
) holds in the null and local-alternative regimes in which the limit laws below are derived: writing
\hat{\beta}:=(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
, we have
\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}_{\perp}=\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}-(\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\beta}
, where the second-moment computation in the proof of Lemma
1
gives
\mathbb{E}\|\sqrt{n}\hat{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=O(1)
,
\|\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq\sqrt{d_{\theta}\bar{k}}\,\sup_{i}\|\hat{g}_{i}\|=O_{p}(1)
(from
\|\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=n^{-2}u^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}u\leq n^{-1}\bar{k}\,\|u\|^{2}
and
\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{K})\leq n\bar{k}
), and
\sqrt{n}\hat{\beta}=O_{p}(1)
by the CLT; hence
\|\sqrt{n}\hat{m}_{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=O_{p}(1)
and
\|W_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq\lambda^{-1}\|\sqrt{n}\hat{m}_{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=O_{p}(1)
. Under a fixed alternative
\|W_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=O_{p}(\sqrt{n})
instead, and the divergence argument of Corollary
1
applies. Similarly,

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\langle W_{0}+R_{w},M_{0}+R_{m}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}

\displaystyle=\langle W_{0},M_{0}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+\langle W_{0},R_{m}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+\langle R_{w},M_{0}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+\langle R_{w},R_{m}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}

\displaystyle=(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{K}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})+o_{p}(1),

with all cross and remainder terms being
o_{p}(1)
by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.

Asymptotic laws of the orthogonalized statistics.

Consequently, the projected statistics inherit the laws established for known
\theta_{0}
, with residuals replaced by their Neyman-orthogonalized counterparts:

Corollary 1
(Null and Fixed Alternative for Projection Tests)

Suppose Assumptions
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
, and
6
hold. Under the null hypothesis
H_{0}
, the projected projection-based test statistics satisfy:

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}(Z_{i}^{\perp})^{2},

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}(Z_{i}^{\perp})^{2},

where
Z^{\perp}=(Z_{1}^{\perp},Z_{2}^{\perp},\dots)^{\top}
is the mean-zero Gaussian sequence with covariance matrix

\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}:=\mathbb{E}\big[\sigma^{2}(X)\,\big(\phi_{i}(X)-\kappa_{i}(X)\big)\big(\phi_{j}(X)-\kappa_{j}(X)\big)\big],\qquad\kappa_{i}(x):=g_{\theta_{0}}(x)^{\top}\,\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}\,\mathbb{E}\big[g_{\theta_{0}}(X)\,\phi_{i}(X)\big],

with
\sigma^{2}(X):=\mathbb{E}[v^{2}\mid X]
and
\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}:=\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta_{0}}(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)^{\top}]
(Assumption
5
(ii)). Under the fixed alternative hypothesis
H_{1}
, let
(\theta^{*},f^{*})
denote the pseudo-true limit of
(\hat{\theta},\hat{f})
, with
\hat{f}(x):=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon(X;\hat{\theta})\mid X=x]
and
f^{*}(x):=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon(X;\theta^{*})\mid X=x]
and
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\theta^{*}}
the projection onto the complement of
\operatorname{span}\{g_{\theta^{*}}\}
. Assume
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\theta^{*}}f^{*}\neq 0
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
and that the rate condition of Assumption
4
(iii) holds around
\theta^{*}
. Then for any fixed
t>0
,
\mathbb{P}(n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(k)}>t)\to 1
for
k=1,2
.

Remark 4
(The projected null law)

Equivalently, the scaled mean embedding of the projected residuals satisfies
\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}_{\perp}\xrightarrow{d}G_{\perp}
in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
, where
G_{\perp}:=\xi-A^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}W
,
\xi
is the Gaussian limit of Theorem
2
,
A:=\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta_{0}}(X)\,k(X,\cdot)]\in\mathcal{H}^{d_{\theta}}
, and
W\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)^{\top}])
is jointly Gaussian with
\xi
through
\operatorname{Cov}(\langle\xi,f\rangle,W)=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)f(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)]
for every
f\in\mathcal{H}
; then
Z_{i}^{\perp}=\langle G_{\perp},e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}/\sqrt{\mu_{i}}
. The limit is
G_{\perp}
rather than
\xi
because the
O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
score-projection correction above operates at the
O_{p}(1)
scale of
\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}_{\perp}
. In the location model
g_{\theta_{0}}\equiv 1
,
A=\mathbb{E}[k(X,\cdot)]
and
W
is a scalar
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{\sigma}^{2})
with
\bar{\sigma}^{2}:=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)]
, so that
G_{\perp}=\xi-Z\,\mathbb{E}[k(X,\cdot)]
, while

\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\sigma^{2}(X)\,(\phi_{i}(X)-m_{i})(\phi_{j}(X)-m_{j})\bigr]
with
m_{i}:=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}(X)]
; under homoscedasticity this reduces to
\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}=\bar{\sigma}^{2}(\delta_{ij}-m_{i}m_{j})
.

Corollary 2
(Local Alternative for Projection Tests)

Suppose Assumptions
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
, and
6
hold. Under the local alternative hypothesis
H_{1,n}
, the projected projection-based test statistics satisfy:

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}\left(Z_{i}^{\perp}+\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}R(X)\phi_{i}(X))\right)^{2},

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\left(Z_{i}^{\perp}+\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}R(X)\phi_{i}(X))\right)^{2},

where
\{Z_{i}^{\perp}\}_{i\geq 1}
are as in Corollary
1
: the noise law, which satisfies
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\mid X]=0
, is unchanged by the alternative, while the mean shift enters reshaped, through
\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}R(X)\phi_{i}(X))
.

3.2
Bootstrapping the Null Distributions

The null limits of Section
2
are infinite weighted sums of squares of dependent Gaussians whose covariance involves the unknown law of the residuals and cannot be tabulated directly. The multiplier bootstrap draws i.i.d. multipliers
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
with zero mean, unit variance, bounded support, independent of the data
\{s_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, and recomputes the statistics on the orthogonalized, multiplied residuals: conditional on the data, the multipliers reproduce the fluctuations of the limits. Validity is conditional consistency in probability, denoted
\xrightarrow{d^{*}}
(Chapter 2.9 of
van der Vaart and Wellner [1996]
); a standard multiplier choice is the Mammen two-point distribution:

\mathbb{P}(V_{i}=0.5(1-\sqrt{5}))=b,\quad\mathbb{P}(V_{i}=0.5(1+\sqrt{5}))=1-b,

where
b=(1+\sqrt{5})/(2\sqrt{5})
; see
Mammen [1993]
for details.

The bootstrap statistics.

Write
\odot
for the element-wise (Hadamard) product. The bootstrap analogues of the test statistics are

\displaystyle n\tilde{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(1)}=n(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\theta}}\odot\boldsymbol{V}))^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{K}\left(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\theta}}\odot\boldsymbol{V})),

\displaystyle n\tilde{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(2)}=(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\theta}}\odot\boldsymbol{V}))^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{K}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\theta}}\odot\boldsymbol{V})),

where
\boldsymbol{V}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})^{\top}
.

Theorem 4

Under Assumptions
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
, and
6
, the multiplier bootstrap statistics converge conditionally on the data, in probability, to the projected null laws:

\displaystyle n\tilde{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(1)}\xrightarrow{d}\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}(Z_{i}^{\perp})^{2},

\displaystyle n\tilde{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(2)}\xrightarrow{d}\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}(Z_{i}^{\perp})^{2},

where
\{Z_{i}^{\perp}\}_{i\geq 1}
are as in Corollary
1
and
\xrightarrow{d}
denotes weak convergence of the conditional law given the data, holding
in probability with respect to the data
. Conditional on the data, the multipliers preserve the joint covariance structure of
(\xi,W)
(Remark
4
), so the bootstrap law coincides with the projected null law of Corollary
1
.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.2.3
.

In practice the bootstrap law is evaluated by Monte Carlo over many multiplier draws; this is the implementation used in the simulations of Section
5
, and its sample-splitting version, valid under data-driven tuning, appears in Section
4
.

4
Nuisance Parameter Selection

The test statistics introduced in Section
2
depend on two tuning parameters that act as nuisance parameters for the testing problem: the bandwidth
\gamma
of the Gaussian kernel
k_{\gamma}(x,y)=\exp(-\gamma\|x-y\|^{2})
and the ridge regularization parameter
\lambda
. Neither parameter enters the hypothesis, yet both must be fixed before the test statistic is computed, and the classical practice of fixing them a priori, typically
\gamma=0.5
or a median rule, is consequential: size and power are highly sensitive to this choice, and the standard defaults can fail badly in higher dimensions (Section
5
). This section develops the theory when the data choose the tuning parameters instead, selecting them by cross-validation on a separate sample; the analysis characterizes the asymptotic behavior of the resulting selector and its effect on the two quantities that define a test, its size and its power. Relative to
Escanciano [2024]
, whose Gaussian-process class holds the kernel fixed, the present section develops the missing tuning layer for the projection family; Remark
6
records the precise relation.

In outline: Section
4.1
sets out the splitting protocol, the criterion, and the selector; Section
4.2
proves the process-level uniform limits on which the analysis rests; Sections
4.3
,
4.4
, and
4.5
then analyze the implemented test regime by regime: its null distribution and size control, its consistency under fixed alternatives, and the laws of the selector and of the implemented statistic under local alternatives; and Section
4.6
closes with an exact energy identity explaining why cross-validation favors configurations favorable for power.

We focus on the Gaussian kernel throughout; the results should extend to other shift-invariant kernels.

4.1
The Cross-Validation Selection Protocol

Both tuning parameters enter the limiting null distributions of Section
2
through spectral weights such as
\mu_{i}(\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}
, where
\{\mu_{i}(\gamma)\}_{i\geq 1}
are the eigenvalues of the integral operator
\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
: the bandwidth determines the spectrum, and hence the class of departures against which the test has power, while the regularization parameter determines the shrinkage applied to each spectral direction. We select them jointly by minimizing a
k
-fold CV estimate of the prediction risk of the KRR fit. Two further components of the protocol are essential: the tuning choice is delegated to a dedicated selection sample, so that the selector and the test statistic are driven by independent noise, and the admissible set is a compact subset bounded away from zero in
\lambda
.

Sample splitting.

A tuning parameter selected from the same data that are later used for testing invalidates the pointwise asymptotic argument underlying the bootstrap: under the null, the selector and the test statistic would be driven by the same noise. We therefore partition the sample into two independent subsamples,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{m}^{\mathrm{sel}}=\{(X_{i}^{\mathrm{sel}},Y_{i}^{\mathrm{sel}})\}_{i=1}^{m}

\displaystyle\text{(selection sample)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{test}}=\{(X_{j}^{\mathrm{test}},Y_{j}^{\mathrm{test}})\}_{j=1}^{n}

\displaystyle\text{(testing sample)},

of total size
N=m+n
, with
\mathcal{D}_{m}^{\mathrm{sel}}\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{test}}
and
m,n\to\infty
at arbitrary relative rates. Let
c:=\lim m/(m+n)\in[0,1]
denote the asymptotic selection fraction. Throughout this section, Pitman (local) alternatives are expressed at the total-sample rate, with shift
R(X)/\sqrt{m+n}
; this is the testing problem of Theorem
3
with the sample size
n
replaced by the total
N=m+n
, and the shift direction
R
carries the standing second-moment convention
\mathbb{E}[R^{2}]<\infty
throughout. The selection sample is used only to choose the tuning; the testing sample only to compute the statistic and the bootstrap at the chosen value. The structural parameter
\hat{\theta}
and the Neyman-orthogonalized residuals
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\theta}}
of Section
3
are formed on each subsample. We write
\varepsilon_{\perp,i}^{\mathrm{sel}}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
, for the orthogonalized residuals on the selection sample.

The criterion and the selector.

Let
\eta:=(\gamma,\lambda)
denote the tuning parameter and let

\Upsilon:=[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]\times[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}],\qquad 0<\gamma_{L}<\gamma_{U}<\infty,\quad 0<\lambda_{L}<\lambda_{U}<\infty,

be a compact set of admissible values, discretized in practice as a finite grid
\Upsilon_{0}\subset\Upsilon
, fixed as
m,n\to\infty
. The lower bound
\lambda_{L}>0
meets the strict-positivity and fixedness requirement of Assumption
2
(iv).

Let
\{\mathcal{I}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{I}_{k}\}
be a partition of
\{1,\ldots,m\}
into
k
folds of approximately equal size, and let
\mathcal{I}(i)
denote the fold containing observation
i
. For each fold
r
and each candidate
\eta\in\Upsilon
, let
\hat{w}_{\eta}^{(-r)}
denote the KRR estimator trained on the selection observations outside fold
r
:
\hat{w}_{\eta}^{(-r)}=\left(C_{\gamma}^{(-r)}+\lambda I\right)^{-1}\hat{m}^{(-r)}
, where
C_{\gamma}^{(-r)}=\frac{1}{m_{r}}\sum_{i\notin\mathcal{I}_{r}}k_{\gamma}(X_{i}^{\mathrm{sel}},\cdot)\otimes k_{\gamma}(X_{i}^{\mathrm{sel}},\cdot)
, and
\hat{m}^{(-r)}=\frac{1}{m_{r}}\sum_{i\notin\mathcal{I}_{r}}\varepsilon_{\perp,i}^{\mathrm{sel}}\,k_{\gamma}(X_{i}^{\mathrm{sel}},\cdot)
, with
m_{r}:=m-|\mathcal{I}_{r}|
. The CV criterion is the average held-out squared prediction error,

\mathrm{CV}_{m}(\eta):=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}}\left(\varepsilon_{\perp,i}^{\mathrm{sel}}-\hat{w}_{\eta}^{(-r)}(X_{i}^{\mathrm{sel}})\right)^{2},

(1)

and the selector minimizes the criterion over the admissible set:

\hat{\eta}_{m}:=(\hat{\gamma}_{m},\hat{\lambda}_{m})=\arg\min_{\eta\in\Upsilon}\mathrm{CV}_{m}(\eta).

(2)

Remark 5

(Leave-one-out analysis in the theory.)
Although the criterion (
1
) is stated for a general number
k
of folds, the asymptotic analysis of the following subsections (and of Appendix
B.3.6
) is carried out for the
leave-one-out
criterion, i.e.
k=m
folds of size one. In that case each fold is a single observation, the training set for observation
i
is
\{1,\ldots,m\}\setminus\{i\}
, and the criterion (
1
) specializes to

\mathrm{CV}_{m}^{\mathrm{LOO}}(\eta):=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big(\varepsilon_{\perp,i}^{\mathrm{sel}}-\hat{w}_{\eta}^{(-i)}(X_{i}^{\mathrm{sel}})\big)^{2},

(3)

where
\hat{w}_{\eta}^{(-i)}:=\big(C_{\gamma}^{(-i)}+\lambda I\big)^{-1}\hat{m}^{(-i)}
is the KRR fit trained on the
m-1
observations other than
i
, with
C_{\gamma}^{(-i)}:=\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{j\neq i}k_{\gamma}(X_{j}^{\mathrm{sel}},\cdot)\otimes k_{\gamma}(X_{j}^{\mathrm{sel}},\cdot)
and
\hat{m}^{(-i)}:=\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{j\neq i}\varepsilon_{\perp,j}^{\mathrm{sel}}k_{\gamma}(X_{j}^{\mathrm{sel}},\cdot)
the empirical covariance and mean embedding over
j\neq i
. The general
k
-fold case is recovered in Remark
12
of the appendix.

4.2
The Uniform Limit Theory

The selection rule (
2
) is an
argmin
over
\Upsilon
, and the test statistic is evaluated at the selected value, so pointwise limit theorems at fixed
\eta
cannot describe either object: the selector’s limit law requires the whole criterion process to converge (argmin continuous mapping theorem), and the limit of
S_{n}^{\perp}(\hat{\eta}_{m})
requires the statistic
process
to converge uniformly, so that the random evaluation can be passed to the limit. This subsection assembles the population objects in which the limits are expressed and proves the two process-level results; we analyze the first projection statistic throughout, and every result of this section holds for the second statistic as well.

For readability we write
\varepsilon_{\perp,i}:=\varepsilon_{\perp,i}^{\mathrm{sel}}
for the residuals on the selection sample and
S_{n}^{\perp}(\eta):=n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(1)}(\eta)
for the projection statistic of Section
3
evaluated at
\eta=(\gamma,\lambda)
, i.e., the quadratic form in
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}
with weight matrix
W_{n}(\eta):=(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}+n\lambda\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}
, where
\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}
is the Gram matrix of
k_{\gamma}
on the sample in question.

Setup and standing conditions.

The results below are expressed in terms of population spectral quantities of the kernel family
k_{\gamma}
. Let
\{\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)\}_{i\geq 1}
and
\{\mu_{i}(\gamma)\}_{i\geq 1}
denote the eigenfunctions and eigenvalues of the integral operator
\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
(Section
2.1
), let
C_{\gamma}:=\mathbb{E}[k_{\gamma}(X,\cdot)\otimes k_{\gamma}(X,\cdot)]
, and define the smoothing kernel

g_{\eta}(x,y):=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)}{\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda}\,\phi_{\ell}(x;\gamma)\,\phi_{\ell}(y;\gamma),

(4)

the kernel of the population smoothing operator
S_{\lambda}:=(C_{\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}C_{\gamma}
, in the sense of an integral kernel on
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
: for every
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
,

(S_{\lambda}f)(x)\;=\;\int g_{\eta}(x,y)\,f(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}(y).

Here
C_{\gamma}
acts on
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
and
\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
; they are intertwined by the inclusion
\iota:\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
through
C_{\gamma}=\iota^{*}\iota
and
\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}=\iota\,\iota^{*}
. The nonzero spectrum
\{\mu_{i}(\gamma)\}_{i\geq 1}
is common to both: the eigenfunctions
\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)
, orthonormal in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
, belong to
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
and satisfy
C_{\gamma}\phi_{i}=\mu_{i}(\gamma)\,\phi_{i}
in
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
, with the dual normalization
\|\phi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}=\mu_{i}(\gamma)^{-1/2}
; all spectral quantities below are read from this pair.
The kernel
g_{\eta}
enters the analysis through the linearized leave-one-out prediction
\hat{w}^{(-i)}_{\eta}(X_{i}^{\mathrm{sel}})=\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{j\neq i}\varepsilon_{\perp,j}\,g_{\eta}(X_{i}^{\mathrm{sel}},X_{j}^{\mathrm{sel}})+r_{i}
of Lemma
12
, where
r_{i}=O_{p}(m^{-1})
under
H_{0}
and local alternatives and
r_{i}=O_{p}(m^{-1/2})
under fixed alternatives.

The alternative regimes.
The limiting objects are indexed by the mean of the residual function in the three regimes of the testing problem; throughout, the orthogonalized residuals decompose as

\varepsilon_{\perp,i}=f_{m}(X_{i})+v_{i}+o_{p}(1),

where
f_{m}\equiv 0
under
H_{0}
,
f_{m}=\boldsymbol{\Pi}f
under a fixed alternative, and
f_{m}=\delta_{m}h
under the local alternative, and the noise satisfies
\mathbb{E}[v\mid X]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[v^{2}\mid X]=\sigma^{2}(X)
. Under a fixed alternative, the effective signal of the orthogonalized residual is
(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)(x):=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\Pi}\varepsilon_{0}\mid X=x]
, the image of the signal
f(x):=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\mid X=x]
of the Introduction under the projection
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
of Section
3
. Thus
\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\equiv 0
under
H_{0}
, while
\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\neq 0
exactly when the misspecification survives the projection, that is, when
f\notin\operatorname{span}\{g_{\theta_{0},1}(X),\ldots,g_{\theta_{0},d_{\theta}}(X)\}
. Write
\delta_{i}(\gamma):=\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)(X)\phi_{i}(X;\gamma)]
, let
w^{*}_{\eta}:=(C_{\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)
denote the population KRR solution, and set

A(\eta):=\big\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}f-w^{*}_{\eta}\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\lambda^{2}\,\delta_{i}(\gamma)^{2}}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}},

(5)

the approximation error of the population KRR fit. For the local analysis, fix
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})\setminus\{0\}
, write
h_{i}(\gamma):=\mathbb{E}[h(X)\phi_{i}(X;\gamma)]
,
A_{h}(\eta):=\sum_{i}\lambda^{2}h_{i}(\gamma)^{2}/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}
,
P_{\eta}h(x):=\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(\gamma)h_{i}(\gamma)\phi_{i}(x;\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)
,
a_{\eta}:=h-P_{\eta}h
,
b_{\eta}(x):=\mathbb{E}[a_{\eta}(X)\,g_{\eta}(X,x)]
, and let
G
be a mean-zero Gaussian process on
\Upsilon
with covariance kernel

\operatorname{Cov}\big(G(\eta),G(\eta^{\prime})\big)=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sigma^{2}(X)\,\big(\Pi(a_{\eta}-b_{\eta})\big)(X)\,\big(\Pi(a_{\eta^{\prime}}-b_{\eta^{\prime}})\big)(X)\Big]+\Omega_{G}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}),

(6)

where
\Omega_{G}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
collects the covariance contribution of the estimation effect of the projection coefficient; its exact form is not needed, since every conclusion drawn from
G
is a level or argmin statement.

The criterion decomposition.
In the null and local-alternative regimes, expanding the square of the leave-one-out error and using the linearization of Lemma
13
(the criterion (
3
) then decomposes into the four terms (I)–(IV) defined there, stated for the infeasible orthogonalized residuals
\varepsilon_{\perp}^{0}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
, with the transfer from the feasible residuals established by Lemma
15
),

\mathrm{CV}_{m}^{\mathrm{LOO}}(\eta)=\underbrace{\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\varepsilon_{\perp,i}^{2}}_{(\mathrm{I})}\;-\;\underbrace{2\,U_{m}(\eta)}_{(\mathrm{II})}\;+\;\underbrace{Q_{m}(\eta)}_{(\mathrm{III})}+\underbrace{R_{m}(\eta)}_{(\mathrm{IV})}+o_{p}(m^{-1}),

where
U_{m}(\eta):=\frac{1}{m(m-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\varepsilon_{\perp,i}\varepsilon_{\perp,j}\,g_{\eta}(X_{i},X_{j})
collects the off-diagonal products of the cross term;
Q_{m}(\eta):=\frac{1}{m(m-1)^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\varepsilon_{\perp,j}^{2}\,g_{\eta}(X_{i},X_{j})^{2}
the diagonal part of the squared prediction; and
R_{m}(\eta):=\frac{2}{m(m-1)^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j<k\\
j,k\neq i\end{subarray}}g_{\eta}(X_{i},X_{j})\,g_{\eta}(X_{i},X_{k})\,\varepsilon_{\perp,j}\varepsilon_{\perp,k}
the off-diagonal (
j\neq k
) part of the squared prediction. Under a fixed alternative, the linearization remainder is only
O_{p}(m^{-1/2})
, negligible at the
\sqrt{m}
scale.
The diagonal part (III) is governed by the noise variance: let
\bar{\sigma}^{2}:=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)]
,
\tau_{\ell}(\gamma):=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)\phi_{\ell}(X;\gamma)^{2}]
, and
V(\eta):=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)^{2}}{(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}}\,\tau_{\ell}(\gamma)
, the leading conditional mean of the diagonal part at the
m^{-1}
scale.

The correlation structure of the residuals.
The selection-sample residuals are the Neyman-orthogonalized residuals of Section
3
, whose conditional covariance given
\mathbf{X}
is
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\operatorname{diag}(\sigma^{2}(X_{j}^{\mathrm{sel}}))\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
rather than the diagonal matrix
D:=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma^{2}(X_{j}^{\mathrm{sel}}))
: for
j\neq k
,

c_{jk}:=\mathbb{E}\big[\varepsilon_{\perp,j}^{\mathrm{sel}}\,\varepsilon_{\perp,k}^{\mathrm{sel}}\mid\mathbf{X}\big]=-\sigma^{2}(X_{j}^{\mathrm{sel}})\,\hat{g}_{j}^{\top}M\hat{g}_{k}-\sigma^{2}(X_{k}^{\mathrm{sel}})\,\hat{g}_{j}^{\top}M\hat{g}_{k}+\hat{g}_{j}^{\top}M(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}D\hat{\mathbb{G}})M\hat{g}_{k}=O_{p}(m^{-1}),

with
M:=(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}
and
\hat{g}_{j}:=\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(X_{j}^{\mathrm{sel}})
. The expansion follows from
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{m}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}M\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}
: since the noise has conditional covariance
D
given
\mathbf{X}
, the orthogonalized residual vector has conditional covariance
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}D\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
, and expanding
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}D\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}})_{jk}
for
j\neq k
, the diagonal matrix
D
contributes nothing, the two cross terms give
-(\hat{\mathbb{G}}M\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}D)_{jk}=-\sigma^{2}(X_{k}^{\mathrm{sel}})\,\hat{g}_{j}^{\top}M\hat{g}_{k}
and its transpose, and the quadratic term gives
\hat{g}_{j}^{\top}M(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}D\hat{\mathbb{G}})M\hat{g}_{k}
, the three displayed terms. The order is
O_{p}(m^{-1})
:
\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}/m\to\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}
(Assumption
5
(ii)) gives
M=O_{p}(m^{-1})
and
\hat{g}_{j}^{\top}M\hat{g}_{k}=O_{p}(m^{-1})
, while
