\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}D\hat{\mathbb{G}}=\sum_{j}\sigma^{2}(X_{j}^{\mathrm{sel}})\hat{g}_{j}\hat{g}_{j}^{\top}=O_{p}(m)
, so each term is
O_{p}(m^{-1})
. The diagonal entries, by contrast, keep their leading value,
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}D\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}})_{jj}=\sigma^{2}(X_{j}^{\mathrm{sel}})+O_{p}(m^{-1})
: only the off-diagonal correlations act at the
m^{-1}
scale. In the location model
g_{\theta_{0}}\equiv 1
under homoscedasticity,
c_{jk}=-\sigma^{2}/m
exactly, the classical negative covariance of centered residuals. Hence
m\,c_{jk}\to c^{*}(X_{j}^{\mathrm{sel}},X_{k}^{\mathrm{sel}})
where, with
\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}:=\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta_{0}}(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)^{\top}]
(Assumption
5
(ii)) and
g_{\theta_{0}}
the conditional score of Section
3
,

\begin{split}c^{*}(x,x^{\prime}):={}&-g_{\theta_{0}}(x)^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}g_{\theta_{0}}(x^{\prime})\big(\sigma^{2}(x)+\sigma^{2}(x^{\prime})\big)+g_{\theta_{0}}(x)^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\sigma^{2}(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)^{\top}\big]\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}g_{\theta_{0}}(x^{\prime})\end{split}

Under homoscedasticity,
c^{*}=-\sigma^{2}\,g_{\theta_{0}}^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}g_{\theta_{0}}
. Under
H_{0}
the conditional means of the two off-diagonal terms (II) and (IV) are driven by these correlations,
c_{ij}=O_{p}(m^{-1})
, and converge to the deterministic projection correction
\nu(\eta):=\mathbb{E}\big[c^{*}(X_{1},X_{2})\,g_{\eta}(X_{1},X_{2})\big]
,
\rho(\eta):=\mathbb{E}\big[c^{*}(X_{2},X_{3})\,g_{\eta}(X_{1},X_{2})\,g_{\eta}(X_{1},X_{3})\big]
, and
\Delta(\eta):=\rho(\eta)-2\nu(\eta)
: indeed,
m\,\mathbb{E}\big[U_{m}(\eta)\mid\mathbf{X}\big]=\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{i\neq j}c_{ij}\,g_{\eta}(X_{i},X_{j})\;\longrightarrow\;\nu(\eta)
(the average
\frac{1}{m(m-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}c^{*}(X_{i},X_{j})g_{\eta}(X_{i},X_{j})
is the
U
-statistic for
\nu(\eta)
, with
c_{ij}=m^{-1}c^{*}(X_{i},X_{j})+o_{p}(m^{-1})
) and
m\,\mathbb{E}\big[R_{m}(\eta)\mid\mathbf{X}\big]=\frac{2}{(m-1)^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j<k\\
j,k\neq i\end{subarray}}c_{jk}\,g_{\eta}(X_{i},X_{j})g_{\eta}(X_{i},X_{k})\;\longrightarrow\;\rho(\eta)
(each pair
(j,k)
enters through the
m-2
indices
i\neq j,k
with weights averaging to
(g_{\eta}\circ g_{\eta})(X_{j},X_{k})
, the finite-
m
factor being
(m-2)/(m-1)
). The diagonal term (III) is unaffected at this scale,
mQ_{m}(\eta)\xrightarrow{p}V(\eta)
, so the deterministic parts of
-2mU_{m}+mQ_{m}+mR_{m}
assemble into
-2\nu(\eta)+V(\eta)+\rho(\eta)
: the correction
\Delta(\eta)
is of the same order as
V(\eta)
, so it cannot be absorbed into the remainder.

The pseudo-true convention under
H_{1}
.
Under a fixed alternative the estimated
parameter converges in general to the pseudo-true value
\theta^{*}\neq\theta_{0}
, and all

\theta_{0}
-subscripted objects of this section, namely the score
g_{\theta_{0}}
, the projection

\boldsymbol{\Pi}
onto the complement of
\operatorname{span}\{g_{\theta_{0}}\}
, the signal

f:=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon(\cdot;\theta_{0})\mid X]
, and the rate condition of
Assumption
4
(iii), are then understood as evaluated at
\theta^{*}
:

g_{\theta^{*}}
,
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\theta^{*}}
,
f^{*}:=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon(\cdot;\theta^{*})\mid X]
,
the rate condition being required around
\theta^{*}
. This is the convention under which Corollary
1
’s fixed-alternative
branch, Theorem
9
, and Theorem
10
are stated; their proofs
(Appendix
B.3.6
) run the corresponding
\theta_{0}
-arguments verbatim with

\theta_{0}
replaced by
\theta^{*}
.

The projected chaos fluctuations.
The fluctuations of
mU_{m}
and
mR_{m}
around these conditional means, governed by the fourth-moment structure of the projected noise, form the projected chaoses of the kernels below. For a kernel
f
, write
(\Pi f\Pi)(x,y):=\Pi_{x}[\Pi_{y}f](x,y)
for the kernel obtained by applying the projection to each argument. The quadratic fluctuation of the CV criterion involves the composition kernel
(g_{\eta}\circ g_{\eta})(x,y):=\int g_{\eta}(x,z)\,g_{\eta}(z,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}(z)
and the projected kernels

K^{\mathrm{sym},\perp}_{\eta}(x,y):=\sigma(x)\,(\Pi g_{\eta}\Pi)(x,y)\,\sigma(y),\qquad K^{D,\perp}_{\eta}(x,y):=\sigma(x)\,\big(\Pi(g_{\eta}\circ g_{\eta})\Pi\big)(x,y)\,\sigma(y),

with eigenvalues
\{\lambda^{\perp}_{k}(\eta)\}_{k\geq 1}
and
\{\lambda^{D,\perp}_{k}(\eta)\}_{k\geq 1}
of the corresponding Hilbert–Schmidt operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
; the projections act on the kernels and do not depend on
\lambda
.

Assumption 7
(Uniform regularity)

(i)
\mathbb{E}\|X\|^{2}<\infty
; (ii) the maps
\gamma\mapsto\mu_{i}(\gamma)
and
\gamma\mapsto\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)
are continuous on
[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
; (iii) the conditional variance of the residual is bounded away from zero and above,
\sigma_{\min}^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}(X):=\mathbb{E}[v^{2}\mid X]\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}<\infty
almost surely; (iv) the kernel family
\{k_{\gamma}\}
is integrally strictly positive definite, so that all eigenvalues
\mu_{i}(\gamma)>0
; (v) there exist
p\in(2,4]
and
\bar{M}_{2p}<\infty
with
\sup_{x\in\operatorname{supp}\mathbb{P}_{X}}\mathbb{E}[v^{2p}\mid X=x]\leq\bar{M}_{2p}
, a conditional moment of the centered noise of order strictly above the fourth.

Assumption
7
states the regularity conditions of the uniform-in-
\eta
theory, and the clauses call for the following comments. The eigenpair conditions of clause (ii) hold for the Gaussian family by Proposition
5
(Appendix
B.3.1
), which derives them from the real-analyticity of
\gamma\mapsto\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
in Hilbert–Schmidt norm and Rellich–Kato perturbation theory
[
Kato, 1980
]
. The integral strict positive definiteness of clause (iv) is satisfied by the Gaussian kernel. Clause (v) is needed for the uniform-in-observation moment bounds of the leave-one-out linearization, where a sample maximum of conditional moments must be controlled deterministically; its
(2p)
-moment clause supplies the order-
p
envelopes of the gradient-moment inputs of the criterion-process proofs and the projected-residual moment bounds consumed in the proofs of Theorems
5
and
6
and of Lemma
17
(Appendix
B.3.4
); in those bounds a fourth moment of a centered quadratic form consumes noise moments of order
2p
, and the requirement
p>2
is forced by the two-dimensional index set over which the suprema are taken.

The two results operate at different levels of uniformity. Theorem
5
is an
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
statement: its stochastic equicontinuity and tightness let the random evaluation pass to the limit, giving
S_{n}^{\perp}(\hat{\eta}_{m})\xrightarrow{d}S_{\infty}(\eta^{*})
(resp.
S_{\infty}(\eta^{*}_{h};h_{c})
) once the selector converges; it feeds Propositions
1
and
2
and Theorem
10
. Theorem
6
governs the
selection
side, identifying the limit criterion process whose deterministic drifts,
V(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)
under
H_{0}
,
\bar{\sigma}^{2}+A(\eta)
under fixed alternatives, and
V(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)+cA_{h}(\eta)
under local alternatives, together with the projected chaoses and, where present, the
\sqrt{m}
Gaussian fluctuations, determine the selector’s law and the bias-variance trade-off of Section
4.6
.

Theorem 5
(Uniform weak convergence of the statistic process)

Under Assumptions
1
–
6
and
7
, under
H_{0}
,
S_{n}^{\perp}\rightsquigarrow S_{\infty}
in
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
, where
S_{\infty}
has continuous sample paths and, for each fixed
\eta
,

S_{\infty}(\eta)=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{i}(\gamma)}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}}\,(Z_{i}^{\perp})^{2},

with
(Z_{1}^{\perp},Z_{2}^{\perp},\dots)^{\top}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma))
as in Corollary
1
(in the eigenbasis
\{\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)\}
of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
). Under the local alternative at the total-sample rate with shift
R/\sqrt{m+n}
(Section
4.1
),
S_{n}^{\perp}\rightsquigarrow S_{\infty}(\cdot;h_{c})
in
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
with
h_{c}:=\sqrt{1-c}\,h
,
h:=\boldsymbol{\Pi}R
,
c:=\lim m/(m+n)
,
h_{c,i}(\gamma):=\mathbb{E}[h_{c}(X)\phi_{i}(X;\gamma)]
(Corollary
2
), and

S_{\infty}(\eta;h_{c}):=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{i}(\gamma)}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}}\,\big(Z_{i}^{\perp}+h_{c,i}(\gamma)\big)^{2},

(cf. Corollary
2
); the factor
\sqrt{1-c}
is the asymptotic testing share of the total sample.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.3.2
.

Remark 6
(Relation to the Gaussian-process class)

The construction of this section places the projection statistics within the Gaussian-process approach to model checks of
Escanciano [2024]
, with one structural difference. The two constructions share their recipe and their limit type: both are quadratic forms in Neyman-orthogonally projected residuals against a kernel, both admit a multiplier bootstrap that does not re-estimate the nuisance parameter, and both null limits are second-order weighted chi-square chaos laws determined by a kernel spectrum, the orthogonal projection onto the score orthocomplement, and the conditional variance of the noise. The difference is the plug-in, and with it the weights: the statistic of
Escanciano [2024]
evaluates the bivariate kernel
K(X_{i},X_{j})
at the sample points, and its null weights are the eigenvalues of the
\sigma^{2}
-measured orthogonalized kernel operator on
L^{2}(\mu)
,
\mathrm{d}\mu=\sigma^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}
(Theorem 4.3 there); ours evaluate the empirical resolvent
(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}/n)(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}/n+\lambda I)^{-2}
, and the null weights of Theorem
5
are the filtered spectral weights
w_{i}(\eta)=\mu_{i}(\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}
attached to the projected noise coordinates. The empirical-resolvent plug-in is what makes leave-one-out cross-validation intrinsic to the family, and the data-driven selection of the kernel-side tuning, which
Escanciano [2024]
pose as an open problem for their class, is what the present section supplies for it. The resolution is also not the routine extension that the derivative-interchange route of their Remark 2 suggests: the criterion fluctuations live at the
m^{-1}
scale with degenerate chaos limits (Theorem
6
), the projection-induced covariance corrections enter the selector’s drift at the same order as the variance term, and the selector retains a non-degenerate random limit (Theorems
7
and
11
).

Theorem 6
(Uniform convergence of the CV criterion processes)

Under Assumptions
1
–
6
and
7
:

(a)
Under
H_{0}
, the
\eta
-dependent part of the CV criterion is
O_{p}(m^{-1})
, and

m\Big[\mathrm{CV}_{m}(\eta)-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\varepsilon_{\perp,i}^{0})^{2}\Big]\;\rightsquigarrow\;\mathbb{H}(\eta):=-2\,\mathcal{C}^{\perp}(\eta)+V(\eta)+\mathcal{D}^{\perp}(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)\quad\text{in }\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon),

where
\mathcal{C}^{\perp}
is the mean-zero Gaussian chaos of order two with
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathcal{C}^{\perp}(\eta),\mathcal{C}^{\perp}(\eta^{\prime}))=2\langle K^{\mathrm{sym},\perp}_{\eta},K^{\mathrm{sym},\perp}_{\eta^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}
;
\mathcal{D}^{\perp}
is the mean-zero Gaussian chaos of order two with
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathcal{D}^{\perp}(\eta),\mathcal{D}^{\perp}(\eta^{\prime}))=2\langle K^{D,\perp}_{\eta},K^{D,\perp}_{\eta^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}
, with cross-covariance
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathcal{C}^{\perp}(\eta),\mathcal{D}^{\perp}(\eta^{\prime}))=2\langle K^{\mathrm{sym},\perp}_{\eta},K^{D,\perp}_{\eta^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}
, and
\Delta(\eta)
is the deterministic projection correction of the setup. Here
\varepsilon_{\perp}^{0}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\varepsilon_{0}
is the infeasible projected residual of Appendix
B.3.5
, at which the criterion in the display is evaluated (Lemma
15
); centering at the feasible level
m^{-1}\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{\perp,i}^{2}
instead shifts the left side by a constant that is free of
\eta
and need not vanish under the sieve clause of Assumption
4
(iii), so every argmin-based conclusion, in particular the selector laws, is unaffected.

(b)
Under a fixed alternative (
\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\neq 0
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
,
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\rVert_{\infty}<\infty
),
\sup_{\eta\in\Upsilon}|\mathrm{CV}_{m}(\eta)-R_{\infty}(\eta)|=O_{p}(m^{-1/2})
with
R_{\infty}(\eta):=\bar{\sigma}^{2}+A(\eta)
.

(c)
Under the local alternative
f_{m}=\delta_{m}h
with
\delta_{m}\to 0
,
\sqrt{m}\,\delta_{m}\to a<\infty
, and
\mathbb{E}[h^{4}(X)]<\infty
, where
h=\boldsymbol{\Pi}h
is an effective (score-annihilated) direction, the convention of the local-alternative pipeline,

\begin{split}m\Big[\mathrm{CV}_{m}(\eta)-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}^{2}\Big]&\;\rightsquigarrow\;\mathbb{H}_{h}^{a}(\eta)\\
&:=-2\,\mathcal{C}^{\perp}(\eta)+V(\eta)+\mathcal{D}^{\perp}(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)+a^{2}A_{h}(\eta)+2a\,G(\eta)\end{split}

in
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
, where
v_{i}:=\varepsilon_{\perp,i}-f_{m}(X_{i})
is the noise component of the residual.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.3.5
.

Theorem
6
identifies the limit criterion
\mathbb{H}
(resp.
\mathbb{H}_{h}^{a}
) whose minimizer is the selector’s limit, and Theorem
5
identifies the limit process in which the statistic is evaluated. Sections
4.3
–
4.5
combine the two, one regime of the testing problem at a time: each regime contributes a law for the selector, and evaluating the statistic and the critical value at the selected tuning yields the size and the consistency of the implemented test and, under local alternatives, the limit law of its statistic.

Throughout Sections
4.3
–
4.5
,
\hat{\eta}_{m}
denotes a random minimizer of the criterion over
\Upsilon
, that is, a measurable selection from the argmin set satisfying

\mathrm{CV}_{m}(\hat{\eta}_{m})=\inf_{\eta\in\Upsilon}\mathrm{CV}_{m}

almost surely; since
\mathrm{CV}_{m}
has continuous sample paths on the compact rectangle, the argmin set is nonempty for every realization (Weierstrass). In the null and local regimes of Theorem
6
the centered process
F_{m}
displayed in Theorem
6
(a) and (c) shares this argmin set, the centering summand being
\eta
-free; under a fixed alternative the selector analysis of Appendix
B.3.6
works with
\mathrm{CV}_{m}
directly.

4.3
Null Distribution and Size Control

The first property of the data-driven test is its behavior under
H_{0}
. The selector converges to a random limit, so the selected tuning remains random even asymptotically and must not be read as an estimate of an optimal value; the randomness is benign for size, because selection and testing use independent samples. This subsection assembles the chain in three steps: the null law of the selector (Theorem
7
), the resulting null law of the implemented statistic (Proposition
1
), and the size guarantee delivered by the multiplier bootstrap at the realized tuning (Theorem
8
), which requires no convergence of the selector at all.

Theorem 7
(Selector under the null)

Suppose Assumptions
1
–
6
and
7
hold and the sample-splitting protocol of Section
4.1
is in place. Under
H_{0}
, assume that the limit criterion
\mathbb{H}
of Theorem
6
(a) has an almost surely unique minimizer over
\Upsilon
. Then any random minimizer
\hat{\eta}_{m}
of
\mathrm{CV}_{m}
over
\Upsilon
satisfies

\hat{\eta}_{m}\xrightarrow{d}\eta^{*}:=\arg\min_{\eta\in\Upsilon}\mathbb{H}(\eta),

Here
\eta^{*}
is random and independent of the testing sample; its law depends on the kernel spectrum, the conditional variance function
\sigma^{2}(\cdot)
, and the joint law of the chaoses
\mathcal{C}^{\perp}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\perp}
.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.3.7
.

Proposition 1
(Joint convergence and evaluation under
H_{0}
)

Under the conditions of Theorem
7
, as
m,n\to\infty
,

\big(\hat{\eta}_{m},\,S_{n}^{\perp}\big)\;\rightsquigarrow\;\big(\eta^{*},\,S_{\infty}\big)\quad\text{in }\Upsilon\times\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon),

the limit components being independent because
\mathcal{D}_{m}^{\mathrm{sel}}\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{test}}
. Consequently, by continuity of the sample paths of the limit process,

S_{n}^{\perp}(\hat{\eta}_{m})\xrightarrow{d}S_{\infty}(\eta^{*})

Proof.
See Appendix
B.3.8
.

It remains to convert this limit law into size control. Random tuning does not break the bootstrap, provided selection and testing use independent samples: conditional on the selection sample,
\hat{\eta}_{m}
is held fixed, and by split independence the law of the statistic at that point is its marginal law, so conditioning on the selection event is non-informative. It therefore suffices to construct a critical value that is pointwise valid at every fixed
\eta
and evaluate it at the realized value; the multiplier bootstrap of Section
3
does exactly this, applied to the testing sample with the selected parameter held fixed: let
\boldsymbol{V}:=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})^{\top}
be an independent copy of the multiplier weights of Section
3
, independent of
\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{test}}
and of
\mathcal{D}_{m}^{\mathrm{sel}}
, and write

\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}(\hat{\eta}_{m}):=n\,\tilde{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(1)}(\hat{\eta}_{m})

(7)

for the bootstrap analogue of
S_{n}^{\perp}
evaluated at
\hat{\eta}_{m}
: the quadratic form in
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}^{\mathrm{test}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\theta}}^{\mathrm{test}}\odot\boldsymbol{V})
with weight matrix
W_{n}(\hat{\eta}_{m})
. The bootstrap critical value is the conditional
(1-\alpha)
-quantile of the bootstrap law,

c_{n,\alpha}(\hat{\eta}_{m}):=\inf\Big\{t\geq 0:\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{V}}\big(\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}(\hat{\eta}_{m})\leq t\mid\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{test}},\hat{\eta}_{m}\big)\geq 1-\alpha\Big\},

and
c_{\infty}(\eta)
denotes the
(1-\alpha)
-quantile of the null limit
S_{\infty}(\eta)
of Theorem
5
. By Theorem
4
the pointwise bootstrap is consistent at every fixed
\eta
, and the map
\eta\mapsto c_{\infty}(\eta)
is continuous on
\Upsilon
(Lemma
23
, Appendix
B.3.9
); hence the pointwise bootstrap at the realized value estimates, by split independence, exactly the law of the statistic conditional on the selection event. No selector asymptotics and no resampling of the selection side are needed.

Theorem 8
(Bootstrap validity under sample splitting)

Under Assumptions
1
–
6
and
7
and the sample-splitting protocol of Section
4.1
, under
H_{0}
, as
m,n\to\infty
at arbitrary relative rates,

\mathbb{P}\big(S_{n}^{\perp}(\hat{\eta}_{m})>c_{n,\alpha}(\hat{\eta}_{m})\big)\;\longrightarrow\;\alpha.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.3.9
.

Remark 7
(No selector limit is needed.)

Theorem
8
does not require
\hat{\eta}_{m}
to converge: the proof (Appendix
B.3.9
) uses only the pointwise null limit, the pointwise bootstrap at the realized selector value, and split independence. The selector limits of Theorems
7
–
11
enter only the power analysis.

4.4
Consistency under Fixed Alternatives

Under a fixed alternative the criterion process converges uniformly to its deterministic limit
\bar{\sigma}^{2}+A(\eta)
, and the selector concentrates on the minimizer of the approximation error:

Theorem 9
(Selector under a fixed alternative)

Suppose Assumptions
1
–
6
and
7
hold and
\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\neq 0
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
. Let
\gamma^{*}:=\arg\min_{\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]}A(\gamma,\lambda_{L})
and suppose
\gamma^{*}
is unique. Then
\partial_{\lambda}A>0
on
\Upsilon
, so every minimizer of
A
has
\lambda=\lambda_{L}
and

\arg\min\nolimits_{\Upsilon}A=\{(\gamma^{*},\lambda_{L})\},

and every random minimizer
\hat{\eta}_{m}=(\hat{\gamma}_{m},\hat{\lambda}_{m})
of
\mathrm{CV}_{m}
over
\Upsilon
satisfies

\operatorname{dist}\bigl(\hat{\eta}_{m},(\gamma^{*},\lambda_{L})\bigr)\xrightarrow{p}0.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.3.10
.

Remark 8

Since
A
is strictly increasing in
\lambda
for every
\gamma
(
\partial_{\lambda}A(\eta)=\sum_{i}2\lambda\,\delta_{i}(\gamma)^{2}\mu_{i}(\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{3}>0
), the
\lambda
-component of every minimizer of
A
is
\lambda_{L}
: under a strong signal, CV selects the minimal-shrinkage configuration, the one maximizing the test’s noncentrality
\|w^{*}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}=\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(\gamma)\delta_{i}(\gamma)^{2}/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}
. If
\gamma^{*}
lies on the boundary of
[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
with non-vanishing slope, the selector concentrates on the boundary point: the consistency display of Theorem
9
gives
\hat{\gamma}_{m}\xrightarrow{p}\gamma^{*}
.

Under a fixed alternative the test is consistent whenever the misspecification survives the projection:

Theorem 10
(Consistency under fixed alternatives)

Under the conditions of Theorem
9
(in particular
\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\neq 0
, i.e.
f\notin\operatorname{span}\{g_{\theta_{0},1}(X),\ldots,g_{\theta_{0},d_{\theta}}(X)\}
, see Section
4.2
), for every fixed
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\big(n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(1)}(\hat{\eta}_{m})>t\big)\to 1.

If instead
\boldsymbol{\Pi}f=0
(the misspecification lies in the nuisance span, at the
pseudo-true parameter as per the convention of this section), the projection annihilates the signal
and the null CLT of Corollary
1
applies verbatim at
\hat{\theta}
: the projected
residuals
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}_{\hat{\theta}}\hat{\varepsilon}
are then asymptotically a
mean-zero projected noise (the component
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(f^{*}-f)
being
o_{p}(1)
-negligible at
the relevant scale), the statistic stays
O_{p}(1)
under the projected null law, and the rejection
probability tends to
\alpha
by the bootstrap size control (Theorem
8
). The
feasible test is consistent iff the signal survives the projection at
\theta^{*}
,

\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\theta^{*}}f^{*}\neq 0
.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.3.11
.

4.5
The Data-Driven Test under Local Alternatives

The third regime of the testing problem is the local one. Under a local alternative the criterion process converges to the drifted field
\mathbb{H}_{h}^{a}
, and the selector limit interpolates between the null law and the deterministic fixed-alternative configuration (Theorem
11
); because
\hat{\eta}_{m}
depends only on the selection sample and
S_{n}^{\perp}
only on the testing sample, their joint convergence is a product structure, and the implemented statistic converges to the drifted limit evaluated at the random tuning (Proposition
2
).

Theorem 11
(Selector under a local alternative)

Suppose Assumptions
1
–
6
and
7
hold and the residual function in the selection sample is
f_{m}=\delta_{m}h
with
\delta_{m}\to 0
and
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})\setminus\{0\}
.

(i)
If
\sqrt{m}\,\delta_{m}\to a<\infty
, assume that the drifted limit criterion
\mathbb{H}_{h}^{a}
of Theorem
6
(c) has an almost surely unique minimizer over
\Upsilon
. Then any random minimizer of
\mathrm{CV}_{m}
over
\Upsilon
satisfies

\hat{\eta}_{m}\xrightarrow{d}\eta^{*}_{h}:=\arg\min_{\eta\in\Upsilon}\mathbb{H}_{h}^{a}(\eta),

a drifted version of the null limit of Theorem
7
, shifted by the deterministic drift
a^{2}A_{h}
and the Gaussian term
2aG
. For
a=0
the limit law is exactly that of Theorem
7
: the local alternative is invisible to the selector. The uniqueness assumption can be relaxed to convergence to the argmin set.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.3.12
.

Remark 9
(The Pitman alternative and the split ratio)

Under the local alternative at the total-sample rate, the residual function in the selection sample is
f_{m}=(m+n)^{-1/2}h
with
h:=\boldsymbol{\Pi}R
, so
\sqrt{m}\,\delta_{m}=\sqrt{m/(m+n)}\to a:=\sqrt{c}
,
c:=\lim m/(m+n)\in[0,1]
, the selection fraction of Section
4.1
. The drift and Gaussian terms in
\mathbb{H}_{h}^{a}
are then
c\,A_{h}(\eta)
and
2\sqrt{c}\,G(\eta)
: the split ratio enters the selector’s limit law. Since
\sqrt{m}\,\delta_{m}\leq 1
, the selector always follows the drifted regime of Theorem
11
(i) with
a=\sqrt{c}
; drifts stronger than the Pitman scale (
\sqrt{m}\,\delta_{m}\to\infty
) are never attained at the total-sample Pitman rate. A balanced split (
c\in(0,1)
) yields the drifted limit of Theorem
11
(i); a testing-dominant split (
n\gg m
,
c\to 0
) leaves the selector asymptotically blind to the alternative (the
H_{0}
law of Theorem
7
) while the test sees the full signal,
\sqrt{1-c}\,h\to h
; a selection-dominant split (
m\gg n
,
c\to 1
) gives the selector its maximal drift (
a=1
) but shrinks the testing share, so the test’s effective signal
h_{c}=\sqrt{1-c}\,h
vanishes.

Proposition 2
(Joint convergence and evaluation under local alternatives)

Under the conditions of Theorem
11
(i) under
H_{1,n}
, the total-sample-rate local alternative of Section
4.1
, as
m,n\to\infty
,

\big(\hat{\eta}_{m},\,S_{n}^{\perp}\big)\;\rightsquigarrow\;\big(\eta^{*}_{h},\,S_{\infty}(\cdot;h_{c})\big)\quad\text{in }\Upsilon\times\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon),

with
h_{c}:=\sqrt{1-c}\,h
, the limit components being independent because
\mathcal{D}_{m}^{\mathrm{sel}}\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{test}}
. Consequently, by continuity of the sample paths of the limit process,

S_{n}^{\perp}(\hat{\eta}_{m})\xrightarrow{d}S_{\infty}(\eta^{*}_{h};h_{c}).

Proof.
See Appendix
B.3.8
.

The split ratio
c
acts through two opposing channels. On the selection side, larger
c
is favorable: the drift
cA_{h}
increases with
\lambda
, pulling the selected configuration toward
\lambda_{L}
, the direction of maximal captured energy, and by Proposition
4
(i) power is non-decreasing in the captured-signal scale at fixed tuning. On the testing side, larger
c
is unfavorable: the effective signal
h_{c}=\sqrt{1-c}\,h
shrinks toward zero, and a selection-dominant split buys better tuning at the price of a weaker test. The choice of the splitting ratio is therefore a design question with an explicit trade-off between selection quality and testing power, beyond the scope of this paper; the simulation study of Section
5
investigates the finite-sample performance of different splitting ratios, and the results seem to favor a relatively small
c
(a testing-dominant split).

4.6
The Energy Decomposition and the Signal-to-Noise Ratio of the Test

The selector results characterize the limit law of
\hat{\eta}_{m}
. This subsection explains why CV-favored configurations are favorable for the test through an exact bookkeeping identity: at every
\eta
the misspecification energy splits into an uncaptured and a captured part, and the captured part contains precisely the signal that drives the statistic. Cross-validation minimizes the uncaptured part, hence maximizes the captured part, and when no signal is present shrinks the noise floor; the same
\lambda
-dial that opens the filter to signal also admits noise, and the criterion balances the two. Theorem
12
is accordingly a two-channel result.

The energy identity.

Fix a residual function
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
: the effective signal
\boldsymbol{\Pi}f
under a fixed alternative, or
h:=\boldsymbol{\Pi}R
under the local alternative of Theorem
3
. Let
w^{*}_{\eta}(h):=(C_{\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}h
denote the population KRR solution, with the spectral representation
w^{*}_{\eta}(h)=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}(\gamma)h_{i}(\gamma)\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)
.

Proposition 3
(Energy identity)

For every
\eta\in\Upsilon
and every
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
,

\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=A_{h}(\eta)+\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+2\lambda\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2},

(8)

where
A_{h}(\eta)=\lVert h-w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
is the approximation error of (
5
). Spectrally,

\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{i}(\gamma)^{2}}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}}\,h_{i}(\gamma)^{2},\qquad\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{i}(\gamma)}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}}\,h_{i}(\gamma)^{2},

so for each fixed
\gamma
and each
h\neq 0
the approximation error is strictly increasing in
\lambda
, while the two captured components are strictly decreasing in
\lambda
(for
h=0
all three components vanish identically in
\lambda
). Consequently, over any set of configurations, minimizing
A_{h}
is equivalent to maximizing the captured energy

E_{h}(\eta):=\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-A_{h}(\eta)=\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+2\lambda\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}.

(9)

Proof.
See Appendix
B.3.13
.

The three terms of (
8
) are the approximation error (the part of the misspecification the fit cannot represent), the explained variance (the captured signal measured in
L^{2}
), and the regularization component, equal to
2\lambda
times the RKHS energy of the fit. Per spectral direction the budget is allocated without interference: dividing (
8
) directionwise,

\left(\frac{\lambda}{\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda}\right)^{\!2}+\left(\frac{\mu_{i}(\gamma)}{\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda}\right)^{\!2}+\frac{2\lambda\mu_{i}(\gamma)}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}}=1.

The bandwidth
\gamma
determines the spectrum and hence which directions carry the budget; the regularization
\lambda
determines, within each direction, how the budget is split between approximation error and captured components. This is why (
2
) chooses
(\gamma,\lambda)
jointly: two dials of the same per-direction allocation.

The signal-to-noise ratio of the test.

Under the local alternative, the limit statistic of Theorem
5
is

\displaystyle S_{\infty}(\eta;h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)\,\big(Z_{i}^{\perp}+h_{i}(\gamma)\big)^{2},\qquad w_{i}(\eta):=\frac{\mu_{i}(\gamma)}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}},

where
(Z_{1}^{\perp},Z_{2}^{\perp},\dots)^{\top}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma))
,
\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)
is the covariance of the projected noise of Corollary
1
(in the eigenbasis
\{\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)\}
of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
), and write
\tau_{i}^{\perp}(\gamma):=\Sigma^{\perp}_{ii}(\gamma)
for its diagonal and
M(\eta):=\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)\tau_{i}^{\perp}(\gamma)
for the null mean of the statistic. The signal carried by the test at
\eta
is the noncentrality

\mathcal{N}(\eta;h):=\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)\,h_{i}(\gamma)^{2}=\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2},

(10)

the RKHS energy of the fitted signal, i.e., the regularization component of (
8
) divided by
2\lambda
. The second projection statistic has the same limit structure with the weights
w_{i}^{(2)}(\eta):=\mu_{i}(\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)
, and its noncentrality is

\mathcal{N}^{(2)}(\eta;h):=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{i}(\gamma)}{\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda}\,h_{i}(\gamma)^{2}=\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lambda\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2},

(11)

the explained-variance component of (
8
) plus half of its regularization component. The two are strictly decreasing in
\lambda
; consequently item (i) of Proposition
4
, its divergence-rate statement, and Theorem
12
hold for
\hat{T}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
verbatim, with
S_{\infty}^{(2)}
and the corresponding quantiles
c_{\infty}^{(2)}
in place of their unmarked counterparts. The noise scale is the null standard deviation
\sigma_{\infty}(\eta):=\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(S_{\infty}(\eta))}=\sqrt{2}\,\lVert W(\eta)^{1/2}\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)W(\eta)^{1/2}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}
with
W(\eta):=\operatorname{diag}(w_{i}(\eta))
, and the signal-to-noise ratio of the limiting test at
\eta
is

\mathcal{S}(\eta;h):=\frac{\mathcal{N}(\eta;h)}{\sigma_{\infty}(\eta)}.

(12)

Throughout,
c_{\infty}(\eta)
denotes the
(1-\alpha)
-quantile of the null law
S_{\infty}(\eta)=S_{\infty}(\eta;0)
and
p(\eta;h):=\mathbb{P}(S_{\infty}(\eta;h)>c_{\infty}(\eta))
the pointwise local power; at the total-rate Pitman alternative the signal enters attenuated,
h_{c}=\sqrt{1-c}\,h
, by the testing share.

The whitened form.

For the power analysis, it is convenient to whiten the projected noise. Writing
Z^{\perp}=\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)^{1/2}U
with
U\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},I)
, the limit statistic takes the diagonal form

S_{\infty}(\eta;h)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\varsigma_{k}(\eta)\big(U_{k}+\vartheta_{k}(\eta;h)\big)^{2},

(13)

where
\{\varsigma_{k}(\eta)\}
are the eigenvalues and
\mathcal{Q}(\eta)
the orthogonal matrix of eigenvectors of
\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)^{1/2}W(\eta)\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)^{1/2}
, and
\vartheta(\eta;h):=\mathcal{Q}(\eta)^{\top}\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)^{-1/2}h
the signal coefficients in the diagonalizing basis; the square root is taken on the support of the generically singular
\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)
, which contains the projected signal
h
by Assumption
7
(iii)–(v).

Proposition 4
(Monotone power and divergence rate)

Let Assumptions
1
–
6
and
7
hold, and fix
\eta\in\Upsilon
.

(i) Unbiasedness and scale monotonicity.
For every projected signal
h
, the map
t\mapsto p(\eta;th)
is nondecreasing on
[0,\infty)
; in particular,
p(\eta;h)\geq\alpha
, so the limiting test is unbiased against every misspecification direction not annihilated by
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
.

(ii) Divergence rate under fixed alternatives.
If
\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\neq 0
, then

S_{n}^{\perp}(\eta)/n\xrightarrow{p}\bigl\lVert w_{\eta}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}=\mathcal{N}(\eta;\boldsymbol{\Pi}f),

the RKHS component of the captured energy
E_{\boldsymbol{\Pi}f}(\eta)
defined in (
9
).

Proof.
See Appendix
B.3.14
.

In the local-alternative regime,
p(\eta;h)
is the rejection probability of the limiting test at fixed
\eta
with the projected signal
h
added to the projected noise; under a fixed alternative only item (ii) applies, the statistic diverging at the noncentrality rate. Item (i) certifies that enlarging the captured signal never hurts power,
p(\eta;h)\geq\alpha
: the infinite-dimensional analogue of the classical unbiasedness of quadratic-form statistics with nonnegative weights against two-sided location alternatives
[
Lehmann and Romano, 2005
]
; it is the directional link from the selection side to the testing side. Item (ii) identifies the divergence rate with the noncentrality
\mathcal{N}(\eta;\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)
, the RKHS component of the captured energy up to the factor
2\lambda
. How much captured energy converts into power depends, beyond these signal-side facts, on the noise channel: the dispersion of the null law through
f_{\eta}(c_{\infty}(\eta))
and
\sigma_{\infty}(\eta)
, the object of Theorem
12
and Condition
1
. The rotation
\mathcal{Q}(\eta)
makes directional monotonicity compatible with generically correlated coordinates: it decorrelates the projected noise, so enlarging the signal moves the mean away from the origin in every direction at once.

CV as a signal-to-noise optimizer.

Two channels connect the CV choice to the signal-to-noise ratio (
12
): a signal channel, through which the selected configuration maximizes the numerator
\mathcal{N}
, and a noise channel, through which it shrinks the denominator
\sigma_{\infty}
when there is no signal to capture. Write
D_{0}(\eta):=V(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)
for the deterministic part of the selector’s limit criterion under
H_{0}
, and
D_{c}(\eta):=D_{0}(\eta)+cA_{h}(\eta)
for the local-alternative case.

Condition 1
(Standing monotonicity)

For every
\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
and every
\ell\geq 1
with
a_{\ell}(\gamma)>0
,

a_{\ell}(\gamma)\leq\frac{\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)\,\tau_{\ell}(\gamma)}{\lambda_{U}},\qquad a_{\ell}(\gamma):=\mathbb{E}\big[c^{*}(X,X^{\prime})\,\phi_{\ell}(X;\gamma)\,\phi_{\ell}(X^{\prime};\gamma)\big],

where
\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)
and
\phi_{\ell}(\cdot;\gamma)
are the Mercer eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of
k_{\gamma}
,
\tau_{\ell}(\gamma)
the spectral noise variances of Section
4.2
, and
c^{*}
the projection-correction kernel of Section
4
. The clause requires that no Mercer direction whose projection-overlap with the estimation error is positive carry an overlap exceeding its own noise leverage at the largest admissible regularization; directions with
a_{\ell}(\gamma)\leq 0
are exempt. Under this clause the drift
D_{0}(\eta)=V(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)
is strictly decreasing in
\lambda
on
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
for every
\gamma
; the termwise argument is recorded in Appendix
G.2
. The clause holds automatically under homoscedasticity,
\sigma^{2}(\cdot)\equiv\bar{\sigma}^{2}
: then
c^{*}
is negative semidefinite, so
a_{\ell}(\gamma)\leq 0
for every direction, score span, kernel, and dimension (Appendix
G.2
), which covers the leading case of the analysis and the primary simulation setting. Outside homoscedasticity the clause is not automatic: Appendix
G.2
records the exact derivative formula of which the clause is the termwise sufficient condition, the location-model configuration in which
a_{\ell}>0
genuinely occurs, and the numerical verification of the clause for the Gaussian kernel and the simulation designs of Section
5
.

Theorem 12
(CV selection boosts the signal-to-noise ratio)

Under Assumptions
1
–
6
and
7
and the sample-splitting protocol of Section
4.1
:

(i) Signal channel (fixed alternative).
If the conditions of Theorem
9
hold, then
\hat{\eta}_{m}\xrightarrow{p}\eta^{*}:=(\gamma^{*},\lambda_{L})
, and
\eta^{*}
minimizes the approximation error,
A(\eta^{*})=\min_{\Upsilon}A
, equivalently maximizes the captured energy,
E_{\boldsymbol{\Pi}f}(\eta^{*})=\max_{\Upsilon}E_{\boldsymbol{\Pi}f}
. Moreover, for every
\gamma
, the maps
\lambda\mapsto E_{\boldsymbol{\Pi}f}(\gamma,\lambda)
and
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{N}(\gamma,\lambda;\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)
are strictly decreasing, so
\lambda_{L}
maximizes the captured energy and the divergence rate of the statistic; the resulting consistency is Theorem
10
.

(ii) Noise channel (null).
Under
H_{0}
, the variance component
V(\eta)
, the null mean
M(\eta)
, and the noise scale
\sigma_{\infty}(\eta)
of the limit statistic are strictly decreasing in
\lambda
for every
\gamma
. Under Condition
1
, the deterministic part
D_{0}(\eta)
of the selector’s limit criterion is strictly decreasing in
\lambda
, so for every
\gamma
its
\lambda
-minimizer over
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
is attained at
\lambda=\lambda_{U}
.

(iii) Local alternatives and the split ratio.
Under Condition
1
, with
c:=\lim m/(m+n)\in[0,1]
, the deterministic part of the selector’s limit criterion is
D_{c}(\eta)
, with
\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)<0
and
\partial_{\lambda}A_{h}>0
. If
D_{c}
has an interior minimizer
\lambda^{*}_{c}\in(\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U})
with
\partial^{2}_{\lambda\lambda}D_{c}(\lambda^{*}_{c})>0
, then
\lambda^{*}_{c}
is strictly decreasing in
c
; at
c=1
, the minimizer of
D_{1}
lies strictly above
\lambda_{L}
whenever
A_{h}^{\prime}(\lambda_{L})<|\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda_{L})|
, and equals
\lambda_{L}
when
A_{h}^{\prime}(\lambda_{L})\geq|\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda_{L})|
, provided
D_{1}
is convex in
\lambda
on
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
.

By the homoscedasticity result of Appendix
G.2
(
a_{\ell}\leq 0
for every
\ell
when
\sigma^{2}(\cdot)\equiv\bar{\sigma}^{2}
), Condition
1
holds automatically under homoscedasticity, so items (ii)–(iii) of Theorem
12
are unconditional there.

Proof.
See Appendix
B.3.15
.

Remark 10

The theorem assembles the mechanism behind the selection analysis: the energy identity (
8
) ties the CV objective to the fitted signal, Proposition
4
ties that signal to power, and items (i)–(iii) give the direction of the CV choice in
\lambda
through the two monotone parts of a trade-off. The signal part: the captured energy and the divergence rate are strictly decreasing in
\lambda
(item (i)), and the uncaptured signal
A_{h}
strictly increasing (item (iii)). The noise part: the noise components
V+\Delta
are strictly decreasing in
\lambda
under Condition
1
, and with them the null mean
M(\eta)
and the noise scale
\sigma_{\infty}(\eta)
(item (ii)). The criterion’s drift thus carries two components with opposite
\lambda
-slopes,
\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)<0
against
\partial_{\lambda}A_{h}>0
: enlarging
\lambda
removes noise from the criterion while forfeiting signal to the approximation error. The split ratio
c:=\lim m/(m+n)
turns this opposition into the selection mechanism. The limit drift of the selector is
D_{c}(\eta)=D_{0}(\eta)+cA_{h}(\eta)
, the noise part present with unit weight and the uncaptured signal weighted by the selection share; when
D_{c}
has an interior minimizer
\lambda^{*}_{c}
with positive second derivative, item (iii) shows that
\lambda^{*}_{c}
is strictly decreasing in
c
. The selected
\lambda
therefore slides along the
\lambda
-axis from the noise-optimal configuration
\lambda_{U}
at
c=0
, where item (ii) minimizes
D_{0}
,
M
, and
\sigma_{\infty}
, toward the signal-optimal configuration
\lambda_{L}
, where item (i) maximizes the captured energy and the divergence rate and the test is consistent (Theorem
10
); the endpoint at
c=1
is decided by the marginal comparison of
A_{h}^{\prime}(\lambda_{L})
against
|\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda_{L})|
, provided
D_{1}
is convex. The two channels of the theorem are the two ends of this path, item (ii) at
c=0
and item (i) at the
\lambda_{L}
limit. The theorem is not a claim that CV maximizes the literal ratio
\mathcal{N}(\eta;h)/\sigma_{\infty}(\eta)
: under a fixed alternative
\lambda_{L}
also maximizes
\sigma_{\infty}
, and under
H_{0}
there is no signal to form a ratio. The exact selector laws are those of Theorems
7
–
11
. For the Gaussian kernel and the simulation designs of Section
5
, Condition
1
is verified numerically on the entire CV grid (Appendix
G.2
, Table
5
and Figure
4
).

5
Simulation Studies and Empirical Applications

This section examines the finite-sample behavior of the projection tests and illustrates them on real data. The first subsection evaluates size and power under the sample-splitting protocol of Section
4.1
across fourteen data-generating processes, benchmarked against the classical kernel conditional moment (KCM) test, and quantifies the value of the selection sample through an oracle protocol. The second subsection applies the tests to three applications: the National Supported Work (NSW) dataset, where the specification of a probit propensity score model and the hypothesis of a zero conditional average treatment effect are tested individually and jointly; the Mincer wage equation; and a log-linear demand equation for cigarettes. Throughout, critical values come from the multiplier bootstrap of Section
3
under the sample-splitting protocol of Section
4
, and results are read against Sections
4.3
–
4.5
and
4.6
.

5.1
Simulation Studies

The projection tests are compared with the KCM test, the quadratic form
\hat{T}=\frac{1}{n}\,\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}
with
(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma})_{ij}=\exp\!\left(-\gamma\lVert x_{i}-x_{j}\rVert^{2}\right)
, computed under the multiplier bootstrap as in
Escanciano [2024]
. Empirical size is reported in Table
4
, and power in Figures
1
and
2
; the oracle protocol isolates the selection-sample value implied by Theorem
12
(iii).

Data-generating processes.

The null DGP and the six alternative designs are each evaluated at both dimensions,
d=10
and
d=20
. The null model is
\mathrm{DGP}_{0}
(null):
Y=1+X^{\top}\beta+e
, with
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
,
\beta=0.5\,\boldsymbol{1}_{d}
, and
e\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
independent of
X
. The fixed alternatives under homoskedasticity are
\mathrm{DGP}_{1}:Y=1+X^{\top}\beta+1.5\exp\!\big(-(X^{\top}\beta)^{2}\big)+e
,
\mathrm{DGP}_{2}:Y=1+X^{\top}\beta+2\cos\!\big(1.2\pi\lVert X\rVert\big)+e
,
\mathrm{DGP}_{3}:Y=1+X^{\top}\beta+0.5\,(X^{\top}\beta)^{2}+e
, and
\mathrm{DGP}_{4}:Y=1+X^{\top}\beta+1.5\exp\!\big(0.25\,X^{\top}\beta\big)+e
, sharing the covariates,
\beta
, and error structure of the null model: smooth global departures (DGP
1
), oscillatory radial departures (DGP
2
), quadratic departures (DGP
3
), and exponential departures (DGP
4
).

The remaining alternatives combine misspecification with heteroskedasticity. For DGP
5
, the first
d/2
covariates are drawn from
\mathrm{Uniform}[0,\,1+0.1(l-1)]
,
l=1,\ldots,d/2
, the remaining ones from
\mathcal{N}(0,(1+0.1(l-d/2))^{2})
; the specification is
\mathrm{DGP}_{5}:Y=1+X^{\top}\boldsymbol{1}_{d}+\lVert X\rVert+|X^{\top}\boldsymbol{1}_{d}|\,e
, a heteroskedastic model with a fixed radial departure. For DGP
6
, the first
d/2
covariates are drawn from
\mathrm{Uniform}[0,l]
,
l=1,\ldots,d/2
, the remaining ones from
\mathcal{N}(0,(1+0.1(l-d/2))^{2})
; the specification is
\mathrm{DGP}_{6}:Y=1+X^{\top}\boldsymbol{1}_{d}+1.5\,\frac{\lVert X\rVert^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}+\sigma(X)\,e
with
\sigma(X)=\sqrt{0.1+\sum_{l\leq d/2}X_{l}+\sum_{l>d/2}X_{l}^{2}}
, a heteroskedastic model with a local (Pitman) departure of order
n^{-1/2}
, as in Theorem
3
. In all models the structural parameter is estimated by ordinary least squares, and the Neyman-orthogonalized residuals are formed with the projection of Section
3
.

Protocols.

Three protocols are compared.

(i) Sample splitting.
Each replication draws a sample of total size
N\in\{200,400\}
and splits it at random into a selection sample (
15\%
) and a testing sample (
85\%
), i.e., selection sizes
30
and
60
against testing sizes
170
and
340
. On the selection sample, the tuning parameters
(\gamma,\lambda)
are chosen by
5
-fold CV over a data-adaptive grid:
20
values of
\gamma
spanning
[\gamma_{\max}/100,\gamma_{\max}]
with
\gamma_{\max}=2/\mathrm{median}\{\lVert x_{i}-x_{j}\rVert^{2}\}
, and
10
values of
\lambda
in
[1,10]
. The projection statistics
\hat{T}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
and
\hat{T}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
are computed on the testing sample with the multiplier bootstrap (Mammen weights,
B=500
replications), and the selected tuning is held fixed, as in Section
4.3
.

(ii) Oracle.
The tuning parameters are selected on an
additional
selection sample of
200
observations and the test is computed on a testing sample of
400
observations, so that the testing budget coincides with that of the KCM test at
n=400
.

(iii) KCM.
The KCM test is applied to the full sample of size
n\in\{200,400\}
without splitting, at the kernel parameters
\gamma\in\{2,1,0.5,0.2,0.1,0.05,0.02\}
and at the median rule
\gamma=1/\mathrm{median}\{\lVert x_{i}-x_{j}\rVert\}
; the values
\gamma=0.5
and the median rule reproduce the fixed- and data-driven variants of
Escanciano [2024]
. All methods use 1000 replications and identical Monte Carlo draws within each replication, so differences in rejection rates are attributable to the method and, for the KCM test, to the kernel parameter. The figures report rejection rates at the
5\%
level; the empirical sizes at all three levels are collected in Table
4
of Appendix
G
.

Size.

The projection tests are close to nominal size under the splitting protocol at all three levels in most configurations. At the
5\%
level, the rejection frequency under
\mathrm{DGP}_{0}
is
0.035
–
0.039
at
d=10
and
0.042
–
0.071
at
d=20
, the
n=200
,
d=20
configuration least accurate: its selection sample of
30
observations leaves the CV choice noisy; under the oracle protocol the corresponding figures are
0.039
and
0.053
. The two statistics
\hat{T}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
and
\hat{T}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
behave essentially identically throughout. The size of the KCM test is governed by the kernel parameter: at
\gamma\geq 0.5
the statistic is effectively degenerate and never rejects (size
\approx 0
at all three levels), while for
\gamma\leq 0.1
the size is approximately nominal in most configurations; the median rule is conservative, with rejection rates below the nominal level in all configurations.

Sensitivity to the kernel parameter and the CV-selected configuration.

Figure
1
reports the
5\%
rejection rates for all methods at equal total sample size, for
n\in\{200,400\}
and
d\in\{10,20\}
. The most striking feature is the sensitivity of the KCM test to the kernel parameter. At
\gamma\geq 1
the statistic never rejects on any alternative, and at
\gamma=0.5
appreciable power survives only on the two strongest
d=10
designs (
\mathrm{DGP}_{3}
:
0.443
and
\mathrm{DGP}_{5}
:
0.187
at
n=400
); the kernel is nearly diagonal and the statistic rarely exceeds its bootstrap quantiles. Power rises as
\gamma
decreases, peaks at an intermediate value (typically
\gamma=0.02
–
0.1
), and then declines or plateaus; the location and height of the peak vary across DGPs and dimensions. In
d=20
even the best kernel parameter delivers modest and uneven power: at
n=400
the grid maximum is
0.559
for
\mathrm{DGP}_{4}
,
0.409
for
\mathrm{DGP}_{5}
, and
0.251
for
\mathrm{DGP}_{6}
. The median rule, the standard data-driven default, is particularly unreliable in high dimensions: for
\mathrm{DGP}_{3}
at
n=400
it delivers
0.104
against
0.944
at
\gamma=0.1
, and for
\mathrm{DGP}_{4}
and
\mathrm{DGP}_{6}
it is essentially powerless (
0.003
and
0.000
).

By contrast, the projection tests, which select
(\gamma,\lambda)
by CV on the selection sample and require no user choice, track the upper envelope of the KCM kernel-parameter family: at
n=400
their rejection rates are
0.871
(
\mathrm{DGP}_{1}
at
d=10
),
0.996
(
\mathrm{DGP}_{3}
at
d=20
),
0.462
(
\mathrm{DGP}_{4}
at
d=20
), and
0.268
(
\mathrm{DGP}_{6}
at
d=20
), comparable to the KCM test at its best kernel parameter (
0.968
,
0.999
,
0.559
, and
0.251
) and strictly better than the KCM test at any fixed kernel parameter in the large-
\gamma
range. The CV-selected configuration thus adapts to the difficulty of the alternative, in line with the analysis of Sections
4.4
and
4.6
: under a signal, the criterion trades the uncaptured signal against the noise and settles near the minimal-regularization configuration that maximizes the noncentrality of the test.

The oracle protocol and the value of the selection sample.

Figure
2
compares the oracle-protocol projection tests with the KCM test at equal testing budgets (
n=400
). The projection tests remain at nominal size and achieve power comparable to the KCM test at its best kernel parameter. They dominate the KCM test at every kernel parameter for
\mathrm{DGP}_{2}
at
d=20
(
0.209
vs. at most
0.165
) and for
\mathrm{DGP}_{4}
(
0.308
vs. at most
0.259
at
d=10
;
0.596
vs. at most
0.559
at
d=20
); they match the best configuration for
\mathrm{DGP}_{6}
at
d=20
(
0.251
vs.
0.251
). In the remaining scenarios the two procedures land within a few points of each other, and the KCM retains an edge on the smooth alternatives
\mathrm{DGP}_{1}
,
\mathrm{DGP}_{2}
,
\mathrm{DGP}_{5}
, and
\mathrm{DGP}_{6}
(all at
d=10
).

The oracle results also quantify the value of the selection sample, the split-ratio channel of Section
4.6
. Enlarging the selection sample from
60
to
200
observations (with the testing sample growing from
340
to
400
) raises the
5\%
rejection rate from
0.871
to
0.915
(
\mathrm{DGP}_{1}
at
d=10
), from
0.209
to
0.308
and from
0.462
to
0.596
(
\mathrm{DGP}_{4}
at
d=10
and
d=20
), and from
0.686
to
0.747
(
\mathrm{DGP}_{5}
at
d=10
), with no material change in size. The testing sample sets the resolution of the test; the selection sample determines how closely the tuning approaches the signal-maximizing configuration, exactly as predicted by the drift analysis of Theorem
12
(iii).

Overall, the simulations deliver the two messages of Section
4
: the projection tests control size under splitting without user-chosen tuning, whereas the classical kernel test depends on a hard-to-choose kernel parameter whose defaults can be catastrophic in high dimensions; and the power of the selected test rises with the split ratio, a larger selection sample pulling the CV choice toward the captured-signal-maximizing configuration.

G
reports additional designs and results, including the sensitivity of the tests to the sample-splitting ratio (Appendix
G.1
).

5.2
Empirical Applications

Three applications are studied. The first is the National Supported Work (NSW) Demonstration, a randomized evaluation of a training program that has become standard in the causal inference literature; there we test, individually and jointly, the correct specification of a probit propensity score model and the null of a zero conditional average treatment effect (CATE). The second is the specification of the Mincer wage equation on the Wooldridge
wage2
data, the leading empirical specification of returns to schooling. The third is the specification of a log-linear demand equation for cigarettes on the pooled state panel, whose structural residual comes from two-stage least squares. In all three applications the residuals are formed as in Section
3
, whose multiplier bootstrap also approximates the null distributions.

First application: the National Supported Work dataset.

The National Supported Work (NSW) Demonstration (1975–1979) randomly assigned participants across the United States to supported-work treatment or control, and its impact on labor market outcomes has been a longstanding focus in economics.

We use the Dehejia–Wahba version
[
LaLonde, 1986
,
Dehejia and Wahba, 1999
]
: 445 observations (185 treated, 260 controls), covariates including age, education, and prior earnings, and primary outcome real earnings in 1978 (
https://users.nber.org/~rdehejia/nswdata2.html
).

To mitigate numerical instability, we divide “age” and “education” by 10 and log-transform all earnings variables, leaving 8 covariates: 4 continuous variables and 4 binary indicators.

Hypotheses and residuals.

Two hypotheses are tested individually and jointly: correct specification of the propensity score model and a zero conditional average treatment effect. Let
Y(1)
and
Y(0)
denote the potential outcomes under treatment and control, respectively, and let
X
be a vector of observed covariates. The treatment indicator is
T
, so the observed outcome is
Y=Y(1)T+Y(0)(1-T)
.

The propensity score is modeled as
P(T=1|X)=\Phi(\beta^{\top}X)
, where
\Phi
denotes the standard normal cumulative distribution function. The CATE function is defined as
\tau(X)=\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)\mid X]
. Under standard unconfoundedness (i.e.,
Y(1)
and
Y(0)
are independent of
T
given
X
) and a correctly specified propensity score,
\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{Y(T-\Phi(\beta^{\top}X))}{\Phi(\beta^{\top}X)(1-\Phi(\beta^{\top}X))}\,\middle|\,X\right)=\tau(X)
a.s. The residual for testing the propensity score model alone is
\varepsilon_{0}=T-\Phi(\beta^{\top}X)
. For testing the joint hypothesis of correct propensity score specification and zero CATE (i.e.,
\tau(X)=0
), the residual vector is
\varepsilon_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}T-\Phi(\beta^{\top}X)\\
\frac{Y(T-\Phi(\beta^{\top}X))}{\Phi(\beta^{\top}X)(1-\Phi(\beta^{\top}X))}\end{pmatrix}
. For joint testing, the statistic sums the component-wise test statistics.

Results.

The projection tests are implemented under the sample-splitting protocol of Section
4.1
(
15\%
selection /
85\%
testing), with the tuning parameters selected by
5
-fold CV on the selection sample over the grid of Section
4.1
; the null distributions are approximated using the multiplier bootstrap with
B=500
replications. As a benchmark, the KCM test is computed on the full sample, without splitting, at the median rule and at
\gamma=0.5
, exactly as in the simulation studies; the ICM test is not reported for these data because its wild-bootstrap implementation is not straightforward in the context of a probit model. Results are presented in Table
1
.

None of the test statistics rejects the null hypothesis of correct propensity score specification; all
p
-values substantially exceed conventional significance levels. By contrast, every statistic strongly rejects the joint null of correct propensity score specification and zero CATE, with
p
-values below the bootstrap resolution (
<0.002
). The probit model thus fits the data well, and the evidence points to a nonzero treatment effect.

To assess the sensitivity of these conclusions to the random split, we repeat the protocol on
50
random
15\%/85\%
splits. The individual
p
-values of the projection tests vary across splits (min, median, max:
0.030
,
0.359
,
0.996
for
\hat{T}^{(1)}
and
0.072
,
0.438
,
0.916
for
\hat{T}^{(2)}
), but the verdict is stable: at the
5\%
level the individual test rejects in
4\%
of the splits for
\hat{T}^{(1)}
and in none for
\hat{T}^{(2)}
, while the joint test rejects in all
50
splits for both statistics. The KCM benchmark, which is computed on the full sample and does not depend on the split, continues not to reject the propensity-score specification (
p=0.572
at the median rule and
0.630
at
\gamma=0.5
; Table
1
) while rejecting the joint null. The CV-selected regularization is stable as well:
\hat{T}^{(1)}
selects the largest admissible
\lambda
in every split, and
\hat{T}^{(2)}
in
56\%
of them. The conclusions of Table
1
are therefore not an artifact of a single split.

Second application: returns to schooling.

The second application studies the Mincer wage equation on the Wooldridge
wage2
data (
n=935
). The null is the conditional-mean restriction
\mathbb{E}[\log(\mathrm{wage})\mid X]=X^{\top}\beta
, tested for two nested specifications: the parsimonious Mincer set
X=(1,\mathrm{educ},\mathrm{exper},\mathrm{tenure})
and the augmented set adding
\mathrm{exper}^{2}
,
\mathrm{tenure}^{2}
, and
\mathrm{IQ}
. The OLS score is
X
, an
X
-measurable direction, so the projection tests apply verbatim with the protocol, grid, and bootstrap of the NSW application.

Neither the projection statistics nor the KCM benchmark rejects either specification at the
5\%
level (
\hat{T}^{(1)}
:
p=0.180
for the parsimonious specification and
p=0.544
for the augmented;
\hat{T}^{(2)}
:
p=0.178
and
0.544
; KCM:
p=0.602
and
0.386
for the parsimonious specification,
0.408
and
0.302
for the augmented, at the median rule and
\gamma=0.5
; Table
2
). The two procedures agree, and the directional augmentation moves the projection statistics toward larger
p
-values.

A split-sensitivity check over
50
random splits confirms the verdict: no split rejects either specification at the
5\%
level for either statistic (
p
-values between
0.060
and
0.898
). The CV-selected regularization concentrates at the largest admissible
\lambda
(
90\%
of the splits for
\hat{T}^{(1)}
under the parsimonious specification,
98\%
under the augmented), the noise-dominated regime to which item (ii) of Theorem
12
draws the selector when the signal channel is weak.

Third application: the demand for cigarettes with instrumental variables.

The third application tests the specification of a log-linear demand equation for cigarettes on the pooled state panel of
Baltagi and Levin [1992]
: 46 states over 1963–1992,
n=1380
, treated as a random sample, as in the textbook two-stage least squares analysis of these data. The equation is
\ln\mathrm{sales}=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\ln P+\beta_{3}\ln\mathrm{ndi}+u
, where sales is per-capita packs,
P
the real average retail price, and
\mathrm{ndi}
real per-capita disposable income. The price is endogenous, and the equation is estimated by two-stage least squares with the real tax-inclusive minimum price as the excluded instrument; the full-sample estimates are a price elasticity of
-0.757
and an income elasticity of
0.248
, in line with the received estimates for these data.

The null is the conditional moment restriction
\mathbb{E}[u\mid Z]=0
with
Z=(1,\ln\mathrm{pimin},\ln\mathrm{ndi})
the exogenous instruments, and the kernel is built on the instrument variables, so the null equivalence of Theorem
1
applies with
Z
in place of
X
as the conditioning variable. The raw score
\nabla_{\theta}u=-X
with
X=(1,\ln P,\ln\mathrm{ndi})
is not
Z
-measurable, because the endogenous
\ln P
is not a function of the instruments; the sieve step of Assumption
6
, vacuous in the first two applications, is therefore not vacuous here. The sieve is the linear span of the instruments: with
\mathbb{G}
the raw-score matrix, the sieve estimate of the conditional score is
\hat{\mathbb{G}}=\boldsymbol{P}_{Z}\mathbb{G}
, the fitted values of the regressors on the instrument set
(1,\widehat{\ln P},\ln\mathrm{ndi})
, where the included exogenous
\ln\mathrm{ndi}
projects onto itself and the endogenous
\ln P
is replaced by its first-stage fitted values. The orthogonalization is the sieve annihilator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{n}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}
; it removes the 2SLS estimation effect up to the asymptotically negligible RKHS remainder of Lemma
3
, and under the null,
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\,g_{\theta_{0}}(Z)^{\top}]=\boldsymbol{0}
by iterated expectations, so the projected null law of Corollary
1
applies with
g_{\theta_{0}}(Z)=\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\theta}u\mid Z]=-\mathbb{E}[X\mid Z]
. Projecting off the raw regressors instead would leave the orthogonalized residual with conditional mean
-\mathbb{E}[X\mid Z]^{\top}\beta_{g}
, where
\beta_{g}=\mathbb{E}[XX^{\top}]^{-1}\mathbb{E}[Xu]\neq\boldsymbol{0}
under endogeneity: the raw annihilator, exact as it is against the estimation effect, would confound the test with the endogeneity itself.

Both projection statistics reject the log-linear specification (
p<0.002
; Table
3
), as does the fixed-
\gamma
KCM benchmark, so the misspecification is strong enough that every procedure detects it. The translog augmentation (adding
(\ln P)^{2}
,
(\ln\mathrm{ndi})^{2}
, and
\ln P\cdot\ln\mathrm{ndi}
) is rejected at the reference split (
p<0.002
), with a price elasticity at the sample mean of
-0.788
, close to the log-linear estimate. A split-sensitivity check over
50
random splits leaves the log-linear verdict untouched, with rejections in every split for both statistics (median
p
below the bootstrap resolution); the rejection of the translog augmentation, by contrast, is split-dependent (
48\%
of the splits at the
5\%
level, median
p=0.091
and
0.104
). Because the panel is pooled, the observations share within-state dependence; under such dependence the iid multiplier bootstrap understates the null variance, so the reported
p
-values are optimistic and the rejection should be read as indicative rather than exact.

6
Conclusion

This paper develops a reproducing kernel-based test for conditional moment restrictions. The test performs kernel ridge regression of the estimated residuals on kernel functions and uses the fit as a diagnostic: under the null the population fit vanishes, while under an alternative it is a nonzero element of the RKHS. Projecting the fit onto itself and onto the mean embedding of the residuals yields two test statistics; Neyman orthogonality projections robustify them against estimation effects, and the multiplier bootstrap delivers critical values.

A distinctive feature of the method is that the kernel and regularization parameters are selected by cross-validation rather than fixed by the user. Under a sample-splitting protocol, the selector has a well-defined asymptotic law, the bootstrap test controls size without any selector limit, and an energy decomposition shows that the CV choice maximizes the captured signal (the test’s noncentrality) and, under the null, shrinks the noise floor of the test. Relative to the Gaussian-process approach to model checks of
Escanciano [2024]
, whose class holds the kernel fixed and poses the data-driven selection of the kernel-side tuning as an open problem, the present results resolve that problem for the projection family. Open problems include the data-driven selection of the prior mean and of non-translation-invariant kernels within that class, selection without sample splitting, and optimality of the selector.

Simulations confirm that the test controls size without user-chosen tuning in most configurations and matches the classical kernel conditional moment test at its best kernel parameter, with a clear advantage in higher dimensions, where fixed kernel parameters can be catastrophic. An application to the National Supported Work dataset illustrates the method on the specification of a propensity score model and on the null of a zero conditional average treatment effect. A second application to the Mincer wage equation complements the first: where the NSW propensity-score specification is accepted, the linear schooling specification and its augmentation with quadratic experience, quadratic tenure, and ability are likewise not rejected, by the projection tests or by the fixed-kernel KCM benchmark. A third application tests a log-linear demand equation for cigarettes on instrumental-variables residuals: because the endogenous price is not a function of the instruments, the orthogonalization projects the regressors off the instrument span before testing, the projection tests and the fixed-
\gamma
KCM benchmark both reject the log-linear specification, and a translog augmentation weakens the evidence against the specification to borderline levels.

Practical guidance.

The empirical evidence supports the following recommendations. (i) The sample-splitting protocol with a
15\%
–
20\%
selection share removes every user choice: the projection tests control size without a selector limit, and the two statistics behave identically throughout. (ii) The power of the selected test is governed by the split ratio, with a modest gain when the oracle protocol enlarges both the selection sample (from
60
to
200
observations) and the testing sample (from
340
to
400
), and is otherwise insensitive to the allocation. (iii) In high dimensions the median rule of the classical kernel test can be catastrophic, while the CV rule continues to track the envelope of the fixed kernel parameters. The accurate reading is not that the CV rule beats the classical test at its best fixed kernel parameter, but that it matches that performance while requiring no user choices and remaining reliable where fixed rules fail.

Appendix

This supplement is organized as follows. Section
A
reviews the background on reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces. Section
B
contains the proofs of all results stated in the main text, organized by the main-paper section whose results they prove; each subsection opens with the auxiliary lemmas on which its proofs rely, together with an indication of where each lemma enters. Sections
C
–
F
collect the technical tools used by the proofs, each with its supporting results: random elements in Hilbert spaces, the singular value decomposition of the restriction operator, useful non-asymptotic bounds, and orthogonal transformations. Section
G
reports additional simulation designs and results.

Appendix A
Introduction to Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Spaces (RKHS)

Reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS) are central to kernel methods in machine learning and statistics. For rigorous and comprehensive treatments of this topic, we refer readers to
Wendland [2005]
,
Rosasco et al. [2010]
,
Muandet et al. [2017]
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a non-empty set. A kernel
k:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}
is a function that satisfies, for all
s,t\in\mathcal{S}
,

k(s,t)=\langle\psi(s),\psi(t)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}},

where
\psi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{H}_{k}
is a feature map, and
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is a Hilbert space equipped with the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
. The feature map
\psi
implicitly maps the input space
\mathcal{S}
into a high-dimensional (possibly infinite-dimensional) feature space
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.

A typical example of a kernel is the Gaussian kernel, defined as

k(s,t)=\exp\left(-\gamma\|s-t\|^{2}\right),\quad\gamma>0.

For the special case where
\gamma=1/2
and
d=1
, the corresponding feature map is given by

\psi(t)=\left(\exp(-t^{2}/2),t\exp(-t^{2}/2),\frac{t^{2}\exp(-t^{2}/2)}{\sqrt{2}},\ldots,\frac{t^{i}\exp(-t^{2}/2)}{\sqrt{i!}},\ldots\right),

which maps
t
to an infinite-dimensional space. Such kernels are called positive definite when, for any
c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,
t_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
, and
i=1,\ldots,n
,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{i}c_{j}k(t_{i},t_{j})\geq 0.

The Moore–Aronszajn theorem guarantees that for every positive definite kernel
k
, there exists a unique RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{k}
associated with it. This RKHS is a Hilbert space consisting of functions
f:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}
such that, for all
t\in\mathcal{S}
, the (Dirac) evaluation functional
E_{t}:\mathcal{H}_{k}\to\mathbb{R}
, defined by
E_{t}(f)=f(t)
, is continuous. This continuity ensures the existence of a reproducing kernel, which satisfies the reproducing property:

f(t)=\langle f,k(\cdot,t)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}},\quad\forall f\in\mathcal{H}_{k},\;t\in\mathcal{S}.

Evaluation thus reduces to an inner product against the kernel, which simplifies computation throughout. In particular, the kernel function itself satisfies

k(s,t)=\langle k(\cdot,s),k(\cdot,t)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}},\quad\forall s,t\in\mathcal{S}.

Appendix B
Proofs of the Main Results

Three recurring mechanisms organize the technical machinery. First, the rank-
d_{\theta}
score projection
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
reshapes every null law through the projected kernels
K^{\mathrm{sym},\perp}
and
K^{D,\perp}
, so Section
B.3.1
develops the transport and Lipschitz calculus that tracks their dependence on
(\gamma,\lambda)
. Second, Fourier transport to a common RKHS supplies the
\gamma
-Lipschitz control underlying every uniform modulus. Third, split independence purchases bootstrap validity with no selector asymptotics: conditional on the realized tuning, the bootstrap reduces to a fixed-
\eta
multiplier CLT.

Each subsection opens with the auxiliary lemmas on which its proofs rely, together with a brief indication of where each lemma enters, and the proofs follow the order of the corresponding main-text results. Operator-theoretic and probabilistic tools used across subsections are gathered in the subsequent appendices.

B.1
Proofs for Section
2
: Test Statistics and Their Asymptotic Properties

This subsection proves the five results of Section
2
. Two lemmas come first because both later proofs invoke them: Lemma
1
re-expresses the scaled KRR estimator in a rotated empirical eigenbasis that converges uniformly to the population eigenfunctions within each eigenvalue cluster, which is what allows the test statistics to be compared with their spectral representations despite eigenvalue multiplicity; and Lemma
2
gives the explicit form of the population solution
w^{*}_{\lambda}
, the object whose vanishing under
H_{0}
is at stake. On this foundation, Theorem
1
characterizes the null hypothesis as the RKHS statement
w^{*}_{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{0}
,
Theorem
2
derives the limits of the projection statistic under the null hypothesis and fixed alternatives,
and Theorem
3
derives its local-alternative limit.

B.1.1
Proof of Lemma
1

Fix the
k
-th eigenvalue cluster (multiplicity
m_{k}
) and set
v_{k-1}=\sum_{l=1}^{k-1}m_{l}
. Write
\nu_{i}:=\sigma_{i}^{2}/n
for the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{K}/n
and define the vector of empirical eigenfunctions and the vector of directional coefficients in this cluster:

\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{k}:=(\hat{e}_{v_{k-1}+1},\ldots,\hat{e}_{v_{k}})^{\top},\qquad\mathbf{D}_{n,k}:=\bigl(\langle\sqrt{n}\hat{m},\hat{e}_{v_{k-1}+1}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}},\ldots,\langle\sqrt{n}\hat{m},\hat{e}_{v_{k}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\bigr)^{\top}.

The contribution of the
k
-th cluster to the empirical KRR estimator is

\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}W_{k}\mathbf{D}_{n,k}=\sum_{j=1}^{m_{k}}\frac{1}{\nu_{v_{k-1}+j}+\lambda}\,\langle\sqrt{n}\hat{m},\hat{e}_{v_{k-1}+j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\,\hat{e}_{v_{k-1}+j},

W_{k}:=\operatorname{diag}\bigl((\nu_{v_{k-1}+1}+\lambda)^{-1},\ldots,(\nu_{v_{k}}+\lambda)^{-1}\bigr).

Cluster reduction.

The rotation is applied cluster-by-cluster. Fixing

E_{k}:=\operatorname{span}\{e_{v_{k-1}+1},\ldots,e_{v_{k}}\}

and
\hat{E}_{k}
its empirical counterpart, write
\Delta_{k}:=P_{E_{k}}-\hat{P}_{\hat{E}_{k}}
. The sample projector splits across the empirical ordering,
\hat{P}_{v}=\sum_{k=1}^{V}\hat{P}_{\hat{E}_{k}}
, and
P_{v}=\sum_{k}P_{E_{k}}
, so
\Delta_{k}
telescopes into prefix differences:

\Delta_{k}=\bigl(P_{\leq v_{k}}-\hat{P}_{\leq v_{k}}\bigr)-\bigl(P_{\leq v_{k-1}}-\hat{P}_{\leq v_{k-1}}\bigr),

and Lemma
25
bounds each prefix,
\lVert P_{\leq v_{k}}-\hat{P}_{\leq v_{k}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}=O_{p}\bigl(n^{-1/2}(\mu^{(v_{k})}-\mu^{(v_{k}+1)})^{-1}\bigr)
, by the triangle inequality giving

\lVert\Delta_{k}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}=O_{p}\bigl(n^{-1/2}\bigl[(\mu^{(v_{k})}-\mu^{(v_{k}+1)})^{-1}+(\mu^{(v_{k-1})}-\mu^{(v_{k-1}+1)})^{-1}\bigr]\bigr)=o_{p}(1)

for each fixed cluster under the eigengap condition of Lemma
25
. No blockwise
Wedin argument, and no cross-cluster orthogonality, is required: the per-cluster convergence

\lVert\Delta_{k}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}=o_{p}(1)
is what licenses the rotation of each cluster
independently. The orthogonal matrices of Appendix
F
align the
empirical basis to the population one within the cluster at the rate

\lVert\tilde{e}_{k,j}-e_{k,j}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
. For reference, the cluster deviations
satisfy the exact trace expansion

\lVert P_{v}-\hat{P}_{v}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\sum_{k}\lVert\Delta_{k}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}-2\sum_{k<l}\bigl(\lVert P_{E_{k}}\hat{P}_{\hat{E}_{l}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}+\lVert P_{E_{l}}\hat{P}_{\hat{E}_{k}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}\bigr)
,
whose cross terms are nonpositive; the total therefore does not dominate the per-cluster terms, and
the prefix argument above is the operative one.

Rotation to an orthonormal basis.

Let
O_{k,n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{k}\times m_{k}}
be the random orthogonal matrix of Appendix
F
, and define the rotated eigenfunctions
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{k}:=O_{k,n}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{k}
and the rotated coefficients
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{k}:=O_{k,n}\mathbf{D}_{n,k}
. By orthogonality,
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{k}=O_{k,n}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{k}
and
\mathbf{D}_{n,k}=O_{k,n}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{k}
, so the cluster contribution becomes

\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}W_{k}\mathbf{D}_{n,k}=\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}\bigl(O_{k,n}W_{k}O_{k,n}^{\top}\bigr)\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{k}.

Replacing empirical weights by population weights.

By Lemma
27
, the empirical eigenvalues in the
k
-th cluster converge to the population value,
\sup_{j=1}^{m_{k}}|\nu_{v_{k-1}+j}-\mu^{(k)}|=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
, and the weight function
t\mapsto(t+\lambda)^{-1}
is Lipschitz with constant
\lambda^{-2}
, so

W_{k}=\frac{1}{\mu^{(k)}+\lambda}\,I_{m_{k}}+\Delta W_{k},\qquad\|\Delta W_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})=o_{p}(1).

The scalar multiple of the identity survives the orthogonal conjugation,
O_{k,n}W_{k}O_{k,n}^{\top}=(\mu^{(k)}+\lambda)^{-1}I_{m_{k}}+O_{k,n}\Delta W_{k}O_{k,n}^{\top}
, so the main term is

\frac{1}{\mu^{(k)}+\lambda}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{k}}\langle\sqrt{n}\hat{m},\tilde{e}_{k,j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\,\tilde{e}_{k,j},

and the remainder is
o_{p}(1)
: orthogonal transformations preserve the operator norm,
\|\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{k}\|_{\ell^{2}}\leq\|\sqrt{n}\hat{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=O_{p}(1)
, where the second inequality follows by Bessel’s inequality. We show that
\mathbb{E}\|\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=O(1)
in both regimes of the lemma’s statement. Writing
\varepsilon_{0}=f+v
with
f:=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\mid X]
and
v:=\varepsilon_{0}-f
, so that
\varepsilon_{0}=v
under the null and
\varepsilon_{0}=R/\sqrt{n}+v
under the local alternative,

\mathbb{E}\|\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}\;=\;\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}k(X,X)]+(n{-}1)\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}k(X,X^{\prime})]\;=\;A_{n}+B_{n}.

Here
A_{n}=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}k(X,X)]\leq\bar{k}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}]<\infty
by Assumption
2
(v). For the off-diagonal term, the cross terms and the
vv^{\prime}
term vanish: the pairs
(X,\varepsilon_{0})
and
(X^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime})
are i.i.d. (Assumption
3
(i), with
(X^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime})
an independent copy) and
\mathbb{E}[v\mid X]=0
, so conditioning on
(X,X^{\prime})
in the tower rule gives
\mathbb{E}[k(X,X^{\prime})\,\mathbb{E}[v\mid X]\,\mathbb{E}[v^{\prime}\mid X^{\prime}]]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[k(X,X^{\prime})\,\mathbb{E}[v\mid X]\,f(X^{\prime})]=0
. Hence, with
m_{R}:=\mathbb{E}[R(X)k(X,\cdot)]
and
m_{f}:=\mathbb{E}[f(X)k(X,\cdot)]
,

\displaystyle B_{n}

\displaystyle=(n{-}1)\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}k(X,X^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=(n{-}1)\iint k(x,x^{\prime})f(x)f(x^{\prime})\,d\mathbb{P}_{X}\,d\mathbb{P}_{X}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(n{-}1)\lVert m_{f}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0,&\text{under }H_{0}\ (f=0),\\[2.0pt]
(1-\tfrac{1}{n})\lVert m_{R}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2},&\text{under }H_{1,n}\ (m_{f}=m_{R}/\sqrt{n}),\end{cases}

so
B_{n}=O(1)
in both regimes. With
\|\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sqrt{m_{k}}
, Cauchy–Schwarz gives

\bigl|\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}\bigl(O_{k,n}\Delta W_{k}O_{k,n}^{\top}\bigr)\tilde{\mathbf{D}}_{k}\bigr|\leq\sqrt{m_{k}}\;o_{p}(1)\;O_{p}(1)=o_{p}(1).

The tail.

For the indices beyond the clustered ones no rotation is needed (we keep the empirical eigenfunctions there). By Lemma
27
,
\sum_{i\geq 1}(\nu_{i}-\mu_{i})^{2}=O_{p}(n^{-1})
(with
\nu_{i}=0
for
i>n
), and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\sqrt{n}\hat{m},\hat{e}_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\|\sqrt{n}\hat{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=O_{p}(1)
by Parseval, so replacing the empirical weights
(\nu_{i}+\lambda)^{-1}
by the population weights
(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{-1}
over all remaining indices changes the expansion by at most

\lambda^{-2}\Bigl(\sum_{i\geq 1}(\nu_{i}-\mu_{i})^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\sqrt{n}\hat{m},\hat{e}_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}=o_{p}(1),

by Cauchy–Schwarz. Assembling the cluster contributions and the tail,

\sqrt{n}\,\hat{w}=\sum_{k=1}^{V}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{k}}\frac{1}{\mu^{(k)}+\lambda}\,\langle\sqrt{n}\hat{m},\tilde{e}_{k,j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\,\tilde{e}_{k,j}\;+\;\sum_{i=v_{V}+1}^{n}\frac{1}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\,\langle\sqrt{n}\hat{m},\hat{e}_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\,\hat{e}_{i}+o_{p}(1),

and relabeling the eigenvalue sequence (accounting for multiplicity) as
\{\mu_{i}\}
and the rotated eigenfunctions as
\{\tilde{e}_{i}\}
(with
\tilde{e}_{i}=\hat{e}_{i}
on the tail) gives the compact form stated in the lemma.

B.1.2
Proof of Lemma
2

Population expansion.

By Assumption
1
and Proposition
6
,
the population second-moment operator

C=\mathbb{E}[k(X,\cdot)\otimes k(X,\cdot)]
admits the spectral decomposition

C=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}\,e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
, where
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}

are the eigenvalues (identical to those of the integral operator

\mathbf{L}_{k}
) and
\{e_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
forms an orthonormal basis of

\mathcal{H}_{k}
with
e_{i}=\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,\phi_{i}
, where
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
are
the orthonormal eigenfunctions of
\mathbf{L}_{k}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
.
Because
\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}<\infty
(Assumption
2
(ii)),
C
is a trace-class,
hence Hilbert–Schmidt and compact operator. The transfer of the eigenpairs of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
to
C
rests on three facts. First, the two operators agree pointwise on
\mathcal{H}_{k}
: for
g\in\mathcal{H}_{k}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
,
|g(X)|\leq\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\sqrt{\bar{k}}
by Cauchy–Schwarz and the reproducing bound, so

(Cg)(y)=\mathbb{E}[k(X,y)\langle k(X,\cdot),g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}]=\mathbb{E}[k(X,y)\,g(X)]=(\boldsymbol{L}_{k}g)(y)
,
the middle equality being the reproducing property; hence
C(\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\phi_{i})=\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\boldsymbol{L}_{k}\phi_{i}=\mu_{i}(\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\phi_{i})
, each
\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\phi_{i}
being a
C
-eigenfunction with eigenvalue
\mu_{i}
. Second, the list
\{\mu_{i}\}
exhausts the positive spectrum of
C
: the Mercer diagonal identity
k(x,x)=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}\phi_{i}(x)^{2}
and monotone convergence give
\operatorname{Tr}(C)=\mathbb{E}[k(X,X)]=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}\,\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}(X)^{2}]=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}<\infty
, so
C
carries no eigenvalue beyond
\{\mu_{i}\}
. Third, under Assumption
1
the family
\{\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\phi_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
, so the spectral decomposition of the resolvent below holds on all of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.
