The population coefficient function is
w^{*}_{\lambda}=(C+\lambda I)^{-1}m^{*}
with
m^{*}=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot)]
.
Applying the spectral decomposition of the resolvent,

w^{*}_{\lambda}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\,\langle m^{*},e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\,e_{i}.

The inner product expands as

\langle m^{*},e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\,e_{i}(X)]=\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\,\phi_{i}(X)]
. The first equality passes the inner product through the expectation, which is legitimate because
\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot)
is Bochner integrable,
\mathbb{E}\|\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}\leq\bar{k}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}]<\infty
(Assumptions
2
(i) and
2
(v)), whence
\mathbb{E}|\varepsilon_{0}\,e_{i}(X)|\leq(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}])^{1/2}\mu_{i}^{1/2}<\infty
by Cauchy–Schwarz; the second equality
uses
e_{i}=\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,\phi_{i}
. Substituting
e_{i}=\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,\phi_{i}

yields the claimed representation:

w^{*}_{\lambda}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\,\phi_{i}(X)]\,\phi_{i}.

The series converges in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
. With
a_{i}:=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\phi_{i}(X)]=\mathbb{E}[f(X)\phi_{i}(X)]
, the equality by the tower property (
\phi_{i}
is
X
-measurable,
f=\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}\mid X)
), and
\|\phi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\mu_{i}^{-1}
, the squared norm of any tail satisfies

\Bigl\|\sum_{i>I}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}a_{i}\phi_{i}\Bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\sum_{i>I}\frac{\mu_{i}\,a_{i}^{2}}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}\leq\lambda^{-2}\sum_{i>I}\mu_{i}\,a_{i}^{2}\longrightarrow 0,

because
\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}\,a_{i}^{2}=\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{k}f,f\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq\bar{k}\,\mathbb{E}[f(X)^{2}]<\infty
by Cauchy–Schwarz and
\|\boldsymbol{L}_{k}\|\leq\bar{k}
(Assumption
2
(i)), with
\mathbb{E}[f(X)^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}]<\infty
by Jensen; the partial sums therefore form a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.

Empirical KRR estimator.

With
C_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{l}k(x_{l},\cdot)\otimes k(x_{l},\cdot)
and

\hat{m}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{l}\varepsilon_{0,l}\,k(x_{l},\cdot)
, Proposition
7
gives the duality
identity

\hat{w}=(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}\hat{m}=\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n+\lambda\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}:

Expanding over the empirical eigenpairs
i\leq r:=\operatorname{rank}\boldsymbol{K}
, where
\hat{e}_{i}(x_{l})=\sigma_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}_{i})_{l}
and
\hat{e}_{i}=(n/\sigma_{i})\,\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
, confirms the identity. The spectral decomposition of the inverse is
(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}=\sum_{i\leq r}(\nu_{i}+\lambda)^{-1}\hat{e}_{i}\otimes\hat{e}_{i}+\lambda^{-1}\bigl(I-\sum_{i\leq r}\hat{e}_{i}\otimes\hat{e}_{i}\bigr)
, and the second summand annihilates
\hat{m}
because
\hat{m}=\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}\in\operatorname{Im}(\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top})=\operatorname{span}\{\hat{e}_{1},\dots,\hat{e}_{r}\}=\operatorname{Im}(C_{n})
; the first summand gives
(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}\hat{m}=\sum_{i\leq r}(\nu_{i}+\lambda)^{-1}\nu_{i}^{1/2}(n^{-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})\hat{e}_{i}
, which is the expansion of
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n+\lambda\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
in the same basis.

Consistency:
\|\hat{w}-w_{\lambda}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\to 0
in probability.

Decompose the error as

\hat{w}-w_{\lambda}^{*}=(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}(\hat{m}-m^{*})+\bigl[(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}-(C+\lambda I)^{-1}\bigr]m^{*}.

First term.

Since
\lambda>0
, we have
\|(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lambda^{-1}
(the spectral mapping theorem applied to the
self-adjoint operator
C_{n}
). Therefore

\bigl\|(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}(\hat{m}-m^{*})\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq\lambda^{-1}\,\|\hat{m}-m^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}.

Under Assumption
2
(i),
k
is bounded, and by Assumption
2
(v)

\mathbb{E}\|\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}k(X,X)]\leq\bar{k}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}]<\infty
. The law of large numbers in the RKHS
(Hilbert-space valued SLLN for i.i.d. random elements with finite
second moment; see e.g.
Hsing and Eubank [2015]
, Theorem 7.1)
gives
\|\hat{m}-m^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\to 0
in probability. Hence the first
term is
o_{p}(1)
.

Second term.

Apply the resolvent identity

(A^{-1}-B^{-1})=A^{-1}(B-A)B^{-1}
with
A=C_{n}+\lambda I
,

B=C+\lambda I
:

(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}-(C+\lambda I)^{-1}=(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}\,(C-C_{n})\,(C+\lambda I)^{-1}.

Using
\|(C+\lambda I)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lambda^{-1}
and

\|(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lambda^{-1}
,

\bigl\|\bigl[(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}-(C+\lambda I)^{-1}\bigr]m^{*}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq\lambda^{-2}\,\|C-C_{n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\|m^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}.

Now,
\|m^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
is finite: with
(X^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime})
an independent copy of
(X,\varepsilon_{0})
(Assumption
3
(i)), the Bochner second-moment identity gives
\|m^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}\,k(X,X^{\prime})]
, the interchange being covered by Tonelli’s theorem since
|\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}k(X,X^{\prime})|\leq\bar{k}\,|\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}|
and
\mathbb{E}|\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}|=(\mathbb{E}|\varepsilon_{0}|)^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}]<\infty
by Cauchy–Schwarz; Jensen’s inequality then bounds
\|m^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\|\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}\leq\bar{k}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}]<\infty
.

For the operator convergence, Lemma
26
(Rosasco 2010,
Theorem 7) gives the Hilbert–Schmidt bound

\|C-C_{n}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq 2\sqrt{2}\,\bar{k}\,\sqrt{\tau}/\sqrt{n}

with probability
\geq 1-2e^{-\tau}
. The operator norm is dominated by
the Hilbert–Schmidt norm (
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
),
so
\|C-C_{n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})=o_{p}(1)
. Consequently the
second term is
o_{p}(1)
.

Combining both terms yields
\|\hat{w}-w_{\lambda}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=o_{p}(1)
,
which completes the proof.

B.1.3
Proof of Theorem
1
(Equivalence of the Null Hypothesis)

(
\Rightarrow
).

Under
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}\mid X)=0
, iterated expectations give

\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\,g(X)]=0
for every bounded measurable
g
; taking
g(\cdot)=k(x,\cdot)
yields

m^{*}(x)=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\,k(x,X)]=0
for every
x
, so
m^{*}=\mathbf{0}
in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
and

w^{*}_{\lambda}=(C+\lambda I)^{-1}m^{*}=\mathbf{0}
.

(
\Leftarrow
).

Suppose
w^{*}_{\lambda}=\mathbf{0}
. Since
\lambda>0
,
(C+\lambda I)
is invertible and

m^{*}=(C+\lambda I)w^{*}_{\lambda}=\mathbf{0}
, i.e.
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\,k(X,y)]=0
for every

y\in\mathcal{X}
. With
f(X):=\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}\mid X)
, fix
y\in\mathcal{X}
: the function
X\mapsto k(X,y)f(X)
is
X
-measurable and integrable,
\mathbb{E}|k(X,y)f(X)|\leq\sqrt{k(y,y)}\,(\mathbb{E}[f(X)^{2}])^{1/2}<\infty
by Cauchy–Schwarz, so the tower property and
m^{*}=\mathbf{0}
give

g(y):=\int_{\mathcal{X}}k(x,y)f(x)\,d\mathbb{P}_{X}(x)=\mathbb{E}[k(X,y)\,f(X)]=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\,k(X,y)]=m^{*}(y)=0;

hence
\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}k(x,y)f(x)f(y)\,d\mathbb{P}_{X}(x)d\mathbb{P}_{X}(y)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}f(y)\,g(y)\,d\mathbb{P}_{X}(y)=0
. By integrally strict positive definiteness of the
kernel (Assumption
2
(vi);
Sriperumbudur et al., 2010
), the quadratic form is strictly
positive for every nonzero
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
, so
f=0
, i.e.
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{0}\mid X)=0
almost surely.

B.1.4
Proof of Theorem
2

The projection test statistics can be expressed as RKHS quadratic forms of the
scaled KRR estimator
\sqrt{n}\hat{w}
and the scaled mean embedding

\sqrt{n}\hat{m}
:

n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(1)}=\|\sqrt{n}\hat{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2},\qquad n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(2)}=\langle\sqrt{n}\hat{w},\sqrt{n}\hat{m}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}.

Weak limit of the scaled estimators.

Under
H_{0}
(so that
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\mid X]=0
), the Hilbert-space
CLT (Theorem
13
; the random elements

\varepsilon_{0,i}k(X_{i},\cdot)
are i.i.d. by Assumption
3
(i) and have
finite second moment by Assumptions
2
(i) and
2
(v),
\mathbb{E}\lVert\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot)\rVert^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}k(X,X)]\leq\bar{k}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}]<\infty
) gives

\sqrt{n}\hat{m}\xrightarrow{d}\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
,
where
\Sigma=\mathbb{E}[(\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot))\otimes(\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot))]
is the covariance operator. In particular

\|\sqrt{n}\hat{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=O_{p}(1)
(tightness of the limit).

The KRR estimator satisfies
\hat{w}=(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}\hat{m}

(see the proof of Lemma
2
). By
Lemma
26
,
\|C_{n}-C\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
, hence

\|C_{n}-C\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{p}(1)
; since

\lambda>0
, the resolvent identity

(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}-(C+\lambda I)^{-1}=(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}(C-C_{n})(C+\lambda I)^{-1}

yields
\|(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}-(C+\lambda I)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lambda^{-2}\,\|C_{n}-C\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{p}(1)
, so

(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}\xrightarrow{p}(C+\lambda I)^{-1}
in operator
norm. The mapping
(A,x)\mapsto Ax
is continuous from

\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{k})\times\mathcal{H}_{k}
to
\mathcal{H}_{k}
, so Slutsky’s theorem in Hilbert spaces
(the continuous mapping theorem for joint weak convergence) yields

\sqrt{n}\hat{w}=(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}(\sqrt{n}\hat{m})\xrightarrow{d}(C+\lambda I)^{-1}\xi.

Convergence of
n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(1)}
.

The squared norm is a continuous functional on
\mathcal{H}_{k}
, hence by the
continuous mapping theorem,

n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(1)}\xrightarrow{d}\bigl\|(C+\lambda I)^{-1}\xi\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}.

Let
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
be the eigenvalues of
C
and
\{e_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}

the corresponding orthonormal eigenfunctions, with
e_{i}=\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\phi_{i}

where
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
are the orthonormal eigenfunctions of

\mathbf{L}_{k}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
(see §
2.1
).
Expanding the limit in this basis and using the orthonormality of

\{e_{i}\}
,

\bigl\|(C+\lambda I)^{-1}\xi\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}\,\langle\xi,e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}.

The variables
Z_{i}:=\langle\xi,e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}/\sqrt{\mu_{i}}
are jointly Gaussian with

\operatorname{Cov}(Z_{i},Z_{j})=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\,\phi_{i}(X)\,\phi_{j}(X)]
; indeed, for any

f,g\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
the reproducing property gives

\langle\Sigma f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\,f(X)\,g(X)]
. Under conditional
homoscedasticity (
\mathbb{E}[v^{2}\mid X]=\sigma^{2}
a.s.) this covariance
reduces to
\sigma^{2}\delta_{ij}
: under
H_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{0}=v
a.s., so,

\phi_{i}\phi_{j}
being
X
-measurable,

\Sigma_{ij}=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}(X)\phi_{j}(X)\,\mathbb{E}[v^{2}\mid X]]=\sigma^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}(X)\phi_{j}(X)]=\sigma^{2}\delta_{ij}
, and a
Gaussian sequence with covariance
\sigma^{2}I
has independent components,
which proves the reduction to independent
N(0,\sigma^{2})
variables stated
in Theorem
2
. In particular

\operatorname{Var}(Z_{i})=\tau_{i}:=\operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon_{0}\,\phi_{i}(X))
, where the equality

\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\,\phi_{i}(X)^{2}]=\operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon_{0}\,\phi_{i}(X))
holds because

\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\,\phi_{i}(X)]=0
under
H_{0}
. The infinite
series below are well defined almost surely: by Tonelli’s theorem (the
summands are nonnegative, so no integrability precondition is needed), the
Mercer expansion and
Assumption
2
(v),

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}Z_{i}^{2}\bigr]=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}\,\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}]\leq\lambda^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\phi_{i}(X)^{2}]=\lambda^{-2}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}k(X,X)]\leq\lambda^{-2}\bar{k}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}]<\infty
(and similarly
for the
\mu_{i}/(\mu_{i}+\lambda)
weights), so the series converge almost
surely by monotone convergence. Consequently

n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(1)}\xrightarrow{d}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}\,Z_{i}^{2}.

Convergence of
n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(2)}
.

The inner product is a continuous functional of the pair

(\sqrt{n}\hat{w},\sqrt{n}\hat{m})
, so the continuous mapping theorem
gives

n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(2)}\xrightarrow{d}\langle(C+\lambda I)^{-1}\xi,\xi\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\,\langle\xi,e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\,Z_{i}^{2},

with
Z=(Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
and

\Sigma_{ij}=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\,\phi_{i}(X)\,\phi_{j}(X)]
, as
claimed.

Consistency under fixed alternatives.

Under a fixed alternative,
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\mid X]\neq 0
, so

m^{*}=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}k(X,\cdot)]\neq\mathbf{0}
in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
: by
Assumption
2
(vi) the kernel is ISPD, and the double-integral argument of
Theorem
1
shows that
m^{*}=0
would force

\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\mid X]=0
.
The law of large numbers in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
gives
\hat{m}\xrightarrow{p}m^{*}\neq\mathbf{0}
(the a.s. form of the SLLN holds as well, but only the in-probability mode is consumed),
so
\|\sqrt{n}\hat{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\to\infty
at rate
\sqrt{n}
.
The resolvent
(C+\lambda I)^{-1}
is injective, so the population fit

w^{*}_{\lambda}=(C+\lambda I)^{-1}m^{*}
is a nonzero element of
\mathcal{H}_{k}

(
m^{*}\neq\mathbf{0}
as established above). The KRR estimator satisfies

\sqrt{n}\hat{w}=(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}(\sqrt{n}\hat{m})
, and since

C_{n}\to C
in operator norm, the resolvents also converge: with

\Delta_{n}:=(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}-(C+\lambda I)^{-1}
,

\|\Delta_{n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lambda^{-2}\|C_{n}-C\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{p}(1)

(resolvent identity; Lemma
26
). Decomposing

\sqrt{n}\hat{w}=\sqrt{n}\,w^{*}_{\lambda}+\sqrt{n}\,(C+\lambda I)^{-1}(\hat{m}-m^{*})+\Delta_{n}(\sqrt{n}\hat{m}),

the two remainders are
o_{p}(\sqrt{n})
: the first by

\|\sqrt{n}(C+\lambda I)^{-1}(\hat{m}-m^{*})\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq\sqrt{n}\,\lambda^{-1}\|\hat{m}-m^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=o_{p}(\sqrt{n})
,
the second by

\|\Delta_{n}(\sqrt{n}\hat{m})\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq o_{p}(1)\,O_{p}(\sqrt{n})=o_{p}(\sqrt{n})
,
where
\|\sqrt{n}\hat{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sqrt{n}\,\|m^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+O_{p}(1)=O_{p}(\sqrt{n})
under the fixed alternative. The reverse triangle inequality then gives

\|\sqrt{n}\hat{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sqrt{n}\,\|w^{*}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+o_{p}(\sqrt{n})=\sqrt{n}\,\|w^{*}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}(1+o_{p}(1))
,
so
\|\sqrt{n}\hat{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\to\infty
at rate
\sqrt{n}
. For

n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(2)}
the divergence additionally needs the
limiting inner product to be strictly positive, which holds since

\langle w^{*}_{\lambda},m^{*}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\langle w^{*}_{\lambda},(C+\lambda I)w^{*}_{\lambda}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\langle w^{*}_{\lambda},Cw^{*}_{\lambda}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+\lambda\|w^{*}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}\geq\lambda\|w^{*}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}>0
,
the inequality being
\langle w^{*}_{\lambda},Cw^{*}_{\lambda}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\geq 0
by positive semidefiniteness of
C
. Moreover,

n\hat{T}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{proj}}=n\langle w^{*}_{\lambda},m^{*}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+o_{p}(n)
:
expanding the inner product around
(w^{*}_{\lambda},m^{*})
,

\langle\hat{w},\hat{m}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\langle w^{*}_{\lambda},m^{*}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+\langle\hat{w}-w^{*}_{\lambda},m^{*}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+\langle w^{*}_{\lambda},\hat{m}-m^{*}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+\langle\hat{w}-w^{*}_{\lambda},\hat{m}-m^{*}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\langle w^{*}_{\lambda},m^{*}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+o_{p}(1),

by Cauchy–Schwarz and
\|\hat{w}-w^{*}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=o_{p}(1)
(Lemma
2
, whose proof uses no null-specific input) together with
\|\hat{m}-m^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=o_{p}(1)
(the Hilbert-space law of large numbers). Hence both quadratic forms

n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(1)}=\|\sqrt{n}\hat{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}
and

n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(2)}=n\bigl(\langle w^{*}_{\lambda},m^{*}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+o_{p}(1)\bigr)
diverge to
+\infty
in probability,
so
\mathbb{P}(n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(k)}>t)\to 1
for any fixed

t>0
.

B.1.5
Proof of Theorem
3

Under the local alternative
H_{1,n}
, the residuals decompose as

\varepsilon_{0,l}=\varepsilon_{0,l}^{*}+n^{-1/2}R(X_{l})
, where

\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{*}\mid X]=0
and
R\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
is
non-zero. The scaled mean embedding then splits into a random fluctuation
and a deterministic shift:

\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}=\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{0,l}^{*}\,k(X_{l},\cdot)}_{\xrightarrow{d}\;\xi}\;+\;\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{l=1}^{n}R(X_{l})\,k(X_{l},\cdot)}_{\xrightarrow{a.s.}\;m_{R}},

where
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
by the Hilbert-space CLT
(Theorem
13
; the i.i.d. elements

\varepsilon_{0,l}^{*}k(X_{l},\cdot)
have finite second moment by
Assumption
2
(v)) and
m_{R}:=\mathbb{E}[R(X)k(X,\cdot)]\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
(membership by Bochner integrability:

\mathbb{E}\|R(X)k(X,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq\sqrt{\bar{k}}\,\mathbb{E}|R(X)|\leq\sqrt{\bar{k}}\,\|R\|_{L^{2}}<\infty
), with the displayed

\xrightarrow{a.s.}
convergence given by the Hilbert-space SLLN
(same theorem). Hence

\sqrt{n}\hat{m}\xrightarrow{d}\xi+m_{R}
in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
, and the sequence is
tight (
\|\sqrt{n}\hat{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=O_{p}(1)
).

Weak limit of the scaled estimators.

Exactly as in the proof of Theorem
2
, the resolvent
convergence
(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}\xrightarrow{p}(C+\lambda I)^{-1}

in operator norm combined with Slutsky’s theorem in Hilbert spaces yields

\sqrt{n}\hat{w}=(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}(\sqrt{n}\hat{m})\xrightarrow{d}(C+\lambda I)^{-1}(\xi+m_{R}).

Convergence of the test statistics.

The test statistics are continuous functionals of

(\sqrt{n}\hat{w},\sqrt{n}\hat{m})
, so the continuous mapping theorem
gives, with
e_{i}=\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,\phi_{i}
as in the proof of
Theorem
2
and

\langle m_{R},e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\mathbb{E}[R(X)\,e_{i}(X)]=\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,\mathbb{E}[R(X)\,\phi_{i}(X)]
,

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}\bigl\|(C+\lambda I)^{-1}(\xi+m_{R})\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}

(14)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}\,\bigl(\langle\xi,e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+\langle m_{R},e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\bigr)^{2}

(15)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}\,\bigl(Z_{i}+\mathbb{E}[R(X)\,\phi_{i}(X)]\bigr)^{2},

(16)

and, similarly,

\displaystyle n\hat{T}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}\langle(C+\lambda I)^{-1}(\xi+m_{R}),\,\xi+m_{R}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\,\bigl(\langle\xi,e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}+\langle m_{R},e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\,\bigl(Z_{i}+\mathbb{E}[R(X)\,\phi_{i}(X)]\bigr)^{2},

where
\{Z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
are the jointly Gaussian variables of
Theorem
2
.
Almost-sure summability.
The passage from the finite-
J

partial sums to the infinite series requires that the limiting series converge almost surely, not
merely in distribution term-by-term; this follows by the Tonelli device used in the proof of
Proposition
2
(the unshifted version of the same series): set
S_{i}:=\mu_{i}(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{-2}(Z_{i}+\mathbb{E}[R(X)\phi_{i}(X)])^{2}
and
d_{i}:=\mathbb{E}[R(X)\phi_{i}(X)]
. By Tonelli,

\mathbb{E}[\sum_{i\geq 1}S_{i}]=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mathbb{E}[S_{i}]
, and

\sum_{i\geq 1}\mathbb{E}[S_{i}]\;\leq\;2\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{-2}\,\bigl(\mathbb{E}Z_{i}^{2}+d_{i}^{2}\bigr).

Both sums are finite by direct computation. First,
Z_{i}
is the coordinate pairing of the noise
element against
\phi_{i}
, so
\mu_{i}Z_{i}^{2}=\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\,\langle k(X,\cdot),\phi_{i}\rangle^{2}\,\mu_{i}

and the sum combines through the kernel diagonal:

\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}\,\mathbb{E}Z_{i}^{2}\;=\;\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\textstyle\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}\phi_{i}(X)^{2}\Bigr]\;\leq\;\bar{k}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}],

since
\sum_{i}\mu_{i}\phi_{i}(x)^{2}\leq k_{\gamma}(x,x)\leq\bar{k}
. Second, Bessel’s inequality in

L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
applied to
R
over the orthonormal system
\{\phi_{i}\}
gives

\sum_{i}d_{i}^{2}\leq\lVert R\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}<\infty
. Collecting,

\mathbb{E}[\sum_{i\geq 1}S_{i}]\leq 2\,\sup_{t}(t+\lambda)^{-2}\,\bigl(\bar{k}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}]+\lVert R\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr)<\infty

(with
(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{-2}\leq\lambda^{-2}
). Finite expectation implies summability almost surely,
so the series converges a.s. and the displayed limits hold as random series. This completes the proof.

B.2
Proofs for Section
3
: Estimation Effects

These proofs support Section
3
, again with the supporting lemma first.
Lemma
3
quantifies the RKHS remainder produced by Neyman orthogonalization when

\hat{\theta}
replaces
\theta_{0}
and is quoted by both corollaries that follow. Corollaries
1

and
2
transfer the null and local-alternative laws from the infeasible statistic to its projected
counterpart, and Theorem
4
establishes conditional consistency of the multiplier bootstrap
for the projected statistic.

B.2.1
Proof of Lemma
3

Throughout the proof, the inverses
\bigl(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}\bigr)^{-1}
and

\bigl(Z^{\top}Z\bigr)^{-1}
are understood on the invertibility events

\lambda_{\min}(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}/n)>0
resp.

\lambda_{\min}(Z^{\top}Z/n)>0
, which hold with probability tending to one by
Assumption
5
(ii) and Assumption
6
(ii) respectively. For each observation
i
, we expand the estimated residual
\varepsilon(s_{i};\hat{\theta})
around the true parameter
\theta_{0}
using a second-order Taylor expansion. To introduce the estimated score matrix
\mathbb{G}
(which forms the projection
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
), we add and subtract the estimated gradient
\hat{g}_{i}^{\top}=\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\hat{\theta})^{\top}
from the first-order term:

\displaystyle\varepsilon(s_{i};\hat{\theta})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(s_{i};\theta_{0})+\hat{g}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\left[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\theta_{0})-\hat{g}_{i}\right]^{\top}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})}_{\text{Term A}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})^{\top}\nabla_{\theta}^{2}\varepsilon(s_{i};\tilde{\theta}_{i})(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})}_{\text{Term B}},

where
\tilde{\theta}_{i}
lies on the line segment between
\hat{\theta}
and
\theta_{0}
. Let
v_{i}=\text{Term A}+\text{Term B}
denote the remainder for observation
i
.

We first determine the order of Term A. Applying the integral form of the vector Mean Value Theorem to the gradient difference yields:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\theta_{0})-\hat{g}_{i}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\theta_{0})-\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\hat{\theta})

\displaystyle=-\left(\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{\theta}^{2}\varepsilon(s_{i};\theta_{0}+t(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}))dt\right)(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}).

Under Assumption
5
(iii), the raw Hessian matrix
\nabla_{\theta}^{2}\varepsilon(s_{i};\theta)
is uniformly bounded over the compact parameter space
\Theta
in the sense that
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\lVert\nabla_{\theta}^{2}\varepsilon(s_{i};\theta)\rVert=O_{p}(1)
pointwise in
i
. Assumption
5
(iii) constrains the raw, possibly non-
X
-measurable Hessian, which is needed because
\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon
need not be
X
-measurable, cf. Assumption
6
. Since
\hat{\theta},\theta_{0}\in\Theta
, the integral of the Hessian along the connecting path is also
O_{p}(1)
. Using Assumption
4
(iii),
\lVert\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}\rVert=O_{p}(n^{-\varrho})
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\theta_{0})-\hat{g}_{i}\right\|\leq O_{p}(1)\cdot\lVert\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}\rVert=O_{p}(n^{-\varrho}).

Consequently, Term A is of order:

\text{Term A}=\left[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\theta_{0})-\hat{g}_{i}\right]^{\top}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})=O_{p}(n^{-\varrho})\cdot O_{p}(n^{-\varrho})=O_{p}(n^{-2\varrho}).

For Term B, the Hessian is
O_{p}(1)
by Assumption
5
(iii), so:

\text{Term B}=O_{p}(n^{-\varrho})\cdot O_{p}(1)\cdot O_{p}(n^{-\varrho})=O_{p}(n^{-2\varrho}).

Combining these, the element-wise remainder is
r_{i}^{(2)}=O_{p}(n^{-2\varrho})
. Stacking the expansions for all
n
observations into vector notation, and letting
\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}=(r_{1}^{(2)},\dots,r_{n}^{(2)})^{\top}
, yields:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\theta}}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}+\mathbb{G}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})+\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)},

where
\mathbb{G}
is the matrix of estimated scores with rows
\hat{g}_{i}^{\top}=\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\hat{\theta})^{\top}
of the expansion above, so that the identity is exact with
\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
collecting Term A and Term B, and
\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}=O_{p}(n^{-2\varrho})
element-wise.

Applying the projection matrix
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{n}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}
, where
\hat{\mathbb{G}}=\boldsymbol{P}_{Z}\mathbb{G}
is the sieve estimate of
g_{\theta_{0}}(X)
, to both sides, and decomposing the raw score as
\mathbb{G}=\hat{\mathbb{G}}+(\mathbb{G}-\hat{\mathbb{G}})
, yields:

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\theta}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}+\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\mathbb{G}-\hat{\mathbb{G}})(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)},

using the finite-sample orthogonality
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\hat{\mathbb{G}}=\boldsymbol{0}
.
Set
\boldsymbol{r}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\mathbb{G}-\hat{\mathbb{G}})(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
.
Unlike the
X
-measurable-score case, the first-order term is not annihilated pointwise by the projection: the projection annihilates only the estimated score
\hat{\mathbb{G}}
, and the idiosyncratic score
\mathbb{G}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}
is
not
exactly orthogonal to the sieve columns in the finite sample.
Elimination holds only in expectation:
\mathbb{G}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}\xrightarrow{p}\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon-g_{\theta_{0}}(X)
, the idiosyncratic score, and
\mathbb{E}[(\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon-g_{\theta_{0}}(X))\,\phi(X)]=0
for every square-integrable
\phi
, because the idiosyncratic score is conditionally mean-zero given
X
(law of iterated expectations).
Both parts of
\boldsymbol{r}
are bounded by the
\ell^{2}
(spectral) route, which is exactly what the RKHS norms require.
With
\boldsymbol{K}=\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\top}
, the two remainder norms read

\displaystyle\|R_{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\sum_{i}\frac{n\,\sigma_{i}^{2}}{(\sigma_{i}^{2}+n\lambda)^{2}}\,(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{r})^{2},\qquad\|R_{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}^{2}(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{r})^{2},

and each is a weighted quadratic form in the coefficient vector
\boldsymbol{r}
: with
w_{i}:=\nu_{i}/(\nu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}\leq\lambda^{-2}\nu_{i}
(so that
n\,\sigma_{i}^{2}/(\sigma_{i}^{2}+n\lambda)^{2}=\nu_{i}/(\nu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}=w_{i}
; here
\lambda
is the single fixed regularization constant of this lemma, and no uniformity over a
\lambda
-range is needed),

\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\,(\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{r})^{2}=\boldsymbol{r}^{\top}\boldsymbol{U}\operatorname{diag}(w_{i})\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{r}\leq\lambda^{-2}\,\boldsymbol{r}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)\boldsymbol{r},

using
\lVert\boldsymbol{K}/n\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)=1
(the Gaussian kernel has
k_{\gamma}(x,x)=1
); the same bound without the weights
w_{i}
covers the
R_{m}
display.
For the first-order part, write
\mathbb{G}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}+(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{g}+(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}\,\boldsymbol{\delta}
, with
\boldsymbol{\delta}:=\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}
the conditionally centered idiosyncratic score matrix (rows
\eta_{0,j}^{\top}=\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{j};\theta_{0})^{\top}-g_{\theta_{0}}(X_{j})^{\top}
),
\boldsymbol{g}
the matrix of population scores, and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}
the matrix of path-averaged Hessians (rows
\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{\theta}^{2}\varepsilon(s_{j};\theta_{0}+t\boldsymbol{\delta})\,\mathrm{d}t
).
The third piece is
O_{p}(n^{-\varrho})
element-wise (Assumption
5
(iii), pointwise in
j
) and, multiplied by
\boldsymbol{\delta}
, is folded into the second-order part
\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
below (redefined to include it).
The sieve-error mean
\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{sieve}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{g}\,\boldsymbol{\delta}
is controlled pathwise: since
(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{g}=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})(\boldsymbol{g}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}})
(the fitted sieve values
\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}=P_{Z}\mathbb{G}
are annihilated by
\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z}
),

(\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{sieve}})^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{sieve}}\leq\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}\rVert^{2}\,\lVert(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{g}\rVert_{F}^{2}\leq\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}\rVert^{2}\,\lVert\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{F}^{2}=O_{p}(n^{-2\varrho})\,o_{p}(n^{2\varrho})=o_{p}(1),

where
\lVert\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{F}^{2}=n\,\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}^{2}=o_{p}(n^{2\varrho})
by Pythagoras, the bridge below, and the sieve estimation-error rate
o_{p}(n^{\varrho-1/2})
of Assumption
6
(iii); the sieve-mean piece is itself
o_{p}(1)
.

Bridge between the population and empirical norms.
Assumption
6
(iii) controls

\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})}
, while the display consumes the
empirical norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}
; the two agree up to a negligible relative
error under the bridge condition
K_{n}=o(\sqrt{n})
, where
K_{n}
is the sieve dimension of Assumption
6
(iii) (the standing dimension restriction
L^{2\varsigma+1}\log n=o(n)
gives
L\log n=o(\sqrt{n})
, hence
L=o(\sqrt{n})
), which covers every
\varrho>0
. The
displayed rate is driven by the maximal term
O_{p}(B^{2}K_{n}r^{2}/\sqrt{n})
; weaker conditions such as

K_{n}\log n=o(n^{2\varrho-1/2})
suffice only for
\varrho\leq 1/2
, since for
\varrho>1/2
they
permit
K_{n}\gg\sqrt{n}
, under which the relative error need not vanish.
Writing the error in the sieve basis,

\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}=\sum_{\ell\leq K_{n}}(\hat{c}_{\ell}-c_{\ell}^{*})\psi_{\ell}
, the difference
of squared norms is the single centered quantity
(P_{n}-P_{X})[(\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}})^{2}]
.
The class
\{h^{2}:\ h=\sum_{\ell\leq K_{n}}b_{\ell}\psi_{\ell},\ \lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})}\leq r\}

consists of functions bounded by
B^{2}r^{2}
(basis
\|\psi_{\ell}\|_{\infty}\leq B
) with VC dimension

O(K_{n})
, so the standard empirical-process maximal inequality gives

\sup_{\lVert h\rVert\leq r}\bigl|(P_{n}-P_{X})[h^{2}]\bigr|=O_{p}\!\left(\frac{B^{2}K_{n}\,r^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

The displayed order applies the VC-type estimate
O_{p}(\sqrt{\mathrm{VC}/n}\,\|\cdot\|_{P,2})
, with generalized norm
\|h^{2}\|_{P,2}\leq B^{2}r^{2}
up to constants and
\mathrm{VC}=O(K_{n})
; the VC form gives the weaker
O_{p}(B^{2}K_{n}^{1/2}r^{2}/\sqrt{n})
, and both forms are
o_{p}(r^{2})
under
K_{n}=o(\sqrt{n})
. Under the side condition

K_{n}=o(\sqrt{n})
this error is
o_{p}(r^{2})
, whence

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})}(1+o_{p}(1))
on the relevant ball, and the empirical-norm consumption is justified. The same bridge is
invoked wherever an
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})
rate is consumed below.

For the centered part
\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}\,\boldsymbol{\delta}
, no higher moments of
\boldsymbol{\delta}
are needed. Let
M:=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})
.
The
\mathbf{X}
-measurability of
M
is not immediate from the display, since
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}

depends on
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
through
\hat{\mathbb{G}}
; it follows from the identity

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z}),

which holds because
(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})v
is orthogonal to

\operatorname{range}(P_{Z})\supseteq\operatorname{range}(\hat{\mathbb{G}})
(the sieve dimension may exceed
d_{\theta}
), and
finite-sample orthogonality gives
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\hat{\mathbb{G}}=\boldsymbol{0}
, hence

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})v=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})v
; and, since the projector

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
is symmetric idempotent, transposing also gives

(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})
. Substituting both identities,

M=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})(\boldsymbol{K}/n)(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z}),

a polynomial in the
\mathbf{X}
-measurable Gram matrix
\boldsymbol{K}
:
M
is
\mathbf{X}
-measurable
as asserted. Write
A:=\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}^{\top}M\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}
; with
\boldsymbol{Q}_{Z}:=\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z}
and
M=\boldsymbol{Q}_{Z}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)\boldsymbol{Q}_{Z}
(shown above),
A=W^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)W
for
W:=\boldsymbol{Q}_{Z}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}
. The rows of
W
are conditionally centered given
\mathbf{X}
but not conditionally independent, the
\mathbf{X}
-measurable
\boldsymbol{Q}_{Z}
mixing the
\eta_{0}
-rows; the fourth-moment calculation therefore pairs indices through the conditional independence of the
\eta_{0}
-rows themselves, and each surviving four-index term is
O(1)
by
\mathbb{E}\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}\rVert^{4}<\infty
(Assumption
5
(iii)) together with
\operatorname{tr}\big[(\boldsymbol{K}/n)\boldsymbol{Q}_{Z}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)\boldsymbol{Q}_{Z}\big]\leq\operatorname{tr}[(\boldsymbol{K}/n)^{2}]\leq\bar{k}^{2}
; also
\big\lVert\mathbb{E}[A\mid\mathbf{X}]\big\rVert_{F}\leq\bar{\Sigma}\,\operatorname{tr}[(\boldsymbol{K}/n)\boldsymbol{Q}_{Z}]\leq\bar{\Sigma}
, with
\bar{\Sigma}:=\sup_{x}\mathbb{E}[\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0,1}\rVert^{2}\mid X=x]<\infty
. Hence
\mathbb{E}\lVert A\rVert_{F}^{2}=O(1)
, and
\lVert A\rVert_{F}=O_{p}(1)
by Markov. Therefore

(\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}})^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}}=\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\top}A\,\boldsymbol{\delta}\leq\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}\rVert^{2}\,\lVert A\rVert_{F}=O_{p}(n^{-2\varrho}),

using only
\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}\rVert=O_{p}(n^{-\varrho})
from Assumption
4
(iii). Thus the centered contribution to both
\|R_{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}
and
\|R_{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}
is
O_{p}(n^{-2\varrho})
(the two displays above differ only by the bounded weights
w_{i}\leq\lambda^{-2}\nu_{i}
, with

\lambda
the single fixed constant of this section).

For the second-order part
\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{proj}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
, with
\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
extended to include the folded-in gradient-drift piece
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{w}=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{w}
with rows
w_{j}=\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}_{j}^{\top}\,\boldsymbol{\delta}
(element-wise
O_{p}(n^{-2\varrho})
, exactly as Term A and Term B; the identity
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})
established above applies), no centering is available:
r_{j}^{(2)}
has a nonzero conditional mean of order
O_{p}(n^{-2\varrho})
(the leading term is a quadratic form in
\boldsymbol{\delta}
built from the conditional mean of the Hessian), and its cross terms do not vanish. The pathwise bound suffices: with
\lVert\boldsymbol{K}/n\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
,

(\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{proj}})^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{proj}}\leq\lVert\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}\rVert^{2}\leq C\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}\rVert^{4}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\lVert\nabla^{2}_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{j};\theta)\rVert^{2}=O_{p}(n^{1-4\varrho}),

The strengthened Assumption
5
(iii) supplies

\mathbb{E}\big[\sup_{\theta}\lVert\nabla^{2}_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta)\rVert^{2}\big]<\infty,

so
\sum_{j}\sup_{\theta}\lVert\nabla^{2}_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{j};\theta)\rVert^{2}=O_{p}(n)
, and
\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}\rVert^{4}=O_{p}(n^{-4\varrho})
by Assumption
4
(iii). The universal constant
C
collects the quadratic and cross terms of Term A, Term B, and the gradient-drift piece. This is the same
O_{p}(n^{1-4\varrho})
as in the bootstrap-remainder computation of the proof of Theorem
4
below. Therefore
\|R_{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\|R_{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=o_{p}(1)
whenever
\varrho>1/4
: the centered and second-order pieces are
O_{p}(n^{-2\varrho})
and
O_{p}(n^{1-4\varrho})
, and the sieve-mean piece is
o_{p}(1)
by the bound above. The between-piece cross terms of the decomposition
\boldsymbol{r}=\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{sieve}}+\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}}+\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{proj}}
are
o_{p}(1)
as well: Cauchy–Schwarz for the positive semidefinite form
(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\mapsto\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)\boldsymbol{b}
bounds each mixed term by the product of the square roots of the corresponding diagonal quadratic forms, giving
o_{p}(1)\cdot O_{p}(n^{-\varrho})=o_{p}(1)
(sieve
\times
centered),
o_{p}(1)\cdot O_{p}(n^{1/2-2\varrho})=o_{p}(1)
for
\varrho>1/4
(sieve
\times
second-order), and
O_{p}(n^{1/2-3\varrho})=o_{p}(1)
for
\varrho>1/4
(centered
\times
second-order). The constants may be taken uniform in
\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
: the proof’s only
\gamma
-dependent inputs are
\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}/n)=1
and the bounds on
\lambda
.

B.2.2
Proofs of Corollaries
1
–
2

By Lemma
3
and the subsequent RKHS norm bounds, replacing
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}
with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
in the scaled coefficient function and mean embedding introduces only an
o_{p}(1)
remainder in the RKHS norm. Because the test statistics are continuous functionals (inner products and squared norms) of these RKHS objects, the continuous mapping theorem allows this substitution, and it remains to determine the law of the projected mean embedding
\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}_{\perp}:=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})_{i}\,k(X_{i},\cdot)
.

The projection correction does
not
vanish at the scaled level. Write
\hat{\beta}:=(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}
for the empirical regression coefficient of
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
on the score matrix (a distinct symbol from the KRR estimator
\hat{w}
); then
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}\hat{\beta}
and

\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}_{\perp}=\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}-\big(R_{n}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}\big)\big(\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\beta}\big),\qquad\hat{m}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{0,i}\,k(X_{i},\cdot).

Under
H_{0}
,
R_{n}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}k(X_{i},\cdot)\,\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(X_{i})^{\top}\xrightarrow{p}A:=\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta_{0}}(X)\,k(X,\cdot)]\in\mathcal{H}^{d_{\theta}}
(the law of large numbers in
\mathcal{H}
, with Assumptions
5
and
6
),
\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}/n\xrightarrow{p}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}
, and, jointly with the CLT
\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}\xrightarrow{d}\xi
for the unprojected embedding (
\xi
being the Gaussian element of Theorem
2
), the Cramér–Wold device applied to the
\mathcal{H}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}
-valued sums
\sum_{i}(\varepsilon_{0,i}k(X_{i},\cdot),\,\varepsilon_{0,i}\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(X_{i}))
yields this joint limit, whose cross-covariance is computed below,

\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\beta}=\Big(\frac{\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}}{n}\Big)^{-1}\frac{\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}\xrightarrow{d}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}W,\qquad W\sim\mathcal{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)^{\top}]\big),

with
\operatorname{Cov}(\langle\xi,f\rangle,W)=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)f(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)]
for every
f\in\mathcal{H}
, the cross-covariance following from
\mathbb{E}[v^{2}\mid X]=\sigma^{2}(X)
.

Score estimation inside the sums.
The summands involve the estimated score

\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(X_{i})
, which is data-dependent through
\hat{\theta}
, so the sums are not
literally built from i.i.d. summands. Decompose

\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}=g_{\theta_{0}}+(\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}})
: the population-score part

n^{-1}\sum_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)\,g_{\theta_{0}}(X_{i})^{\top}
is the i.i.d. mean to which the LLN/CLT
statements apply, while the sieve-error part is bounded by

\bar{k}\,\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}\leq\bar{k}\,\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}
. In the vacuous-sieve
regime (
K_{n}=p
fixed), pointwise Lipschitz continuity of
x\mapsto\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(x)
in

\hat{\theta}
gives
\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{\infty}=O_{p}(\lVert\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}\rVert)=O_{p}(n^{-\varrho})=o_{p}(1)
, so the replacement is
o_{p}(1)
and every displayed limit is unchanged. For a
genuine growing sieve, the sup-norm deviation requires a triangular-array treatment beyond
Assumption
6
(iii): the limit statements below therefore carry the explicit side condition

\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{\infty}=O_{p}(n^{-1/4})

(sufficient for all uses here; implied, e.g., by Lipschitz sieve bases with

\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}=O_{p}(K_{n}^{-\varsigma})
,

\varsigma K_{n}/n\to 0
, and
\varrho>1/4
). Hence, by the continuous mapping theorem,

\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}_{\perp}\xrightarrow{d}G_{\perp}:=\xi-A^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}W,

a non-degenerate Gaussian element different from
\xi
. In the location model
g_{\theta_{0}}\equiv 1
,
G_{\perp}=\xi-Z\,\mathbb{E}[k(X,\cdot)]
with
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{\sigma}^{2})
,
\bar{\sigma}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)]
: the law of the noise with the constant direction removed. The directional projections
Z_{i}^{\perp}:=\langle G_{\perp},e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}/\sqrt{\mu_{i}}
are jointly Gaussian with the covariance matrix
\Sigma^{\perp}
of Corollary
1
:
\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)(\phi_{i}(X)-\kappa_{i}(X))(\phi_{j}(X)-\kappa_{j}(X))]
, where
\kappa_{i}
is the population
L^{2}
-projection coefficient of
\phi_{i}
onto
\operatorname{span}(g_{\theta_{0}})
,
\kappa_{i}(x):=g_{\theta_{0}}(x)^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta_{0}}(X)\phi_{i}(X)]
. The exact distributional limits of Corollary
1
follow by the same continuous functionals as in the proof of Theorem
2
. Under the local alternative
H_{1,n}
,
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=R/\sqrt{n}+\boldsymbol{v}
: the noise law is unchanged (
\mathbb{E}[v\mid X]=0
), and the mean shift is
\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}R(X)\phi_{i}(X))
. To see the shift, split the scaled projected embedding
\sqrt{n}\,\hat{m}_{\perp}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)
as in the null case into the noise part (unchanged, converging to
G_{\perp}
) and the shift part
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}R)_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)/\sqrt{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}R)_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)\xrightarrow{p}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\Pi}R(X)k(X,\cdot)]
.
In addition, the
\hat{\beta}
-part is not negligible:
\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\beta}=(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}/n)^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}/\sqrt{n}\xrightarrow{d}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}(\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta_{0}}(X)R(X)]+W^{\prime})
, so
(R_{n}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\beta}
contributes the deterministic limit
A^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta_{0}}(X)R(X)]
. The two deterministic parts combine into the projected shift

\mathbb{E}[R(X)k(X,\cdot)]-A^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta_{0}}(X)R(X)]=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\Pi}R(X)k(X,\cdot)],

whose
\phi_{i}
-coefficient is
\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\Pi}R(X)\phi_{i}(X)]
, as claimed. Under a fixed alternative with pseudo-true limit
(\theta^{*},f^{*})
as in Corollary
1
:
\hat{\theta}\to\theta^{*}
, conditional mean
f^{*}
, rate condition around
\theta^{*}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\theta^{*}}f^{*}\neq 0
, so that
\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\neq 0
. The population KRR fit on the projected residuals is
w^{*}_{\lambda,\perp}\neq 0
(the kernel integral operator is injective), and the consistency of Lemma
2
transfers to the projected residuals: the empirical mean embedding
n^{-1}\sum_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\theta}})_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)
converges to
m_{\perp}:=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\,k(X,\cdot)]\neq\boldsymbol{0}
by the same injectivity. Two laws of large numbers combine:
n^{-1}\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{\hat{\theta},i}\,k(X_{i},\cdot)\xrightarrow{p}\mathbb{E}[f^{*}\,k(X,\cdot)]
, the conditional mean of
\varepsilon_{\hat{\theta}}
converging to
f^{*}
through
\hat{\theta}\xrightarrow{p}\theta^{*}
and the sup-norm side condition of Assumption
6
(iii); and the projection correction converges, with
\hat{\beta}_{\hat{\theta}}:=(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\hat{\theta}}\xrightarrow{p}\Gamma_{\theta^{*}}^{-1}\,\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta^{*}}(X)\,f^{*}(X)]
and
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}\xrightarrow{p}\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta^{*}}(X)\,k(X,\cdot)]
, to
\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta^{*}}(X)\,k(X,\cdot)]\,\Gamma_{\theta^{*}}^{-1}\,\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta^{*}}(X)\,f^{*}(X)]
; by the projection identity
\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\Pi}R\,k]=\mathbb{E}[R\,k]-\mathbb{E}[g\,k]\,\Gamma^{-1}\,\mathbb{E}[g\,R]
established above, applied at
(R,\,g,\,\Gamma)=(f^{*},\,g_{\theta^{*}},\,\Gamma_{\theta^{*}})
, the two limits combine into
m_{\perp}=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\,k(X,\cdot)]
; this gives
\lVert\hat{w}_{\perp}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\xrightarrow{p}\lVert w^{*}_{\lambda,\perp}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}>0
; moreover
\langle w^{*}_{\lambda,\perp},m_{\perp}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\langle(C+\lambda I)^{-1}m_{\perp},m_{\perp}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\geq\lambda\lVert w^{*}_{\lambda,\perp}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}>0
, and both statistics diverge:
\mathbb{P}(n\hat{T}^{(k)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}>t)\to 1
for
k=1,2
and every fixed
t>0
.

B.2.3
Proof of Theorem
4

Estimation effect is asymptotically negligible.

By Lemma
3
, the orthogonalisation removes the
first-order estimation effect: the RKHS remainders
R_{w}
and
R_{m}
involving

\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}
are
o_{p}(1)
. Hence, in the asymptotic analysis we
may replace the projected bootstrap residual

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}\odot\mathbf{V})

by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}\odot\mathbf{V})
.
The lemma is stated for the unmultiplied difference; the multiplied
remainder requires a separate argument, because the multipliers break the
annihilation of the sieve-projected part,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}))=\boldsymbol{0}
(the diagonal multiplier does not commute with the projection), while the projection annihilates only
\hat{\mathbb{G}}
, not the raw score.
Write
\boldsymbol{\Delta}:=\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=\mathbb{G}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\theta+\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
with
\boldsymbol{\Delta}\theta:=\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}
,
\mathbb{G}
the matrix of raw scores with rows
\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\hat{\theta})^{\top}
, and
\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
the second-order remainder of Lemma
3
(Term A + Term B).
Set
\mathsf{W}_{n}:=(\boldsymbol{K}/n+\lambda I)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)(\boldsymbol{K}/n+\lambda I)^{-1}
, so that
\lVert\sqrt{n}\,\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n+\lambda I)^{-1}\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\mathsf{W}_{n}\boldsymbol{u}
for every vector
\boldsymbol{u}
.
The conditional second moment of the scaled multiplied remainder satisfies, using
\mathbb{E}[V_{i}^{2}]=1
,

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lVert\sqrt{n}\,\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}/n+\lambda I)^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}\odot\boldsymbol{V})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}\mid D_{n}\bigr]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathsf{W}_{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\big)_{ii}\,\Delta_{i}^{2}.

For the first-order part: the multiplied first-order piece is

\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{(1)}:=(\mathbb{G}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\theta)\odot\boldsymbol{V}
, and since

\mathbb{E}[V_{i}^{2}]=1
its conditional second moment is computed from the unmultiplied squares; that
second moment equals
(\boldsymbol{\Delta}\theta)^{\top}B_{n}\,(\boldsymbol{\Delta}\theta)
with
B_{n}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathsf{W}_{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\big)_{ii}\,\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\hat{\theta})\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\hat{\theta})^{\top}
, and
\lVert B_{n}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{p}(\sqrt{n})
:
by exchangeability
\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathsf{W}_{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\big)_{11}\big]=n^{-1}\,\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{tr}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathsf{W}_{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\big)\big]\leq n^{-1}\,\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}(\mathsf{W}_{n})]\leq n^{-1}\lambda^{-2}\,\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)]=O(n^{-1})
(using
\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol{K}/n)=1
), and
\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathsf{W}_{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\big)_{11}\leq\lVert\mathsf{W}_{n}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1/(4\lambda)
, so
\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathsf{W}_{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\big)_{11}^{2}\big]\leq(1/(4\lambda))\,\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathsf{W}_{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\big)_{11}\big]=O(n^{-1})
.
Hence
\mathbb{E}\lVert B_{n}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq n\,\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathsf{W}_{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\big)_{11}\,\lVert\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S_{1};\hat{\theta})\rVert^{2}\big]\leq n\,\big(\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathsf{W}_{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\big)_{11}^{2}\big]\big)^{1/2}\big(\mathbb{E}\lVert\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S_{1};\hat{\theta})\rVert^{4}\big)^{1/2}=O(\sqrt{n})
(the fourth moment of the raw score by Assumption
5
(iii)),
so
\lVert B_{n}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{p}(\sqrt{n})
by Markov, and the first-order contribution is
O_{p}\big(\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}\theta\rVert^{2}\,\sqrt{n}\big)=O_{p}(n^{1/2-2\varrho})=o_{p}(1)
for
\varrho>1/4
.
For the second-order part,
\sum_{i}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathsf{W}_{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\big)_{ii}\,r_{i}^{(2)2}\leq\lVert\mathsf{W}_{n}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\lVert\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}\rVert^{2}=O_{p}(n^{1-4\varrho})=o_{p}(1)
(the second-moment version of Lemma
3
under Assumption
5
(iii)).
The expansion in
\Delta_{i}=\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\hat{\theta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\theta+r_{i}^{(2)}
also carries the first
\times
second-order cross term
2\sum_{i}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathsf{W}_{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\big)_{ii}\big(\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\hat{\theta})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\theta\big)r_{i}^{(2)}
; Cauchy–Schwarz for the weighted inner product with weights
w_{i}:=\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\mathsf{W}_{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\big)_{ii}\geq 0
bounds it by
2\big[(\boldsymbol{\Delta}\theta)^{\top}B_{n}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}\theta)\big]^{1/2}\big[\sum_{i}w_{i}\big(r_{i}^{(2)}\big)^{2}\big]^{1/2}=O_{p}\big(n^{1/4-\varrho}\big)\,O_{p}\big(n^{1/2-2\varrho}\big)=O_{p}\big(n^{3/4-3\varrho}\big)=o_{p}(1)
for
\varrho>1/4
(Assumption
4
(iii)), using the two bounds just established.
Hence the conditional second moment of the multiplied remainder is
o_{p}(1)
, so it is conditionally
o_{p}(1)
by Markov, and the replacement is justified; the same bound holds for the
\boldsymbol{R}_{m}
-type remainder. The projection itself is
not
negligible at the scaled level: the
componentwise convergence

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}\xrightarrow{p}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
does not survive at the

O_{p}(1)
scale of the scaled mean embedding (see the proof of
Corollary
1
). The bootstrap embedding therefore retains
the projection, in the
multiply-then-project
form that matches the
bootstrap statistics of Section
3
,

\tilde{m}_{\perp}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}\odot\boldsymbol{V})\big)_{i}\,k(X_{i},\cdot),

and its conditional limit is the projected law of Corollary
1
.

Multiplier CLT in the RKHS.

Using
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}
for the score projection of the multiplied residuals,

\sqrt{n}\,\tilde{m}_{\perp}=\tilde{m}^{*}-\big(R_{n}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}\big)\,\hat{w}^{*},\qquad\tilde{m}^{*}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}\,\varepsilon_{0,i}\,k(X_{i},\cdot),\qquad\hat{w}^{*}:=\Big(\frac{\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}}{n}\Big)^{-1}\frac{\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{V}\odot\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})}{\sqrt{n}}.

Conditional on the data
D_{n}
, the pair
(\tilde{m}^{*},\hat{w}^{*})
converges
jointly: the multiplier CLT in Hilbert spaces
(Theorem
14
) applied to the i.i.d. elements

U_{i}:=\varepsilon_{0,i}\,k(X_{i},\cdot)
(bounded kernel,

\mathbb{E}[\|U_{i}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}]\leq\bar{k}\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}]<\infty

by Assumption
2
(v))
gives
\tilde{m}^{*}\xrightarrow{d}\xi^{*}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)

conditionally on the data (a.s. by the theorem, hence in probability), with

\Sigma=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\,k(X,\cdot)\otimes k(X,\cdot)]
; the
finite-dimensional conditional CLT gives

\hat{w}^{*}\xrightarrow{d}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}W^{*}
, with

W^{*}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)^{\top}])

(conditionally, in probability). The Lindeberg input is assembled in two
steps: the tail bound
\max_{i\leq n}\varepsilon_{0,i}^{2}=o_{p}(n)
holds by
Chebyshev’s inequality and the union bound,

\mathbb{P}(\max_{i\leq n}\varepsilon_{0,i}^{2}>\varepsilon^{2}n)\leq n\,\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\mathbf{1}(\varepsilon_{0}^{2}>\varepsilon^{2}n)]/(\varepsilon^{2}n)=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\mathbf{1}(\varepsilon_{0}^{2}>\varepsilon^{2}n)]/\varepsilon^{2}\to 0

by dominated convergence under
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}]<\infty

(Assumption
2
(v)); and the bounded support of the multipliers
(
|V_{i}|\leq M
) converts this max-bound into the Lindeberg form for the
elements
a_{i}:=\varepsilon_{0,i}\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(X_{i})
,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{V}\bigl[\mathbf{1}\bigl(|V_{i}a_{i}|>\epsilon\sqrt{n}\bigr)\bigr]\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}\,\mathbf{1}\Bigl(a_{i}^{2}>\frac{\epsilon^{2}n}{M^{2}}\Bigr)\leq\frac{M^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}n}\,\Bigl(\max_{i\leq n}a_{i}^{2}\Bigr)\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}=o_{p}(1),

using
a_{i}^{2}\mathbf{1}(a_{i}^{2}>\epsilon^{2}n/M^{2})\leq a_{i}^{4}\,M^{2}/(\epsilon^{2}n)
,

\max_{i\leq n}a_{i}^{2}\leq\max_{i\leq n}\varepsilon_{0,i}^{2}\,\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}=o_{p}(n)

by the tail bound and the sup-norm side condition of Assumption
6
(iii)
together with the uniform score bound of Assumption
5
(i), and

n^{-1}\sum_{i}a_{i}^{2}=O_{p}(1)
by the law of large numbers under
Assumptions
2
(v) and
5
(i);
and the conditional cross-covariance satisfies

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{0,i}^{2}\,k(X_{i},\cdot)\,\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(X_{i})^{\top}\xrightarrow{p}\mathbb{E}\big[\sigma^{2}(X)\,k(X,\cdot)\,g_{\theta_{0}}(X)^{\top}\big],

so
(\xi^{*},W^{*})
share the joint covariance structure of
(\xi,W)
in
Remark
4
. The joint convergence of the pair

(\tilde{m}^{*},\hat{w}^{*})
is the conditional multiplier CLT in the product
space
\mathcal{H}_{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}
for the elements

z_{i}:=(U_{i},\varepsilon_{0,i}\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(X_{i}))
, invoked through
the Cramér–Wold device
applied to the characteristic-functional argument of
Theorem
14
. Its three ingredients are as follows.
(i) Conditional Lindeberg for the joint elements: with bounded support

|V_{i}|\leq M
and

\lVert z_{i}\rVert^{2}=\varepsilon_{0,i}^{2}\big(\|k(X_{i},\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}+\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(X_{i})^{2}\big)\leq\varepsilon_{0,i}^{2}\big(\bar{k}+(\bar{G}+\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{\infty})^{2}\big)=\varepsilon_{0,i}^{2}\,O_{p}(1)
,
the two-step chain just displayed applies verbatim to
\lVert z_{i}\rVert^{2}

in place of
\varepsilon_{0,i}^{2}
.
(ii) Replacement of
\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}
by
g_{\theta_{0}}
inside the

\varepsilon_{0}^{2}
-weighted sums: the replacement error in the
cross-moment display above is bounded by

\bar{k}^{1/2}\,\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{\infty}\,n^{-1}\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{0,i}^{2}=O_{p}(n^{-1/4})=o_{p}(1)
, and the corresponding replacement inside the
linear statistic
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i}V_{i}\varepsilon_{0,i}(\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}})(X_{i})

has conditional second moment at most

\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\,n^{-1}\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{0,i}^{2}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})=o_{p^{*}}(1)
, both by the sup-norm side condition of
Assumption
6
(iii); the unweighted corollary sums are covered by
the same replacement in the proof of
Corollaries
1
and
2
.
(iii) Diagonal blocks of the joint covariance operator:

n^{-1}\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{0,i}^{2}k(X_{i},\cdot)\otimes k(X_{i},\cdot)\xrightarrow{a.s.}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}k(X,\cdot)\otimes k(X,\cdot)]

and

n^{-1}\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{0,i}^{2}g_{\theta_{0}}(X_{i})g_{\theta_{0}}(X_{i})^{\top}\xrightarrow{a.s.}\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)^{\top}]

by Mourier’s strong law in the separable Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}_{k}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}}
(first moment

\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}(\bar{k}+\bar{G}^{2})]<\infty
by
Assumptions
2
(v) and
5
(i), (iii)), combined with the
replacement of (ii). Since

R_{n}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}}\xrightarrow{p}A:=\mathbb{E}[g_{\theta_{0}}(X)k(X,\cdot)]
,
Slutsky’s theorem gives, conditionally on the data and in probability with respect to it (the
strengthened almost-sure form would require summability rates for the empirical second moments along

n
that are not established here and are not needed below; accordingly the theorem states the
conditional limit in probability),

\sqrt{n}\,\tilde{m}_{\perp}\;\xrightarrow{d}\;\xi^{*}-A^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}W^{*}=:G_{\perp}^{*}\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{H}_{k},

where
\xrightarrow{d}
denotes conditional weak convergence in probability over the multiplier
weights, matching the notation of the theorem statement, and
G_{\perp}^{*}
has the projected law of
Corollary
1
(not the infeasible law of Theorem
2
).

Spectral expansion of the limit.

Let
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
and
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
be the eigenvalues and
eigenfunctions of the integral operator
\mathbf{L}_{k}
, with RKHS basis

e_{i}=\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,\phi_{i}
(see §
2.1
). The covariance
operator
\Sigma^{\perp}
of
G_{\perp}
satisfies

\langle\Sigma^{\perp}e_{i},e_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sqrt{\mu_{i}\mu_{j}}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[\sigma^{2}(X)\,\big(\phi_{i}(X)-\kappa_{i}(X)\big)\,\big(\phi_{j}(X)-\kappa_{j}(X)\big)\bigr]=\sqrt{\mu_{i}\mu_{j}}\,\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij},

with
\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}
as in Corollary
1
.
In general this matrix is
not diagonal
and is generically singular
(the directions in
\operatorname{span}(g_{\theta_{0}})
are annihilated by the
projection): even under homoscedasticity

\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}=\sigma^{2}(\delta_{ij}-\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}g^{\top}]\Gamma^{-1}\mathbb{E}[g\phi_{j}])
,
which is diagonal only when the nuisance directions are aligned with the
eigenbasis (e.g. the location model). Expanding
G_{\perp}
in the
orthonormal basis
\{e_{i}\}
gives

G_{\perp}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\zeta_{i}^{\perp}\,e_{i}
with

\zeta_{i}^{\perp}:=\langle G_{\perp},e_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
, where the vector

(\zeta_{1}^{\perp},\zeta_{2}^{\perp},\dots)
is
jointly
Gaussian with

\operatorname{Cov}(\zeta_{i}^{\perp},\zeta_{j}^{\perp})=\langle\Sigma^{\perp}e_{i},e_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sqrt{\mu_{i}\mu_{j}}\,\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}
.
Equivalently, defining
Z_{i}^{\perp}:=\zeta_{i}^{\perp}/\sqrt{\mu_{i}}
, we have

\zeta_{i}^{\perp}=\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,Z_{i}^{\perp}
and

G_{\perp}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,Z_{i}^{\perp}\,e_{i},

where
Z^{\perp}=(Z_{1}^{\perp},Z_{2}^{\perp},\dots)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma^{\perp})
with

\operatorname{Cov}(Z_{i}^{\perp},Z_{j}^{\perp})=\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}
.

Weak convergence of the bootstrap KRR estimator.

The bootstrap KRR estimator is
\tilde{w}_{\perp}=(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}\tilde{m}_{\perp}
. By Lemma
26
,

\|C_{n}-C\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
, hence

\|C_{n}-C\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{p}(1)
. Since
\lambda>0
,

(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}\xrightarrow{p}(C+\lambda I)^{-1}
in operator
norm. The mapping
(A,x)\mapsto Ax
is continuous from

\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{k})\times\mathcal{H}_{k}
to
\mathcal{H}_{k}
, so Slutsky’s theorem in Hilbert
spaces (the continuous mapping theorem for joint weak convergence) yields,
conditionally on
D_{n}
,

\sqrt{n}\,\tilde{w}_{\perp}=(C_{n}+\lambda I)^{-1}(\sqrt{n}\,\tilde{m}_{\perp})\xrightarrow{d}(C+\lambda I)^{-1}G_{\perp}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{i}}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\,Z_{i}^{\perp}\,e_{i}.

Convergence of the test statistics.

All test statistics are continuous functions of the scaled KRR estimator
(or the pair
(\sqrt{n}\tilde{w}_{\perp},\sqrt{n}\tilde{m}_{\perp})
).
Applying the continuous mapping theorem:

Projection-based statistics:

\displaystyle n\tilde{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\langle\sqrt{n}\tilde{w}_{\perp},\sqrt{n}\tilde{w}_{\perp}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\xrightarrow{d^{*}}\bigl\|(C+\lambda I)^{-1}G_{\perp}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}\,(Z_{i}^{\perp})^{2},

\displaystyle n\tilde{T}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\langle\sqrt{n}\tilde{w}_{\perp},\sqrt{n}\tilde{m}_{\perp}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\xrightarrow{d^{*}}\langle(C+\lambda I)^{-1}G_{\perp},G_{\perp}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\lambda}\,(Z_{i}^{\perp})^{2}.

The limit distributions coincide with those in
Corollary
1
, establishing the claimed bootstrap
consistency.

B.3
Proofs for Section
4
: Nuisance Parameter Selection

This subsection proves the results of Section
4
: the two uniform-in-
\eta
limit theorems and their consequences. The organization is by theorem family, with the auxiliary lemmas of each family collected before the proof they enter. The
operator and kernel tools
serve both theorems: the Fourier isometry and the cross-kernel calculus for the transport to the common space, the Sobolev maximal inequality within cells, the residual split of the orthogonalized residuals, and the Lipschitz properties of the transported empirical objects and of the resolvent. The proof of Theorem
5
then proceeds from the pointwise limits through the matrix representation and its gradient moments to boundedness and asymptotic equicontinuity via the equicontinuity lemma (Lemma
8
, whose proof runs on the Sobolev maximal inequality within cells and on the containment of short pairs by shifted grids, Lemma
7
), tightness in
C(\Upsilon)
, and the local alternative; the sample-path continuity of the limit fields is established separately in its own subsubsection and cited by the downstream results. The lemmas specific to the criterion process are collected in their own subsubsection before the proof of Theorem
6
: the leave-one-out prediction linearization and the criterion decomposition built on it, the replacement cost of the feasible residuals, the conditional variance formulas, the bilinear representation of the criterion’s quadratic statistics, and the chaos identification; the proof of Theorem
6
follows in parts (a)–(c), each with its own road map. The selector’s limit laws, bootstrap validity under sample splitting, the energy identity, the monotone-power proposition, and the two-channel selection theorem complete the subsection.

B.3.1
Auxiliary Lemmas for the Uniform Convergence Results

The following lemmas collect the spectral and analytic ingredients used in the proofs of the uniform convergence results (Theorems
5
and
6
): the Fourier isometry of the Gaussian RKHS (Lemma
4
), the Sobolev maximal inequality within cells (Lemma
6
), the containment of short pairs by shifted grids (Lemma
7
), the equicontinuity lemma that converts a uniform gradient moment into asymptotic equicontinuity (Lemma
8
), the residual split of Fact
2
, the order-
p
moment bound for centered quadratic forms in conditionally independent innovations (Lemma
9
), and the Lipschitz properties of the transported empirical objects and of the resolvent (Lemma
10
), with its corollary on Lipschitz-modulus equicontinuity (Corollary
3
); the cross-kernel closed form (Lemma
5
) and the derivative-feature calculus built on it (Fact
1
) are shared by the proofs of parts (a) and (b) of Lemma
10
. The conditional Lipschitz modulus and the uniform boundedness of the multiplied-then-projected embedding, the conditional counterpart of part (a) consumed by the bootstrap uniformity result (Lemma
24
), are recorded separately in Lemma
11
. Notation used throughout, ahead of the proofs in which the
objects reappear:
g_{\eta}
is the smoothing kernel of (
4
) in the main text,

P_{\eta}:=S_{\lambda}=C_{\gamma}(C_{\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}
the population smoothing operator on

L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
(the two symbols interchangeable,
P_{\eta}
making the joint dependence on

\eta=(\gamma,\lambda)
explicit),
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection onto the
nuisance-orthogonal complement (a contraction), and
\Upsilon
the compact tuning rectangle.
Three further lemmas serve the CV-criterion proof: the bilinear
representation of the criterion’s quadratic statistics (Lemma
16
), which exhibits

mU_{m}
and the order-two part of
mR_{m}
as quadratic forms of the single scaled mean embedding
against deterministic population operators; the uniform bound for the anchor-centered remainder
(Lemma
17
), which renders that remainder
O_{p}(m^{-1/2})
uniformly in
\eta
;
and the chaos identification (Lemma
18
), which transfers the chaos specification of
Theorem
6
to these quadratic forms by a Wick computation in the white-noise
coupling. Proposition
5
, finally, certifies clause (ii) of
Assumption
7
for the Gaussian family and is consumed by every result of the section
through that clause.

Throughout this subsection we use the following standard notation for the empirical objects of the testing-sample analysis (proof of Theorem
5
):
C_{n,\gamma}:=n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot)\otimes k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot)
is the empirical covariance operator on
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
with spectral decomposition
C_{n,\gamma}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nu_{i,n}(\gamma)\,\hat{e}_{i,n}^{(\gamma)}\otimes\hat{e}_{i,n}^{(\gamma)}
(eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}/n
, Nyström eigenfunctions);
M_{n}(\gamma):=n^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{\perp,j}\,k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot)\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
is the scaled mean embedding of the orthogonalized residuals; and
P_{>K}^{(\gamma)}:=\sum_{i=K+1}^{n}\hat{e}_{i,n}^{(\gamma)}\otimes\hat{e}_{i,n}^{(\gamma)}
is the empirical projector onto the tail eigenspace
\{\hat{e}_{i,n}^{(\gamma)}:i>K\}
. The transported objects
\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma}:=J_{\gamma}C_{n,\gamma}J_{\gamma}^{-1}
,
\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma):=J_{\gamma}M_{n}(\gamma)
, and
\widetilde{P}_{>K}^{(\gamma)}:=J_{\gamma}P_{>K}^{(\gamma)}J_{\gamma}^{-1}
live on the common space
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
via the Fourier isometry
J_{\gamma}
of Lemma
4
.

Proposition 5
(Eigenpair continuity for the Gaussian family)

Let
k_{\gamma}(x,y)=\exp(-\gamma\|x-y\|^{2})
,
\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
,
0<\gamma_{L}\leq\gamma_{U}<\infty
, on a probability space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P}_{X})
with
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\operatorname{supp}\mathbb{P}_{X}
infinite. Then there exist continuous parameterizations of the spectral data: maps
\gamma\mapsto\mu_{i}(\gamma)
and
\gamma\mapsto\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
,
i\geq 1
, continuous on
[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
, such that for every
\gamma
the numbers
0<\mu_{1}(\gamma)\geq\mu_{2}(\gamma)\geq\cdots
enumerate the nonzero spectrum of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
with multiplicities, each
\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)
is a unit-norm eigenfunction of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
for
\mu_{i}(\gamma)
, and
\{\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)\}_{i\geq 1}
is orthonormal in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
. In particular, clause (ii) of Assumption
7
holds for the Gaussian family.

Proof.
For
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the
\alpha
-th
\gamma
-derivative of the kernel is
\partial_{\gamma}^{\alpha}k_{\gamma}(x,y)=(-\|x-y\|^{2})^{\alpha}e^{-\gamma\|x-y\|^{2}}
, and the maximum of
r^{2\alpha}e^{-\gamma r^{2}}
over
r\geq 0
is attained at
r^{2}=\alpha/\gamma
, so

\sup_{x,y}\bigl|\partial_{\gamma}^{\alpha}k_{\gamma}(x,y)\bigr|\leq\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{e\,\gamma_{L}}\Bigr)^{\!\alpha},\qquad\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}].

Since
\mathbb{P}_{X}
is a probability measure, constants lie in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{X})
, and dominated convergence applies with these constant dominators:
\gamma\mapsto\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
is
C^{\infty}
in Hilbert–Schmidt norm, the
\alpha
-th derivative being the integral operator with kernel
\partial_{\gamma}^{\alpha}k_{\gamma}
, and

\bigl\|\partial_{\gamma}^{\alpha}\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{e\,\gamma_{L}}\Bigr)^{\!\alpha}\leq\alpha!\,\Bigl(\frac{2}{\gamma_{L}}\Bigr)^{\!\alpha},

the last inequality from the Stirling bound
\alpha!\geq(\alpha/e)^{\alpha}
. The Taylor remainder of
\gamma\mapsto\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
of order
n
about any point of the interval is therefore at most

\sup_{\gamma}\|\partial_{\gamma}^{n+1}\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\,|h|^{n+1}/(n+1)!\leq(2/\gamma_{L})^{n+1}|h|^{n+1}
,
which tends to zero whenever
|h|<\gamma_{L}/2
: the map is real-analytic with a radius uniform on
[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
, and, by the mean-value form of the derivative bound at
\alpha=1
,
\sup_{x,y}|k_{\gamma_{1}}(x,y)-k_{\gamma_{2}}(x,y)|\leq\frac{|\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}|}{e\,\gamma_{L}}
, whence

\bigl\|\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma_{1}}}-\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma_{2}}}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{HS}(L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X}))}\;\leq\;\frac{|\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}|}{e\,\gamma_{L}}\qquad\text{for all }\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}].

For the eigenpair parameterization, fix
\gamma
;
\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
is self-adjoint and positive on
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
; positivity follows from the Fourier representation

e^{-\gamma\|x-y\|^{2}}=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int e^{i\omega\cdot(x-y)}(2\gamma)^{-d/2}e^{-\|\omega\|^{2}/(4\gamma)}\,d\omega
,
which gives

\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}f,f\rangle=(2\pi)^{-d/2}(2\gamma)^{-d/2}\int\big|\int e^{i\omega\cdot x}f(x)\,d\mathbb{P}_{X}(x)\big|^{2}e^{-\|\omega\|^{2}/(4\gamma)}\,d\omega\geq 0
.
The operator is Hilbert–Schmidt, hence compact, with
\operatorname{tr}\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}=\int k_{\gamma}(x,x)\,d\mathbb{P}_{X}(x)=1<\infty
, so its nonzero spectrum consists of positive eigenvalues of finite multiplicity accumulating only at zero, and the enumeration is infinite: the operator has infinite rank, because for every support point
x_{0}\in\operatorname{supp}\mathbb{P}_{X}
the section
k_{\gamma}(\cdot,x_{0})
lies in the closure of the range, being the dominated-convergence limit of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}f_{n}
for the normalized indicators of the balls of radius
1/n
about
x_{0}
, of positive mass since
x_{0}
is a support point; if that closure had dimension at most
r
, then
r+1
distinct support points
x_{1},\dots,x_{r+1}
(which exist because
\operatorname{supp}\mathbb{P}_{X}
is infinite) would admit coefficients
c\neq 0
with
\sum_{j}c_{j}k_{\gamma}(\cdot,x_{j})=0
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
, since
r+1
vectors in a space of dimension at most
r
are linearly dependent; but this combination is continuous and vanishes
\mathbb{P}_{X}
-a.s., hence vanishes at every support point and in particular at each
x_{j}
, so
c
annihilates the Gram matrix
(k_{\gamma}(x_{j},x_{k}))_{j,k}
, which is strictly positive definite for the Gaussian kernel on distinct points, whence
c=0
, a contradiction. Analyticity makes
\gamma\mapsto\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
a self-adjoint holomorphic family of type (a) in the real parameter
\gamma
. By Rellich’s theorem for such families
[
Kato, 1980
, Ch. II, §6]
, locally around every parameter value, each isolated cluster of eigenvalues of finite total multiplicity admits finitely many real-analytic eigenvalue branches with real-analytic orthonormal eigenfunction branches spanning the cluster’s eigenspace; the reduction to that theorem is finite-dimensional: positivity and the strict positivity of the individual eigenvalues at the parameter value isolate each finite cluster from the spectrum, which accumulates only at zero, and the spectral projection onto the isolated cluster
[
Kato, 1980
, Ch. IV, §3]
is norm-continuous in
\gamma
, in fact real-analytic, and has finite rank, so that the compression of the family to its range is a finite-dimensional self-adjoint analytic family to which the Rellich theorem applies verbatim. The ordered eigenvalues of a norm-continuous self-adjoint family are continuous and the spectral projections onto isolated groups of eigenvalues are norm-continuous
[
Kato, 1980
, Ch. IV, §3]
, so the locally analytic branches order consistently and glue to continuous maps on the whole interval. For the eigenfunctions, two distinct real-analytic branches coincide identically only within a constant multiplicity cluster and otherwise cross at most finitely often on the compact interval (their difference is analytic and not identically zero), and at each crossing the adjacent charts’ frames span the same crossing eigenspace, in which they differ by a unitary mixing; finitely many such patchings produce continuous
L^{2}
-valued orthonormal eigenfunction selections. This proves the proposition.

Lemma 4
(Fourier isomorphism for the Gaussian kernel)

Fix
\gamma_{0}\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
. With the Fourier convention
\hat{f}(\omega)=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)e^{-i\omega\cdot x}dx
, the Gaussian kernel satisfies
[
Wendland, 2005
, Theorem 10.12]

\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}=c_{\gamma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\hat{f}(\omega)|^{2}e^{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma)}\,d\omega,\qquad c_{\gamma}:=(2\pi)^{-d/2}(2\gamma)^{d/2}.

The map
J_{\gamma}:\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}\to\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
defined through

\widehat{J_{\gamma}f}(\omega):=\sqrt{c_{\gamma}/c_{\gamma_{0}}}\;\hat{f}(\omega)\exp\Bigl(\frac{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}}{8}\Bigl(\frac{1}{\gamma}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{0}}\Bigr)\Bigr)

is an isometric isomorphism:
\lVert J_{\gamma}f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}=\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}
. Equivalently,
J_{\gamma}
is the Fourier multiplier

J_{\gamma}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}w_{\gamma}\mathcal{F},\qquad\text{i.e.}\quad\widehat{J_{\gamma}f}(\omega)=w_{\gamma}(\omega)\hat{f}(\omega),

where
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
denote the Fourier transform on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(in the convention displayed above) and its inverse, and

w_{\gamma}(\omega):=\Bigl(\frac{s_{\gamma_{0}}(\omega)}{s_{\gamma}(\omega)}\Bigr)^{1/2},\qquad s_{\gamma}(\omega):=\hat{k}_{\gamma}(\omega)=(2\gamma)^{-d/2}e^{-\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma)}

is the square root of the ratio of the
spectral densities

s_{\gamma}
of the kernels
k_{\gamma}
; by the shift rule, the Fourier transform of
k_{\gamma}(x,\cdot)
at frequency
\omega
is
e^{-i\omega\cdot x}s_{\gamma}(\omega)
. Consequently, the transported kernel
\tilde{k}_{\gamma}(x,y):=\langle J_{\gamma}k_{\gamma}(\cdot,x),J_{\gamma}k_{\gamma}(\cdot,y)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}
coincides with
k_{\gamma}(x,y)
.

Proof.
The proof proceeds in four steps: the norm formula, the isometry, the multiplier form, and the transported kernel.

The norm formula.

The kernel
k_{\gamma}
is translation invariant, and its Fourier transform is the Gaussian

\hat{k}_{\gamma}(\omega)=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\gamma\lVert x\rVert^{2}}e^{-i\omega\cdot x}\,dx=(2\gamma)^{-d/2}e^{-\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma)},

using
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-a\lVert x\rVert^{2}}e^{-i\omega\cdot x}dx=(\pi/a)^{d/2}e^{-\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4a)}
at
a=\gamma
. Wendland (2004, Theorem 10.12) gives the native-space formula
\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int|\hat{f}(\omega)|^{2}/\hat{k}_{\gamma}(\omega)\,d\omega
; since
1/\hat{k}_{\gamma}(\omega)=(2\gamma)^{d/2}e^{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma)}
, this is the displayed formula
\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}=c_{\gamma}\int|\hat{f}(\omega)|^{2}e^{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma)}d\omega
with
c_{\gamma}=(2\pi)^{-d/2}(2\gamma)^{d/2}
.

Isometry.

Apply the norm formula at
\gamma_{0}
to
J_{\gamma}f
and substitute the definition of
J_{\gamma}
:

\displaystyle\lVert J_{\gamma}f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=c_{\gamma_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{c_{\gamma}}{c_{\gamma_{0}}}|\hat{f}(\omega)|^{2}\exp\Bigl(\frac{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}}{4\gamma_{0}}+\frac{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}}{4}\Bigl(\frac{1}{\gamma}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{0}}\Bigr)\Bigr)\,d\omega

\displaystyle=c_{\gamma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\hat{f}(\omega)|^{2}e^{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma)}\,d\omega=\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}.

Hence
J_{\gamma}
is an isometry. The swapped map is the inverse. Define
J_{\gamma_{0}}
by the identical formula with
\gamma
and
\gamma_{0}
interchanged; it maps
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
to
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
by the same computation as above with the two parameters swapped, and its multiplier is

w_{\gamma_{0}}(\omega)=\Bigl(\frac{s_{\gamma}(\omega)}{s_{\gamma_{0}}(\omega)}\Bigr)^{1/2}=\frac{1}{w_{\gamma}(\omega)}:

the square root of the reciprocal ratio is the reciprocal of the square root, because both densities are strictly positive. Multipliers compose pointwise, so for every
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{J_{\gamma_{0}}J_{\gamma}f}(\omega)

\displaystyle=w_{\gamma_{0}}(\omega)\,w_{\gamma}(\omega)\,\hat{f}(\omega)=\hat{f}(\omega),\qquad\text{and symmetrically}

\displaystyle\widehat{J_{\gamma}J_{\gamma_{0}}g}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\hat{g}(\omega),\quad g\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}.

The Fourier transform being injective on
L^{2}
, both compositions are the identity on their respective spaces:
J_{\gamma_{0}}=J_{\gamma}^{-1}
, so
J_{\gamma}
is onto, and a surjective isometry is an isometric isomorphism
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}\to\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
.

The multiplier form.

The operator identity
J_{\gamma}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}w_{\gamma}\mathcal{F}
asserts that
J_{\gamma}
acts as the three-step pipeline

transform
f
, multiply pointwise by the scalar function
w_{\gamma}(\omega)
, transform back
.
The verification is immediate from the definition of
J_{\gamma}
