through its transform: substituting the explicit form of the ratio of the spectral densities computed above,

\frac{s_{\gamma_{0}}}{s_{\gamma}}=(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma_{0}})^{d/2}e^{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}(1/\gamma-1/\gamma_{0})/4}
,
together with
\sqrt{c_{\gamma}/c_{\gamma_{0}}}=(\gamma/\gamma_{0})^{d/4}
, turns the defining display into

(J_{\gamma}f)\widehat{\ }(\omega)=\Bigl(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma_{0}}\Bigr)^{d/4}e^{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}(1/\gamma-1/\gamma_{0})/8}\,\hat{f}(\omega)=w_{\gamma}(\omega)\,\hat{f}(\omega).

The right-hand side is also the Fourier transform of
\mathcal{F}^{-1}[w_{\gamma}\cdot\mathcal{F}f]
; since the Fourier transform is injective on
L^{2}
, it follows that

J_{\gamma}f=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[w_{\gamma}\,\mathcal{F}f]
for every
f
, i.e.,
J_{\gamma}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}w_{\gamma}\mathcal{F}
. By the shift rule, the transported feature function has transform

(J_{\gamma}k_{\gamma}(x,\cdot))\widehat{\ }(\omega)=w_{\gamma}(\omega)s_{\gamma}(\omega)e^{-i\omega\cdot x}=e^{-i\omega\cdot x}\sqrt{s_{\gamma_{0}}(\omega)s_{\gamma}(\omega)}
,
the geometric mean of the two spectral densities.

The transported kernel.

By the shift rule and the multiplier form, the Fourier transform of
J_{\gamma}k_{\gamma}(\cdot,x)
is
w_{\gamma}(\omega)s_{\gamma}(\omega)e^{-i\omega\cdot x}
, and
\overline{\widehat{J_{\gamma}k_{\gamma}(\cdot,y)}}(\omega)=w_{\gamma}(\omega)s_{\gamma}(\omega)e^{i\omega\cdot y}
(real-valued functions). Using the polarization of the norm formula at
\gamma_{0}
and the isometry condition
w_{\gamma}^{2}s_{\gamma}=s_{\gamma_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{k}_{\gamma}(x,y)

\displaystyle=c_{\gamma_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}w_{\gamma}(\omega)^{2}s_{\gamma}(\omega)^{2}\,e^{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma_{0})}\,e^{i\omega\cdot(x-y)}\,d\omega

\displaystyle=c_{\gamma_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}s_{\gamma_{0}}(\omega)s_{\gamma}(\omega)\,e^{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma_{0})}\,e^{i\omega\cdot(x-y)}\,d\omega

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}s_{\gamma}(\omega)\,e^{i\omega\cdot(x-y)}\,d\omega=k_{\gamma}(x,y),

where the third equality uses
c_{\gamma_{0}}s_{\gamma_{0}}(\omega)e^{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma_{0})}=(2\pi)^{-d/2}
(the constants of
c_{\gamma_{0}}
and
s_{\gamma_{0}}
cancel exactly), and the last equality is the inversion formula
k_{\gamma}(x,y)=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}s_{\gamma}(\omega)e^{i\omega\cdot(x-y)}d\omega
, i.e. the Fourier transform of the Gaussian
s_{\gamma}
evaluated at
x-y
.

Lemma 5
(Cross-kernel of two transported features)

For
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define the cross-kernel

K_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(x,y):=\langle J_{\gamma_{1}}k_{\gamma_{1}}(\cdot,x),\,J_{\gamma_{2}}k_{\gamma_{2}}(\cdot,y)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}
,
where
J_{\gamma}:\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}\to\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
is the transport of Lemma
4
. Then

K_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(x,y)=c_{12}\,k_{\gamma_{12}}(x,y)=\frac{2^{d/2}(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})^{d/4}}{(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2})^{d/2}}\,\exp\Bigl(-\frac{2\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}}\,\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}\Bigr),

(17)

a Gaussian kernel at the harmonic-mean bandwidth
\gamma_{12}:=2\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}/(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2})\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
, scaled by the constant

c_{12}:=2^{d/2}(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})^{d/4}/(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2})^{d/2}
,
which satisfies
c_{12}\in[c_{\min},1]
with
c_{\min}:=2^{d/2}(\gamma_{L}\gamma_{U})^{d/4}/(\gamma_{L}+\gamma_{U})^{d/2}>0
, equals
1
if and only if
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
, and depends on neither
x
nor
y
nor the reference bandwidth
\gamma_{0}
.

Proof.
Write
\tilde{u}(x;\gamma):=J_{\gamma}k_{\gamma}(\cdot,x)
. By the shift rule and the multiplier form of the transport,
J_{\gamma}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}w_{\gamma}\mathcal{F}
with
w_{\gamma}=(s_{\gamma_{0}}/s_{\gamma})^{1/2}
and
s_{\gamma}(\omega)=(2\gamma)^{-d/2}e^{-\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma)}
the spectral density of
k_{\gamma}
in the unitary convention,

\widehat{\tilde{u}(x;\gamma)}(\omega)=w_{\gamma}(\omega)s_{\gamma}(\omega)e^{-i\omega\cdot x}=\sqrt{s_{\gamma_{0}}(\omega)s_{\gamma}(\omega)}\,e^{-i\omega\cdot x}.

The norm formula of Lemma
4
,
c_{\gamma_{0}}e^{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma_{0})}=(2\pi)^{-d/2}/s_{\gamma_{0}}(\omega)
, polarizes to

\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int\hat{f}(\omega)\overline{\hat{g}(\omega)}/s_{\gamma_{0}}(\omega)\,d\omega
.
Substituting the two transformed features and using the cancellation

w_{\gamma_{1}}(\omega)w_{\gamma_{2}}(\omega)s_{\gamma_{1}}(\omega)s_{\gamma_{2}}(\omega)/s_{\gamma_{0}}(\omega)=\sqrt{s_{\gamma_{1}}(\omega)s_{\gamma_{2}}(\omega)}
,
which records that the reference density entering through the two multipliers cancels the reference
density of the inner-product weight exactly; the result is

K_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(x,y)=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sqrt{s_{\gamma_{1}}(\omega)s_{\gamma_{2}}(\omega)}\;e^{-i\omega\cdot(x-y)}\,d\omega,

(18)

already free of
\gamma_{0}
. For the Gaussian family the geometric mean of two spectral densities is again a spectral density, up to scaling:

\sqrt{s_{\gamma_{1}}(\omega)s_{\gamma_{2}}(\omega)}=(4\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})^{-d/4}e^{-\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}(1/\gamma_{1}+1/\gamma_{2})/8}=(4\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})^{-d/4}e^{-\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma_{12})}=c_{12}\,s_{\gamma_{12}}(\omega)
,
where
(1/\gamma_{1}+1/\gamma_{2})/8=(1/\gamma_{12})/4
by
1/\gamma_{12}=(1/\gamma_{1}+1/\gamma_{2})/2
, and the prefactor checks as
c_{12}(2\gamma_{12})^{-d/2}=(4\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})^{-d/4}
. Inverting the transform by
(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int s_{\gamma_{12}}(\omega)e^{-i\omega\cdot t}d\omega=k_{\gamma_{12}}(t)
yields (
17
). Finally,
c_{12}\leq 1
by AM–GM with equality iff
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
, and
c_{12}\geq c_{\min}>0
by the explicit form
c_{12}=(1-x^{2})^{d/4}
, read off the closed form (
17
), with
x:=(\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1})/(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2})
: on the square the smaller coordinate is at least
\gamma_{L}
and the coordinate gap at most
\gamma_{U}-\gamma_{L}
, so

|x|=\frac{|\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}|}{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}}\leq\frac{|\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}|}{2\gamma_{L}+|\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}|}\leq\frac{\gamma_{U}-\gamma_{L}}{\gamma_{L}+\gamma_{U}}
,
the last fraction being increasing in the gap and attained at the corners
(\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U})
and
(\gamma_{U},\gamma_{L})
; therefore
1-x^{2}\geq 1-\bigl(\frac{\gamma_{U}-\gamma_{L}}{\gamma_{U}+\gamma_{L}}\bigr)^{2}=\frac{4\gamma_{L}\gamma_{U}}{(\gamma_{L}+\gamma_{U})^{2}}
and

c_{12}=(1-x^{2})^{d/4}\geq\Bigl(\frac{4\gamma_{L}\gamma_{U}}{(\gamma_{L}+\gamma_{U})^{2}}\Bigr)^{d/4}=c_{\min}
;
and
\gamma_{12}\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
because a harmonic mean of points of an interval remains in the interval.

The following maximal inequality is used in the proofs of Theorems
5
and
6
: it converts a uniform pointwise gradient moment into a within-cell oscillation bound, with no discretization, no grids, and no two-point increment bounds.

Lemma 6
(Sobolev maximal inequality, within-cell form)

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in Q}
be a real-valued random field on an axis-aligned cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
of side length
\delta>0
, with
C^{1}
paths and Euclidean norm
\|\cdot\|
, and let
K
be a subset of
Q
. Suppose
p>d
and

\sup_{t\in Q}\mathbb{E}\|\nabla X_{t}\|^{p}\leq N_{p}<\infty.

(19)

Then for every
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sup_{t,s\in K}|X_{t}-X_{s}|^{p}\Bigr]\leq C_{d,p}\,N_{p}\,\delta^{p},\qquad\text{hence}\qquad\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{t,s\in K}|X_{t}-X_{s}|\geq\varepsilon\Bigr)\leq C_{d,p}\,N_{p}\,\delta^{p}\,\varepsilon^{-p},

(20)

where
C_{d,p}
is a universal constant (Morrey’s constant), independent of the field, the law, and the set
K
.

Proof.
Fix
\omega
and put
f:=X(\omega)
. Morrey’s inequality (
W^{1,p}(Q)\hookrightarrow C^{0,1-d/p}(Q)
for
p>d
; a cube of side
\delta
) gives

\sup_{t,s\in Q}|f(t)-f(s)|\leq C_{d,p}\,\delta^{1-d/p}\,\Bigl(\int_{Q}\|\nabla f\|^{p}\Bigr)^{1/p},

and the supremum over the subset
K
is bounded by that over
Q
; the constant depends only on
(d,p)
(side-independent by scaling: for
g(y)=f(\delta y)
on the unit cube,
\lVert\nabla g\rVert_{p}=\delta^{1-d/p}\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{L^{p}(Q)}
and the suprema coincide). Raise to the power
p
and take expectations: each
X_{t}
is a random variable and
\nabla X_{t}
is a pointwise limit of measurable difference quotients, so
(t,\omega)\mapsto\lVert\nabla X_{t}(\omega)\rVert^{p}
is jointly measurable (continuous
t
-sections, measurable
\omega
-sections), as is
\sup_{t,s\in K}\lvert X_{t}-X_{s}\rvert^{p}
(a countable dense subset of
K
suffices, the paths being continuous), and Tonelli and the uniform bound (
19
) give

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sup_{t,s\in K}|X_{t}-X_{s}|^{p}\Bigr]\leq C_{d,p}^{p}\,\delta^{p-d}\int_{Q}\mathbb{E}\|\nabla X_{t}\|^{p}\,dt\leq C_{d,p}^{p}\,\delta^{p-d}\cdot\delta^{d}\cdot N_{p}=C_{d,p}^{p}\,N_{p}\,\delta^{p},

since
\operatorname{vol}(Q)=\delta^{d}
; Markov’s inequality yields the probability form, and the weak event ”
\geq\varepsilon
” follows from the strict one by the monotone limit
\varepsilon^{\prime}\uparrow\varepsilon
.

The following containment lemma supplies the geometry by which a within-cell oscillation bound, such as (
20
), becomes a bound on the supremum over all pairs of tuning parameters at distance at most
\delta
; its point is that the discretization is chosen after the pair, so the increment over a short pair is bounded by a single cell oscillation with no multiplicity factor.

Lemma 7
(Shifted grids contain short pairs)

Let
L_{1},L_{2}
be the side lengths of
\Upsilon
, let
0<\delta\leq\min(L_{1},L_{2})/2
, put
h:=2\delta
, and for
\boldsymbol{\tau}=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\in\{0,h/2\}^{2}
let
\mathcal{G}^{(\boldsymbol{\tau})}_{\delta}
consist of the sets

\Upsilon\cap\bigl\{\bigl[\gamma_{L}+\tau_{1}+kh,\ \gamma_{L}+\tau_{1}+(k+1)h\bigr]\times\bigl[\lambda_{L}+\tau_{2}+lh,\ \lambda_{L}+\tau_{2}+(l+1)h\bigr]\bigr\},\qquad k,l\in\mathbb{Z},

and let
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}:=\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathcal{G}^{(\boldsymbol{\tau})}_{\delta}
. Then

(i)

every
K\in\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
is contained in
\Upsilon
and is contained in a square of side
h
that is contained in
\Upsilon
, and
\lvert\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\rvert\leq 4\,(\lfloor L_{1}/h\rfloor+2)\,(\lfloor L_{2}/h\rfloor+2)\leq C_{\Upsilon}\,\delta^{-2}
;

(ii)

if
\lVert\eta-\eta^{\prime}\rVert\leq\delta
, then
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
lie in a single element of
\mathcal{G}^{(\boldsymbol{\tau})}_{\delta}
for some
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\{0,h/2\}^{2}
; consequently

\sup_{\lVert\eta-\eta^{\prime}\rVert\leq\delta}\bigl\lvert S(\eta)-S(\eta^{\prime})\bigr\rvert\leq\max_{K\in\mathcal{G}_{\delta}}\ \sup_{t,s\in K}\bigl\lvert S(t)-S(s)\bigr\rvert

for every real-valued field
S
on
\Upsilon
.

Proof.
For one coordinate, the two families of lines
\{\gamma_{L}+kh\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{\gamma_{L}+h/2+kh\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
are the even and the odd points of the lattice
\gamma_{L}+(h/2)\mathbb{Z}
, so a point of the first family and a point of the second are at distance at least
h/2
. Let
I_{1}
be the interval spanned by the two
\gamma
-coordinates; it has length at most
\lVert\eta-\eta^{\prime}\rVert\leq\delta=h/2
. If both families had a line strictly inside
I_{1}
, those two lines would be at distance at least
h/2
while both lie strictly inside an interval of length at most
h/2
, which is impossible. Hence at least one
\tau_{1}\in\{0,h/2\}
has no line of its family strictly inside
I_{1}
, and the same argument gives
\tau_{2}\in\{0,h/2\}
for the
\lambda
-coordinate. With
\boldsymbol{\tau}=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
neither coordinate interval contains a line of the corresponding family, so both points lie in one element of
\mathcal{G}^{(\boldsymbol{\tau})}_{\delta}
, which is (ii). For (i): cells are contained in
\Upsilon
by construction; a cell truncated at the boundary of
\Upsilon
is contained in the square of side
h
obtained by extending the cell away from that boundary, since a closed interval of length
w\leq h
inside an interval of length
L\geq h
lies in the subinterval
\bigl[\min(\alpha,L-h),\,\min(\alpha,L-h)+h\bigr]
determined by its own left endpoint
\alpha
, clamped at
L-h
, and the two-coordinate version of the clamp applies coordinatewise to corner cells; that square lies in
\Upsilon
because
h\leq\min(L_{1},L_{2})
; the cells are the product cells of the two coordinate grids, each coordinate contributing at most
\lfloor L/h\rfloor+2
cells per family (the two boundary cells being degenerate), whence
\lvert\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\rvert\leq 4(\lfloor L_{1}/h\rfloor+2)(\lfloor L_{2}/h\rfloor+2)\leq C_{\Upsilon}\,\delta^{-2}
. Here and below
C_{\Upsilon}
denotes a constant depending only on
\Upsilon
.

Lemma 8
(Equicontinuity and tightness from a uniform gradient moment)

Let
(S_{m})
be real-valued fields on
\Upsilon
and suppose that for every
m
there is a decomposition
S_{m}=S^{c}_{m}+a_{m}+e_{m}
in which
S^{c}_{m}
has
C^{1}
paths and such that

(i)

there exist
p\in(2,4]
and
N_{p}<\infty
such that
\sup_{m}\sup_{\eta\in\Upsilon}\mathbb{E}\lVert\nabla_{\eta}S^{c}_{m}(\eta)\rVert^{p}\leq N_{p}
;

(ii)

there are a deterministic
\Lambda<\infty
and random variables
r_{m}=o_{p}(1)
such that
\lvert a_{m}(\eta)-a_{m}(\eta^{\prime})\rvert\leq\Lambda\lVert\eta-\eta^{\prime}\rVert+2r_{m}
for all
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\Upsilon
;

(iii)

\sup_{\eta\in\Upsilon}\lvert e_{m}(\eta)\rvert=o_{p}(1)
.

Then for every
\varepsilon>0
,

\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\ \limsup_{m\to\infty}\ \mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{\lVert\eta-\eta^{\prime}\rVert\leq\delta}\bigl\lvert S_{m}(\eta)-S_{m}(\eta^{\prime})\bigr\rvert>\varepsilon\Bigr)=0.

If in addition
S_{m}(\eta_{0})=O_{p}(1)
for some fixed
\eta_{0}\in\Upsilon
, then
\sup_{\eta\in\Upsilon}\lvert S_{m}(\eta)\rvert=O_{p}(1)
, and consequently
(S_{m})
is asymptotically tight in
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
, so that finite-dimensional convergence of
(S_{m})
implies weak convergence in
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
.

Proof.
Fix
0<\delta\leq\min(L_{1},L_{2})/2
, put
h:=2\delta
, and let
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
be as in Lemma
7
. For
\lVert\eta-\eta^{\prime}\rVert\leq\delta
, Lemma
7
(ii) applied to
S^{c}_{m}
and the triangle inequality give

\bigl\lvert S_{m}(\eta)-S_{m}(\eta^{\prime})\bigr\rvert\leq\max_{K\in\mathcal{G}_{\delta}}\ \sup_{t,s\in K}\bigl\lvert S^{c}_{m}(t)-S^{c}_{m}(s)\bigr\rvert+\Lambda\,\delta+2r_{m}+2\sup_{\eta}\lvert e_{m}(\eta)\rvert.

By Lemma
7
(i) each
K
is contained in a square of side
h
contained in
\Upsilon
, on which Lemma
6
applies at the exponent
p\in(2,4]
,
p>d=\dim\Upsilon=2
; hypothesis (i) therefore gives

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sup_{t,s\in K}\bigl\lvert S^{c}_{m}(t)-S^{c}_{m}(s)\bigr\rvert^{p}\Bigr]\leq C_{2,p}\,N_{p}\,h^{p}=2^{p}\,C_{2,p}\,N_{p}\,\delta^{p}

for every
K
. Markov’s inequality at level
\varepsilon/2
and the union bound over the at most
C_{\Upsilon}\delta^{-2}
cells of
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
yield

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{K\in\mathcal{G}_{\delta}}\sup_{t,s\in K}\bigl\lvert S^{c}_{m}(t)-S^{c}_{m}(s)\bigr\rvert>\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\Bigr)\leq\lvert\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\rvert\,\frac{2^{p}C_{2,p}N_{p}\delta^{p}}{(\varepsilon/2)^{p}}\leq\frac{2^{2p}\,C_{\Upsilon}C_{2,p}N_{p}\,\delta^{p-2}}{\varepsilon^{p}},

which is independent of
m
and vanishes as
\delta\downarrow 0
, the exponent
p-2>0
being exactly where
p>2
enters; and for each fixed
\delta
with
\Lambda\delta<\varepsilon/2
,

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\Lambda\delta+2r_{m}+2\sup_{\eta}\lvert e_{m}(\eta)\rvert>\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\Bigr)\longrightarrow 0\qquad(m\to\infty)

by (ii) and (iii), the deterministic
\Lambda\delta
being absorbed in the level. Hence the first display.

For the boundedness claim, chain along a fixed net: let
\delta_{0}:=\min(L_{1},L_{2})/2
and let
\eta=\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{J}=\eta^{\prime}
be points of
\Upsilon
with
\lVert\zeta_{j}-\zeta_{j-1}\rVert\leq\delta_{0}
and
J\leq\lceil\operatorname{diam}\Upsilon/\delta_{0}\rceil
, a deterministic constant. Then

\sup_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\Upsilon}\bigl\lvert S_{m}(\eta)-S_{m}(\eta^{\prime})\bigr\rvert\leq J\max_{\lVert t-s\rVert\leq\delta_{0}}\bigl\lvert S_{m}(t)-S_{m}(s)\bigr\rvert,

and the display above at the fixed scale
\delta_{0}
bounds the right-hand side in probability: it gives

\max_{\lVert t-s\rVert\leq\delta_{0}}\bigl\lvert S_{m}(t)-S_{m}(s)\bigr\rvert\leq\max_{K\in\mathcal{G}_{\delta_{0}}}\ \sup_{t,s\in K}\bigl\lvert S^{c}_{m}(t)-S^{c}_{m}(s)\bigr\rvert+\Lambda\,\delta_{0}+2r_{m}+2\sup_{\eta}\lvert e_{m}(\eta)\rvert,

whose first term is
O_{p}(N_{p}^{1/p}h)=O_{p}(N_{p}^{1/p}\delta_{0})
, the cells of
\mathcal{G}_{\delta_{0}}
being finitely many and the bound
2^{p}C_{2,p}N_{p}\delta_{0}^{p}
of the display above holding uniformly over them, and whose remaining terms are deterministic or
o_{p}(1)
, so the right-hand side is
O_{p}(1)
and hence
\sup_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\lvert S_{m}(\eta)-S_{m}(\eta^{\prime})\rvert=O_{p}(1)
. Therefore
\sup_{\eta}\lvert S_{m}(\eta)\rvert\leq\lvert S_{m}(\eta_{0})\rvert+\sup_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\lvert S_{m}(\eta)-S_{m}(\eta^{\prime})\rvert=O_{p}(1)
by the pointwise hypothesis. Asymptotic tightness in
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
follows from asymptotic equicontinuity together with boundedness and the total boundedness of
\Upsilon
(the sufficiency direction of Theorem 1.5.7 there), and Theorem 1.5.4 of
van der Vaart and Wellner [1996]
then converts finite-dimensional convergence and asymptotic tightness into weak convergence in
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
.

The following fact records the derivative-feature calculus of the Gaussian transport: the feature difference of a bandwidth pair, normalized by the bandwidth gap, is controlled, uniformly over all coefficient vectors, by the supremum of a one-dimensional derivative-feature process, so that the two-dimensional pair square is reduced to the bandwidth interval.

Fact 1
(Derivative features of the Gaussian transport)

Let
\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x):=J_{\gamma}k_{\gamma}(x,\cdot)\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
,
\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
. Then the following hold.

(i)
The map
\gamma\mapsto\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)
is continuously differentiable on
[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with deterministic constants
L_{d},L_{dd}
, depending only on
(\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U},d)
, such that

\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}\leq L_{d}
and

\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}}(x)-\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{2}}(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}\leq L_{dd}\,|\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}|

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and all
\gamma,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
.

(ii)
For every
n
, every weights
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
, and every
\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
with
T:=\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}
,

n^{-1}\sum_{j,k}a_{j}a_{k}\,\bar{W}_{jk}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\;\leq\;\sup_{\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]}D_{\boldsymbol{a}}(\gamma),\qquad D_{\boldsymbol{a}}(\gamma):=\Bigl\lVert\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\,\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(X_{j})\Bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2},

where
\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}=T^{-2}\Delta\boldsymbol{G}
is the normalized feature-difference Gram of Lemma
10
(a).

(iii)
Let
\widetilde{C}_{\gamma}:=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(X)\otimes\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(X)\bigr]
on
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
, and let
\bar{k}:=\sup_{\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}],\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}k_{\gamma}(x,x)
. Then
\gamma\mapsto\widetilde{C}_{\gamma}
is continuously differentiable in Hilbert–Schmidt norm with
\bigl\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\widetilde{C}_{\gamma}\bigr\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq 2\,\bar{k}^{1/2}L_{d}
, and consequently
\bigl\lVert\widetilde{C}_{\gamma_{1}}-\widetilde{C}_{\gamma_{2}}\bigr\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq 2\,\bar{k}^{1/2}L_{d}\,\lvert\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}\rvert
for all
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
.

Proof.

(i)
Under the identification of Lemma
4
,
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
is, through the Fourier transform, the weighted space
L^{2}\bigl(s_{\gamma_{0}}^{-1}(\omega)(2\pi)^{-d/2}\,d\omega\bigr)
, and by the computation of Lemma
5
the transported feature has Fourier transform

\widehat{\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)}(\omega)=\sqrt{s_{\gamma_{0}}(\omega)s_{\gamma}(\omega)}\;e^{-i\omega\cdot x}=:\sigma_{\gamma}(\omega)\,e^{-i\omega\cdot x},

with the inner-product formula
\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int\hat{f}\,\overline{\hat{g}}\,/s_{\gamma_{0}}
recorded in the proof of that lemma. Writing
m_{\gamma}(\omega):=\partial_{\gamma}\log s_{\gamma}(\omega)
, so that

m_{\gamma}(\omega)=\frac{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}}{4\gamma^{2}}-\frac{d}{2\gamma},\qquad\partial_{\gamma}\sigma_{\gamma}(\omega)=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\sigma_{\gamma}(\omega)\,m_{\gamma}(\omega),

by the product and chain rules applied to
s_{\gamma}(\omega)=(2\gamma)^{-d/2}e^{-\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}/(4\gamma)}
, the pointwise derivative of the transformed feature is
\partial_{\gamma}\bigl(\sigma_{\gamma}e^{-i\omega\cdot x}\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{\gamma}m_{\gamma}e^{-i\omega\cdot x}
. Since
s_{\gamma}=(2\pi)^{d/2}
times the
N(0,2\gamma I_{d})
density,
(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int s_{\gamma}(\omega)\phi(\omega)\,d\omega=\mathbb{E}\phi(\Omega_{\gamma})
for
\Omega_{\gamma}\sim N(0,2\gamma I_{d})
, whose second and fourth moments are
\mathbb{E}\lVert\Omega_{\gamma}\rVert^{2}=2\gamma d
and
\mathbb{E}\lVert\Omega_{\gamma}\rVert^{4}=(2\gamma)^{2}d(d+2)
; the norm formula therefore gives, with
x
entering only through the unimodular phase,

\bigl\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int s_{\gamma}\,\frac{m_{\gamma}^{2}}{4}\,d\omega=\tfrac{1}{4}\,\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\frac{\lVert\Omega_{\gamma}\rVert^{2}}{4\gamma^{2}}-\frac{d}{2\gamma}\Bigr)^{2}=\frac{d}{8\gamma^{2}},

the last equality by expanding the square and inserting the two moments. The same computation applies at every
\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
, so
L_{d}:=\sqrt{d}\,/\bigl(2\sqrt{2}\,\gamma_{L}\bigr)
bounds the derivative uniformly. For the derivative of the derivative, one further differentiation gives

\partial_{\gamma}^{2}\sigma_{\gamma}(\omega)=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\sigma_{\gamma}(\omega)\Bigl(\dot{m}_{\gamma}(\omega)+\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\gamma}(\omega)^{2}\Bigr),\qquad\dot{m}_{\gamma}(\omega):=\partial_{\gamma}m_{\gamma}(\omega)=-\frac{\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}}{2\gamma^{3}}+\frac{d}{2\gamma^{2}},

and hence, by the inequality
(u+v)^{2}\leq 2u^{2}+2v^{2}
and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for the cross term,

\bigl\lVert\partial_{\gamma}^{2}\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\dot{m}_{\gamma}+\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\gamma}^{2}\Bigr)^{2}\leq\tfrac{1}{4}\Bigl(2\,\mathbb{E}\dot{m}_{\gamma}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}m_{\gamma}^{4}\Bigr)=:L_{dd}^{2},

where the rightmost display is a polynomial in
(d,\gamma^{-1})
,
\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
, built from the Gaussian moments of
\lVert\Omega_{\gamma}\rVert^{2}
up to order four, all finite, so its supremum over the compact interval is finite and depends only on
(\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U},d)
; in particular
L_{dd}
can be taken from this bound. Continuous differentiability of
\gamma\mapsto\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)
into
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
, and of its derivative, follows from the pointwise computations above by dominated convergence in the weighted space, the dominating functions being of the form (polynomial in
\lVert\omega\rVert
)
\times
Gaussian, whose weighted second moments are finite by the same Gaussian-moment display; the mean value theorem, in its integral form for the
C^{1}
map
\gamma\mapsto\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)
, then gives the announced Lipschitz bound

\bigl\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}}(x)-\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{2}}(x)\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}=\Bigl\lVert\int_{\gamma_{1}}^{\gamma_{2}}\partial_{\gamma}^{2}\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)\,d\gamma\Bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}\leq L_{dd}\,\lvert\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}\rvert.

(ii)
Both sides are invariant under the swap
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\mapsto(\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1})
, the left because
\Delta\boldsymbol{G}
is symmetric in the pair and the right because it is pair-free, so assume
T>0
. By the isometry for the two diagonal factors and the cross-kernel closed form (
17
) with its symmetry for the mixed factors,
\Delta G_{jk}=\bigl\langle(\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{2}}-\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}})(X_{j}),(\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{2}}-\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}})(X_{k})\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}
, so that

n^{-1}\sum_{j,k}a_{j}a_{k}\,\bar{W}_{jk}=\Bigl\lVert\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\,\frac{\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{2}}(X_{j})-\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}}(X_{j})}{T}\Bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}.

By the fundamental theorem of calculus for the
C^{1}
Hilbert-valued path of part (i), applied at each
X_{j}
,

\frac{\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{2}}(x)-\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}}(x)}{T}=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}+tT}(x)\,dt,

and the finite sum commutes with the Bochner integral, so

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\,\frac{\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{2}}(X_{j})-\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}}(X_{j})}{T}=\int_{0}^{1}S_{t}\,dt,\qquad S_{t}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\,\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}+tT}(X_{j}).

Jensen’s inequality for the probability measure
dt
on
[0,1]
and the convexity of the squared norm give
\bigl\lVert\int_{0}^{1}S_{t}\,dt\bigr\rVert^{2}\leq\int_{0}^{1}\lVert S_{t}\rVert^{2}dt\leq\sup_{t\in[0,1]}D_{\boldsymbol{a}}(\gamma_{1}+tT)
, and the segment
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]
lies in
[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
, so the last supremum is at most
\sup_{\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]}D_{\boldsymbol{a}}(\gamma)
. Combining the displays proves (ii).

(iii)
By part (i), the integrand of the Bochner expectation is continuously differentiable into the Hilbert–Schmidt class with, by the product rule for rank-one tensors and
\lVert a\otimes b\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}=\lVert a\rVert\,\lVert b\rVert
,

\displaystyle\partial_{\gamma}\bigl(\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)\otimes\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x))\otimes\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)+\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)\otimes(\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)),

\displaystyle\Bigl\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\bigl(\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)\otimes\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)\bigr)\Bigr\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\,L_{d}\,\bar{k}^{1/2},

using part (i) for the first factor and
\lVert\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}=\lVert J_{\gamma}k_{\gamma}(x,\cdot)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}=k_{\gamma}(x,x)\leq\bar{k}
for the second. The right-hand side is deterministic and
\gamma
-free, so it dominates the difference quotients of the integrand, and dominated convergence for the Bochner integral differentiates under the expectation; Jensen’s inequality, the norm being convex, then gives
\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\widetilde{C}_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\bigl(\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(X)\otimes\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(X)\bigr)\bigr\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq 2\,\bar{k}^{1/2}L_{d}
, and continuous differentiability of
\gamma\mapsto\widetilde{C}_{\gamma}
follows from the same domination applied to the derivative. The mean value theorem in integral form concludes:

\bigl\lVert\widetilde{C}_{\gamma_{1}}-\widetilde{C}_{\gamma_{2}}\bigr\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}=\Bigl\lVert\int_{\gamma_{1}}^{\gamma_{2}}\partial_{\gamma}\widetilde{C}_{\gamma}\,d\gamma\Bigr\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq 2\,\bar{k}^{1/2}L_{d}\,\lvert\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}\rvert,

which proves (iii). For the unit-normalized Gaussian kernel
\bar{k}=\sup_{\gamma,x}k_{\gamma}(x,x)=1
.

The following elementary fact records the split of the orthogonalized residuals into the true residuals, the score-projection term, and an estimation remainder, together with the regime-qualified orders of that remainder; it is stated for a generic sample size.

Fact 2
(Residual split)

Let
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}
denote the orthogonalized residual vector computed on a sample of size
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
denote the true residual vector on that sample. Under Assumptions
4
(iii) and
5
and, in the sieve regime, Assumption
6
(ii)–(iii), with the inverses understood on the invertibility events of the proof of Lemma
3
,
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\delta}
with

\boldsymbol{\delta}=-\hat{\mathbb{G}}\hat{\beta}+\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}
,
where
\hat{\beta}:=(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}
is the estimation remainder. When the score
\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon
is
X
-measurable, the first-order effect is the score direction
\hat{\mathbb{G}}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}
, with
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}:=\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}
, which the projection annihilates pointwise,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\hat{\mathbb{G}}=\boldsymbol{0}
; the remainder
\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}
is then the projected second-order remainder of that proof and satisfies
\lVert\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}\rVert=O_{p}(N^{1/2-2\varrho})
. In the sieve regime the projection annihilates only the fitted score, and the conditionally centered idiosyncratic first-order piece survives it, so
\lVert\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}\rVert=O_{p}(N^{1/2-\varrho})
. In both regimes
\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}/N)\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}=o_{p}(1)
, uniformly in
\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
, by Lemma
3
. The statement is sample-size-agnostic: it holds verbatim at any sample size, in particular at the selection sample of size
m
(with
m
in place of
N
throughout).

Proof.
The split is finite-sample algebra composed from three identities recorded in the proof of Lemma
3
. The second-order expansion of the estimated residual around
\theta_{0}
gives, exactly,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}+\mathbb{G}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}+\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
, with
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}:=\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}
,
\mathbb{G}
the raw-score matrix with rows
\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(s_{i};\hat{\theta})^{\top}
, and
\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
the Term A plus Term B remainder, element-wise
O_{p}(N^{-2\varrho})
. Projecting both sides with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{N}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}
, where
\hat{\mathbb{G}}=P_{Z}\mathbb{G}
, and decomposing
\mathbb{G}=\hat{\mathbb{G}}+(\mathbb{G}-\hat{\mathbb{G}})
, the finite-sample orthogonality
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\hat{\mathbb{G}}=\boldsymbol{0}
of that proof leaves

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}+\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\mathbb{G}-\hat{\mathbb{G}})\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}+\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}.

The score-projection formula
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}
, that is, the definition of the empirical projector, applied to
\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
, gives
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}\hat{\beta}
. Substituting this display into the previous one and collecting the last two terms into

\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\mathbb{G}-\hat{\mathbb{G}})\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}+\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}

yields
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}\hat{\beta}+\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}
, the claimed split with
\boldsymbol{\delta}=-\hat{\mathbb{G}}\hat{\beta}+\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}
. All three identities are pathwise and hold at any sample size, which also gives the sample-size-agnostic clause.

Suppose first that the score
\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon
is
X
-measurable, so that the sieve step is vacuous and
\hat{\mathbb{G}}=\mathbb{G}
. Then
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\mathbb{G}-\hat{\mathbb{G}})\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}=\boldsymbol{0}
, whence
\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
, and since
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
is symmetric idempotent with
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
,

\lVert\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}\rVert=\lVert\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}\rVert\leq\lVert\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}\rVert=O_{p}(N^{1/2-2\varrho}),

the last step being the second-order computation of the proof of Lemma
3
, under Assumptions
4
(iii) and
5
(iii):

\lVert\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}\rVert^{2}\leq C\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}\rVert^{4}\sum_{i}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\lVert\nabla_{\theta}^{2}\varepsilon(s_{i};\theta)\rVert^{2}=O_{p}(N^{1-4\varrho}),

the sum being
O_{p}(N)
by the law of large numbers under
\mathbb{E}\big[\sup_{\theta}\lVert\nabla_{\theta}^{2}\varepsilon(S;\theta)\rVert^{2}\big]<\infty
.

In the sieve regime, decompose the raw score as in that proof,

\mathbb{G}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}+(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{g}+(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta},

with
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}
the conditionally centered idiosyncratic score matrix,
\boldsymbol{g}
the population-score matrix with rows
g_{\theta_{0}}(X_{i})^{\top}
, and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}
the path-averaged Hessian matrix; the third piece, multiplied by
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}
, is folded into
\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
, which is redefined accordingly and remains element-wise
O_{p}(N^{-2\varrho})
. The estimation remainder then splits into the three pieces of that proof,

\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}=\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}}+\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{sieve}}+\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{proj}},

with

\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}\,\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}\,\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta},\qquad\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{sieve}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{g}\,\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})(\boldsymbol{g}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}})\,\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta},

the two equalities by the identity
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})
recorded in that proof, the second additionally using
(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}=\boldsymbol{0}
for the fitted sieve values
\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}=P_{Z}\mathbb{G}
(projector idempotence), and
\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{proj}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
as above. For the three norms, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, the contraction
\|\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
, and Assumption
4
(iii) give

\lVert\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}}\rVert\leq\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}\rVert=O_{p}(N^{1/2})\,O_{p}(N^{-\varrho})=O_{p}(N^{1/2-\varrho}),

where
\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}=O_{p}(N^{1/2})
follows from Markov’s inequality, since
\mathbb{E}\lVert\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq N\,\mathbb{E}\lVert\eta_{0,1}\rVert^{2}
and the entries of
\eta_{0,1}
have a bounded fourth moment by Assumption
5
(iii) and Jensen’s inequality applied to
g_{\theta_{0}}(X)=\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S;\theta_{0})\mid X]
; next,

\lVert\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{sieve}}\rVert\leq\lVert\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}-\boldsymbol{g}\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}\rVert=o_{p}(N^{\varrho})\,O_{p}(N^{-\varrho})=o_{p}(1),

the first bound being the sieve estimation-error rate of Assumption
6
(iii) through the empirical-norm bridge of the proof of Lemma
3
,
\lVert\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}-\boldsymbol{g}\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}=N\,\lVert\hat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}-g_{\theta_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}^{2}=o_{p}(N^{2\varrho})
; and
\lVert\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{proj}}\rVert\leq\lVert\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}\rVert=O_{p}(N^{1/2-2\varrho})
as in the
X
-measurable case, the second-order computation of that proof covering the folded-in gradient-drift terms. The triangle inequality yields
\lVert\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}\rVert=O_{p}(N^{1/2-\varrho})
for
\varrho\leq 1/2
and
O_{p}(1)
in general.

Finally, at the RKHS quadratic-form level, the spectral route of the proof of Lemma
3
bounds each piece in the form
\boldsymbol{r}_{k}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}/N)\boldsymbol{r}_{k}
: the centered piece by
O_{p}(N^{-2\varrho})
, the sieve-mean piece by
o_{p}(1)
, and the second-order piece by
O_{p}(N^{1-4\varrho})
; in the
X
-measurable regime only the second-order piece is present. Expanding the square and bounding each cross term by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for the positive semidefinite form
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\mapsto\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}/N)\boldsymbol{v}
, together with
2ab\leq a^{2}+b^{2}
,

\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}^{\top}\Bigl(\frac{\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}}{N}\Bigr)\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}\leq 3\sum_{k\in\{\mathrm{center},\mathrm{sieve},\mathrm{proj}\}}\boldsymbol{r}_{k}^{\top}\Bigl(\frac{\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}}{N}\Bigr)\boldsymbol{r}_{k}=o_{p}(1),

the second step collecting the piecewise bounds just cited, and the last holding for the standing exponent
\varrho>1/4
, uniformly in
\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
by the closing remark of that proof. This proves the quadratic-form claim and, with it, the fact.

The following lemma records the order-
p
moment bound for centered quadratic forms in conditionally independent innovations; it is consumed in the proofs of Lemma
10
, of Theorem
5
, and of Lemma
17
, and is stated for a generic conditioning field and a generic coefficient matrix.

Lemma 9
(Order-
p
moment of a centered quadratic form)

Let
p\in(2,4]
, let
\mathcal{G}
be a sub-
\sigma
-field, and let
\varepsilon_{0,1},\dots,\varepsilon_{0,n}
be real random variables which, conditionally on
\mathcal{G}
, are independent and centered, with conditional variances
\sigma_{j}^{2}:=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0,j}^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}
and
\sup_{j\leq n}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0,j}^{2p}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq\bar{M}_{2p}<\infty
. Then every symmetric
\mathcal{G}
-measurable
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}A\,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}-\operatorname{tr}(AD)\bigr|^{p}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{G}\Bigr]\leq C_{p}\bigl(\sigma_{\max},\bar{M}_{2p}\bigr)\,\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p},\qquad D:=\operatorname{diag}\bigl(\sigma_{1}^{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}^{2}\bigr),

(21)

for a constant
C_{p}
depending only on
(p,\sigma_{\max},\bar{M}_{2p})
.

Proof.
Work conditionally on
\mathcal{G}
throughout; all coefficient matrices below are
\mathcal{G}
-measurable. Expanding the quadratic form and centering its diagonal part at its conditional mean,

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}A\,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}-\operatorname{tr}(AD)=T_{1}+T_{2},\qquad T_{1}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{jj}\bigl(\varepsilon_{0,j}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2}\bigr),\qquad T_{2}:=\sum_{j\neq k}A_{jk}\,\varepsilon_{0,j}\varepsilon_{0,k},

the diagonal mean being
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0,j}^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}]=\sigma_{j}^{2}
entrywise and the off-diagonal conditional mean vanishing under the conditional centeredness and independence; the two families are bounded separately and combined by
(x+y)^{p}\leq 2^{p-1}(x^{p}+y^{p})
at the end.

The diagonal family.
Conditionally on
\mathcal{G}
the summands of
T_{1}
are independent and centered, and Rosenthal’s inequality at order
p
gives

\mathbb{E}\bigl[|T_{1}|^{p}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr]\leq C_{p}^{R}\Bigl\{\sum_{j=1}^{n}|A_{jj}|^{p}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl|\varepsilon_{0,j}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2}\bigr|^{p}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr]+\Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{jj}^{2}\,\nu_{j}\Bigr)^{p/2}\Bigr\},\qquad\nu_{j}:=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\varepsilon_{0,j}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2}\bigr)^{2}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr],

with
C_{p}^{R}
the Rosenthal constant. The summand moments obey
\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon_{0,j}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2}|^{p}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq 2^{p-1}\bigl(\bar{M}_{2p}+\sigma_{\max}^{2p}\bigr)
, by
|a-b|^{p}\leq 2^{p-1}(a^{p}+b^{p})
, the hypothesis
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0,j}^{2p}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq\bar{M}_{2p}
, and Jensen’s inequality
\sigma_{j}^{2p}\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2p}
; and
\nu_{j}\leq\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0,j}^{4}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq\bar{M}_{2p}^{2/p}
by the Lyapunov interpolation
\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon_{0,j}|^{q}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq\bar{M}_{2p}^{q/(2p)}
at
q=4
. The power mean
\sum_{j}|A_{jj}|^{p}\leq\bigl(\sum_{j}A_{jj}^{2}\bigr)^{p/2}
for
p\geq 2
, the diagonal bound
\sum_{j}A_{jj}^{2}\leq\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
, and
\bigl(\sum_{j}A_{jj}^{2}\nu_{j}\bigr)^{p/2}\leq\bar{M}_{2p}\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}
then give

\mathbb{E}\bigl[|T_{1}|^{p}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr]\leq C_{p}\bigl(\sigma_{\max},\bar{M}_{2p}\bigr)\,\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}.

The off-diagonal family.
Let
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime}
be an independent copy of
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
given
\mathcal{G}
and set
S_{j}^{\prime}:=\sum_{k\neq j}A_{jk}\varepsilon_{0,k}^{\prime}
, so that
\sum_{j}\varepsilon_{0,j}S_{j}^{\prime}
is the decoupled form of
T_{2}
. The decoupling inequality for homogeneous chaos forms of order two
[
de la Peña and Giné, 1999
, Ch. 3]
gives

\mathbb{E}\bigl[|T_{2}|^{p}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr]\leq C_{p}^{dec}\,\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Bigl|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{0,j}S_{j}^{\prime}\Bigr|^{p}\Bigm|\mathcal{G}\Bigr],

with
C_{p}^{dec}
the decoupling constant. Conditioning further on
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime}
and applying Rosenthal’s inequality to the linear form in the conditionally independent, centered variables
\varepsilon_{0,j}
, whose
p
-th moments satisfy
\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon_{0,j}|^{p}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq\bar{M}_{2p}^{1/2}
by the Lyapunov interpolation at
q=p
,

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Bigl|\sum_{j}\varepsilon_{0,j}S_{j}^{\prime}\Bigr|^{p}\Bigm|\mathcal{G},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime}\Bigr]\leq C_{p}^{R}\Bigl\{\bar{M}_{2p}^{1/2}\sum_{j}|S_{j}^{\prime}|^{p}+\sigma_{\max}^{p}\Bigl(\sum_{j}(S_{j}^{\prime})^{2}\Bigr)^{p/2}\Bigr\},

the second summand using
\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}^{2}(S_{j}^{\prime})^{2}\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}\sum_{j}(S_{j}^{\prime})^{2}
. The two summands are unconditioned over
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime}
separately. For the first, Rosenthal’s inequality applied to each linear form
S_{j}^{\prime}
in the independent, centered variables
\varepsilon_{0,k}^{\prime}
gives

\mathbb{E}\bigl[|S_{j}^{\prime}|^{p}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr]\leq C_{p}^{R}\Bigl\{\bar{M}_{2p}^{1/2}\sum_{k\neq j}|A_{jk}|^{p}+\sigma_{\max}^{p}\Bigl(\sum_{k\neq j}A_{jk}^{2}\Bigr)^{p/2}\Bigr\},

and summing over
j
, the power mean
\sum_{j,k}|A_{jk}|^{p}\leq\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}
and the superadditivity
\sum_{j}\bigl(\sum_{k}A_{jk}^{2}\bigr)^{p/2}\leq\bigl(\sum_{j,k}A_{jk}^{2}\bigr)^{p/2}=\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}
for
p/2\geq 1
yield

\bar{M}_{2p}^{1/2}\,\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{j}|S_{j}^{\prime}|^{p}\Bigm|\mathcal{G}\Bigr]\leq C_{p}\,\bar{M}_{2p}\,\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}.

For the second,
\sum_{j}(S_{j}^{\prime})^{2}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime\top}M\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime}
with
M:=(A^{\mathrm{od}})^{\top}A^{\mathrm{od}}
symmetric positive semidefinite,
A^{\mathrm{od}}
denoting the off-diagonal part of
A
; at
q:=p/2\in(1,2]
, splitting around the conditional mean, then applying
(a+b)^{q}\leq 2^{q-1}(a^{q}+b^{q})
and Lyapunov’s inequality
\bigl(\mathbb{E}|W|^{q}\bigr)^{1/q}\leq\bigl(\mathbb{E}W^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}
for
q\leq 2
to the centered part, gives

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime\top}M\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime}\bigr)^{q}\Bigm|\mathcal{G}\Bigr]\leq 2^{q-1}\Bigl\{\Bigl(\operatorname{Var}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime\top}M\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)\Bigr)^{q/2}+\bigl|\operatorname{tr}(MD)\bigr|^{q}\Bigr\}.

The variance is computed by expanding the square and keeping the surviving pairings, the conditional independence and centeredness leaving only the diagonal squares and the two orderings of each off-diagonal index pair:

\operatorname{Var}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime\top}M\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\prime}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr)=\sum_{j}M_{jj}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\varepsilon_{0,j}^{\prime 2}-\sigma_{j}^{2}\bigr)^{2}\bigm|\mathcal{G}\bigr]+2\sum_{j\neq k}M_{jk}^{2}\,\sigma_{j}^{2}\sigma_{k}^{2}\leq\bigl(\bar{M}_{2p}^{2/p}+2\sigma_{\max}^{4}\bigr)\,\|M\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2},

the bound using
\mathbb{E}[(\varepsilon_{0,j}^{\prime 2}-\sigma_{j}^{2})^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0,j}^{\prime 4}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq\bar{M}_{2p}^{2/p}
and
\sigma_{j}^{2}\sigma_{k}^{2}\leq\sigma_{\max}^{4}
; the trace term obeys
|\operatorname{tr}(MD)|\leq\|D\|_{\mathrm{op}}\operatorname{tr}M\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}\|A^{\mathrm{od}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
, since
M
and
D
are positive semidefinite, and
\|M\|_{\mathrm{F}}=\|(A^{\mathrm{od}})^{\top}A^{\mathrm{od}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\|A^{\mathrm{od}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
by submultiplicativity. Hence

\sigma_{\max}^{p}\,\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Bigl(\sum_{j}(S_{j}^{\prime})^{2}\Bigr)^{p/2}\Bigm|\mathcal{G}\Bigr]\leq C_{p}\bigl(\sigma_{\max},\bar{M}_{2p}\bigr)\,\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p},

and the two unconditioned summands combine into
\mathbb{E}[|T_{2}|^{p}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq C_{p}(\sigma_{\max},\bar{M}_{2p})\,\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}
.

Assembly.
The split display and
(x+y)^{p}\leq 2^{p-1}(x^{p}+y^{p})
give

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}A\,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}-\operatorname{tr}(AD)\bigr|^{p}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{G}\Bigr]\leq C_{p}\bigl(\sigma_{\max},\bar{M}_{2p}\bigr)\,\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p},

which is (
21
).

Lemma 10
(Lipschitz properties on the common space)

Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma}

\displaystyle:=J_{\gamma}C_{n,\gamma}J_{\gamma}^{-1},

\displaystyle\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma)

\displaystyle:=J_{\gamma}M_{n}(\gamma)=n^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{\perp,j}\,J_{\gamma}k_{\gamma}(\cdot,X_{j}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}^{(\gamma)}_{>K}

\displaystyle:=J_{\gamma}P^{(\gamma)}_{>K}J_{\gamma}^{-1}

all three objects act on the common space
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
. Assume Assumptions
1
–
6
and
7
(iii),(v), and, under the local alternative, the Pitman calibration
N\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=O(1)
of the regime-coverage paragraph below. Then:

(a)

there exists
L_{M}=O_{p}(1)
with
\lVert\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma_{1})-\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}\leq L_{M}|\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}|
for all
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
, and

\overline{M}_{n}:=\sup_{\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]}\lVert\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}=O_{p}(1)
;

(b)

there exists
L_{C}=O_{p}(1)
with
\lVert\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{1}}-\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{2}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq L_{C}|\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}|
for all
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
, where the Hilbert–Schmidt difference
is taken on the common space; consequently
r_{n}:=\sup_{\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]}\lVert C_{n,\gamma}-C_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{p}(1)
;

(c)

the transported resolvent
\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta):=(\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}
satisfies, for all

\eta_{1}=(\gamma_{1},\lambda_{1}),\eta_{2}=(\gamma_{2},\lambda_{2})\in\Upsilon
,

\lVert\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta_{1})-\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta_{2})\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lambda_{L}^{-2}\bigl(L_{C}|\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}|+|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|\bigr),\qquad\lVert\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lambda_{L}^{-1},

with
L_{C}
as in part (b).

Under fixed alternatives the same estimates hold with both sides inflated by powers of
\sqrt{n}
.

Proof.

(a) Mean embedding.
The argument combines the feature-distance geometry of the transported kernels with the derivative-feature calculus of Fact
1
: the pair supremum of the modulus is reduced to a one-dimensional derivative-process supremum and closed by a conditional second-moment modulus with dyadic chaining, and the boundedness half is read off at a base point through the quadratic-form clause of Fact
2
.

Gram–matrix reduction.
By linearity and the binomial identity on norms,

\displaystyle\lVert\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma_{1})-\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j,k}\varepsilon_{\perp,j}\varepsilon_{\perp,k}\,\Delta G_{jk},

\displaystyle\Delta G_{jk}

\displaystyle:=k_{\gamma_{1}}(r_{jk})+k_{\gamma_{2}}(r_{jk})-2K_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(X_{j},X_{k}),

with
r_{jk}:=\lVert X_{j}-X_{k}\rVert
: the two same-bandwidth terms reduce to native kernel entries because
J_{\gamma}
is an isometry,

\langle J_{\gamma}k_{\gamma}(\cdot,x),J_{\gamma}k_{\gamma}(\cdot,y)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}=\langle k_{\gamma}(\cdot,x),k_{\gamma}(\cdot,y)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}=k_{\gamma}(x,y)
,
and the mixed term is the cross-kernel

K_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(x,y):=\langle J_{\gamma_{1}}k_{\gamma_{1}}(\cdot,x),J_{\gamma_{2}}k_{\gamma_{2}}(\cdot,y)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}
.
By Lemma
5
,
K_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}=c_{12}\,k_{\gamma_{12}}

with
\gamma_{12}\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
and
c_{12}\in[c_{\min},1]
as stated there;
in particular
c_{12}=1
at
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
, recovering the isometry. Note that
\Delta\boldsymbol{G}
is positive semidefinite: it is the Gram matrix of the feature differences

J_{\gamma_{1}}k_{\gamma_{1}}(\cdot,X_{j})-J_{\gamma_{2}}k_{\gamma_{2}}(\cdot,X_{j})
, the symmetry of the cross term being part of (
17
).

Residual decomposition.
Throughout part (a), write
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}-\hat{\mathbb{G}}\hat{\beta}+\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}
with the three pieces of Fact
2
, applied at the testing sample of size
n
; the uniformity step below consumes the three pieces separately (the projected score term through the rank-
d_{\theta}
projection identity
\hat{\mathbb{G}}\hat{\beta}=H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
with
H:=\hat{\mathbb{G}}(\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}
, the estimation remainder through the quadratic-form clause of the fact), never the Euclidean norm of
\boldsymbol{\delta}
, which is not bounded in the sieve regime.

Regime coverage.
The stochastic arguments below assume that the noise part of
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
is conditionally centered given
\mathbf{X}
; this paragraph records the split and the envelopes by which the local alternative reduces to that case. Under
H_{0}
write
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=\boldsymbol{v}
, a vector of conditionally independent, conditionally centered entries with
\mathbb{E}[v^{2}\mid X]=\sigma^{2}(X)
. Under
H_{1,n}
write
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=\boldsymbol{g}+\boldsymbol{v}
with the raw shift vector
\boldsymbol{g}:=(g(X_{1}),\dots,g(X_{n}))^{\top}
,
g:=c_{N}R
, and
\boldsymbol{v}
the conditionally centered noise. The effective signal of the orthogonalized residuals is
c_{N}\boldsymbol{\Pi}R
: at the testing sample, of size
N=n
,
c_{N}=(m+n)^{-1/2}
and
h:=\boldsymbol{\Pi}R
, the convention of the uniform-split paragraph of the proof of Theorem
5
; there, under
H_{1,n}
, the symbol
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
denotes the centered noise (the two readings coincide under
H_{0}
), while the raw reading
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=\boldsymbol{g}+\boldsymbol{v}
is local to the present paragraph; at the selection sample, of size
N=m
(Lemma
16
below),
c_{N}=\delta_{m}
with
\sqrt{m}\,\delta_{m}\to a<\infty
and
h
the fixed local-alternative direction. The score-span component of the raw shift is carried asymptotically by the projected-score piece
-\hat{\mathbb{G}}\hat{\beta}
of the residual decomposition, and every envelope below is stated for the raw vector because both components share the same integrability,
\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{\Pi}R)^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[R^{2}]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}[((\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{\Pi})R)^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[R^{2}]<\infty
, by the contraction property of the
L^{2}
-projection
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
and the standing convention
\mathbb{E}[R^{2}]<\infty
of Section
4.1
. In both regimes the Pitman calibration holds: by the law of large numbers,

N^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N}h^{2}(X_{j})\;\xrightarrow{p}\;\mathbb{E}\bigl[h^{2}\bigr]\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}\bigl[R^{2}\bigr]\;<\;\infty,

(22)

and likewise
N^{-1}\sum_{j}R^{2}(X_{j})\xrightarrow{p}\mathbb{E}[R^{2}]
, so
\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert^{2}=Nc_{N}^{2}\,\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,(1+o_{p}(1))=O_{p}(1)
, the factor
Nc_{N}^{2}
being
n/(m+n)\to 1-c
at the testing sample and
m\delta_{m}^{2}\to a^{2}<\infty
at the selection sample. The mean part costs only entrywise envelopes. By Cauchy–Schwarz against
|\Delta G_{jk}|\leq WT^{2}
, the entrywise bound of the feature-distance paragraph below,

\bigl\lvert n^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\Delta\boldsymbol{G}\,\boldsymbol{g}\bigr\rvert\;\leq\;WT^{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert^{2}\;=\;O_{p}(T^{2}),

uniformly in the pair index, and at fixed alternatives the same bound is
O_{p}(nT^{2})
, the inflation recorded in the closing statement of the lemma. On the
\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}
-scale, the trace bound

\lambda_{\max}\bigl(n^{-1}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\bigr)\;\leq\;n^{-1}\operatorname{tr}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\;=\;n^{-1}\sum_{j}\bar{W}_{jj}\;\leq\;W,

holds, the diagonal entries sharing the entrywise bound, so
n^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{g}\leq W\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert^{2}=O_{p}(1)
, and the cross term follows from the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for the positive semidefinite form
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\mapsto n^{-1}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{v}
,

\bigl\lvert n^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\,\boldsymbol{v}\bigr\rvert\;\leq\;\bigl(W\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\Bigl(\sup_{\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}}n^{-1}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{v}\Bigr)^{1/2}\;=\;O_{p}(1),

the second factor being bounded in the uniformity step below; the
\boldsymbol{v}
-part enters the derivative-process argument there verbatim.

Feature distance and the second-order entrywise bound.
By symmetry of the statement in
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
, assume
\gamma_{2}\geq\gamma_{1}
and put
T:=\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}
. Write
\tilde{u}_{a}(x):=J_{\gamma_{a}}k_{\gamma_{a}}(x,\cdot)
for the transported features, so that
\lVert\tilde{u}_{a}(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}=\lVert k_{\gamma_{a}}(x,\cdot)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{a}}}=\sqrt{k_{\gamma_{a}}(x,x)}=1
, and
f(x):=\tilde{u}_{1}(x)-\tilde{u}_{2}(x)
for the feature difference. The feature distance is exact and second-order:

\lVert f(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}=k_{\gamma_{1}}(x,x)+k_{\gamma_{2}}(x,x)-2K_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(x,x)=2\bigl(1-c_{12}\bigr),

the mixed term being the cross-kernel on the diagonal,
K_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(x,x)=c_{12}\,k_{\gamma_{12}}(x,x)=c_{12}
by Lemma
5
. With the closed form
c_{12}=2^{d/2}(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})^{d/4}/(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2})^{d/2}
of that lemma, equivalently
c_{12}=\bigl(1-x^{2}\bigr)^{d/4}
by
4\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}=(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2})^{2}(1-x^{2})
, where
x:=(\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1})/(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2})
, the elementary inequality
1-(1-u)^{\alpha}\leq\max(1,\alpha)\,u
for
u\in[0,1]
(concavity at
\alpha\leq 1
, Bernoulli at
\alpha\geq 1
) and
x\leq T/(2\gamma_{L})
give

\lVert f(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}\leq L_{k}^{\prime}\,T,\qquad L_{k}^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{\gamma_{L}}\sqrt{\frac{c_{d}}{2}},\qquad c_{d}:=\max\Bigl(1,\frac{d}{4}\Bigr),

a deterministic,
\mathbf{X}
-free constant. The entrywise bound follows without any expansion in the bandwidth gap: by exact Gram algebra and the symmetry of the cross-kernel (part of (
17
)),

\Delta G_{jk}=\bigl\langle f(X_{j}),f(X_{k})\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}},

so that, by Cauchy–Schwarz,

|\Delta G_{jk}|\leq\lVert f(X_{j})\rVert\,\lVert f(X_{k})\rVert\leq W\,T^{2},\qquad W:=L_{k}^{\prime 2},

uniformly in
j,k
and
\mathbf{X}
; on the diagonal the bound is sharp in structure,
\Delta G_{jj}=2(1-c_{12})\leq WT^{2}
. Since
\Delta\boldsymbol{G}
is, as recorded after the Gram–matrix reduction, the Gram matrix of the feature differences
f(X_{j})
, scaling by the positive number
T^{-2}
preserves symmetry and positive semidefiniteness in

\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}:=T^{-2}\Delta\boldsymbol{G},\qquad|\bar{W}_{jk}|\leq W.

The Gram–matrix reduction therefore yields the exact pathwise identity

\lVert\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma_{1})-\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}=|\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}|^{2}\,n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}
,
with no remainder term; consistency of signs is visible on both sides, since the left side is a squared norm while the right side is a nonnegative quadratic form in a positive semidefinite matrix. It remains to bound the quadratic form uniformly over pairs. Split the residuals by the decomposition recorded above,
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}-H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}+\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}
, and expand the square, bounding each cross term by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for the positive semidefinite form
(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\mapsto n^{-1}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{v}
, together with
2ab\leq a^{2}+b^{2}
applied twice, as in the proof of Fact
2
:

n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}\;\leq\;3\,n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}\;+\;3\,n^{-1}(H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}(H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})\;+\;3\,n^{-1}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}},

and the three terms on the right are bounded separately, uniformly over the pair.

The noise term.
For the first term, Fact
1
(ii) reduces the pair supremum to a one-dimensional supremum of the derivative-feature process,

\sup_{\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}}n^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{a}\;\leq\;\sup_{\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]}D_{\boldsymbol{a}}(\gamma)\qquad\text{at}\qquad\boldsymbol{a}\in\{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}\},

where
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
under
H_{0}
; under
H_{1,n}
, expanding the quadratic form of the regime-coverage split
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=\boldsymbol{g}+\boldsymbol{v}
with
2ab\leq a^{2}+b^{2}
gives

\sup_{\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}}n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}\leq 2W\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert^{2}+2\sup_{\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}}n^{-1}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{v}
,
the first summand by the mean-part envelope of that paragraph. It therefore suffices to bound
\sup_{\gamma}D_{v}(\gamma)
, where
D_{v}(\gamma):=D_{\boldsymbol{a}}(\gamma)\big|_{\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{v}}=\bigl\lVert\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j}v_{j}\,\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(X_{j})\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}
; the argument is a conditional second-moment modulus with dyadic chaining. Conditionally on
\mathbf{X}
, the summands
v_{j}\,\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{L}}(X_{j})
are independent and centered, with
\mathbb{E}[v_{j}^{2}\mid\mathbf{X}]=\sigma^{2}(X_{j})\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}
(Assumption
7
(iii)), so expanding the squared norm and keeping the diagonal pairings gives the base-point bound

\mathbb{E}\bigl[D_{v}(\gamma_{L})\bigm|\mathbf{X}\bigr]=n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma^{2}(X_{j})\,\bigl\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{L}}(X_{j})\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}\;\leq\;\sigma_{\max}^{2}L_{d}^{2}

by Fact
1
(i). For the modulus, introduce the derivative-feature Gram and its increments,

G^{(d)}_{\gamma,jk}:=\bigl\langle\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(X_{j}),\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma}(X_{k})\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}},\qquad\Delta^{(d)}_{\gamma,jk}:=G^{(d)}_{\gamma_{1},jk}-G^{(d)}_{\gamma_{2},jk},

so that
D_{v}(\gamma)=n^{-1}\sum_{j,k}v_{j}v_{k}G^{(d)}_{\gamma,jk}
by the Gram representation of Fact
1
(ii); adding and subtracting
\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{2}}(X_{j})
in the first factor of each inner product, then applying the triangle inequality and Fact
1
(i),

\displaystyle\bigl|\Delta^{(d)}_{\gamma,jk}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}}(X_{j})-\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{2}}(X_{j})\bigr\rVert\,\bigl\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}}(X_{k})\bigr\rVert

\displaystyle+\bigl\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{2}}(X_{j})\bigr\rVert\,\bigl\lVert\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}}(X_{k})-\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{2}}(X_{k})\bigr\rVert

\displaystyle\leq L_{dd}^{\prime}\,\lvert\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}\rvert,

uniformly in
j,k
and
\mathbf{X}
, with the deterministic constant
L_{dd}^{\prime}:=2L_{d}L_{dd}
. Splitting
D_{v}(\gamma_{1})-D_{v}(\gamma_{2})=n^{-1}\sum_{j}v_{j}^{2}\Delta^{(d)}_{\gamma,jj}+n^{-1}\sum_{j\neq k}v_{j}v_{k}\Delta^{(d)}_{\gamma,jk}
and using
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
, the two parts have conditional second moments

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Bigl(n^{-1}\textstyle\sum_{j}v_{j}^{2}\Delta^{(d)}_{\gamma,jj}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigm|\mathbf{X}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq n^{-1}\textstyle\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}\bigl[v_{j}^{4}\bigm|\mathbf{X}\bigr]\bigl(\Delta^{(d)}_{\gamma,jj}\bigr)^{2}\;\leq\;\bar{M}_{4}L_{dd}^{\prime\,2}\,\lvert\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}\rvert^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Bigl(n^{-1}\textstyle\sum_{j\neq k}v_{j}v_{k}\Delta^{(d)}_{\gamma,jk}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigm|\mathbf{X}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=2n^{-2}\textstyle\sum_{j\neq k}\sigma^{2}(X_{j})\sigma^{2}(X_{k})\bigl(\Delta^{(d)}_{\gamma,jk}\bigr)^{2}\;\leq\;2\sigma_{\max}^{4}L_{dd}^{\prime\,2}\,\lvert\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}\rvert^{2},

the first line by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
(\sum_{j}x_{j})^{2}\leq n\sum_{j}x_{j}^{2}
and the interpolated fourth moment
\mathbb{E}[v^{4}\mid X]\leq\bar{M}_{4}:=\bar{M}_{2p}^{2/p}
of Assumption
7
(v) (Lyapunov interpolation), the second by the conditional independence and centeredness of the entries, which leave the two orderings of each index pair as the only surviving pairings. Hence

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(D_{v}(\gamma_{1})-D_{v}(\gamma_{2})\bigr)^{2}\Bigm|\mathbf{X}\Bigr]\;\leq\;C_{D}\,\lvert\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}\rvert^{2},\qquad C_{D}:=\bigl(2\bar{M}_{4}+4\sigma_{\max}^{4}\bigr)L_{dd}^{\prime\,2}.

For the chaining, let
\mathcal{G}_{J}:=\{\gamma_{L}+kh_{J}:0\leq k\leq 2^{J}\}
with
h_{J}:=(\gamma_{U}-\gamma_{L})2^{-J}
be the dyadic grids; the paths
\gamma\mapsto D_{v}(\gamma)
are continuous by Fact
1
(i), and the dyadic expansion of
\gamma-\gamma_{L}
in binary digits, each partial sum and its successor being adjacent points of
\mathcal{G}_{J}
, telescopes the increment into level maxima,

\sup_{\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]}\bigl|D_{v}(\gamma)-D_{v}(\gamma_{L})\bigr|\;\leq\;\sum_{J\geq 0}\ \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{J}\\
\lvert\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}\rvert=h_{J}\end{subarray}}\bigl|D_{v}(\gamma)-D_{v}(\gamma^{\prime})\bigr|,

the limit term vanishing by path continuity; each level contains
2^{J}
adjacent pairs, so the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for maxima within a level, followed by integration over
\mathbf{X}
of the modulus display, gives

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\max_{\lvert\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}\rvert=h_{J}}\bigl|D_{v}(\gamma)-D_{v}(\gamma^{\prime})\bigr|\Bigr]\;\leq\;\bigl(2^{J}\bigr)^{1/2}\bigl(C_{D}h_{J}^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\;=\;C_{D}^{1/2}(\gamma_{U}-\gamma_{L})\,2^{-J/2},

and the geometric series at ratio
2^{-1/2}
sums to
(1-2^{-1/2})^{-1}
, whence
\mathbb{E}\bigl[\sup_{\gamma}|D_{v}(\gamma)-D_{v}(\gamma_{L})|\bigr]\leq K_{D}:=C_{D}^{1/2}(\gamma_{U}-\gamma_{L})(1-2^{-1/2})^{-1}
. Combining with the base-point bound,
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\gamma}D_{v}(\gamma)]\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}L_{d}^{2}+K_{D}<\infty
, so
\sup_{\gamma}D_{v}(\gamma)=O_{p}(1)
by Markov’s inequality; with the mean-part envelope of the regime-coverage paragraph this bounds the first term,
\sup_{\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}}n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=O_{p}(1)
in both regimes.

The projected-score term.
For the second term, put
\varphi_{j}:=(\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{1}}(X_{j})-\tilde{u}_{\gamma_{2}}(X_{j}))/T
, so that
\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}
is the Gram matrix of the
\varphi_{j}
, of norms at most
L_{k}^{\prime}
by the feature-distance bound, and
n^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{a}=\bigl\lVert n^{-1/2}\sum_{j}a_{j}\varphi_{j}\bigr\rVert^{2}
for every
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
; the tight entrywise form of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,
n^{-1}(\sum_{j}|x_{j}|)^{2}\leq\sum_{j}x_{j}^{2}
, then gives the deterministic majorization

n^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{a}\;\leq\;n^{-1}\Bigl(\sum_{j}|a_{j}|\,\lVert\varphi_{j}\rVert\Bigr)^{2}\;\leq\;W\,\lVert\boldsymbol{a}\rVert^{2},\qquad\text{that is,}\qquad n^{-1}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\preceq W\boldsymbol{I}_{n},

uniformly over the pair. With
\boldsymbol{a}=H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
, and
H
symmetric idempotent (the orthogonal projector onto
\operatorname{range}(\hat{\mathbb{G}})
, of rank
d_{\theta}
on the invertibility events of Fact
2
),

n^{-1}(H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}(H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0})\;\leq\;W\,\lVert H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}\rVert^{2}\;=\;W\,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0},

uniformly over the pair, and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=O_{p}(1)
under the convention of Remark
2
, which licenses the adaptive projector: by (
21
) at
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}=\sqrt{d_{\theta}}
for the centered part and the tracial centering
\lvert\operatorname{tr}(HD)\rvert\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}d_{\theta}
, in both regimes.

The estimation remainder.
For the third term, the majorization just displayed extends the quadratic-form clause of Fact
2
from
\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}/n
to
n^{-1}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}
: the recorded splits of the proof of Lemma
3
consume, beside the projector identities, only positive semidefiniteness and the contraction of the quadratic-form matrix, and
n^{-1}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}
shares both, with
W
in place of one (
\lambda_{\max}(n^{-1}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}})\leq W
). In the
X
-measurable regime only the second-order piece is present, and

n^{-1}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}\;\leq\;W\,\lVert\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{proj}}\rVert^{2}\;=\;O_{p}\bigl(n^{1-4\varrho}\bigr)\;=\;o_{p}(1),

the rate being the second-order computation of that proof, whose exponent is negative under
\varrho>1/4
. In the sieve regime the second-order and sieve-mean pieces obey the same majorization,

\displaystyle n^{-1}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{proj}}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{proj}}

\displaystyle\leq W\,\lVert\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{proj}}\rVert^{2}=O_{p}\bigl(n^{1-4\varrho}\bigr)=o_{p}(1),

\displaystyle n^{-1}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{sieve}}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{sieve}}

\displaystyle\leq W\,\lVert\boldsymbol{g}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}\rVert^{2}=o_{p}\bigl(n^{2\varrho}\bigr)\,O_{p}\bigl(n^{-2\varrho}\bigr)=o_{p}(1),

the second line by the contraction
\lVert\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
and the sieve estimation-error rate of Assumption
6
(iii) in the form recorded in that proof. The centered piece requires its fluctuation structure and is reduced to one dimension first: by Fact
1
(ii) at
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}}
and the symmetry of
\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z}
,

\sup_{\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}}^{\top}\bigl(n^{-1}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\bigr)\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}}\;\leq\;\sup_{\gamma}\ \boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}^{\top}\tilde{A}(\gamma)\,\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta},\qquad\tilde{A}(\gamma):=\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}^{\top}M_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0},

with
M_{\gamma}:=\bigl(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z}\bigr)\bigl(n^{-1}G^{(d)}_{\gamma}\bigr)\bigl(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z}\bigr)
,
where
\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}}=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}
is the split of that proof, the identity following from the Gram representation of Fact
1
(ii) applied to
\boldsymbol{a}=(\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z})\boldsymbol{\eta}_{0}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}
, the symmetry of
\boldsymbol{I}-P_{Z}
transferring the projector from the weights to the Gram. The same fourth-moment computation as in the centered part of the proof of Lemma
3
, applied conditionally on
\mathbf{X}
to the
\mathbf{X}
-measurable matrices
M_{\gamma}
, gives, with finite constants
C_{A},C_{A}^{\prime}
depending only on
(d_{\theta},L_{d},L_{dd}^{\prime})
and the score moments of Assumption
5
(iii),

\mathbb{E}\bigl\lVert\tilde{A}(\gamma_{L})\bigr\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq C_{A},\qquad\mathbb{E}\bigl\lVert\tilde{A}(\gamma_{1})-\tilde{A}(\gamma_{2})\bigr\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq C_{A}^{\prime}\,\lvert\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}\rvert^{2},

using
\lVert M_{\gamma_{L}}\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\lVert n^{-1}G^{(d)}_{\gamma_{L}}\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\leq L_{d}^{2}
and
\lVert M_{\gamma_{1}}-M_{\gamma_{2}}\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\lVert n^{-1}(G^{(d)}_{\gamma_{1}}-G^{(d)}_{\gamma_{2}})\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\leq L_{dd}^{\prime}\lvert\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}\rvert
entrywise, the entrywise increment bound having been displayed above; the same dyadic chaining as for
D_{v}
, applied to the field
\gamma\mapsto\lVert\tilde{A}(\gamma)\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}
(continuous paths by the same increment bound), yields
\sup_{\gamma}\lVert\tilde{A}(\gamma)\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}=O_{p}(1)
, whence

\sup_{\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}}^{\top}\bigl(n^{-1}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\bigr)\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{center}}\;\leq\;\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\theta}\rVert^{2}\ \sup_{\gamma}\lVert\tilde{A}(\gamma)\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\;=\;O_{p}\bigl(n^{-2\varrho}\bigr)\,O_{p}(1)\;=\;o_{p}(1).

The cross terms between the three pieces are absorbed by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and
2ab\leq a^{2}+b^{2}
with the factor
3
, as in the proof of the fact. Hence
\sup_{\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}}n^{-1}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}=o_{p}(1)
, uniformly over the pair, the majorization and the one-dimensional reduction being pair-free.

Combining the three terms in the three-term bound,

\begin{gathered}\lVert\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma_{1})-\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}\;\leq\;L_{M}\,\lvert\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}\rvert,\\
L_{M}^{2}:=3\sup_{\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}}\Bigl[n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}+W\,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}+n^{-1}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}^{\top}\bar{\boldsymbol{W}}\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}\Bigr],\end{gathered}

whose bracket is
O_{p}(1)+W\,O_{p}(1)+o_{p}(1)
in both regimes, the middle summand being the convention-licensed projected-score term of the preceding step (Remark
2
); this proves the first half of part (a).

Uniform boundedness.
By the modulus just established it suffices to bound the process at a single point: for any
\gamma_{*}\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
,

\sup_{\gamma\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]}\lVert\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}\;\leq\;\lVert\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma_{*})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}+L_{M}(\gamma_{U}-\gamma_{L}).

By the isometry
J_{\gamma_{*}}
and the reproducing property,
\lVert\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma_{*})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}=\lVert M_{n}(\gamma_{*})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{*}}}^{2}=n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma_{*}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}
, the normalized Gram matrix being positive semidefinite and contracting,
\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma_{*}}/n)\leq\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma_{*}}/n)=1
(
k_{\gamma}(x,x)=1
by unit normalization). Expanding the square of the residual decomposition with the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and
2ab\leq a^{2}+b^{2}
, as in the uniformity step,

n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma_{*}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\perp}\;\leq\;3\,Q(\gamma_{*})+3\,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}H\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}\;+\;3\,\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}^{\top}\bigl(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma_{*}}/n\bigr)\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}},\qquad Q(\gamma):=n^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}\geq 0,

where the third term is
o_{p}(1)
uniformly in
\gamma
by the quadratic-form clause of Fact
2
and the second is the convention-licensed projected-score term of the projected-score step. For the first, under
H_{0}
the diagonal carries the whole expectation, the off-diagonal expectations vanishing under
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\mid X]=0
:

\mathbb{E}\bigl[Q(\gamma_{*})\bigr]=n^{-1}\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\varepsilon_{0,j}^{2}\bigr]\,k_{\gamma_{*}}(X_{j},X_{j})=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\bigr],

finite by Assumption
2
(v), so
Q(\gamma_{*})=O_{p}(1)
by Markov’s inequality. Under
H_{1,n}
, write
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=\boldsymbol{g}+\boldsymbol{v}
as in the regime-coverage paragraph and split
Q(\gamma_{*})=Q^{\boldsymbol{v}}(\gamma_{*})+2\,n^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma_{*}}\boldsymbol{v}+Q^{\boldsymbol{g}}(\gamma_{*})
; the same diagonal computation, with
\mathbb{E}[v^{2}\mid X]=\sigma^{2}(X)
and the off-diagonal expectations vanishing, gives
\mathbb{E}[Q^{\boldsymbol{v}}(\gamma_{*})]\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}
(Assumption
7
(iii)), hence
Q^{\boldsymbol{v}}(\gamma_{*})=O_{p}(1)
by Markov’s inequality; next,

Q^{\boldsymbol{g}}(\gamma_{*})\;\leq\;\lambda_{\max}\bigl(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma_{*}}/n\bigr)\,\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert^{2}\;\leq\;\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert^{2}\;=\;O_{p}(1)

by (
22
) read at the raw shift function; and the cross term is conditionally centered given
\mathbf{X}
, with conditional variance

\operatorname{Var}\bigl(n^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma_{*}}\boldsymbol{v}\bigm|\mathbf{X}\bigr)=n^{-2}\sum_{j}\sigma^{2}(X_{j})\,\bigl[(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma_{*}}\boldsymbol{g})_{j}\bigr]^{2}\;\leq\;\sigma_{\max}^{2}\,n^{-1}\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert^{2},

by the row bound
(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma_{*}}\boldsymbol{g})_{j}\leq\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert
(the Gram rows have unit norm,
k_{\gamma}(x,x)=1
), a data-random bound whose expectation is
\sigma_{\max}^{2}\,n^{-1}\,\mathbb{E}\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert^{2}=O(1/n)
by the expectation form of (
22
); since the cross term is conditionally centered, the iterated expectation gives
\mathbb{E}\bigl[(n^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma_{*}}\boldsymbol{v})^{2}\bigr]=O(1/n)
, and Chebyshev’s inequality yields
n^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma_{*}}\boldsymbol{v}=o_{p}(1)
. Hence
\lVert\widetilde{M}_{n}(\gamma_{*})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}^{2}=O_{p}(1)+W\,O_{p}(1)+o_{p}(1)
, and with the base-point triangle this completes the boundedness half.

(b) Covariance.
The operators
C_{n,\gamma_{1}}
and
C_{n,\gamma_{2}}
act on the different spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{1}}
,
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{2}}
, so their Hilbert–Schmidt difference is taken after transporting both to the common space
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
. Writing
\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma):=J_{\gamma}k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot)
for the transported kernels,
\lVert\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}=\lVert k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}=\sqrt{k_{\gamma}(X_{j},X_{j})}=1
(the transport is an isometry, Lemma
4
, and the Gaussian kernel is unit-normalized), one has
\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma}=n^{-1}\sum_{j}\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma)\otimes\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma)
. Rank-one telescoping makes the Lipschitz bound deterministic. With
f_{j}:=\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma_{1})-\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma_{2})
, the feature-distance bound of part (a) gives
\lVert f_{j}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}\leq L_{k}^{\prime}\,|\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}|
for every
j
, and the exact operator identity

\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma_{1})\otimes\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma_{1})-\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma_{2})\otimes\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma_{2})=f_{j}\otimes\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma_{1})+\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma_{2})\otimes f_{j}

(check by expanding the right side) together with the triangle inequality for the Hilbert–Schmidt norm gives

\lVert\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{1}}-\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{2}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j}\bigl(\lVert f_{j}\rVert\,\lVert\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma_{1})\rVert+\lVert\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma_{2})\rVert\,\lVert f_{j}\rVert\bigr)\leq 2L_{k}^{\prime}\,T,

using
\lVert\tilde{u}_{j}(\gamma)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}=1
. Hence

\lVert\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{1}}-\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{2}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq L_{C}\,T

with the
deterministic
constant
L_{C}:=2L_{k}^{\prime}
: no stochastic input enters the Lipschitz constant.
For the uniform operator convergence, cover
[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
by a grid of mesh
\delta
. At each grid point the two operators act on the same space
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{j}}
, and
\lVert C_{n,\gamma_{j}}-C_{\gamma_{j}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lVert C_{n,\gamma_{j}}-C_{\gamma_{j}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}=o_{p}(1)
(Lemma
26
; the operator norm is dominated by the Hilbert–Schmidt norm). Between grid points, the Lipschitz bound just established applies verbatim to the population operators, by the pointwise kernel Lipschitz bound:
\lVert C_{\gamma_{a}}-C_{\gamma_{b}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lVert C_{\gamma_{a}}-C_{\gamma_{b}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq L_{C}^{\circ}|\gamma_{a}-\gamma_{b}|
with the deterministic population constant
L_{C}^{\circ}:=C(\gamma_{L})
, since

\lVert C_{\gamma_{a}}-C_{\gamma_{b}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(k_{\gamma_{a}}(X,X^{\prime})-k_{\gamma_{b}}(X,X^{\prime})\bigr)^{2}\bigr]\leq C(\gamma_{L})^{2}|\gamma_{a}-\gamma_{b}|^{2}

by the same pointwise bound. Hence, for
r_{n}:=\sup_{\gamma}\lVert C_{n,\gamma}-C_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}

r_{n}\leq\max_{j}\lVert C_{n,\gamma_{j}}-C_{\gamma_{j}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}+(L_{C}+L_{C}^{\circ})\delta=o_{p}(1)+O_{p}(1)\delta,

and since
\limsup_{n\to\infty}r_{n}\leq(L_{C}+L_{C}^{\circ})\delta
for every
\delta>0
, sending
\delta\to 0
after
n\to\infty
yields
r_{n}=o_{p}(1)
.

(c) Resolvent.
Transport to the common space:
\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta)=(\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}
, where
\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma}=J_{\gamma}C_{n,\gamma}J_{\gamma}^{-1}
is self-adjoint and positive semidefinite on
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
(unitary conjugation, Lemma
4
), so
\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma}+\lambda I\geq\lambda I
and
\lVert\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lambda^{-1}\leq\lambda_{L}^{-1}
. For
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
, set
A_{j}:=\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{j}}+\lambda_{j}I
; the resolvent identity
A_{1}^{-1}-A_{2}^{-1}=A_{1}^{-1}(A_{2}-A_{1})A_{2}^{-1}
gives

\lVert\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta_{1})-\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta_{2})\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lVert\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta_{1})\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\lVert A_{2}-A_{1}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\lVert\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta_{2})\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lambda_{L}^{-2}\,\lVert(\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{1}}-\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{2}})+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})I\rVert_{\mathrm{op}},

and
\lVert\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{1}}-\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{2}}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lVert\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{1}}-\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma_{2}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq L_{C}|\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}|
by (b), while
\lVert(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})I\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|
, yielding the displayed bound. No spectral gap or eigenfunction tracking enters:
\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta)=f(\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma})
for the smooth map
f(t)=(t+\lambda)^{-1}
, which is operator-Lipschitz.
