\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)\neq\boldsymbol{0}
: by Assumption
7
(iii),
\sigma^{2}\geq\sigma_{\min}^{2}>0
a.s., and the infinite eigenbasis
\{\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)\}
is not contained in the finite-dimensional span of the score directions, so
\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)\not\equiv 0
for all but finitely many
i
. A nontrivial weighted sum of independent
\chi^{2}_{1}
variables has a density on
(0,\infty)
, so
F_{\eta}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
and strictly increasing on
(0,\infty)
; in particular the
(1-\alpha)
-quantile
c_{\infty}(\eta)
is the unique solution of
F_{\eta}(c_{\infty}(\eta))=1-\alpha
.

Quantile continuity.
By Pólya’s theorem, the weak convergence
F_{\eta_{n}}\xrightarrow{w}F_{\eta}
together with the continuity of
F_{\eta}
gives the uniform convergence
\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|F_{\eta_{n}}(t)-F_{\eta}(t)|\to 0
. Since
F_{\eta}
is continuous and strictly increasing, the quantiles converge:
c_{\infty}(\eta_{n})\to c_{\infty}(\eta)
. Hence
\eta\mapsto c_{\infty}(\eta)
is continuous on
\Upsilon
.

Lemma 24
(Uniform conditional bootstrap consistency)

Under the conditions of Theorem
5
, the bootstrap process
\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}
of (
7
) converges conditionally on the testing sample, in probability, to
S_{\infty}
in
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
:

\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}\;\rightsquigarrow\;S_{\infty}\quad\text{in }\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon),\text{ conditionally given }\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{test}},\text{ in probability}.

Proof.
Throughout,
\mathbb{E}^{*}
and
\xrightarrow{d^{*}}
denote the conditional expectation and conditional convergence in distribution given the testing sample
\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{test}}
, over the multiplier weights
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}
(i.i.d.,
\mathbb{E}[V]=0
,
\mathbb{E}[V^{2}]=1
, bounded with
\mathbb{E}[V^{4}]<\infty
, e.g. the Mammen multipliers), and all statements are uniform in
\eta\in\Upsilon
unless otherwise noted. Write

\widetilde{M}_{n}^{*}(\gamma):=n^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}\odot\boldsymbol{V})\big)_{j}k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot)\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}

for the multiplied-then-projected embedding of (
7
), so that
\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}(\eta)=\lVert(C_{n,\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}\widetilde{M}_{n}^{*}(\gamma)\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}
. The proof follows the three-ingredient structure of the proof of Theorem
5
: conditional fidi convergence, conditional asymptotic equicontinuity, and conditional tightness, which together give the conditional weak convergence in
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
. Norms are read on the common space
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
through the isometries
J_{\gamma}
of Lemma
10
, which preserve native norms, so that
\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}(\eta)=\lVert\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta)\widetilde{M}_{n}^{*}(\gamma)\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}
with the transported objects of Lemmas
10
and
11
.

Conditional fidi convergence.
The bootstrap statistic is built on
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}\odot\boldsymbol{V})
; we replace
\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}
by
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}
at the embedding level. By the expansion of the estimated residual in the proof of Lemma
3
,
\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}=\mathbb{G}\boldsymbol{\delta}+\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
with
\boldsymbol{\delta}:=\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}
the second-order remainder, so the multiplied residual-replacement error is
\boldsymbol{r}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\mathbb{G}\boldsymbol{\delta}\odot\boldsymbol{V})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}\odot\boldsymbol{V})
. The second-order part is pathwise small:
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
being a projection and the multipliers bounded by
\bar{V}
, writing
\boldsymbol{u}:=\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}\odot\boldsymbol{V}
,

\displaystyle\Bigl\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bigl(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr)_{j}k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot)\Bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\,\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{u}\leq\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma})}{n}\,\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{V}^{2}\,\lVert\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}\rVert^{2}=O_{p}\bigl(n^{1-4\varrho}\bigr)=o_{p}(1),

uniformly in
\gamma
: the spectral bound uses the entrywise-bounded kernel, the last inequality
\lVert\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}\odot\boldsymbol{V}\rVert^{2}\leq\bar{V}^{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{r}^{(2)}\rVert^{2}
, and the rate is the second-moment version of Lemma
3
under Assumption
5
(iii). The first-order part admits no pointwise annihilation, since
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\operatorname{diag}(\boldsymbol{V})\neq\operatorname{diag}(\boldsymbol{V})\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
, but its conditional second moment vanishes: by
\mathbb{E}^{*}[V_{j}V_{k}]=\delta_{jk}
and the cyclicity of the trace,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{*}

\displaystyle\Bigl\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bigl(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\mathbb{G}\boldsymbol{\delta}\odot\boldsymbol{V})\bigr)_{j}k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot)\Bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\bigr)_{ii}\,(\mathbb{G}\boldsymbol{\delta})_{i}^{2}\leq\bigl(1+O_{p}(1)\bigr)\,\frac{\lVert\mathbb{G}\boldsymbol{\delta}\rVert^{2}}{n}

\displaystyle=O_{p}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}\rVert^{2})=O_{p}\bigl(n^{-2\varrho}\bigr)=o_{p}(1),

the inequality being the entrywise bound
(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}})_{ii}\leq 1+O_{p}(1)
of Lemma
11
(the leverage control (
24
) of that lemma, at
A=\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}
of envelope one), the rates using the raw-score second moments of Assumption
5
(iii),
\lVert\mathbb{G}\boldsymbol{\delta}\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}\rVert^{2}\sum_{j}\sup_{\theta}\lVert\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon(S_{j};\theta)\rVert^{2}=n\,O_{p}(1)\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}\rVert^{2}
by the law of large numbers under
\mathbb{E}\bigl[\sup_{\theta}\lVert\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon\rVert^{2}\bigr]<\infty
(implied by the fourth-moment clause of E(iii)), valid in both regimes, and the estimator rate
\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}\rVert=O_{p}(n^{-\varrho})
of Assumption
4
(iii); Markov’s inequality then makes the first-order part
o_{p^{*}}(1)
. The replacement is therefore negligible at the embedding level, and it suffices to consider
\widetilde{M}_{n}^{*}(\gamma)
. Fix
\eta
. The conditional CLT of the proof of Theorem
4
gives
\widetilde{M}_{n}^{*}(\gamma)\xrightarrow{d^{*}}G_{\perp,\gamma}
in
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
, in probability over the data. The derivation has three pieces. The multiplier CLT in the RKHS (Theorem
14
) applied to
U_{j}:=\varepsilon_{0,j}k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot)
gives
\tilde{m}^{*}:=n^{-1/2}\sum_{j}V_{j}U_{j}\xrightarrow{d^{*}}\xi_{\gamma}^{*}
with
\xi_{\gamma}^{*}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\gamma})
. The score-projection term satisfies
(R_{n,\gamma}^{\top}\hat{\mathbb{G}})\hat{w}^{*}\xrightarrow{d^{*}}A_{\gamma}^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}W^{*}
jointly (same
\boldsymbol{V}
). Their difference is the projected Gaussian element
G_{\perp,\gamma}=\xi_{\gamma}^{*}-A_{\gamma}^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}W^{*}
of Corollary
1
. Since
(C_{n,\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}\xrightarrow{p}(C_{\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}
(Lemma
10
(b)) and the squared norm is continuous, the continuous mapping theorem gives
\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}(\eta)\xrightarrow{d^{*}}S_{\infty}(\eta)
conditionally, in probability. The finite-dimensional marginals converge jointly: the same multiplier CLT applied to the direct sum
\bigoplus_{l=1}^{J}\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{l}}
(the product-space argument of the fidi paragraph of the proof of Theorem
5
) gives
(\widetilde{M}_{n}^{*}(\gamma_{1}),\dots,\widetilde{M}_{n}^{*}(\gamma_{J}))\xrightarrow{d^{*}}(G_{\perp,\gamma_{1}},\dots,G_{\perp,\gamma_{J}})
, and the continuous mapping with the block-diagonal resolvents
(C_{n,\gamma_{l}}+\lambda_{l}I)^{-1}
gives
(\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}(\eta_{1}),\dots,\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}(\eta_{J}))\xrightarrow{d^{*}}(S_{\infty}(\eta_{1}),\dots,S_{\infty}(\eta_{J}))
. Under the local alternative at the total-sample rate (Section
4.1
), the projected residual vector splits as
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}+\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{R}/\sqrt{m+n}+\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}
(the local-alternative paragraph of the proof of Theorem
5
), the multiplied version of the estimation remainder being conditionally negligible by the same delta-pairing device,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{*}

\displaystyle\Bigl\lVert n^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bigl(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}(\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}\odot\boldsymbol{V})\bigr)_{j}k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot)\Bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{K}_{\gamma}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\bigr)_{ii}\,(\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}})_{i}^{2}\leq\bigl(1+O_{p}(1)\bigr)\,n^{-1}\lVert\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{est}}\rVert^{2}=o_{p}(1),

the rate again being the second-moment version of Lemma
3
, and Markov’s inequality then making the norm
o_{p^{*}}(1)
. The multiplied shift embedding is

\sqrt{\frac{n}{m+n}}\;\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}V_{j}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{R})_{j}\,k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot),

with conditional mean zero,
\mathbb{E}^{*}[V_{j}]=0
, and conditional second moment, computed from
\mathbb{E}^{*}[V_{j}V_{k}]=\delta_{jk}
and
k_{\gamma}(X_{j},X_{j})=1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{*}

\displaystyle\Bigl\lVert\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{m+n}}\;\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}V_{j}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{R})_{j}\,k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot)\Bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\frac{(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{R})_{j}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{R})_{k}}{m+n}\,\mathbb{E}^{*}[V_{j}V_{k}]\,k_{\gamma}(X_{j},X_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(m+n)}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{R})_{j}^{2}\,k_{\gamma}(X_{j},X_{j})\leq\frac{\lVert\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{R}\rVert^{2}}{n(m+n)}

\displaystyle=O_{p}\bigl((m+n)^{-1}\bigr)=o_{p^{*}}(1),

uniformly in
\gamma
, the inequality using that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}
is a projection and
\boldsymbol{R}
has finite
L^{2}
norm,
\lVert\hat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}\boldsymbol{R}\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert\boldsymbol{R}\rVert^{2}=O_{p}(n)
; Markov’s inequality then makes the norm
o_{p^{*}}(1)
uniformly in
\gamma
. The multiplied shift embedding therefore vanishes conditionally, and the same central limit
S_{\infty}
obtains.

Conditional asymptotic equicontinuity.
The three ingredients of Corollary
3
transfer to the conditional setup. (R) The transported resolvent
\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta):=(\widetilde{C}_{n,\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}
is data-measurable, hence deterministic conditional on the data, and satisfies
\lVert\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta_{1})-\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta_{2})\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lambda_{L}^{-2}(L_{C}|\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}|+|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|)
and
\lVert\widetilde{A}_{n}(\eta)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\lambda_{L}^{-1}
pointwise (Lemma
10
(c)). (E) and (B) hold for the multiplied embedding by Lemma
11
: there exists
L_{M}^{*}=O_{p}(1)
with
\lVert\widetilde{M}_{n}^{*}(\gamma_{1})-\widetilde{M}_{n}^{*}(\gamma_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{0}}}\leq L_{M}^{*}|\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}|
for all
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in[\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}]
, and
\overline{M}_{n}^{*}=O_{p}(1)
, both conditionally on the data in
\mathbb{P}^{*}
-probability; the leverage control (
24
) that replaces the transfer from Lemma
10
(a) is carried out once in that lemma. The chain of the proof of Corollary
3
, which combines (R) with the reverse triangle inequality and the telescope and involves no distributional input, then gives

|\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}(\eta_{1})-\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}(\eta_{2})|\leq L_{n}^{*}\,\lVert\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}\rVert,\qquad L_{n}^{*}=O_{p}(1),

and the covering argument yields conditional asymptotic equicontinuity:

\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\limsup_{n}\mathbb{P}^{*}\bigl(\sup_{\lVert\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}\rVert\leq\delta}|\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}(\eta_{1})-\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}(\eta_{2})|>\varepsilon\bigr)=0

for every
\varepsilon>0
, where the outer
o_{p^{*}}(1)
is uniform over the net of a fixed mesh and the limit
\delta\downarrow 0
is taken after
n\to\infty
.

Conditional tightness.
The norm bound from the preamble,
\sup_{\Upsilon}|\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}(\eta)|\leq\lambda_{L}^{-2}\overline{M}_{n}^{*2}
, together with
\overline{M}_{n}^{*}=O_{p}(1)
from Lemma
11
, yields
\sup_{\Upsilon}|\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}|=O_{p}(1)
conditionally.

The conditional fidi convergence, conditional asymptotic equicontinuity, and conditional tightness give the conditional weak convergence
\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}\rightsquigarrow S_{\infty}
in
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
, in probability over the data (the conditional version of Theorem 1.5.4 of
van der Vaart and Wellner [1996]
). Since
\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp}\in C(\Upsilon)
pathwise by the Lipschitz bound above, the bounded-continuous clause of that theorem, read conditionally, gives

\mathbb{E}^{*}\bigl[\psi(\tilde{S}_{n}^{\perp})\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\psi(S_{\infty})\bigr]\xrightarrow{p}0\qquad\text{for every bounded continuous }\psi:C(\Upsilon)\to\mathbb{R}.

B.3.10
Proof of Theorem
9
(The Fixed-Alternative Selector)

Proof of Theorem
9
.

Write
f_{m}(\gamma):=\mathrm{CV}_{m}((\gamma,\lambda_{L}))
for the criterion along the
bottom edge,
f:=\bar{\sigma}^{2}+A
, continuous on
\Upsilon
, for its
deterministic counterpart, and
A(\gamma):=A((\gamma,\lambda_{L}))
for the
restriction of
A
to the bottom edge. By
Theorem
6
(b),

\sup_{\eta\in\Upsilon}\lvert\mathrm{CV}_{m}(\eta)-f(\eta)\rvert=O_{p}(m^{-1/2})
; since

\bar{\sigma}^{2}
does not depend on
\eta
, the deterministic argmin sets coincide,

\arg\min_{\Upsilon}f=\arg\min_{\Upsilon}A
, and since
O_{p}(m^{-1/2})=o_{p}(1)
the
approximation is uniform at a vanishing rate, so the identification arguments may be
run on either process with the same argmin set.

Reduction to the bottom edge.

The spectral form of the approximation error is

A(\eta)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\lambda^{2}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)_{\ell}(\gamma)^{2}/(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}
, where
e_{\eta}:=\boldsymbol{\Pi}f-w^{*}_{\eta}

denotes the approximation-error function of Section
4.2
: the
spectral form of the population coefficient function,

(w^{*}_{\eta})_{\ell}=\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)_{\ell}/(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)
, gives the coefficient identity

(e_{\eta})_{\ell}=(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)_{\ell}-(w^{*}_{\eta})_{\ell}=\Bigl(1-\frac{\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)}{\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda}\Bigr)(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)_{\ell}=\frac{\lambda}{\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda}\,(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)_{\ell},

and squaring, summing over
\ell
, and applying Parseval’s identity for the
orthonormal eigenbasis of
L_{\gamma}
, complete in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
because the
spectral theorem for the compact self-adjoint operator
L_{\gamma}
leaves only the kernel,
which integral strict positive definiteness (Assumption
7
(iv)) makes trivial,

\langle\phi,L_{\gamma}\phi\rangle_{L^{2}}>0
for every
\phi\neq 0
, reproduces the spectral
form of
A=\lVert e_{\eta}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
. Each summand

\lambda^{2}/(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}
is a rational function of
\lambda
,
and its derivative is computed in two steps,

\partial_{\lambda}\Bigl[\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}}\Bigr]=\frac{2\lambda}{(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}}-\frac{2\lambda^{2}}{(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{3}}=\frac{2\lambda\,\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)}{(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{3}}:

the first equality applies the product rule to

\lambda^{2}(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{-2}
, and the second collects the two
fractions over their common denominator. Termwise differentiation under the sum is
licensed by the Weierstrass M-test: for
\lambda\in[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
and

\mu_{\ell}\geq 0
,
\mu_{\ell}/(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{3}\leq 1/\lambda^{2}

because
\mu_{\ell}\leq\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda
and

\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda\geq\lambda
, so the
\ell
th derivative summand is
bounded by
(2/\lambda_{L})(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)_{\ell}(\gamma)^{2}
, and

\sum_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)_{\ell}(\gamma)^{2}=\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}<\infty
by Parseval; since the original series converges at

\lambda=\lambda_{L}
, uniform convergence of the differentiated series follows, and

\partial_{\lambda}A
is obtained by termwise differentiation, the standard theorem
for series of continuously differentiable functions. Hence

\partial_{\lambda}A(\eta)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{2\lambda\,\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)}{(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{3}}\,(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)_{\ell}(\gamma)^{2}\;>\;0\qquad\text{for }\lambda\geq\lambda_{L}>0.

The sum is strictly positive: no eigenvalue
\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)
vanishes, by integral
strict positive definiteness (Assumption
7
(iv)), every summand is
nonnegative, and at least one coefficient
(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)_{\ell}(\gamma)
is
nonzero since
\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\neq 0
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
. By the mean value
theorem,
A(\gamma,\cdot)
is strictly increasing on
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]

for every fixed
\gamma
, so every minimizer of
A
over
\Upsilon
has

\lambda=\lambda_{L}
. Minimizing over
\Upsilon
therefore
reduces to minimizing
\gamma\mapsto A(\gamma,\lambda_{L})
, whose unique minimizer is

\gamma^{*}
by hypothesis, and any minimizer
(\gamma,\lambda_{L})
of
A
satisfies

A(\gamma,\lambda_{L})=A(\gamma^{*},\lambda_{L})
, hence
\gamma=\gamma^{*}
by that
uniqueness. Hence
\arg\min_{\Upsilon}A=\{(\gamma^{*},\lambda_{L})\}
.

Consistency.

Identification of
\arg\min_{\Upsilon}f=\arg\min_{\Upsilon}A=\{(\gamma^{*},\lambda_{L})\}

together with the uniform approximation

\lVert\mathrm{CV}_{m}-f\rVert_{\infty}=\sup_{\Upsilon}\lvert\mathrm{CV}_{m}-f\rvert=O_{p}(m^{-1/2})=o_{p}(1)
by Theorem
6
(b) puts part (i) of
Lemma
22
in force at
x_{m}=\hat{\eta}_{m}
with
S=\Upsilon
: the
minimizer identity
\mathrm{CV}_{m}(\hat{\eta}_{m})=\inf_{\Upsilon}\mathrm{CV}_{m}
holds
almost surely by definition, and the argmin set of the limit is

\{(\gamma^{*},\lambda_{L})\}
, whence

\operatorname{dist}\bigl(\hat{\eta}_{m},(\gamma^{*},\lambda_{L})\bigr)\xrightarrow{p}0
,
which is the theorem’s display; part (i) presupposes no interiority of the
minimizer, so the boundary case
\gamma^{*}\in\{\gamma_{L},\gamma_{U}\}
is
admissible. This completes the proof of Theorem
9
.

B.3.11
Proof of Theorem
10
(Consistency under Fixed Alternatives)

We prove
\mathbb{P}(S_{n}^{\perp}(\hat{\eta}_{m})>t)\to 1
for every fixed
t>0
whenever

\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\neq 0
, and limiting rejection probability
\alpha
otherwise. Work on the
testing sample: the orthogonalized residuals satisfy

\varepsilon_{\perp,j}=\boldsymbol{\Pi}f(X_{j})+v_{j}+o_{p}(1)
uniformly in
j
, the
o_{p}(1)
being the
estimation-effect remainder (Lemma
3
); the remainder contributes

o_{p}(1)
to
n^{-1}S_{n}^{\perp}(\eta)
uniformly in
\eta
(its
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
-norm contribution is

o_{p}(1)
after the
n^{-1}
scaling), and is ignored. Split the empirical embedding into signal and noise,

\hat{m}_{\perp}=\hat{m}_{\rm sig}+\hat{m}_{\rm noise}
, with

\hat{m}_{\rm sig}(\eta):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j}\boldsymbol{\Pi}f(X_{j})\,k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot),\qquad\hat{m}_{\rm noise}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j}v_{j}\,k_{\gamma}(X_{j},\cdot),

so that
S_{n}^{\perp}(\eta)=\lVert(C_{n,\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}(\hat{m}_{\rm sig}+\hat{m}_{\rm noise})\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}
.

Uniform signal consistency.
With
w^{*}_{\eta}=P_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)
and

P_{\eta}=C_{\gamma}(C_{\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}
,

\sup_{\eta\in\Upsilon}\bigl\lVert(C_{n,\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}\hat{m}_{\rm sig}(\eta)-w^{*}_{\eta}\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

Indeed, adding and subtracting
(C_{\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}C_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\Pi}f=w^{*}_{\eta}
: the
resolvent-perturbation term is bounded by
\lambda_{L}^{-2}\,r_{n}\,\sup_{\eta}\|\hat{m}_{\rm sig}(\eta)\|

with
r_{n}=\sup_{\gamma}\lVert C_{n,\gamma}-C_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
(the fixed-
\gamma
bound of Lemma
26
upgraded over the compact
interval by the grid–oscillation device of the proof of that lemma) and
\sup_{\eta}\|\hat{m}_{\rm sig}(\eta)\|\leq\bar{k}^{1/2}\,n^{-1}\sum_{j}|\boldsymbol{\Pi}f(X_{j})|=O_{p}(1)
(the testing sample has size
n
); while

\sup_{\eta}\|(C_{\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}(\hat{m}_{\rm sig}-C_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)\|\leq\lambda_{L}^{-1}\sup_{\eta}\|\hat{m}_{\rm sig}-C_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\|=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
by the
uniform law of large numbers, the envelope being
\bar{k}^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\|_{L^{2}}
(only

\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\in L^{2}
with
\mathbb{E}|(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)(X)|<\infty
is needed, not boundedness;
when additionally
\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\|_{\infty}<\infty
, as assumed for part (b), the same rate
holds in the bounded case).

Negligibility of the noise contributions.
By the law of large numbers,

\sup_{\eta}\|\hat{m}_{\rm noise}\|\leq\bar{k}^{1/2}\bigl(n^{-1}\sum_{j}v_{j}^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}=O_{p}(1)
, whence

n^{-1}\lVert(C_{n,\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}\hat{m}_{\rm noise}\rVert^{2}\leq n^{-1}\lambda_{L}^{-2}O_{p}(1)=O_{p}(n^{-1})\to 0
and

n^{-1}\,2\,|\langle(C_{n,\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}\hat{m}_{\rm sig},(C_{n,\gamma}+\lambda I)^{-1}\hat{m}_{\rm noise}\rangle|\leq 2n^{-1}\lambda_{L}^{-2}O_{p}(1)O_{p}(1)=o_{p}(1)
, both uniformly in
\eta
. Collecting,

n^{-1}S_{n}^{\perp}(\eta)=N(\eta)+o_{p}(1)\quad\text{uniformly in }\eta,\qquad N(\eta):=\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}.

Positivity and continuity of the limit.
In spectral form

N(\eta)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)\delta_{\ell}(\gamma)^{2}/(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}

with
\delta_{\ell}(\gamma)=\langle\boldsymbol{\Pi}f,\phi_{\ell}(\cdot;\gamma)\rangle
: each summand
is nonnegative and
N(\eta)=0
would force
\delta_{\ell}=0
for every
\ell
, i.e.

\boldsymbol{\Pi}f=0
, excluded by hypothesis; and
\eta\mapsto N(\eta)
is continuous on the compact

\Upsilon
(Assumption
7
(ii)). Hence

N_{\min}:=\min_{\eta\in\Upsilon}N(\eta)>0
.

Conclusion.
Since
\hat{\eta}_{m}\in\Upsilon
always,

n^{-1}S_{n}^{\perp}(\hat{\eta}_{m})\;\geq\;N_{\min}+o_{p}(1)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathbb{P}\bigl(S_{n}^{\perp}(\hat{\eta}_{m})>t\bigr)\to 1

for every fixed
t>0
(indeed for
t=o(n)
). If instead
\boldsymbol{\Pi}f=0
, the projection
annihilates the signal, the statistic obeys the projected null law
S_{n}^{\perp}=O_{p}(1)
of
Corollary
1
, and Theorem
8
applies unchanged and gives limiting rejection probability
\alpha
;
its proof uses only the pointwise null limit, the pointwise bootstrap at the realized value of the
selector, and the independence of the splits.

B.3.12
Proof of Theorem
11
(The Selector under a Local Alternative)

Proof.
Throughout,
F_{m}(\eta):=m[\mathrm{CV}_{m}(\eta)-m^{-1}\sum_{i}v_{i}^{2}]
denotes the centered criterion under the local alternative.
Minimizers of
\mathrm{CV}_{m}
coincide with those of
F_{m}
, the discarded summand
being
\eta
-free: any random minimizer
\hat{\eta}_{m}
of
\mathrm{CV}_{m}
over
\Upsilon
,
that is, any measurable selection from its argmin set, satisfies

F_{m}(\hat{\eta}_{m})=\inf_{\eta\in\Upsilon}F_{m}(\eta)
almost surely, the exact-minimizer
hypothesis of Lemma
22
(ii). The decomposition (
48
)
of the proof of Theorem
6
(c), multiplied by
m
, reads

F_{m}=F_{m}^{({\rm null})}+m\delta_{m}^{2}A_{h}+2m\delta_{m}C_{m}^{0}
, where

F_{m}^{({\rm null})}\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{H}
in
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
(whence

\|F_{m}^{({\rm null})}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}(1)
),
\sqrt{m}\,C_{m}^{0}\rightsquigarrow G

(whence
\|C_{m}^{0}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}(m^{-1/2})
), and
A_{h}
is continuous on the compact
\Upsilon
. The approximation error attached to

C_{m}^{0}
in (
48
) is
o_{p}(m^{-1/2})
uniformly in
\eta
, in particular

O_{p}(m^{-1/2})
, and becomes

o_{p}(\delta_{m}m^{1/2})
after multiplication by
2m\delta_{m}
; it is absorbed by the remaining

o_{p}
terms.

When
\sqrt{m}\,\delta_{m}\to a<\infty
,
m\delta_{m}^{2}\to a^{2}
and

m\delta_{m}C_{m}^{0}=a\sqrt{m}\,O_{p}(m^{-1/2})(1+o(1))=O_{p}(1)
, so

F_{m}\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{H}_{h}^{a}=\mathbb{H}+a^{2}A_{h}+2aG
in
\ell^{\infty}(\Upsilon)
; this is
exactly Theorem
6
(c). The paths of
\mathbb{H}_{h}^{a}
are continuous almost surely:
those of the chaoses by Appendix
B.3.3
;

A_{h}
is continuous by uniform convergence of its Mercer series on compact
\Upsilon
: the partial
sums
\gamma\mapsto\sum_{\ell\leq K}h_{\ell}(\gamma)^{2}
are continuous with constant limit

\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
, so Dini’s theorem gives
\sup_{\gamma}\sum_{\ell>K}h_{\ell}(\gamma)^{2}\to 0
,
while the weights satisfy
\lambda^{4}/(\mu_{\ell}+\lambda)^{4}\leq\lambda_{L}^{-4}
; and the

G
-component by a separate argument: the limiting covariance

\Sigma_{G}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)h_{\eta}^{h}(X)h_{\eta^{\prime}}^{h}(X)]
is

\gamma
-Lipschitz, since
\|h_{\eta}^{h}-h_{\eta^{\prime}}^{h}\|_{L^{2}}\leq L_{h}|\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}|
by the
kernel-level calculus of Lemma
10
(a) applied to

h_{\eta}^{h}=\boldsymbol{\Pi}(a_{\eta}^{h}-b_{\eta}^{h})
(a bounded combination of
g_{\eta}
and its
resolvent transform, with
\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\|\leq 1
); hence the increments of the Gaussian limit
G

satisfy
\mathbb{E}[(G(\eta)-G(\eta^{\prime}))^{2}]\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}L_{h}^{2}|\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}|^{2}
, and a
centered Gaussian process on a compact interval with squared increments of order
O(|\Delta|^{2})

has a continuous modification (Kolmogorov–Chentsov). Every component is therefore carried by

C(\Upsilon)
, so the weak convergence may be read in
C(\Upsilon)
;
\mathbb{H}_{h}^{a}
attains its
minimum almost surely uniquely by hypothesis, and Part (ii) of Lemma
22
with

S=\Upsilon
yields
\hat{\eta}_{m}\xrightarrow{d}\eta^{*}_{h}:=\arg\min_{\Upsilon}\mathbb{H}_{h}^{a}
. For
a=0
,

\mathbb{H}_{h}^{0}=\mathbb{H}
: the local alternative is invisible to the selector, recovering
Theorem
7
.

This completes the proof of Theorem
11
.

B.3.13
Proof of Proposition
3
(The Energy Identity)

Proof.
Write
w:=w^{*}_{\eta}(h)
. By definition of the population KRR solution in Section
4.6
,
w
satisfies the normal equation

(C_{\gamma}+\lambda I)\,w=\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}h,

the first-order condition of the regularized risk minimization
\min_{w\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}\{\mathbb{E}[(h(X)-w(X))^{2}]+\lambda\lVert w\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}\}
. Taking the
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
-inner product with
w
,

\langle C_{\gamma}w,w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}+\lambda\lVert w\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}=\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}h,w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}.

By the reproducing property and the definition of the embedding
\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}
,

\langle C_{\gamma}w,w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}=\mathbb{E}\big[w(X)\,\langle k_{\gamma}(X,\cdot),w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[w(X)^{2}\big]=\lVert w\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2},

and

\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}h,w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}=\mathbb{E}\big[h(X)\,\langle k_{\gamma}(X,\cdot),w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[h(X)w(X)\big]=\langle h,w\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Hence

\lVert w\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lambda\lVert w\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}=\langle h,w\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Expanding
\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\lVert(h-w)+w\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
gives

\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\lVert h-w\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+2\langle h-w,w\rangle_{L^{2}}+\lVert w\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2},

and the cross term is evaluated by (*):

\langle h-w,w\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle h,w\rangle_{L^{2}}-\lVert w\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\lambda\lVert w\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2},

so that

\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=A_{h}(\eta)+\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+2\lambda\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2},

which is (
8
).

For the spectral forms, expand
w
in the eigenbasis
\{e_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
of
C_{\gamma}
,
e_{i}:=\sqrt{\mu_{i}(\gamma)}\,\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)
: since
\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}h=\sum_{i\geq 1}\sqrt{\mu_{i}(\gamma)}\,h_{i}(\gamma)\,e_{i}
and
C_{\gamma}e_{i}=\mu_{i}(\gamma)e_{i}
, the normal equation gives

w=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{i}(\gamma)}\,h_{i}(\gamma)}{\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda}\,e_{i}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{i}(\gamma)\,h_{i}(\gamma)}{\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda}\,\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma).

Substituting this expansion into the three components of (
8
) and using the orthonormality of
\{\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)\}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
and of
\{e_{i}\}
in
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
yields the displayed spectral forms. The per-direction budget holds because the identity holds for every
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})
: specializing to
h
supported on a single direction (e.g.
h=\phi_{j}(\cdot;\gamma)
, for which
h_{i}=\delta_{ij}
) gives

\left(\frac{\lambda}{\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda}\right)^{\!2}+\left(\frac{\mu_{i}(\gamma)}{\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda}\right)^{\!2}+\frac{2\lambda\mu_{i}(\gamma)}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}}=1.

The monotonicity claims follow by differentiating the spectral forms (the interchange of differentiation and summation is justified by dominated convergence on
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
,
\lambda_{L}>0
, e.g.
|\partial_{\lambda}[\mu_{i}^{2}h_{i}^{2}/(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{2}]|\leq 2\mu_{i}^{2}h_{i}^{2}\lambda_{L}^{-3}
with
\sum_{i}\mu_{i}h_{i}^{2}\leq\bar{k}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}<\infty
):

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}A_{h}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{2\lambda\,h_{i}(\gamma)^{2}\,\mu_{i}(\gamma)}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{3}}>0,

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{2\mu_{i}(\gamma)^{2}\,h_{i}(\gamma)^{2}}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{3}}<0,

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}\big\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(h)\big\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{2\mu_{i}(\gamma)\,h_{i}(\gamma)^{2}}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{3}}<0,

where the strictness uses
h\neq 0
(so
\sum_{i\geq 1}h_{i}(\gamma)^{2}=\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}>0
by Parseval) and
\mu_{i}(\gamma)>0
for all
i
(Gaussian kernel, Assumption
2
). Finally, since
E_{h}(\eta)=\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-A_{h}(\eta)
and
\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
is independent of
\eta
, minimizing
A_{h}
is equivalent to maximizing
E_{h}
over any set of configurations.

B.3.14
Proof of Proposition
4
(Monotone Power and Divergence Rate)

Proof.
Fix
\eta=(\gamma,\lambda)\in\Upsilon
. Throughout,
S_{\infty}:=S_{\infty}(\eta)
,
c:=c_{\infty}(\eta)
,
h_{i}:=h_{i}(\gamma)
,
Z:=Z^{\perp}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma))
, and
\eta
-arguments are suppressed where no confusion can arise. Let
w_{i}(\eta)
denote the weight sequence of either projection statistic,
w_{i}(\eta)=\mu_{i}(\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}
for
\hat{T}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
and
w_{i}(\eta)=\mu_{i}(\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)
for
\hat{T}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
, and write
W:=W(\eta)=\operatorname{diag}(w_{i}(\eta))
. The argument below uses only that
w_{i}(\eta)>0
for every
i
and that
\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)<\infty
, and both properties hold in each case: positivity holds by Assumption
7
(iv) and
\lambda\geq\lambda_{L}
, and summability because
w_{i}(\eta)\leq\mu_{i}(\gamma)/\lambda^{2}\leq\lambda_{L}^{-2}\mu_{i}(\gamma)
in the first case and
w_{i}(\eta)\leq\mu_{i}(\gamma)/\lambda\leq\lambda_{L}^{-1}\mu_{i}(\gamma)
in the second, while
\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}(\gamma)=\operatorname{tr}\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}=\mathbb{E}[k_{\gamma}(X,X)]=1
, the trace of a positive trace-class integral operator with continuous kernel being the integral of its kernel diagonal and
k_{\gamma}(x,x)=1
for the Gaussian kernel. In particular
\lVert W\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=\sup_{i}w_{i}(\eta)<\infty
. The covariance
\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)
is a bounded positive self-adjoint operator on
\ell^{2}
: with
f_{c}:=\sum_{i\geq 1}c_{i}\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)
for
c\in\ell^{2}
, Assumption
7
(iii), Parseval’s identity
\lVert f_{c}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\lVert c\rVert_{\ell^{2}}^{2}
, and the contraction property
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}\rVert_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leq 1
give

c^{\top}\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)c=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\sigma^{2}(X)(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c})(X)^{2}\bigr]\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c})(X)^{2}\bigr]=\sigma_{\max}^{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}\lVert c\rVert_{\ell^{2}}^{2},

the form
q(c):=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\sigma^{2}(X)(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c})(X)^{2}\bigr]
, symmetric and bounded on
\ell^{2}
by the display, is the quadratic form of a unique bounded positive self-adjoint operator (polarization and the Riesz representation), whose matrix in the canonical basis is the entrywise
(\Sigma^{\perp})_{ij}
of Corollary
1
; a self-adjoint operator is bounded with norm equal to the supremum of its quadratic form, so
\lVert\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}
and
\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}
denotes its bounded positive square root. The whitened weights
\varsigma_{k}(\eta)\geq 0
are the eigenvalues of the positive compact operator
\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}W\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}
, compact because
W
is diagonal with
w_{i}(\eta)\to 0
(as
\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)<\infty
) while
\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}
is bounded, and products of bounded and compact operators are compact; and they are summable: the diagonal sum of the positive operator
W^{1/2}\Sigma^{\perp}W^{1/2}
satisfies
(\Sigma^{\perp})_{ii}=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)(\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{i})(X)^{2}]\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{i}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}
, so that
W^{1/2}\Sigma^{\perp}W^{1/2}
has finite diagonal sum and is therefore trace class; the same holds of
\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}W\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}=X^{*}X
with
X:=W^{1/2}\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}
Hilbert–Schmidt,
\lVert X\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)\,(\Sigma^{\perp})_{ii}<\infty
, and
\operatorname{tr}(X^{*}X)=\operatorname{tr}(XX^{*})
; hence

\sum_{k\geq 1}\varsigma_{k}(\eta)=\operatorname{tr}\bigl(\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}W\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}\bigr)=\operatorname{tr}\bigl(W^{1/2}\Sigma^{\perp}W^{1/2}\bigr)=\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)\,(\Sigma^{\perp})_{ii}\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)<\infty,

the first equality being the identity between the trace and the eigenvalue sum for a positive trace-class operator, the second the cyclicity of the trace, and the third the computation of the trace as the diagonal sum in the canonical basis. Moreover
\varsigma_{1}(\eta)>0
. Since
\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}W\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}=(W^{1/2}\Sigma^{\perp 1/2})^{*}(W^{1/2}\Sigma^{\perp 1/2})
, the
C^{*}
-identity gives
\varsigma_{1}(\eta)=\lVert\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}W\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=\lVert W^{1/2}\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}
, the operator norm of a positive compact operator being its largest eigenvalue. If
W^{1/2}\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}=0
, then applying the operator to
e_{i}
gives
\sqrt{w_{i}(\eta)}\,\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}e_{i}=0
for every
i
, hence
\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}=0
because
w_{i}(\eta)>0
, hence
\Sigma^{\perp}=0
; the latter is excluded because, by Assumption
7
(iii),
\sigma^{2}\geq\sigma_{\min}^{2}>0
a.s., while the infinite eigenbasis
\{\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)\}
is not contained in the finite-dimensional span of the score directions, so
\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)\not\equiv 0
for some
i
and
(\Sigma^{\perp})_{ii}\geq\sigma_{\min}^{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{i}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}>0
(the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
23
).

Shifted quadratic form.

By the display of Theorem
5
(for
\hat{T}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
; the corresponding limit for
\hat{T}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
is the same display with the weights
w_{i}^{(2)}
, cf. Corollaries
1
and
2
), the limit of
S_{n}^{\perp}(\eta)
under the local
alternative with the scaled shift
th
is the shifted quadratic form

S_{\infty}(\eta;th)=\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)\bigl(Z_{i}^{\perp}+t\,h_{i}(\gamma)\bigr)^{2}=(Z+t\,h)^{\top}W(Z+t\,h),

where
h:=(h_{i}(\gamma))_{i}
denotes the coefficient vector of the projected signal
h=\boldsymbol{\Pi}R

in the eigenbasis of
C_{\gamma}
, an element of
\ell^{2}
with

\lVert h\rVert_{\ell^{2}}=\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})}
by Parseval. Whiten the noise by writing

Z=\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}U
with
U\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},I)
on
\ell^{2}
, the standard scaling
representation of a centered Gaussian vector with covariance
\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)
; since

\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)
is generically singular, all inverses of the square root are understood in the
Moore–Penrose sense on the support of the covariance.

Support inclusion.

The scaling representation is available only if the shift lies in the range of
\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}
,
which holds for every projected signal. Let
T:L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})\to\ell^{2}
be the analysis operator of
the projected eigenbasis,
(Tf)_{i}:=\langle f,\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X})}
; then

\lVert Tf\rVert_{\ell^{2}}^{2}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\langle\boldsymbol{\Pi}f,\phi_{i}\rangle^{2}=\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
by Parseval, so
T
is bounded with
\lVert T\rVert\leq 1
, and
its Gram operator satisfies
TT^{*}=M_{0}
, where

M_{0}:=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\phi}\,\boldsymbol{\phi}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Pi}]
with

\boldsymbol{\phi}:=(\phi_{i}(\cdot;\gamma))_{i}
is the Gram matrix of the projected basis: for

c\in\ell^{2}
, with
f_{c}:=\sum_{i\geq 1}c_{i}\phi_{i}
, the defining property of the adjoint gives

T^{*}c=\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c}
, and hence

(TT^{*}c)_{i}=\bigl\langle\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c},\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{i}\bigr\rangle=\sum_{j\geq 1}c_{j}\bigl\langle\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{j},\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{i}\bigr\rangle=(M_{0}c)_{i}.

By Assumption
7
(iii), for every
c\in\ell^{2}
,

c^{\top}\Sigma^{\perp}(\gamma)c=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\sigma^{2}(X)(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c})(X)^{2}\bigr]\geq\sigma_{\min}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c})(X)^{2}\bigr]=\sigma_{\min}^{2}\,c^{\top}M_{0}c,

the last identity holding because the display above shows that
M_{0}c
is the coefficient vector of

\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c}
, its
i
th entry being
\langle\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c},\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{i}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c},\phi_{i}\rangle
, so that
c^{\top}M_{0}c=\langle\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c},f_{c}\rangle=\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}f_{c}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
. In operator form this is the non-vacuous domination

TT^{*}=M_{0}\leq\sigma_{\min}^{-2}\,\Sigma^{\perp}=\bigl(\sigma_{\min}^{-1}\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}\bigr)\bigl(\sigma_{\min}^{-1}\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}\bigr)^{*}.

Douglas’ range-inclusion lemma, which states that for bounded operators
A,B
on a Hilbert space one
has
\operatorname{ran}A\subseteq\operatorname{ran}B
if and only if
AA^{*}\leq\kappa^{2}BB^{*}
for some

\kappa>0
, applied with
A:=T
and
B:=\sigma_{\min}^{-1}\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}
yields

\operatorname{ran}T\subseteq\operatorname{ran}\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}
. The coefficient vector of a
projected signal is such a range point,
h=TR
, since
h_{i}=\langle\boldsymbol{\Pi}R,\phi_{i}\rangle=\langle R,\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{i}\rangle=(TR)_{i}
, the projection
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
being self-adjoint.
Consequently
h\in\operatorname{ran}\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}
with
h^{\top}\Sigma^{\perp+}h<\infty
, and the
whitened shift
v:=\Sigma^{\perp-1/2}h
is well defined, with
v\in\ell^{2}
and
\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}v=h
.

Whitened diagonal form.

The shift passes through the square root,

Z+t\,h=\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}U+t\,\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}v=\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}(U+t\,v),

and substituting into the shifted quadratic form gives

S_{\infty}(\eta;th)=\bigl(U+t\,v\bigr)^{\top}\,\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}W\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}\,\bigl(U+t\,v\bigr).

The operator
\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}W\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}
is positive semidefinite,

g^{\top}\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}W\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}g=\lVert W^{1/2}\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}g\rVert^{2}\geq 0
for every

g\in\ell^{2}
, and compact, so by the spectral theorem for compact self-adjoint operators its
eigenvalues
\varsigma_{k}(\eta)\geq 0
admit an orthonormal eigenbasis; write

\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}W\Sigma^{\perp 1/2}=\mathcal{Q}(\eta)\operatorname{diag}(\varsigma_{k}(\eta))\mathcal{Q}(\eta)^{\top}

with
\mathcal{Q}(\eta)
orthogonal. Rotating the noise,
\tilde{U}:=\mathcal{Q}(\eta)^{\top}U\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},I)
by orthogonal invariance of the standard Gaussian, and setting

\vartheta(\eta;h):=\mathcal{Q}(\eta)^{\top}\Sigma^{\perp-1/2}h
, so that
\vartheta(\eta;th)=t\,\vartheta(\eta;h)
by linearity, the statistic becomes, after relabeling
\tilde{U}
as
U
,

S_{\infty}(\eta;th)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\varsigma_{k}(\eta)\,\bigl(U_{k}+t\,\vartheta_{k}(\eta;h)\bigr)^{2}:

the diagonal expansion of the quadratic form in the eigenbasis. The series are under control: since

\vartheta(\eta;h)=\mathcal{Q}(\eta)^{\top}v
with
v\in\ell^{2}
,

\sum_{k\geq 1}\varsigma_{k}(\eta)\,\vartheta_{k}(\eta;h)^{2}\leq\lVert\varsigma(\eta)\rVert_{\ell^{1}}\,\lVert\vartheta(\eta;h)\rVert_{\ell^{\infty}}^{2}<\infty,

and by Tonelli’s theorem and
\mathbb{E}[U_{k}]=0
,
\mathbb{E}[U_{k}^{2}]=1
,

\sum_{k\geq 1}\varsigma_{k}(\eta)\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[(U_{k}+t\,\vartheta_{k}(\eta;h))^{2}\bigr]=\sum_{k\geq 1}\varsigma_{k}(\eta)+t^{2}\sum_{k\geq 1}\varsigma_{k}(\eta)\,\vartheta_{k}(\eta;h)^{2}<\infty,

so the series of nonnegative summands converges almost surely at each fixed
t
.

Scale monotonicity.

For a standard normal
U
and real shifts with
|a|\leq|b|
,
(U+b)^{2}
stochastically dominates

(U+a)^{2}
. The law of
(U+a)^{2}
depends only on
|a|
, since replacing
U
by
-U
gives

\mathbb{P}((U+a)^{2}\leq r)=\mathbb{P}((U-a)^{2}\leq r)
for every
r\geq 0
; assume
0\leq a\leq b
and put

\ell:=\sqrt{r}
, so that
(U+b)^{2}\leq r
is the event
U\in[-b-\ell,\,-b+\ell]
. With
\varphi
the
standard normal density,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl((U+b)^{2}\leq r\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{-b-\ell}^{-b+\ell}\varphi(x)\,dx=\int_{b-\ell}^{b+\ell}\varphi(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{a-\ell}^{a+\ell}\varphi(x)\,dx+\int_{a+\ell}^{b+\ell}\bigl[\varphi(x)-\varphi(x-2\ell)\bigr]\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\int_{a-\ell}^{a+\ell}\varphi(x)\,dx=\mathbb{P}\bigl((U+a)^{2}\leq r\bigr),

the second equality uses that
\varphi
is even, the third combines additivity over intervals with the
substitution
x\mapsto x-2\ell
in
\int_{a-\ell}^{b-\ell}\varphi(x)\,dx
, and the inequality rests on
the pointwise domination
\varphi(x)\leq\varphi(x-2\ell)
on
[a+\ell,b+\ell]
:
\varphi
is even and
non-increasing on
[0,\infty)
, while
x\geq a+\ell\geq\ell\geq 0
and
|x-2\ell|\leq x
there (if

x\geq 2\ell
then
|x-2\ell|=x-2\ell\leq x
, and if
x<2\ell
then

|x-2\ell|=2\ell-x\leq\ell-a\leq a+\ell\leq x
).
For
0\leq s\leq t
the shifts satisfy
|s\,\vartheta_{k}(\eta;h)|\leq|t\,\vartheta_{k}(\eta;h)|
for every

k
, so with
F_{s,k},F_{t,k}
the distribution functions of
(U+s\,\vartheta_{k}(\eta;h))^{2}
and

(U+t\,\vartheta_{k}(\eta;h))^{2}
the display gives
F_{s,k}\geq F_{t,k}
pointwise. To pass from the
coordinates to the sum, couple the two scales over common randomness: pointwise ordered distribution
functions have pointwise ordered generalized inverses,
F^{-1}_{t,k}\geq F^{-1}_{s,k}
on
(0,1)
, so
for independent uniforms
V_{k}
the variables
X_{k}:=F^{-1}_{s,k}(V_{k})
and
Y_{k}:=F^{-1}_{t,k}(V_{k})

have the laws of the
s
- and
t
-summands with
X_{k}\leq Y_{k}
almost surely, simultaneously over
k
;
the coordinates are independent, so the coupled vectors have the correct joint laws. The partial sums
satisfy
\sum_{k\leq J}\varsigma_{k}(\eta)X_{k}\leq\sum_{k\leq J}\varsigma_{k}(\eta)Y_{k}
almost surely
(
\varsigma_{k}(\eta)\geq 0
), and both series converge almost surely as
J\to\infty
by the bookkeeping
display, so the ordering passes to the limits:
S_{\infty}(\eta;s)\leq S_{\infty}(\eta;t)
almost surely
under the coupling, i.e.
S_{\infty}(\eta;th)
is stochastically non-decreasing in
t\geq 0
. The critical
value
c
does not depend on
t
, and therefore
t\mapsto p(\eta;th)=\mathbb{P}(S_{\infty}(\eta;th)>c)

is non-decreasing on
[0,\infty)
.

Unbiasedness.

At
t=0
the statistic is null:
S_{\infty}(\eta;0)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\varsigma_{k}(\eta)U_{k}^{2}
is a nontrivial
weighted sum of independent
\chi^{2}_{1}
variables, since
\varsigma_{1}(\eta)>0
, and as such has a
distribution function that is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
and strictly increasing on
(0,\infty)
(the
same convolution argument as in the proof of Lemma
23
); the critical value
c
is
therefore the exact solution of
F_{\eta}(c)=1-\alpha
, and
p(\eta;0)=\mathbb{P}(S_{\infty}(\eta)>c)=\alpha
.
Scale monotonicity with
t=1
gives
p(\eta;h)=p(\eta;1\cdot h)\geq p(\eta;0)=\alpha
, which proves
item (i).

Item (ii) records the divergence rate under a fixed alternative: at each fixed
\eta
,
S_{n}^{\perp}(\eta)/n=\lVert\hat{w}_{\eta}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}\xrightarrow{p}\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}
(see Appendix
B.3.11
), and by the spectral form of the population KRR solution,

\bigl\lVert w^{*}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}}^{2}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{i}(\gamma)\,\delta_{i}(\gamma)^{2}}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}}=\mathcal{N}(\eta;\boldsymbol{\Pi}f):

the divergence rate of the test is the noncentrality, the RKHS component of the captured energy.

Combining the two items completes the proof of Proposition
4
.

B.3.15
Proof of Theorem
12
(CV Selection Boosts the Signal-to-Noise Ratio)

Proof.

(i) Signal channel.

By Theorem
9
,
\hat{\eta}_{m}\xrightarrow{p}\eta^{*}=(\gamma^{*},\lambda_{L})
. By Proposition
3
,
E_{\boldsymbol{\Pi}f}(\eta)=\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-A(\eta)
with
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
independent of
\eta
, so the minimizers of
A
are exactly the maximizers of
E_{\boldsymbol{\Pi}f}
:
A(\eta^{*})=\min_{\Upsilon}A
and
E_{\boldsymbol{\Pi}f}(\eta^{*})=\max_{\Upsilon}E_{\boldsymbol{\Pi}f}
. Proposition
3
also gives
\partial_{\lambda}E_{\boldsymbol{\Pi}f}(\eta)=-\partial_{\lambda}A(\eta)<0
for every
\eta
, and from the spectral form of the noncentrality (
10
),

\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{N}(\eta;\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)=-\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{2\mu_{i}(\gamma)\,\delta_{i}(\gamma)^{2}}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{3}}<0

(
\boldsymbol{\Pi}f\neq 0
and all
\mu_{i}(\gamma)>0
): for every
\gamma
,
\lambda=\lambda_{L}
maximizes both
E_{\boldsymbol{\Pi}f}(\cdot,\gamma)
and
\mathcal{N}(\cdot,\gamma;\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)
over
\lambda
. Since the statistic diverges at the rate
S_{n}^{\perp}(\eta)/n\xrightarrow{p}\mathcal{N}(\eta;\boldsymbol{\Pi}f)
(Proposition
4
), the selected configuration carries the maximal divergence rate in the regularization direction, and consistency,
\mathbb{P}(S_{n}^{\perp}(\hat{\eta}_{m})>t)\to 1
for every fixed
t
, is Theorem
10
.

(ii) Noise channel.

Under
H_{0}
, Theorem
6
(a) identifies the deterministic part of the selector’s limit criterion as
V(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)
, the drift of
\mathbb{H}(\eta)=-2\mathcal{C}^{\perp}(\eta)+V(\eta)+\mathcal{D}^{\perp}(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)
. Since
\tau_{i}(\gamma)=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)\phi_{i}(X;\gamma)^{2}]>0
for every
i
(
\sigma^{2}\geq\sigma_{\min}^{2}>0
a.s. by Assumption
7
(iii) and
\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}^{2}]=1
),

\partial_{\lambda}V(\eta)=-\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{2\mu_{i}(\gamma)^{2}\,\tau_{i}(\gamma)}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{3}}<0.

All term-by-term differentiations of this item, in the display above and in the displays below, are licensed by dominated convergence on
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
,
\lambda_{L}>0
: with
w_{i}(\eta)=\mu_{i}(\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}
the weights of the limit statistic and
w_{i}^{\prime}:=\partial_{\lambda}w_{i}=-2\mu_{i}(\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{3}
, the bounds
\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)\leq\lambda_{L}^{-2}
and
\sum_{i\geq 1}|w_{i}^{\prime}|\leq 2\lambda_{L}^{-3}
hold because
w_{i}(\eta)\leq\lambda_{L}^{-2}\mu_{i}(\gamma)
and
|w_{i}^{\prime}|\leq 2\lambda_{L}^{-3}\mu_{i}(\gamma)
while
\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}(\gamma)=\operatorname{tr}\boldsymbol{L}_{k_{\gamma}}=\mathbb{E}[k_{\gamma}(X,X)]=1
(as in the proof of Proposition
4
), and the bound
|\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}(\gamma)|\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}
holds by Cauchy–Schwarz in
L^{2}
,
|\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}(\gamma)|=|\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}(X)(\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{i})(X)(\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{j})(X)]|\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{i}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}\phi_{j}\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}
(the contraction property
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Pi}\rVert_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leq 1
and
\lVert\phi_{i}\rVert_{L^{2}}=1
); the differentiated series below therefore converge absolutely, the undifferentiated ones converge at each point of
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
by the same bounds, and the display above reduces through
2\mu_{i}^{2}/(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{3}=2w_{i}\,\mu_{i}/(\mu_{i}+\lambda)\leq 2w_{i}
and
\tau_{i}(\gamma)\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}
(the upper bound of Assumption
7
(iii)) to a series dominated by
2\sigma_{\max}^{2}\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)
.

The noise floor of the statistic moves with
V
: the null mean
M(\eta)=\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)\tau_{i}^{\perp}(\gamma)
(with
\tau_{i}^{\perp}(\gamma):=\Sigma^{\perp}_{ii}(\gamma)
the diagonal of the projected covariance of Corollary
1
) satisfies

\partial_{\lambda}M(\eta)=\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}^{\prime}\,\tau_{i}^{\perp}(\gamma)=-2\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{i}(\gamma)\,\tau_{i}^{\perp}(\gamma)}{(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{3}}\;<\;0.

The null variance

\sigma_{\infty}(\eta)^{2}=2\sum_{i,j\geq 1}w_{i}(\eta)w_{j}(\eta)\,\big(\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}(\gamma)\big)^{2}

is strictly decreasing in
\lambda
as well. Writing
S_{\infty}=\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}(Z_{i}^{\perp})^{2}
, the Gaussian fourth-moment identity

\mathbb{E}[(Z_{i}^{\perp})^{2}(Z_{j}^{\perp})^{2}]=\tau_{i}^{\perp}(\gamma)\tau_{j}^{\perp}(\gamma)+2(\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}(\gamma))^{2}

gives
\operatorname{Cov}((Z_{i}^{\perp})^{2},(Z_{j}^{\perp})^{2})=2(\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}(\gamma))^{2}
, hence

\operatorname{Var}(S_{\infty})=\sum_{i,j\geq 1}w_{i}w_{j}\,\operatorname{Cov}\bigl((Z_{i}^{\perp})^{2},(Z_{j}^{\perp})^{2}\bigr)=2\sum_{i,j\geq 1}w_{i}w_{j}\,\bigl(\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}(\gamma)\bigr)^{2}.

Differentiating term-by-term, with
w_{i}^{\prime}:=\partial_{\lambda}w_{i}=-2\mu_{i}/(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{3}<0
for every
i
, and using the symmetry of the summand under
(i,j)\mapsto(j,i)
,

\partial_{\lambda}\sigma_{\infty}^{2}=2\sum_{i,j}\bigl(w_{i}^{\prime}w_{j}+w_{i}w_{j}^{\prime}\bigr)\bigl(\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}(\gamma)\bigr)^{2}=4\sum_{i,j}w_{i}^{\prime}w_{j}\bigl(\Sigma^{\perp}_{ij}(\gamma)\bigr)^{2}<0.

Thus the null mean and the null variance of the limit statistic are strictly decreasing in
\lambda
without further assumptions. The argument used only that
w_{i}>0
, that
w_{i}^{\prime}<0
, the
\ell^{1}
bounds of the license above, and the non-degeneracy of
\Sigma^{\perp}
, and all four are shared by the second statistic’s weight sequence
w_{i}^{(2)}(\eta):=\mu_{i}(\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)
:
w_{i}^{(2)}>0
,
(w_{i}^{(2)})^{\prime}=-\mu_{i}(\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}<0
, and
\sum_{i\geq 1}w_{i}^{(2)}\leq\lambda_{L}^{-1}
by the same trace bound, and
\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}(\gamma)/(\mu_{i}(\gamma)+\lambda)^{2}\leq\lambda_{L}^{-2}
for the differentiated series. The conclusions of this item therefore hold for
\hat{T}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
verbatim, with
S_{\infty}^{(2)}
and its null mean and variance in place of their unmarked counterparts. The direction of the selector’s drift is governed by Condition
1
: under that condition,
V(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)
is strictly decreasing in
\lambda
, hence minimized at the largest admissible regularization, in contrast with the signal regime of (i), where the drift
A
is minimized at
\lambda_{L}
. Under homoscedasticity the condition holds automatically, so the drift conclusion is unconditional there; beyond homoscedasticity it holds numerically for the Gaussian kernel and the simulation designs (Appendix
G.2
). Hence the configurations favored by the
H_{0}
drift carry a smaller null mean and a smaller null variance than the configurations favored under a signal: CV closes the spectral filter when there is no signal to capture.

(iii) Local alternatives and the split ratio.

Under the local alternative at the total-sample rate, the residual function in the selection sample is
f_{m}=(m+n)^{-1/2}h
(the Remark following Theorem
11
), so
\sqrt{m}\,\delta_{m}=\sqrt{m/(m+n)}\to\sqrt{c}=:a
with
c:=\lim m/(m+n)\in[0,1]
, and Theorem
6
(c) gives the limit criterion
\mathbb{H}_{h}^{a}=-2\mathcal{C}^{\perp}+V+\mathcal{D}^{\perp}+\Delta+cA_{h}+2aG
: the deterministic part is
D_{c}(\eta):=V(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)+cA_{h}(\eta)
. From (ii), under Condition
1
, and Proposition
3
,
\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)<0
and
\partial_{\lambda}A_{h}>0
:
D_{c}
is the sum of a decreasing and an increasing function of
\lambda
, and at an interior minimizer
\lambda^{*}_{c}
with
\partial_{\lambda}^{2}D_{c}(\lambda^{*}_{c})>0
, the first-order condition
\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda^{*}_{c})+c\,\partial_{\lambda}A_{h}(\lambda^{*}_{c})=0
holds and the implicit function theorem applies, the map
(c,\lambda)\mapsto\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda)+c\,\partial_{\lambda}A_{h}(\lambda)
being continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
(c,\lambda_{c}^{*})
: the drift
V+\Delta
and
A_{h}
are spectral sums of summands real-analytic in
\lambda
, identified by the license of (ii) for
V
, by the Mercer diagonalization of the correction terms,
\Delta(\eta)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\bigl[(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)/(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda))^{2}-2\,\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)/(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)\bigr]\,a_{\ell}(\gamma)
with
a_{\ell}(\gamma):=\mathbb{E}[c^{*}(X,X^{\prime})\phi_{\ell}(X;\gamma)\phi_{\ell}(X^{\prime};\gamma)]
(Appendix
G.2
), for
\Delta
, and by the domination of its second-derivative series below for
A_{h}
; the second
\lambda
-derivative series are dominated uniformly on
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
by
6\sigma_{\max}^{2}\lambda_{L}^{-4}\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)
for
V
, by
2(1+2\lambda_{U})\lambda_{L}^{-4}\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}(\gamma)h_{i}^{2}<\infty
for
A_{h}
, and by
(2\lambda_{L}^{-4}+4\lambda_{L}^{-3})\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)|a_{\ell}(\gamma)|\leq(2\lambda_{L}^{-4}+4\lambda_{L}^{-3})\,\mathbb{E}|c^{*}(X,X^{\prime})|<\infty
for
\Delta
, whose
\ell
-th summand has second derivative
\Delta_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}=\bigl[6\mu_{\ell}^{2}/(\mu_{\ell}+\lambda)^{4}-4\mu_{\ell}/(\mu_{\ell}+\lambda)^{3}\bigr]a_{\ell}=2\mu_{\ell}(\mu_{\ell}-2\lambda)a_{\ell}/(\mu_{\ell}+\lambda)^{4}
, bounded through
\lvert\mu_{\ell}-2\lambda\rvert\leq\mu_{\ell}+2\lambda
,
\mu_{\ell}^{2}\leq\mu_{\ell}
(the trace bound), and
\lambda/(\mu_{\ell}+\lambda)\leq 1
; the last bound rests on
|a_{\ell}(\gamma)|\leq\mathbb{E}[|c^{*}(X,X^{\prime})|\phi_{\ell}(X;\gamma)^{2}]
(
2ab\leq a^{2}+b^{2}
, the symmetry of
c^{*}
in its arguments, and the exchangeability of the i.i.d. pair), on the Mercer identity
\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)\phi_{\ell}(x;\gamma)^{2}=k_{\gamma}(x,x)=1
, on monotone convergence, and on Assumptions
5
(i), (ii) and
7
(iii); and gives

\frac{d\lambda^{*}_{c}}{dc}=-\frac{\partial_{\lambda}A_{h}(\lambda^{*}_{c})}{\partial_{\lambda}^{2}D_{c}(\lambda^{*}_{c})}<0:

the drift-optimal
\lambda
is strictly decreasing in
c
while it is an interior minimizer.
At
c=0
the drift is
V+\Delta
, minimized at the largest admissible
\lambda
(the variance-optimal point).
At
c=1
the drift is
V+\Delta+A_{h}
, minimized at a point
\lambda^{*}_{1}
that lies strictly above
\lambda_{L}
whenever the signal slope at
\lambda_{L}
is dominated by the variance slope,
A_{h}^{\prime}(\lambda_{L})<|\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda_{L})|
(the minimizer may then sit at the right boundary
\lambda_{U}
as well, if
D_{1}^{\prime}(\lambda_{U})\leq 0
). The strictness is the first-order necessary condition at the left endpoint: were
D_{1}
minimized at
\lambda_{L}
, this condition would give
D_{1}^{\prime}(\lambda_{L})\geq 0
, contradicting
D_{1}^{\prime}(\lambda_{L})=\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda_{L})+A_{h}^{\prime}(\lambda_{L})<0
, the inequality combining the hypothesis
A_{h}^{\prime}(\lambda_{L})<|\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda_{L})|
with
\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda_{L})<0
.
Conversely, if
A_{h}^{\prime}(\lambda_{L})\geq|\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda_{L})|
and
D_{1}
is convex on
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
, then
D_{1}^{\prime}(\lambda_{L})=\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda_{L})+A_{h}^{\prime}(\lambda_{L})\geq 0
, since
A_{h}^{\prime}(\lambda_{L})\geq|\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda_{L})|\geq-\partial_{\lambda}(V+\Delta)(\lambda_{L})
; convexity makes
D_{1}^{\prime}
nondecreasing on
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
, so
D_{1}^{\prime}\geq 0
there,
D_{1}
is nondecreasing, and its minimizer over
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
is
\lambda_{L}
.
This additional condition is not implied by the standing assumptions alone, since
A_{h}
is not convex in
\lambda
in general: its second derivative
A_{h}^{\prime\prime}=\sum_{i}2\mu_{i}h_{i}^{2}(\mu_{i}-2\lambda)/(\mu_{i}+\lambda)^{4}
can be negative.
The statement of Theorem
12
(iii) is formulated accordingly.
In particular,
\lambda^{*}_{c}
decreases monotonically in
c
while interior, interpolating between the variance-optimal and the balanced configurations.
For intermediate
c
, since the drift
D_{c}
is minimized at a
\lambda
decreasing in
c
, and the captured energy
E_{h}=\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-A_{h}
is increasing as
\lambda
decreases (Proposition
3
), the drift of the selector’s limit law concentrates on configurations of increasing captured energy, equivalently of increasing noncentrality, as
c
grows.

Appendix C
Random Elements in Hilbert Spaces

The results of this section are quoted throughout Appendix
B
: the Hilbert-space law of large numbers supports the consistency arguments of Appendices
B.1
–
B.3
, the Hilbert-space central limit theorem underpins the functional limit laws of Appendix
B.3
, and the multiplier central limit theorem is invoked in the bootstrap proofs of Appendix
B.2
;
Hsing and Eubank [2015]
provides a comprehensive treatment.

Throughout this section,
\mathcal{H}
is separable with norm and inner product
\|\cdot\|
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
; random elements are understood with respect to
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, which coincides with the
\sigma
-field generated by the evaluation maps
x\mapsto\langle x,f\rangle
, so an
\mathcal{H}
-valued random element is measurable as soon as all coordinates
\langle X,f\rangle
are measurable, and its law is determined by these one-dimensional marginals
[
Hsing and Eubank, 2015
, Ch. 7]
. For
\mathbb{E}\|X\|<\infty
, the mean element is the Bochner integral
\mathbb{E}(X)=\int X\,dP
, characterized by
\langle\mathbb{E}(X),f\rangle=\mathbb{E}\langle X,f\rangle
; for
\mathbb{E}\|X\|^{2}<\infty
, the covariance operator
\Sigma=\mathbb{E}[(X-\mu)\otimes(X-\mu)]=\mathbb{E}[X\otimes X]-\mu\otimes\mu
is positive, self-adjoint, and trace-class.

Let
X_{1},X_{2},\dots
be i.i.d. random elements in
\mathcal{H}
with mean element
\mathbb{E}(X)
, and let
\bar{X}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
denote the sample mean. The following result records the strong law of large numbers and the central limit theorem for random elements in Hilbert spaces.

Theorem 13

If
\mathbb{E}(\|X\|)<\infty
, then

\bar{X}_{n}\xrightarrow{a.s.}\mathbb{E}(X).

If
\mathbb{E}(\|X\|^{2})<\infty
, then

\sqrt{n}(\bar{X}_{n}-\mathbb{E}(X))\xrightarrow{d}\xi,

where
\xi
is a Gaussian random element in
\mathcal{H}
with mean zero and covariance operator

\Sigma=\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mathbb{E}(X))\otimes(X-\mathbb{E}(X))\right].

The multiplier central limit theorem stated next is a key tool for establishing the consistency of the multiplier bootstrap procedure.

Theorem 14

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space (such as an RKHS). Let
X,X_{1},X_{2},\dots
be i.i.d. random elements in
\mathcal{H}
with
\mathbb{E}[\|X\|^{2}]<\infty
, mean element
\mu=\mathbb{E}[X]
, and covariance operator
\Sigma=\mathbb{E}[(X-\mu)\otimes(X-\mu)]
.

Let
V,V_{1},V_{2},\dots
be i.i.d. real-valued random variables independent of
X,X_{1},X_{2},\dots
, satisfying
\mathbb{E}[V]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[V^{2}]=1
. Define the multiplier process

S_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}(X_{i}-\mu).

Then, the conditional distribution of
S_{n}
given the data
\mathbf{X}_{n}=(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
converges weakly to a Gaussian random element
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
in
\mathcal{H}
almost surely. Furthermore, the same conclusion holds if the true mean
\mu
is replaced by the sample mean
\bar{X}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
, yielding the bootstrap multiplier process
\tilde{S}_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}(X_{i}-\bar{X}_{n})
.

Proof sketch.

Conditional fidi convergence follows from the conditional characteristic function

\hat{\phi}_{n}(tf)=\prod_{j\leq n}\phi_{V}(tY_{j}/\sqrt{n})
with
Y_{j}=\langle X_{j}-\mu,f\rangle
: the SLLN gives

n^{-1}\sum_{j}Y_{j}^{2}\to\sigma_{f}^{2}=\langle\Sigma f,f\rangle
and
\max_{j}Y_{j}^{2}/n\to 0
almost surely, so

\log\hat{\phi}_{n}(tf)\to-\tfrac{1}{2}t^{2}\sigma_{f}^{2}
a.s. for each
t
and each
f
in a countable dense set of
directions, which suffices by separability. Conditional tightness follows from

\mathbb{E}[\|(I-P_{K})S_{n}\|^{2}\mid\mathbf{X}_{n}]=n^{-1}\sum_{i}\|(I-P_{K})(X_{i}-\mu)\|^{2}\to\operatorname{tr}((I-P_{K})\Sigma)\to 0
as
K\to\infty
, together with the
K=0
boundedness bound;
Prokhorov’s theorem then identifies the conditional limit with
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
, Gaussian measures on
a separable Hilbert space being determined by their characteristic functionals. The sample-mean version
follows from
\tilde{S}_{n}=S_{n}-\bar{V}_{n}\sqrt{n}(\bar{X}_{n}-\mu)
with

\mathbb{E}[\|\bar{V}_{n}\sqrt{n}(\bar{X}_{n}-\mu)\|^{2}\mid\mathbf{X}_{n}]=\|\bar{X}_{n}-\mu\|^{2}=o_{a.s.}(1)
and
Slutsky’s theorem in
\mathcal{H}
. See
[
van der Vaart and Wellner, 1996
, Th. 2.9.2]
for this multiplier scheme in
generality.

Appendix D
Singular Value Decomposition of
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}

The propositions of this section are quoted by the rotated-eigenbasis proof of Appendix
B.1
and by the auxiliary lemmas of Appendix
B.3
.
The spectral analysis of
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}
rests on the following two propositions from
Rosasco et al. [2010]
.

Proposition 6

The following facts hold for the population operators.

1.

The operators
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
and
C
are positive, self-adjoint, compact, and trace-class. Consequently,
\sigma(\boldsymbol{L}_{k}),\sigma(C)\subset[0,\bar{k}]
, where
\sigma(\cdot)
denotes the spectrum.

2.

The non-zero spectra of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
and
C
coincide. If
\mu
is a non-zero eigenvalue with associated eigenfunctions
\phi
(in
L^{2}(\rho)
) and
e
(in
\mathcal{H}
), normalized to unit norm, then

\phi(x)=\mu^{-1/2}e(x)\text{ for }\mathbb{P}_{X}\text{-a.e. }x\in\mathcal{X},\quad e(x)=\mu^{-1/2}\int_{\mathcal{X}}k(x,x^{\prime})\phi(x^{\prime})\,d\rho(x^{\prime})\text{ for all }x\in\mathcal{X}.

3.

The spectral decompositions are given by:

\boldsymbol{L}_{k}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}\phi_{i}\otimes\phi_{i},\quad C=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i},

where
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
forms an orthonormal basis (ONB) of
\operatorname{Ker}(\boldsymbol{L}_{k})^{\perp}
, and
\{e_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
forms an ONB of
\operatorname{Ker}(C)^{\perp}
.

Remark 13

Under Assumption
1
, item 3 strengthens to:
\{e_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
is an ONB of all of

\mathcal{H}_{k}
and
\operatorname{ker}(C)=\{0\}
. Indeed,
\{\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
is a complete orthonormal
basis of
\mathcal{H}_{k}
, and
C(\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\phi_{i})=\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,\boldsymbol{L}_{k}\phi_{i}=\mu_{i}(\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\phi_{i})
, so each basis vector is a
C
-eigenvector with eigenvalue
\mu_{i}>0
;
expanding any
f\in\operatorname{ker}(C)
in this basis gives
a_{j}\mu_{j}=0
for every
j
, hence
f=0
. In
particular, when
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is infinite-dimensional (e.g. under the universality of Assumption
2
(i)),
C
has infinitely many strictly positive eigenvalues.

Proposition 7

The following facts hold for the empirical operators.

1.

The operator
C_{n}
is finite rank, self-adjoint, and positive semi-definite. The matrix
K/n
is symmetric and positive semi-definite. The spectrum
\sigma(C_{n})
contains finitely many non-zero elements, all in
[0,\bar{k}]
.

2.

The non-zero spectra of
K/n
and
C_{n}
coincide. If
\hat{\mu}
is a non-zero eigenvalue with associated eigenvector
\boldsymbol{u}
(in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
) and eigenfunction
\hat{e}
(in
\mathcal{H}
), normalized to unit norm,

\boldsymbol{u}=(n\hat{\mu})^{-1/2}\bigl(\hat{e}(x_{1}),\dots,\hat{e}(x_{n})\bigr)^{\top},\quad\hat{e}=(n\hat{\mu})^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}k(x_{i},\cdot),

where
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
is the
i
-th component of
\boldsymbol{u}
.

3.

The spectral decompositions are given by
K/n=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\nu_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\top}
and
C_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\nu_{i}\hat{e}_{i}\otimes\hat{e}_{i}
,
where
r=\operatorname{rank}(K)
,
\{\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
forms an ONB of
\operatorname{Ker}(K)^{\perp}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\{\hat{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
forms an ONB of
\operatorname{Ker}(C_{n})^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{H}
.

Remark 14

From Proposition
7
, under the scaled inner product
\langle a,b\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=n^{-1}a^{\top}b
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the restriction operator and its adjoint admit finite-dimensional SVD representations:
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\hat{e}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\sigma_{i}/n)\,\hat{e}_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
, where
\sigma_{i}^{2}
are the eigenvalues of the Gram matrix
\boldsymbol{K}
(equivalently
\sigma_{i}=\sqrt{n\nu_{i}}
, so that
\nu_{i}=\sigma_{i}^{2}/n
are the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{K}/n
as in the main text) and
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
are the Euclidean-unit eigenvectors.
The factor
1/n
in
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{\top}
reflects the adjoint with respect to the scaled inner product; the
\sigma_{i}
are the singular values of
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}
with respect to the
Euclidean
inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(with respect to the scaled inner product the singular values are
\sqrt{\nu_{i}}=\sigma_{i}/\sqrt{n}
and the left singular vectors are
\sqrt{n}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
).

Appendix E
Useful Non-asymptotic Results

The three results of this section are quoted repeatedly across Appendix
B
: the spectral-projection bound supports the orthogonal-transformations analysis of the next section; the bound on the empirical operators is invoked in the consistency, equivalence, bootstrap, and uniform-convergence proofs of Appendices
B.1
–
B.3
; and the eigenvalue bounds enter the rotated-eigenbasis proof of Appendix
B.1
.

E.1
Finite Sample Probability Bound for Spectral Projection Estimation

Lemma 25

Let
V
be an integer and
v
be the sum of the multiplicities of the first
V
distinct eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
, such that
\mu^{(1)}\geq\mu^{(2)}\geq\dots\geq\mu^{(v)}>\mu^{(v+1)}
. Let
r=\operatorname{rank}(K)
and
\{\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
be the eigenvectors corresponding to the non-zero eigenvalues of
K
in decreasing order. Denote by
\{\hat{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
the corresponding Nyström extensions (Proposition
7
).

Given
\tau>0
, if the sample size
n
satisfies
n>128\bar{k}^{2}\tau/(\mu^{(v)}-\mu^{(v+1)})^{2}
, then with probability at least
1-2e^{-\tau}
, we have

\left\|\hat{P}_{v}-P_{v}\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}\leq\frac{32\bar{k}^{2}\tau}{n(\mu^{(v)}-\mu^{(v+1)})^{2}},

where
P_{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{v}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}
,
\hat{P}_{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{v}\hat{e}_{i}\otimes\hat{e}_{i}
, and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
denotes the Hilbert–Schmidt norm.

Proof.
See Theorem 12 of
Rosasco et al. [2010]
.

E.2
Bound on the Second-Moment Operator

Lemma 26

The operators
C
and
C_{n}
are Hilbert-Schmidt. In addition, with confidence
1-2\exp(-\tau)
, we have

\|C-C_{n}\|_{HS}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}\bar{k}\sqrt{\tau}}{\sqrt{n}}.

Proof.
See Theorem 7 of
Rosasco et al. [2010]
.

E.3
Bounds on the Eigenvalues

Lemma 27

Let
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
and
\{\sigma^{2}_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
be the at most countable families of eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{L}_{k}
and
K
, respectively, where
\sigma^{2}_{i}=0
for
i>r
. Then, for any
\tau>0
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-\tau}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\mu_{i}-\sigma^{2}_{i}/n\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8\bar{k}^{2}\tau}{n},

\displaystyle\sup_{i\geq 1}\left|\mu_{i}-\sigma^{2}_{i}/n\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}\bar{k}\sqrt{\tau}}{\sqrt{n}}.

Proof.
See Propositions 10 and 11 of
Rosasco et al. [2010]
.

Appendix F
Orthogonal Transformations

The random orthogonal matrices constructed here are exactly those appearing in the rotated-eigenbasis expansion of Lemma
1
, proved in Appendix
B.1
.
The alignment between the empirical and population subspaces (
\hat{E}
and
E
) can be characterized explicitly via an orthogonal transformation. Define the
v\times v
matrix
M
by
M_{ij}=\langle e_{i},\hat{e}_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
(the inner product of the
i
-th population eigenfunction with the
j
-th empirical eigenfunction), and consider two functions
f\in\hat{E}\subset\mathcal{H}_{k}
and
g\in E\subset\mathcal{H}_{k}
expressed in the respective bases,

f=\sum_{i=1}^{v}v_{i}\,\hat{e}_{i},\qquad g=\sum_{j=1}^{v}u_{j}\,e_{j},

normalized so that
\lVert v\rVert_{2}=\lVert u\rVert_{2}=1
. The cosine of the angle between the two functions is their inner product,
\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{v}v_{i}u_{j}\langle e_{j},\hat{e}_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sum_{j=1}^{v}u_{j}(Mv)_{j}=u^{\top}Mv
; hence the singular values
\{\tilde{\sigma}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{v}
of
M
are the cosines of the principal angles between
E
and
\hat{E}
, and
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}\in[0,1]
.

Let
P_{v}
and
\hat{P}_{v}
denote the orthogonal projections onto
E
and
\hat{E}
. The matrix of the operator
\hat{P}_{v}P_{v}
restricted to
\hat{E}
, in the empirical basis, is

\langle\hat{e}_{i},\hat{P}_{v}P_{v}\,\hat{e}_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\langle\hat{e}_{i},P_{v}\hat{e}_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sum_{k=1}^{v}\langle\hat{e}_{i},e_{k}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\langle e_{k},\hat{e}_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=(M^{\top}M)_{ij},

so the eigenvalues of
M^{\top}M
are precisely the squared singular values
\{\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}\}_{i=1}^{v}
of
M
. The squared Hilbert–Schmidt norm of the difference of the projections expands as

\displaystyle\lVert P_{v}-\hat{P}_{v}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\bigl((P_{v}-\hat{P}_{v})^{2}\bigr)=\operatorname{Tr}(P_{v})+\operatorname{Tr}(\hat{P}_{v})-2\operatorname{Tr}(P_{v}\hat{P}_{v})

\displaystyle=2v-2\operatorname{Tr}(M^{\top}M)=2\sum_{i=1}^{v}(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}),

and Lemma
25
gives
\lVert P_{v}-\hat{P}_{v}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=O_{p}(n^{-1})
, so
\sum_{i=1}^{v}\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}=v-O_{p}(n^{-1})
.

Let
M=\boldsymbol{U}\Sigma\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}
be the singular value decomposition, with
\boldsymbol{U},\boldsymbol{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{v\times v}
orthogonal and
\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(\tilde{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\sigma}_{v})
. Construct the random orthogonal matrix
O_{n}:=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}
, so that
O_{n}^{\top}
is the orthogonal Procrustes solution (
\arg\max_{O\in O(v)}\operatorname{Tr}(MO)=\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}
), and define the rotated empirical eigenfunctions
\tilde{e}_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{v}(O_{n})_{ij}\,\hat{e}_{j}
. Then

\langle e_{i},\tilde{e}_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sum_{k=1}^{v}(O_{n})_{jk}\langle e_{i},\hat{e}_{k}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=(MO_{n}^{\top})_{ij},

and
MO_{n}^{\top}=\boldsymbol{U}\Sigma\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}
is symmetric positive semidefinite, so

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{v}\lVert e_{i}-\tilde{e}_{i}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\bigl(v-\operatorname{Tr}(\boldsymbol{U}\Sigma\boldsymbol{U}^{\top})\bigr)=2\Bigl(v-\sum_{i=1}^{v}\tilde{\sigma}_{i}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2\Bigl(v-\sum_{i=1}^{v}\tilde{\sigma}_{i}^{2}\Bigr)=O_{p}(n^{-1}),

using
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}\in[0,1]
. Since
v
is finite,
\lVert e_{i}-\tilde{e}_{i}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=O_{p}(n^{-1})
for each
i
: the rotated empirical eigenfunctions are consistent estimators of the population eigenfunctions at the parametric rate. Finally, orthogonal transformations preserve the Euclidean norm, so the squared norm of the projection of any
f\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
onto the empirical subspace is invariant to the rotation:

\sum_{i=1}^{v}\langle f,\hat{e}_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{v}\langle f,\tilde{e}_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}.
