G.1
Sensitivity to the Sample-Splitting Ratio

The main protocol fixes the split at
15\%
selection /
85\%
testing (Section
4.1
). This subsection varies the splitting ratio while holding the
testing
sample size fixed at
400
, so that the comparison across ratios isolates the effect of the split on the test itself rather than on its testing budget. For each testing share
t\in\{0.5,0.6,0.7,0.8,0.85,0.9\}
, the total sample is the
N
closest to
400/t
and the selection sample has
N-400
observations, namely
400,267,171,100,71,44
. The configuration
t=0.85
is the main protocol of Section
5
and is marked by a dotted vertical line in Figure
3
.

Figure
3
separates the fixed alternatives (
\mathrm{DGP}_{1}
–
\mathrm{DGP}_{5}
), whose misspecification does not vanish with the sample size, from the local alternative (
\mathrm{DGP}_{6}
), whose misspecification
D=1.5\,\lVert X\rVert^{2}/\sqrt{N}
implements the total-rate Pitman shift of Section
4.1
. For the local designs, the effective signal at testing share
t
is
h_{c}=\sqrt{t}\,h
, attenuated by the testing share as in Section
4.6
. As
t\to 0
the effective signal vanishes; as
t\to 1
the test sees the full signal while the selector, asymptotically blind to the alternative, follows its null law. The limiting power is of order one throughout. Both endpoints lie outside the tested grid; panels (c) and (d) verify the order-one character and the intermediate behavior.

Size.

The empirical size is robust to the splitting ratio. At the
5\%
level the rejection rates under the null design range from
0.038
to
0.055
at
d=10
and from
0.039
to
0.069
at
d=20
(
\mathrm{DGP}_{0}
), within two to three simulation standard errors of the nominal level at every ratio. The mild conservatism at the largest testing shares (
t\geq 0.85
), where the CV choice rests on
44
–
71
selection observations, mirrors the small-selection-sample effect documented for
n=200
in the main simulations; the
d=20
cell at
t=0.5
is mildly liberal (
0.069
). The invariance of size to the allocation of the sample is the empirical counterpart of Theorem
8
, which requires no selector limit and holds for any split.

Power under fixed alternatives.

With the testing budget held fixed, the power of the fixed-alternative designs is largely insensitive to the split (panels (a) and (b) of Figure
3
). The saturated designs are flat at every ratio (
\mathrm{DGP}_{3}
:
1.000
at
d=10
and at least
0.996
at
d=20
), and the intermediate designs move by at most a few points between
t=0.5
(selection sample of
400
) and
t=0.9
(selection sample of
44
):
\mathrm{DGP}_{1}
:
0.950\to 0.933
at
d=10
and
0.397\to 0.357
at
d=20
;
\mathrm{DGP}_{2}
:
0.195\to 0.179
at
d=20
, while at
d=10
it is the only design whose power
rises
with the testing share (
0.316\to 0.398
);
\mathrm{DGP}_{4}
:
0.282\to 0.283
at
d=10
and
0.569\to 0.522
at
d=20
. The designs for which the tuning choice matters most show the largest declines. The tuning is cheap in data: with
44
selection observations the rejection rates are within a few points of the oracle protocol of Section
5
, which spends
200
selection observations at the same testing size (
\mathrm{DGP}_{1}
:
0.933
vs.
0.915
at
d=10
;
\mathrm{DGP}_{5}
:
0.737
vs.
0.747
at
d=10
and
0.372
vs.
0.387
at
d=20
), with one exception:
\mathrm{DGP}_{4}
at
d=20
, where the larger selection sample raises power from
0.522
to
0.596
.

Power under local alternatives.

The local design
\mathrm{DGP}_{6}
implements the total-rate Pitman shift
D=1.5\,\lVert X\rVert^{2}/\sqrt{N}
; the amplitude
1.5
was chosen so that the limit power at the CV-selected configuration is non-degenerate and the split-ratio dependence is empirically resolvable without saturating the test. The observed
5\%
rejection rates at the largest testing shares exceed those at
t=0.5
: from
0.193
at
t=0.5
to
0.247
at
t=0.9
for
\mathrm{DGP}_{6}
at
d=10
, and from
0.205
to
0.253
at
d=20
(
\hat{T}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
). Across the grid the rates range over
0.19
–
0.30
(panels (c) and (d) of Figure
3
), moving in the direction of the signal attenuation: the effective signal
h_{c}=\sqrt{t}\,h
increases in
t
, and by Proposition
4
(i) the pointwise power is non-decreasing in the captured-signal scale at fixed tuning. The rates at
t=0.8
–
0.9
exceed those at
t=0.5
in every cell, and the values are consistent with the oracle protocol at the same testing size (
\mathrm{DGP}_{6}
:
0.215
at
d=10
and
0.251
at
d=20
).

Figure 3
:
Rejection rates at the
5\%
level for the projection tests
\hat{T}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
(solid lines) and
\hat{T}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{proj},\perp}
(dashed lines) with the testing sample size fixed at
400
and the testing share
t
on the horizontal axis, one line per alternative DGP: fixed alternatives (
\mathrm{DGP}_{1}
–
\mathrm{DGP}_{5}
) in panels (a) and (b), the local alternative (
\mathrm{DGP}_{6}
) in panels (c) and (d). The dotted vertical line marks the main protocol
t=0.85
; the dashed horizontal line marks the nominal
5\%
level.

G.2
Numerical Verification of the Standing Monotonicity Condition

Condition
1
of Section
4.6
imposes a per-direction clause on the noise component of the selector’s limit criterion,
D(\eta):=V(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)
; the strict decrease of
D
in
\lambda
on
\Upsilon
is the consequence consumed by Theorem
12
(ii). This subsection derives the exact per-direction drift formula of which the clause of Condition
1
is the termwise sufficient sign condition, proves that the clause implies the strict decrease consumed by Theorem
12
, exhibits the automaticity of the clause under homoscedasticity, and verifies the clause numerically for the Gaussian kernel against the simulation designs of Section
5
, by empirical spectral approximation of the integral operator of
k_{\gamma}
against
P_{X}=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. On an iid sample
X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}
with Gram matrix eigendecomposition
K=U\operatorname{diag}(\nu)U^{\top}
, the empirical analogues of the drift components are

V(\eta)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}w_{\ell}^{2}\,\tau_{\ell},\qquad\Delta(\eta)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\big(w_{\ell}^{2}-2w_{\ell}\big)\,a_{\ell},\qquad w_{\ell}:=\frac{\mu_{\ell}}{\mu_{\ell}+\lambda},\ \ \mu_{\ell}:=\frac{\nu_{\ell}}{N},

with
\tau_{\ell}:=u_{\ell}^{\top}\operatorname{diag}(\sigma^{2}(X_{i}))\,u_{\ell}
and
a_{\ell}:=u_{\ell}^{\top}C^{*}u_{\ell}/N
, where
C^{*}_{ij}:=c^{*}(X_{i},X_{j})
is the projection-correction kernel of Section
4
; the sample sizes are
N=1200
(
d=10
) and
N=1000
(
d=20
). The grid mirrors the selection protocol of Section
4.1
: 20 values of
\gamma
in
[\gamma_{\max}/100,\gamma_{\max}]
with
\gamma_{\max}=2/\mathrm{median}\{\lVert X_{i}-X_{j}\rVert^{2}\}
, and
\lambda\in[1,10]
, checked on both the 10-point grid of the simulations and a 41-point refinement. The simulation designs of Section
5
are conditional-mean models

Y=\alpha+X^{\top}\beta+D(X)+\sigma\,e,\qquad X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),\qquad e\perp\!\!\!\perp X,\qquad e\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),

with
\alpha=1
,
\beta=0.5\,\mathbf{1}_{d}
, and
\sigma=1
. The null design takes
D\equiv 0
(the linear design
\mathrm{DGP}_{0}
of Section
5
), so that
\varepsilon_{0}:=Y-\alpha-X^{\top}\beta
obeys
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{0}\mid X]=0
with conditional variance
\sigma^{2}(X)\equiv 1
, and the score of the residual with respect to
\theta=(\alpha,\beta^{\top})^{\top}
is
\nabla_{\theta}\varepsilon=-(1,X^{\top})
, an
X
-measurable direction with second-moment matrix
\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}=\operatorname{diag}(1,I_{d})
. The alternative designs add a misspecification
D
(e.g.
D(x)=1.5\exp(-(x^{\top}\beta)^{2})
or
D(x)=2\cos(1.2\pi\lVert x\rVert)
); these enter only the alternative drift
A(\eta)
of Theorem
12
(i) and play no role in the null drift of (ii) under verification. For the affine score, the projection-correction kernel of Section
4
reduces to the closed forms

c^{*}(x,x^{\prime})=-(1+x^{\top}x^{\prime})\quad(\sigma^{2}\equiv 1),\qquad c^{*}(x,x^{\prime})=-\big(\sigma^{2}(x)+\sigma^{2}(x^{\prime})\big)(1+x^{\top}x^{\prime})+1+\tfrac{d+1}{d}\,x^{\top}x^{\prime},

with

\sigma^{2}(x)=\tfrac{1+\lVert x\rVert^{2}/d}{2},

and for the location-model score
g_{\theta_{0}}(x)=-1
simply
c^{*}\equiv-\sigma^{2}
under homoscedasticity.

The drift
V(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)
depends on the design only through
P_{X}
,
\sigma^{2}(\cdot)
, and the score span
g_{\theta_{0}}
; the check therefore covers eight configurations:
d\in\{10,20\}
with homoscedastic noise (
\sigma^{2}\equiv 1
, the simulation setting) and heteroscedastic noise (
\sigma^{2}(x)=(1+\lVert x\rVert^{2}/d)/2
), and four score spans: the simulation score
g_{\theta_{0}}(x)=-(1,x^{\top})
, the location-model score
g_{\theta_{0}}(x)=-1
, the quadratic span
g_{\theta_{0}}(x)=-\big(1,\,x^{\top},\,(\lVert x\rVert^{2}-d)/\sqrt{2d}\,\big)
, and the no-nuisance case (
\Delta\equiv 0
).

An exact derivative formula and a structural condition.
The correction terms of Section
4.2
diagonalize in the Mercer basis: expanding
g_{\eta}(x,y)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}w_{\ell}(\lambda)\phi_{\ell}(x;\gamma)\phi_{\ell}(y;\gamma)
,
w_{\ell}(\lambda):=\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)/(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)+\lambda)
, gives
\nu(\eta)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}w_{\ell}a_{\ell}
and
\rho(\eta)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}w_{\ell}^{2}a_{\ell}
with
a_{\ell}(\gamma):=\mathbb{E}[c^{*}(X,X^{\prime})\phi_{\ell}(X;\gamma)\phi_{\ell}(X^{\prime};\gamma)]
(the
X_{1}
-integration in
\rho
is the identity,
\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\ell}(X_{1})\phi_{k}(X_{1})]=\delta_{\ell k}
), so that, together with
V(\eta)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}w_{\ell}^{2}\tau_{\ell}(\gamma)
,

D(\eta)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\bigl[w_{\ell}^{2}\tau_{\ell}+(w_{\ell}^{2}-2w_{\ell})a_{\ell}\bigr],\qquad\partial_{\lambda}D(\eta)=-2\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{\ell}^{2}\tau_{\ell}-\lambda\mu_{\ell}a_{\ell}}{(\mu_{\ell}+\lambda)^{3}},

where the two
a_{\ell}
-terms of the derivative combine into
2\lambda\mu_{\ell}a_{\ell}/(\mu_{\ell}+\lambda)^{3}
, giving the displayed numerator. Condition
1
is the termwise sufficient condition for the sign of this display: if
a_{\ell}(\gamma)\leq\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)\tau_{\ell}(\gamma)/\lambda_{U}
for every
\ell
with
a_{\ell}(\gamma)>0
, then on
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
each numerator
\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)\bigl(\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)\tau_{\ell}(\gamma)-\lambda\,a_{\ell}(\gamma)\bigr)\geq 0
, so
\partial_{\lambda}D(\eta)\leq 0
on
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
with strict inequality on
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U})
whenever some
\tau_{\ell}(\gamma)>0
; hence
D(\eta)
is strictly decreasing in
\lambda
on
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
for every
\gamma
, the minimizer conclusion of Theorem
12
(ii) holds, and the boundary comparison at
\lambda_{L}
in Theorem
12
(iii) carries the strict sign. The per-direction clause is the structural form of this sufficient condition: the per-direction requirement
w_{\ell}\tau_{\ell}\geq(1-w_{\ell})a_{\ell}
, binding at or before the upper endpoint
\lambda_{U}
where
w_{\ell}
is smallest. In words, the projection-correlation overlap
a_{\ell}
of each Mercer direction with itself must not exceed the direction’s own noise variance
\mu_{\ell}\tau_{\ell}
divided by the largest admissible regularization. This per-direction condition is conservative: it is sufficient but not necessary, because directions with
a_{\ell}<0
contribute negative numerators
-\lambda\mu_{\ell}a_{\ell}
that can offset directions with
a_{\ell}>0
. The condition is automatic under homoscedasticity,
\sigma^{2}\equiv\bar{\sigma}^{2}
: then
c^{*}(x,x^{\prime})=-\bar{\sigma}^{2}g_{\theta_{0}}(x)^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}g_{\theta_{0}}(x^{\prime})
, hence
a_{\ell}=-\bar{\sigma}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\ell}(X;\gamma)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)]^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\ell}(X;\gamma)g_{\theta_{0}}(X)]\leq 0
for every
\ell
, and
D(\gamma,\cdot)
is strictly decreasing for every kernel, every design, and every dimension. In the location model,
g_{\theta_{0}}\equiv\pm 1
,
c^{*}(x,x^{\prime})=\bar{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}(x)-\sigma^{2}(x^{\prime})
and
a_{\ell}=m_{\ell}(\bar{\sigma}^{2}m_{\ell}-2\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}\phi_{\ell}])
with
m_{\ell}:=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\ell}]
: here
a_{\ell}
can be positive, e.g. when
\sigma^{2}
is small where
\phi_{\ell}
is large, so the monotonicity is not automatic and the structural condition above is the operative sufficient condition. The large-
\lambda
asymptotics of the derivative follow from the exact formula: by the Mercer expansion
\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\mu_{\ell}(\gamma)a_{\ell}(\gamma)=\mathbb{E}[c^{*}(X_{1},X_{2})\,k_{\gamma}(X_{1},X_{2})]
, so
\partial_{\lambda}D(\eta)=2\lambda^{-2}\,\mathbb{E}\big[c^{*}(X_{1},X_{2})\,k_{\gamma}(X_{1},X_{2})\big]+O(\lambda^{-3})
. Under homoscedasticity,
c^{*}(x,x^{\prime})=-\bar{\sigma}^{2}g_{\theta_{0}}(x)^{\top}\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1}g_{\theta_{0}}(x^{\prime})
is a negative semidefinite kernel (a Gram matrix of
\Gamma_{\theta_{0}}^{-1/2}g_{\theta_{0}}
), so
\mathbb{E}[c^{*}(X_{1},X_{2})k_{\gamma}(X_{1},X_{2})]\leq 0
for every score span and every dimension, and the leading term is nonpositive. In the location model
g_{\theta_{0}}\equiv\pm 1
the coefficient specializes to
\bar{\sigma}^{2}\mathbb{E}[p]-2\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}p]
, giving
\partial_{\lambda}D(\eta)=-2\bigl(2\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}p]-\bar{\sigma}^{2}\mathbb{E}[p]\bigr)/\lambda^{2}+O(\lambda^{-3})
with
p(x):=\mathbb{E}[k_{\gamma}(x,X)]\geq 0
, which has a nonpositive leading term whenever
\bar{\sigma}^{2}\leq 2\min\sigma^{2}
(as
2\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{2}p]\geq 2\min\sigma^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[p]
); all configurations of Table
5
satisfy
\bar{\sigma}^{2}\leq 2\min\sigma^{2}
. The threshold concerns only the leading term of the large-
\lambda
expansion and does not by itself control
\partial_{\lambda}D
on a finite interval
[\lambda_{L},\lambda_{U}]
, where the
O(\lambda^{-3})
remainder and the intermediate-
\lambda
per-direction terms could in principle change the sign; the finite-
\lambda
behavior for the designs considered is covered by the numerical checks of Table
5
.

Table
5
reports, for each configuration, the minimal relative gap between consecutive grid values,

\min_{\gamma,\,j}\frac{D(\gamma,\lambda_{j+1})-D(\gamma,\lambda_{j})}{D(\gamma,\lambda_{j})},

computed over all 20 grid values of
\gamma
on the 41-point grid. In every configuration the drift is strictly decreasing in
\lambda
at every
\gamma
: the minimal relative gap ranges from
-5.5\%
to
-10.7\%
(attained at the largest grid value of
\gamma
), the 10-point grid of the simulations yields gaps of at least
22\%
in magnitude, and
D
loses between
82\%
and
99\%
of its value over
\lambda\in[1,10]
. Figure
4
displays the drift as a function of
\lambda
at the smallest, middle, and largest grid values of
\gamma
for four representative configurations.

with

;

\sigma^{2}\equiv 1
, heterosc. means
\sigma^{2}(x)=(1+\lVert x\rVert^{2}/d)/2
. “Strictly decreasing” holds if
D(\gamma,\lambda_{j+1})<D(\gamma,\lambda_{j})
at every consecutive pair
(j,\gamma)
on the grid. The spectral sample sizes are
N=1200
(
d=10
) and
N=1000
(
d=20
).

Figure 4
:
The drift
D(\eta)=V(\eta)+\Delta(\eta)
as a function of
\lambda
at the smallest, middle, and largest grid values of
\gamma
, for four configurations:
d=10,20
with homoscedastic noise and the linear-regression score (the simulation designs),
d=10
with heteroscedastic noise, and the location model. The drift is strictly decreasing in
\lambda
at every
\gamma
in all configurations.
